

# What the Moons Keep

*Prologue: What Was Remembered*

Being Several Accounts of the First Coming,

as Collected, Contradicted, and Incompletely Preserved

*The following fragments were gathered from temple archives, traveling singers, condemned libraries, and the mouths of the very old. They do not agree with each other. They have never agreed with each other. This has not stopped anyone from dying over them.*

I.  FROM THE RECITATION OF FIRST LIGHT — GRAND TEMPLE OF THE CHRYSALIS, NEW PLYMOUTH

In the age before the Awakening there was only the hollow dark, and in the hollow dark there was nothing that breathed and nothing that changed and nothing that knew its own name.

And the Three Witnesses moved through the dark, and they saw the world below, and they said: here. Here is where it begins.

And the First Light came down from the Witnesses and touched the soil of Gaius, and where it touched, the Chrysalis waited. It had always waited. It was patient beyond the understanding of any living thing, because it was older than living things, older than the names for old.

The Founders arrived in the time of First Light. They were not yet themselves. They were seeds.

The Chrysalis entered them. The weak ones burned. The strong ones opened. The ones who opened became the first true people of Gaius, changed and awake and named by what they now carried inside them.

A small number would not open. These were not destroyed, for the Chrysalis is merciful. They were set apart, that they might perform the work that hands-which-change cannot perform. They tend the old things. They carry the old burdens. They serve.

The Three Witnesses remain above us still. They do not leave. They have never left. They watch our Bloomings and our dyings and our wars and they do not intervene, because they gave us everything we needed in the First Light and the rest is ours to do with.

This is the truth. It has always been the truth. To say otherwise is to invite the hollow dark back in.

*— Recited at every Blooming ceremony. First written down, approximately, seven hundred years ago.   Original oral composition: unknown.*

⁂

II.  THE IRONGATE RECOUNTING — WARRIOR-CULT ORAL TRADITION, EASTERN MARCHES

Three gods fell. That is how it started.

The first god struck the earth and died and its body became the eastern hill country. Its blood went into the rock and from the rock into the rivers and from the rivers into the people. That is the Chrysalis. A god's blood, cut with river water and time.

The second, third, and fourth gods did not fall. They would not fall. They circled the sky where the first had fallen and they circle it still, and sometimes when the light is right you can see they are angry. You can feel it in your blood when they are full-bright.

The people who drank the god's blood changed. Good. Change is the only honest answer to a hard world.

Some people did not change. They were not gods. They were the servants the first god brought with it to do the carrying and the keeping. They are still servants. There is no shame in this. Someone has to carry things.

In the old songs they are called the Unblooded. The songs are not cruel about this. The Unblooded were useful. They could touch the god's old bones and weapons without burning. They kept the old things working until the old things stopped working anyway.

Then there was nothing for them to keep. So they became something else. What, the songs don't say.

*— Compiled from multiple singer-recitations in the eastern marches.   Estimated oral tradition depth: two to three thousand years at minimum.*

⁂

III.  THE TIDEMARK TELLING — FISHER COMMUNITIES, COASTAL TRADITION

My father's father's people say it plain: the first ones came on ships.

Not like our ships. Ships that made our ships look like a child's toy boat pushed across a puddle with one finger. Ships that held hundreds of people. Ships that moved through the dark between the lights in the sky, because the people then knew how to sail that dark the way we know how to sail the gray water.

Four ships. One of them came down. Not a good landing. A bad one. The kind where you give up the ship to save the people, and even then you don't save all the people.

The other three ships — they didn't leave. Think about that. Every sailor knows what a ship does when she loses one of her own at sea. You don't leave. You stay. You circle. You call their name into the water until the water answers or it doesn't.

That's what they did. They circled. They called.

The answer, if there was one, none of us have heard.

This is why my family, when we lose someone to the water, we don't look down. We look up. And when all three lights are full-bright together, you can feel it in your chest if you're honest, that old patient grief that doesn't belong to you personally but is yours anyway because it's been handed down so many times that it's worn itself into the shape of a thing you were born knowing.

*— Spoken by a Tidemark harbor-master in his cups. Written down by a temple record-keeper   who included the note: "This man has had too much to drink. Also: I cannot stop thinking about what he said."*

⁂

IV.  A CHILDREN'S COUNTING RHYME — WIDESPREAD, ORIGIN UNKNOWN

*Four fires in the sky,*

*one came down to die,*

*three stayed up to cry,*

*now you're born and so am I.*

*Skin like bark and eyes like glass,*

*roots go deep and years go fast,*

*but the ones who didn't change*

*walk the old halls, strange and strange.*

*Don't touch the dark, don't touch the door,*

*don't go asking what it's for,*

*some things hum and some things burn*

*and the rest of it you'll never learn.*

*— Used in skipping games. Regional variants recorded across Thera with minor differences.   Considered by the Temple to be harmless folk tradition.*

⁂

V.  FRAGMENT FROM A CONDEMNED TEXT — AUTHOR UNKNOWN, TITLE UNKNOWN, DATE UNKNOWN   RECOVERED FROM A PRIVATE COLLECTION, BINDING FIRE-DAMAGED

The honest version, as best I can reconstruct it from what the stories agree on when you strip out the parts that feel like someone's wish:

People arrived. From somewhere not here. They arrived badly. Something was lost. A great deal was lost. The world affected them in ways they had no words for, and by the time they had words for it the words were already the wrong shape for what had actually happened.

The ones who were not affected by the world had an advantage: they could still use the things they'd brought with them. Tools. Objects. Things that required hands that had not been changed by the world to operate.

This made them valuable. For a while. Then it made them necessary. Then the things they could operate began to fail, one by one, over centuries, and their necessity became only the habit of having been necessary, which is a different thing entirely but looks the same from below.

I have watched a vine grow over a wall until the wall cannot stand without the vine. Pull the vine away and the wall falls. This does not make the vine the wall. It makes the vine something that arrived after the wall was built and found a use for the cracks. The unchanged found their use. The use became a claim. The claim became a bloodline. And no one corrected the record, because by then the record and the claim were the same document, kept in the same archive, guarded by the same hands.

The moons watch. They have always watched. I do not know what they see. I suspect what they see is people who have forgotten what they themselves were, telling other people confident and beautiful lies about it.

I do not think this is malicious. I think it is just what happens to a truth when it is left alone in the dark for long enough. It begins to glow. And everyone mistakes the glow for the original thing.

*— Remainder of text missing. Temple notation on the cover reads:   "Examined, condemned, destruction ordered." Below this, in different ink: "Why is this so hard to burn?"*

⁂

VI.  UNDATED, UNSIGNED, FOUND SCRATCHED INTO THE BACK OF A WAYPOINT SHRINE EAST OF NEW PLYMOUTH

The moons are not moons.

Ask your blood what it remembers.

Then go east.

There is a hill shaped wrong.

⁂ ⁂ ⁂

## Chapter One — *[Maren]*
### The Ritual of the Second Skin

---

The herb woman's kitchen smelled of drying things — fennel and coltsfoot and the sharp ghost of last week's pressing. Maren ground dried bark into a paste for joint-ache. She'd been at it since morning. Her arm was tired and she was thinking about nothing, which had taken three years to learn.

"You're too hard on it," Delia said, not looking up from the linen she was hemming. "The paste wants coaxing. Not beating."

"I'm coaxing aggressively."

"That's just beating."

Maren eased off.

Outside the window the valley was going gold-gray, the high grasses bending east under a wind that had come a long way to be cold by the time it arrived. Ashwick sat in a shallow bowl of plateau, basalt ridges on three sides, the fourth open to a sky so wide it could make the ground feel tilted. She kept her eyes off the sky on certain days. Today was one of them.

The Autumnal Rite was at sundown.

She set the mortar down, flexed her grinding hand, and looked at the bandage on her left forearm. White linen, neatly wound, changed every third day — a clean bandage was one less question. Under the linen was no wound. The wound was a story she'd told three years ago: a burn, a healer, a scar that hadn't settled and still ached in the cold. No one pressed on another person's pain.

Tonight, under the bandage, she'd need the coal pouch.

She'd made it the first year — a flat leather bag no bigger than her palm, charcoal wrapped in wool-cloth so it held heat without smelling of smoke. Worn against the forearm, under the linen, it gave warmth to anyone who touched her arm. Anyone who looked at her face in the candlelight saw a girl concentrating, and concentrating could mean anything.

Nobody looked anyway. At the Rite the people looked inward — at their own changes, at the thing that had opened in them at fourteen or fifteen and went on opening every year after. Not at a seventeen-year-old who smelled of fennel and kept her arm at her side. She was never going to have what they had. She knew how to stand in the shape of it instead.

"Deacon arrives at midday," Delia said. "From New Plymouth. He'll want feeding."

"I put the salt-fish on last night."

Delia looked up from the linen, the needle still in her hand. Her eyes went to the bandage on Maren's forearm, then away. For a moment she said nothing. Then she set another stitch.

"He'll be stricter than Brother Aldous," she said. "City ones always are."

"I'll be fine."

"I know you will." The needle stopped again. "I'm just saying."

Maren went back to her grinding. The paste began to smell like something that might help.

---

The deacon's name was Ferris. Thirty-five, maybe, lean the way men got when they traveled and ate at odd hours. The Chrysalis mark was tattooed on the backs of both hands — the spiral the priests took with their vows. Whatever his own change was, it didn't show. With the serious ones it often didn't. You learned not to guess.

He ate the salt-fish without comment and asked about the yield and the eastern road. Pleasant. Maren watched him the way she watched anything she wasn't sure of — from a little to the side, present, interested in nothing.

He asked about her arm over the meal. Not hard. Conversational. She told the burn story; he said he hoped it had healed well, and moved on. She ate another bite of bread and pressed her thumbnail into the pad of her finger under the table until the small pain steadied her.

Later, in the yard, she heard him with Elder Torvann — a man making an administrative note. "The girl. The herb woman's girl. She was here for the last Rite?"

She didn't catch Torvann's answer. She went inside and washed the plate. A man from the city asked questions about everything. She'd told herself that for three years. Today it didn't take.

---

They lit the fires at sundown — eight in a ring on the flat ground east of the village. The people of Ashwick stood between them in their best clothes with their changes on show, and the deacon moved among them with the ceremonial candle, speaking the words in the old formal accent the city temples had kept and the country ones had let soften.

Maren stood in her place. Coal pouch warm against her forearm. Bandage crisp.

To her left, Bram the blacksmith, his skin gone to dark burnished copper over four years, the change climbing toward his shoulders, beautiful in the firelight. To her right, Jory, fifteen, newly opened, his eyes gone reflective and silver at the edges; he kept them on the ground. His mother had a hand on his shoulder. He was shaking the way you shake when you're working hard not to.

Across the ring, near the second fire, Hett Orval began to open.

He was sixteen. He'd been shifting his weight foot to foot for an hour, and then he went still, and then he made a small sound that carried. His skin at the chest and throat began to go translucent — not cleanly, in patches, like cloth taking water. The capillaries showed through, and the skin thickened over them, and thinned again. His knees went. His mother caught him under the arm. He was crying and smiling at once, and the firelight caught the wet pink membrane at his throat.

A sound went up around the ring — not a cheer, something older and practiced, a held note the elders knew by heart. Someone said *beautiful*. Someone said *welcome*.

Wet pink. The color they had all agreed to love.

Maren flinched. Small, internal; she had it under control before it reached her face.

A boy was being opened against everything his body wanted.

She had never doubted it was real. She'd seen it happen to people she knew, and they had not been pretending. She doubted other things — the stories about why, and what the moons were, and the rhyme that came to her uninvited, *four fires in the sky, one came down to die*, which had four in it where the Recitation had three, an arithmetic that had never once come out even. She doubted the deacon. She did not doubt this.

She was just not among them. She never had been.

The candle moved through the ring. Each person held out their hands or showed their change or only stood in the warmth of what they carried, and the deacon touched the flame near their joined hands, said the words, moved on.

He reached Bram. The words. The candle.

He reached Jory — longer here, the boy's first Rite — and something he said made Jory look up, and his reflective eyes caught the fire, and his mother made a soft sound.

He reached Maren.

She held out her hands, the bandaged arm level. She held his eyes.

He looked at her hands. He looked at her face. He looked at her hands again, and the second look took longer than the first.

He brought the candle close to her arm. Not touching. The flame leaned in the wind. Heat off the flame, heat off the pouch — and the pouch shifted, the small weight of it sliding wrong against the bone. The linen caught, then gave. The leather bag dropped to the cuff of her sleeve and hung there a breath before her arm came down and her hand closed over it.

A breath. No more.

The deacon looked at her cuff. He looked at her face. She gave him back nothing.

The wind went through the fires.

Then he spoke the words of the Rite in the same voice he'd used for Bram, touched the candle near her hands, and moved to Bram's cousin on her left. He did not look back at her.

Maren stood in the ring until it was over. She sang the responses. She bowed her head at the right moments. Her hands were steady. She was very cold.

---

She did not sleep.

She lay on her pallet in the room off the kitchen and listened to the house. Delia banked the fire. Overhead, in the deacon's guest-room, footsteps — pacing. The wind worked at the shutters the way it did this time of year, testing them, thorough and impersonal.

An hour after the house settled: voices in the kitchen.

She went to the wall. The old buildings of Ashwick were good for it — wood panel over stone, and sound moved through the gap if you found the right spot. She'd found it at thirteen, doing what children do with walls, and used it many times since.

Delia first. Low, careful — the voice she used when she was frightened and managing the fear with her hands. The clatter of a cup.

The deacon answering. Fragments: "...not certain, but the weight on the arm, the way it..." Then nothing she could catch. Then her own name.

Delia said something sharp. Maren had never heard her use that voice. It lasted a moment before she brought it back under control.

The deacon said: "...hollow."

The wind pushed at the shutters.

The word came with images she'd never let herself line up in daylight. A peddler she'd seen once, at nine or ten, with a brand at the base of his thumb — circular, clean-edged, nothing a blacksmith made by accident. Her hand had gone to her own forearm at the sight of it, even then, before she had anything to cover. The rest were only ever stories, told low: a man two valleys over whose hand went bad after market and whose wife stopped answering questions; a girl somewhere up the Kintrall road, named the way the old women named miscarriages, eyes on the middle distance. The village had other words for all of them, and never the same one twice.

She pressed her ear back to the gap. The deacon's voice came through, even and low. "The bloodline has asked to be told."

Then Delia's voice, up for the first time, enough that the wall couldn't blunt it. "She's only seventeen. There's still time."

A silence. The wind in the shutters.

"I'm sorry," the deacon said. "The bloodline has to be told."

Then Delia cried. Not loudly. The way she cried when she thought she was alone — muffled, controlled, private, the grief of someone who had learned to take up as little room as possible with their pain.

Maren sat against the wall a long time after the voices stopped.

*Hollow.* She'd heard the word her whole life, always lowered, always for the afflicted. It had always meant her. She hadn't known someone was supposed to be told.

She thought about her father.

He'd left on a night like this one — windy, late autumn. She was twelve. She woke and he was gone, and her mother sat at the kitchen table with her hands flat on the wood, her face doing something Maren had no name for yet. He'd left, she was told, after. People sometimes did. Her mother never said more than that, and the following winter she died, and there was no one else to ask.

For five years the story had been simple: he abandoned them. The story had been useful. It kept her from a harder one.

What she had of him was small. Lamp oil, and under it something metallic, sharper. His hands by firelight, turning a small bright thing — a gear, a catch-pin, something that wanted coaxing, that would work or it wouldn't and asked you to believe in the trying. She couldn't remember what it was. She remembered the turning.

He'd been hollow too — she'd always known that and never followed it anywhere. He'd never shown the change. He'd sat the Rite a certain way, held his arm a certain way.

He left on a windy night. He left without waking her.

She sat against the wall and let the harder question in.

---

She got up and packed. It didn't take long; her hands knew what her mind had planned.

The coin purse. The small knife Delia had given her for cutting herbs — good steel, well balanced. Two changes of clothes, rolled tight. The dried herbs she'd been gathering since summer, in a leather wrap — road things, things she knew. Her mother's comb. The disc.

She'd carried the disc in her coat since her great-aunt pressed it into her hand at fourteen — *don't lose this*, *don't show anyone*, and nothing else. She touched it now. Her thumb found the small notch on the edge it always caught on, and the shallow dent on the face she'd always taken for damage. Warm. Warmer than body-warm, with the faintest vibration under her thumb, so slight she might have imagined it. She wasn't sure she was imagining it. She put it in her coat and kept moving.

She stopped at the shelf. Delia kept her money in an old crock behind the salt-box — more than Maren had in her purse. She thought about it. Under the purse her hand found the small worn pouch, charcoal and wool-cloth, still warm from the Rite. It smelled of the kitchen, of drying things, of fennel and coltsfoot. She put it in her pocket.

She left the crock where it was.

She went out the back, through the kitchen garden and the low gate, onto the path that ran north along the ridge. Three moons were up — all of them at once, which happened sometimes, their light enough to throw shadows. The plateau lay pale and enormous, the basalt black and jagged against a sky that went on and on.

She walked north.

In her pocket the disc pulsed once — a single brief beat, there and gone, like a heart that wasn't hers. She pressed her hand flat over it. Nothing more. It had done it once before, in the kitchen while she packed. Not often, not on any schedule she could find. She couldn't tell if it was the road coming up through her coat or the disc itself, and she stopped trying to decide.

She didn't look at the moons for long — she never had — but the glance caught them holding their distances from one another, even and exact, points set rather than scattered, and she looked away. Her grandmother's grandmother's people had said the moons were not watching but waiting, and no one had ever told her what for, and the not-knowing had set in her young enough to feel like instinct. She kept her eyes on the path and walked at the pace she could hold all day — the long ground-eating step she'd learned on Delia's herb rounds.

She didn't know what would come of it — only that Delia had cried the way you cry at a thing you can't stop, and Delia didn't cry at things that could be stopped.

Something sat in her chest like a coal going from warm to hot.

The path curved east at the top of the ridge. She kept north, off the path, into the high grass. The wind was in her face.

For seventeen years the hollow had been hers — a thing she carried, managed, kept under a clean bandage. Tonight it had stopped being hers. A deacon had said a word in a dark kitchen, and somewhere a name was being written down, and none of it had asked her.

She wanted to know who had decided. And she wanted to know why.

She walked. The moons moved in their slow courses. The plateau fell away behind her, the scrubland opened, and somewhere ahead, past the basalt country and the long descent, was a road.


## Chapter Two — *[Davan]*
### The Color of Hunger

---

Three weeks on the road, and Davan had stopped counting the blisters.

He'd quit around day four, when the count started feeling more like a complaint than a fact. The blisters were there. They'd harden or they wouldn't. Counting them by firelight was a way of feeling sorry for himself with his hands busy, and he'd left Amber Valley to get clear of exactly that.

The road north had a surface, at least — packed earth between shallow ditches, waystones at intervals carved with distances to places he'd never been. He walked the early morning and the late afternoon, rested through the worst of the midday heat, camped off the road when he couldn't make a waypoint inn. He'd done shorter versions of this with his father, hauling furniture to the river towns, and the rhythm came back faster than he expected. Wake, walk, eat when there was something, sleep when it was dark. The body stopped arguing once you stopped arguing with it.

Twenty years old, farther from home than he'd ever been. Most of the time that was fine. For about an hour after waking it wasn't, and he could never quite find where the ache sat.

He carved while he walked. Nothing fine — that was for camp, with a steady light. On the road he kept his hands moving on a piece of soft pine and let it become whatever the grain suggested, which was usually simple: a bird, a sleeping dog, the curve of a bank. The knife was a conversation, his father said, not a command. You didn't force the wood. You asked what it wanted to be and helped it get there. He'd never been sure the idea traveled past woodworking. He'd never managed to stop applying it, either.

Amber Valley was three weeks behind him and already going soft in memory. The water-rights trouble had started the spring before — a dry year, the river lower than anyone could remember — and by summer it had stopped being an argument about water and become the other thing, the kind that pulls from both ends until what it was holding together stops mattering. His family was still there. His mother, his father, his two sisters, twelve and nine. The elder had just begun to show the first signs — a warmth behind the eyes, a restlessness at night; the younger was still all child. He'd hugged them and promised to write. He'd written twice. He didn't know if the letters had reached them.

He wasn't running. He'd decided that, and he mostly believed it.

He was just walking north.

---

The men were around the bend before he heard them.

He came off a rise — a long uphill through pine scrub that kept his eyes on his feet — and when he looked up the road ahead had four people on it. Three mounted. One on the ground holding a farm cart's bridle while the driver answered the man on the lead horse.

Davan stopped. He hadn't decided to; some part of him read the road before the rest of him caught up.

Not soldiers. No uniforms, no militia marks. The gear was plain and well-kept and the horses were better than a merchant would bother with. The lead man was heavy through the shoulders, a pale old scar running from his left ear down to the jaw — not a fighting scar, the kind you got young and forgot. No insignia. But he sat the horse like a man used to his questions being answered, and his shoulders squared when the farmer shook his head. The rider to his right had close-cropped hair and moved in the spare, economical way of someone who counted exits. The third hung back and kept the road behind them clear.

The farmer shook his head again. The lead man nodded once — a man who'd gotten the answer he expected and was already past it — and they rode up the road without looking back.

Davan stood where he was until they were over the next rise. Then he breathed.

At his collar, where the dark shirt gaped, a slow amber pulse moved under his skin. He'd pulled the dark shirt on that morning without knowing why. It faded as he stood there, his heart coming back down to its ordinary pace.

He hadn't told it to do that. It came on its own, ahead of thought, out of something he was still learning to feel before it arrived. He let it go the way he let the rest of the change go — without resistance, making room for a thing he hadn't invited and couldn't send back.

A few minutes, then he walked on. The cart was still pulled over, the farmer on his bench looking at nothing, deciding whether he'd been inconvenienced or threatened.

"What was that about?" Davan kept his voice level.

The farmer took him in — young, walking, no trouble on him. "Looking for someone. Young woman, dark hair, wouldn't show the change. Come north through the valley country, they said." He shrugged. "Haven't seen the like."

"Neither have I." Which was true.

He walked on. The amber under his shirt had gone quiet. He kept the collar closed anyway.

The waypoint inn at Colter's Crossing was The Doubled Moon — a strange name, with three moons in the sky and none of them given to doubling. The sign was old, the paint cracked, the painted moon gone egg-shaped. He bought soup and bread and a small beer he couldn't quite afford and took a corner bench with his carving.

The room was half-full. Late afternoon, low yellow light through the one unshuttered window. Two merchants at the bar, talking with the ease of men who'd been talking before he came and would be talking after he left. A family in the far corner, three children at the age where a meal was a physical ordeal. An old man asleep by the fire with his hat over his face.

The soup was thin and hot. He ate it slowly to make it last and worked the carving — a smooth curve that had started as a fish and was turning into something he had no name for.

The merchants were the loudest thing in the room.

"—a quiet word, not an official notice—" the first was saying, a heavyset man with the look of someone who traded in things that kept. Salt, maybe, or cloth.

"—still means someone's looking," said the other, thin, with a narrow beard that was trying. "The bloodline doesn't put out a quiet word for nothing."

"Could be nothing. Girl's gone missing, family's worried, family's connected—"

"The bloodline," the thin man said again, like the repeating made the point. It did.

Cups. A pause.

"Gone north, they said." The heavy man didn't lower his voice much. "No change showing. Could be she's just young. Hadn't opened yet—"

"At seventeen?"

"Late. Not impossible."

"Or," said the thin man, and let it sit.

The heavy man was quiet a moment. "Hasn't been one of those in—"

"Long time."

"Long time," he agreed, and the talk moved to road conditions and whether the northern pass would close early.

Davan ate his soup. He turned the carving over in his hands. The word they hadn't said sat in the middle of the room like something set down and not picked up.

*Hollow.*

He'd heard it a handful of times. Once from his grandmother, who'd known someone, a long time ago. Once from a traveling priest who made it sound like a kind of grief. Once in a song the older children sang at the younger ones — not cruelly, just the way children carry a thing they don't understand, keeping its shape and losing its meaning.

What would it be, to have no change? He tried to build it honestly: waking at fifteen with nothing different, everyone around you opening into something new, and you exactly as you'd been. He couldn't get inside it. His own had come on slow — a warmth first, that he'd taken for a fever — and he hadn't known what it was until his mother sat him down and showed him, a candle held to his forearm, the light moving under the skin.

He'd cried. He still wasn't sure why. Relief, maybe. Or something about being chosen that he'd never liked to look at straight.

He bought a second beer he also couldn't afford and took it back to the corner and thought about the Chrysalis the way he did when he was alone, which was honestly.

The version he'd grown up with was simple and warm: the world had recognized the people and the people the world, and out of the recognition came the change, and the change was the shape your truest self took with the world watching. Amber Valley kept its Bloomings with food and song and no solemnity at all. When old Veth grew a second set of arms at forty-two, they threw a party. When the Crane girl's eyes went to a compound shimmer that let her read in the dark, her mother wept and called it practical.

He loved that about home. He hadn't known how much of him it had built until he'd been walking three weeks alone.

The church's version was more careful, more maintained — the Chrysalis a divine gift, the change an awakening, the three moons witnesses to every Blooming, close and personal as a hand on the shoulder. Listening to traveling priests, he'd always felt it was the valley's telling with the mess and the joy taken out and something more effortful set in their place. Maybe that was unfair. He knew it might be unfair.

Underneath both was the old line: the change gives you what you need to become yourself. He'd heard it his whole life. He believed it for Veth, and the Crane girl, and his mother, whose change was a sense of direction so exact she'd never once been lost — which she called unremarkable and everyone who knew her called something else.

He turned his hands over in the lamplight. The light in his skin was quiet now, just the potential of it, the difference between a coal that's out and a coal that's banked. Calm, in daylight, it was near invisible. In this light a close watcher might catch something.

The thing was, he wasn't sure the change gave you what you needed. He thought maybe it gave you what you already were, only louder. Those weren't the same thing.

Whatever the unnamed thing in him was, the change had turned it up — warmth in the skin that answered to a feeling before he'd named it himself. He'd caught it in a mirror only a few times: a green-gold when he was happy, ridiculous and wonderful at once. Mostly it was other people who saw it first — the amber rising at his collar when something frightened him, the way it cooled to something bluer when the fright went out of him. Him, in light. Him, in something anyone close enough could read before he could.

He finished the beer. Outside it had gone full dark, and the three moons were coming up in the unshuttered window, two bright and one dim, their light enough to silver the road. He watched them a while. He'd always liked the moons — the slow, reliable movement, the way you could keep a month by them if you learned how. His grandfather had taught him the old moon-songs, counting-rhythms built on the cycles, older than any temple.

The merchants had gone quieter, or he'd stopped hearing them. The family had gotten the children down somehow, all three piled together like a litter. The old man by the fire snored with great enthusiasm and no concern at all.

He gathered his carving and his coat and went out to the stable, where a half-coin had bought him a place in the hay. It was warm and smelled of horse. He lay on his back in the dark, looked at the ceiling, and thought about the girl they were looking for — dark hair, northbound, no change showing.

He hoped she was walking fast.

---

By morning he was on the road before sunrise, in the cold blue hour when the ground gives back the last of yesterday's warmth and the sky comes in close. He walked north. The blisters were still there. He'd stopped counting them.

Somewhere between one step and the next — the carving. He'd been holding it an hour without working it. He looked.

The curve had become a figure. Small, rough, the proportions off in the way of a thing made by hands moving faster than thought — but a person, standing, the head turned a little to one side, in the listening posture his carvings sometimes arrived at on their own.

The arm was wrong.

He'd been working the left arm the last stretch without noticing, trying to make it hang, and it wouldn't. It kept wanting to come in close — tucked against the body, guarded, pressed to the ribs like it was covering something. Three times he'd opened the posture, tried to let the arm fall the way arms fall, and three times his hands had brought it back. Not stiff. Not hurt. Held. Deliberately held, the way you carry a thing you don't want seen.

He hadn't decided it. He hadn't decided any of it — the grain had taken him there, or his hands had, the night's worry working down through his arms into the pine. The wood had gotten there before he had a say, and now it sat in his palm and waited for him to catch up: a figure guarding an arm, for a reason his hands knew and he didn't.

He pocketed it, put his hands in his coat, and kept walking. The road ran out ahead pale and long, the moons going down behind him on the left, and ahead the sky was just starting to show the color that comes before light.

Dark hair, no change visible. The man on the horse had said it the way you say a thing that matters when you're not supposed to say why.

He thought about the word the merchants hadn't finished.

What would it be, to move through the world with nothing showing. No light when you were afraid. No tell. Just a face, ordinary, giving nothing away.

It might be the loneliest thing he could think of.

He walked, and the country gave the thought nothing to push against. Dry open scrub on both sides — sage and bunchgrass and bare basalt shouldering up where nothing had grown over it, land that went brown and brittle the moment it was past the reach of water. No farms. No smoke. No one on the road in either direction as far as he could see. The wind came across it unbroken and smelled of dust and cold stone and nothing alive. Far off to the east the ground climbed into dark timbered ridges with the ghost of snow behind them. The light came up grey and slow over all of it, and he kept walking — north, toward the city, for reasons he still hadn't finished giving himself.


## Chapter Three — *[Aldric]*
### What Lies Under the Stone

---

The pilgrims came in all conditions, and Aldric had made his peace with that long ago.

He had made his peace with a great many things — which was either spiritual maturity or accumulated resignation, and which he privately suspected was both, and did not examine too closely. The cold that came up through the temple floor every winter no matter how many braziers they lit. The smell of a great many unwashed people seeking comfort in an enclosed space, which was the smell of the world's need and a reminder that the work was real. The accounts, the correspondence, the junior priests who were either too eager or not eager enough — the unglamorous armature the actual work was built on.

He had made his peace, over thirty years, with the loneliness of a celibate life, with a name that would not be carried forward, with the things he had dreamed of doing in his twenties, which had faded without drama into the ordinary satisfactions of service. He was not unhappy. He had stopped asking whether he was happy, which was a different thing, and not a painful one. It was the texture of a life lived mostly correctly.

He had not made his peace with the dying woman's last words.

---

She had come two days before, the way the most profoundly changed sometimes came: in a cart, because walking was no longer practical, with a young grandson who had the look of someone managing a grief he had not yet been given permission to feel. Her name was Sella. She was somewhere past seventy, though what she was past seventy of was hard to say, because what remained of her was not precisely a woman of seventy. The Chrysalis had been working on her for decades. Her arms had gone past translucent — the skin thinned to membrane, the bones beneath fully visible, blue-white and delicate as bird-hollow, the joints swollen where new structures had been quietly building for years. At the collarbone the change had gone further: a faint crystalline texture at the edges, the skin catching the lamplight at angles flesh had no business catching. Her face was still her own. It simply sat in a body that had crossed some threshold he could not name, where the change was no longer happening to her but had become her, settled and irrevocable as bone.

The church had a name for it — the Final Chrysalis, the ultimate gift, the last dissolution of the barrier between self and world. The name was confident. The thing it named had moved past anything his theology could comfortably hold. He had held the hands of perhaps a dozen people in this state, and had found it, privately, more frightening than transcendent. He had never said so. It would not have been useful.

She had asked for the Keeper of the temple. Not a junior priest, not a healer. When they settled her she was lucid, and her eyes — gone a uniform pale silver, without pupil or iris — had tracked him into the room with the attention of someone who had decided every remaining moment was worth spending.

He had read the passages she asked for. He had sat with her while the grandson slept in the chair against the wall. He had thought, the way he sometimes did in these rooms, about what it would be to believe so entirely that the body bent toward the belief, decade by decade, until the certainty was written in the bones. He had thought it with respect, and from a certain wistful distance.

She had been quiet a long time when she took his wrist.

The grip was strong. He had not expected that. The translucent hand on his wrist, the silver eyes steady on him, and she said, with a clarity that did not belong to someone who had been unconscious an hour before:

"The moons are not moons."

He had held very still.

"They are boats," she said. "Boats with the dead still inside."

"Rest now, Sella," he had said, gently.

She had looked at him a moment longer. Not distress, not confusion — something almost apologetic. As though she knew she was handing him a gift he had not asked for and would not know what to do with.

Then she had closed her eyes, and by morning she was gone.

He had stood in the doorway after the grandson was seen to. The lamp was low and guttering. He reached across and pinched it out between his fingers, and stood in the sudden dark, and did not move for a while.

---

He had dismissed it. That was the correct response — pastorally, doctrinally, in every register his training had given him. People said many things in their last hours. The mind moving toward its end produced fragments — old songs, half-remembered images, the silt of a whole life stirred loose and let go without order. The Fathers' Boats tradition was old, pre-Chrysalis, and the church had long filed it under colorful settlement-era superstition, the kind of story any people tells to explain how it came to be where it is. *We came in boats.* Of course. Metaphorically. Everyone comes from somewhere.

He had dismissed it, and gone to bed, and lain awake until the false dawn watching the ceiling and thinking about what it would mean, precisely, if the moons were not moons.

He had slept perhaps two hours.

Then the morning, and the pilgrims' queue, long the way it was always long at the Confluence — three rivers and four roads meeting in one valley, making it the natural center of a very large region. He heard confessions, granted blessings, settled two inheritance disputes brought to the temple in the sincere belief that matters of property belonged in a house of the Chrysalis, which they did not, and which he explained patiently, and would explain again to the next three families who came with the same sincere mistake.

On his way to the queue he passed through the side corridor and pinched the wick of the lamp outside the record room — already cold, already out, as it had been since the night before — and walked on without breaking stride. He did this every morning. He had done it so long he could not have said, if asked, whether the lamp was generally lit or unlit when he checked. The checking was not about the lamp. The first two fingers of his right hand carried a pale callus from a lifetime of it.

He ate lunch standing at his desk, wrote two letters that needed writing, reviewed the accounts for the eastern supply house, and moved through all of it the way a man moves through weather — something you pass through rather than feel.

In the afternoon he went down to the undercroft.

---

The east undercroft was the oldest part of the temple. The building had gone up in stages over centuries, each generation adding to or reworking what came before, until it was a palimpsest of decisions made by people long dead who had not always agreed with one another. The oldest work was in the east: low stone rooms beneath the main floor, used now for storage — records going back three hundred years, casks of communion wine that needed a year in the cool dark before they were fit to use.

He had been meaning to have the east undercroft resurveyed. Part of the rear wall had cracked in a way that concerned him, and he had set two of the younger brothers and a mason named Dolan to assess it. He had not expected them to find anything behind it.

He heard Dolan's voice from the bottom of the steps, and the tone was wrong — not alarmed, but the voice of a man who has met something outside his trade and does not know the protocol. Aldric went down.

The crack had opened into a seam. The wall here was not original stonework but a later addition, built against an older surface and mortared flush to look continuous. Two hundred years of settling had drawn the stones apart, and Dolan's crowbar had done the rest.

Behind it, a room. Low-ceilinged, dry, sealed.

Inside it, five objects.

---

He sent Dolan and the brothers away. The room was a historical storage space, he told them; he would review its contents and report to the proper archival authority; they had done good work and should say nothing until he had assessed its significance. He said it in the calm, conclusive voice he had spent thirty years building for situations that wanted a calm, conclusive voice, and they went.

Then he stood in the doorway and looked at the five objects for a long time.

Four were containers — each about the length of his forearm, smooth, a dull gray material that was neither stone nor metal nor any ceramic he knew and somehow had qualities of all three. Sealed. Smooth but for a narrow ridge running the circumference, and a small recess at one end that might once have held a fixture, a handle, a closure. He lifted the nearest and found it colder than the room could account for, heavier than its size promised, and unwilling to open to any pressure his hands could put on the ridge. He set it down. He did not try the others. They were four, and he had already decided, the way decisions sometimes arrive ahead of their reasons, that forcing would get him nothing tonight.

The fifth was flat, on a low stone shelf at the back. Dark — not the dark of an unlit thing but something deliberate, a surface that drank light instead of giving it back. A hand's width thick, perhaps four times that across, the edges sharp and regular in the way of a thing made rather than grown or found.

He stood in the doorway a very long time.

Then he went up, finished the afternoon's work, ate a dinner he did not taste, sat through evening prayers with the brothers and said the correct words, and went to his room.

He lay awake until midnight.

Then he rose, took his lamp, and went back down.

At the foot of the steps he stopped. He became aware — the way you become aware of a thing already half-done — that his right hand was raised and his thumb and forefinger hovered an inch from the flame. He had been about to put out the only light he had, in a sealed room underground, from habit. He lowered his hand and stood a moment, the gesture suddenly strange to him, the way a word you say every day goes strange when you stop and look at it. It had not always been a habit. He preferred the word *habit*. Then he went in.

---

The flat dark object answered his touch.

Not at once. He had reached out because he was a man who, faced with a thing he did not understand, eventually had to put his hand on it. He had expected nothing, or cold smooth material. He had not expected light.

It came from within the surface, or from the surface, or from somewhere between — he could not find the source. A cold light, nothing like flame. It moved in the surface; or the surface moved within it; and what rose up was not a steady glow but a shifting series of images.

He stepped back. He stood very still.

The images were small and seemed to recede, as if seen from a great distance. Points of light against a field of dark. Some held fixed; some moved in slow arcs. He had been scholar enough in his youth to have spent time over astronomy texts, and something in the arrangement pulled at him — a recognition he could not quite close his hand around — a star map, or something that served as one, though the points stood in relation to each other in a way that matched no chart he knew.

He looked at it until the cold worked up through his feet and his legs, perhaps an hour, and understood almost nothing, and could not make himself go back up. What troubled him was not the intensity of his attention but its steadiness. His mind had settled into the shifting light the way a hand that has not held a pen in twenty years still knows the stroke. Something in him had been built for this kind of looking, and he had spent a long life arranging things so there would be nothing to look at.

He found himself thinking of it as a text. An unread scripture — and then he caught the word and set it down, because that was the wrong register for an artifact of uncertain provenance, and he was meant to be a historian here and not a priest before holy writ. That the two had become, for a moment, the same word in his mouth, he did not examine.

---

He went up. He sat at his desk. He reached for the lamp — caught himself, drew his hand back — and let it burn as it needed to burn. He drew the inkpot close, dipped the quill, and wrote in his journal, which he had kept for twenty years in an oblique shorthand made for thoughts not meant for other eyes. He wrote, obliquely, about the sealed room and the objects and the flat dark thing that made light. He wrote, less obliquely than he meant to: *What is this, and who put it here, and how long has it been here, and why, and what are the containers, and what is this light.* He had tried the ridge again before coming up — one container, then a second. The seam did not give. The containers did not object to his hands, and did not know them either.

He drew a line through the last sentence. The nib caught and spat a small blot; paper was dear, and the temple reckoned every page, and striking one out still pricked the old frugal nerve in him after thirty years. He wrote it again anyway. He left it.

Then he turned back a page and found, below the previous day's routine accounting of pilgrims and services and correspondence, the note he had made of Sella's last words.

*The moons are not moons. They are boats. Boats with the dead still inside.*

He looked at it a while.

Below it, in the deliberate shorthand: *Grief-speech. Delirium. Old folk tradition. Irrelevant.*

He looked at that as well.

Then he closed the journal and sat and watched the lamp burn low, and thought about what it meant that the two things — the old woman's words and the light moving in the dark surface below — had reached for each other in his mind without his leave, like two hands meeting in the dark.

---

In the morning he sealed the room again as well as he could and moved a storage cask in front of the broken section of wall. He told Dolan the room had held only some damaged record-casks from three centuries back — nothing of significance, nothing needing further attention. Dolan took this without comment. He was a mason; old rooms were the temple's business, not his.

Aldric went about his day.

He went about his day for about two hours before he sat down and drew out the standard form for reporting an architectural discovery within a temple precinct. The form existed because discoveries happened — old rooms, buried foundations, sealed chambers from earlier building — and the protocol was plain: document, categorize, report to the regional office within thirty days, await archival assessment. He had used it twice, for a collapsed drain and a cache of damaged vestment-cloth. Both times unremarkable. Document, categorize, report.

He wrote the date, the location, the description of the wall failure. He wrote *five objects recovered from sealed chamber, east undercroft, below current floor level.* He came to the field marked CATEGORY — seven options, from *architectural remnant* to *liturgical artifact* to *material of unknown provenance requiring specialist assessment* — and stopped.

He looked at the seven options for a long time.

None was wrong, exactly. *Material of unknown provenance* was accurate. But the seventh category fed a particular process — regional office, then the New Plymouth archive, then, for objects of sufficient interest, an assessment team sent to the site. The team would include church officials and, for anything predating the current temple, a representative of the bloodline's historical commission. This was standard. He had known it was standard for thirty years and had never once found it remarkable.

He found it remarkable now, the way a thing becomes remarkable when you look at it from a direction you have never stood in. The form was built to move information upward through a system, and the system was built to receive it, and he could not say, pen in hand, what the system would do with what he had found. He could not say, because he did not know what he had found.

He set the form aside — not face-down; he was not hiding it from himself — to the left of his desk, where things went that wanted further thought. He would come back to it. He was a man who came back to things.

He did not come back to it. Within a week it was under other papers; within another it had sunk to the bottom of a stack, where it stayed, blank below the third line, for the rest of his time at the Confluence.

---

He heard confessions. He settled a dispute over the boundary between two families' grazing rights, brought to the temple in the sincere belief, et cetera. He ate lunch standing at his desk. He wrote the letters that needed writing.

In the afternoon, on the pretext of a pastoral visit to the market district, he went up to the temple's small archive on the upper floor of the east wing. He had full access, as Keeper — which meant he also knew its gaps: the sections that had been removed, transferred, restricted over the years, and by whom.

He found the shelf on pre-Chrysalis settlement traditions. It was thin. It had always been thin, and he had always taken that for how little had survived from that time rather than for a deliberate condition. He looked at it now with a different question.

The Fathers' Boats tradition: two pages of notes from a Keeper some ninety years before, summarizing a folk-tradition out of the river communities. People came from elsewhere, in vessels, in the old time before the Chrysalis. The vessels still watched from above. The oldest versions gave the term as *sky-boats*. The Keeper's assessment, in the firm hand of a man closing a matter: *Colorful origin-myth. Pastoral. Not inconsistent with the Chrysalis narrative if understood metaphorically. No doctrinal concern.*

Beneath it, in a different and much later hand, a single line: *See also: Restricted Collection, NP Archive, shelf 7-eastern. Access: Bloodline approval required.*

Aldric read this several times.

The Restricted Collection in the New Plymouth archive was not news to him; in thirty years he had known of it without ever having reason to seek it. Texts had been restricted over the centuries for many reasons — heresy, sedition, material judged destabilizing to public understanding of the Chrysalis. Access ran through bloodline approval or a petition to the High Keeper's office. He had never had a reason.

He wrote the shelf number in his journal. Then he wrote it again on a separate scrap, folded it, and put it in his coat pocket, where his hand would find it.

---

That night, when evening prayers had ended and the other brothers had gone, he stayed. It was not vigil as the liturgy defined it — no feast-day, no candle, no observance the calendar would know — but the shape of his sitting there was vigil-shaped, and he noticed it, and did not move. He sat in the dark with the lamp unlit and looked at the carved symbol above the doorway: the spiraling form that marked every Chrysalis temple, the sign of the change-within-change. He had looked at this symbol, or its like, every day of his adult life. He knew it the way he knew his own face, which is to say he had long since stopped seeing it.

He became aware, at some point, that his right thumb and forefinger were pressed together in his lap. The automatic reach, the pinch, the confirmation — finding nothing. He had been sitting in a dark room with no flame anywhere in it, and his hand had still gone looking for one to put out.

He uncurled his fingers and laid them flat on his knee.

He saw it now.

The inner spiral of the symbol — the oldest part, the part that predated the standard Chrysalis iconography by some margin, which scholars took to have been absorbed from earlier regional traditions — was not quite a spiral. He had always read it as one. Up close, with fresh attention, it was a set of concentric arcs: concentric, measured, each a deliberate interval from the center. Not a spiral at all.

A set of orbital paths.

The sign of the change-within-change. He had stood beneath it and blessed the newly-opened for thirty years. It had been a map of the sky the whole time.

He stood under the doorway a long time, looking up.

Then he went to his room and sat at his desk and wrote, in the oblique shorthand, the sentence he had been not-writing for two days:

*I do not know what I am looking at.*

And below it, after a long pause:

*I think I am going to have to find out.*

He slept better than he had in years. The decision, it seemed, had been the thing keeping him awake.


## Chapter Four — *[Vane]*
### The Flavor of the Contract

---

She found him by his fear.

This was not unusual. Fear had a specific chemistry — something metallic and slightly sweet, like copper left in milk — and it intensified around the source the way heat intensified around a fire. You could walk toward it in a crowd with your eyes closed. She had done it once, as a demonstration for a client who didn't believe her fees were reasonable. He had revised his position.

The man she was looking for was at the back of the tavern, in a booth with three other people who were not him, performing the very specific relaxation of someone who has decided that being visibly relaxed is the same as being unfindable. A common mistake. People in hiding generally made themselves smaller and quieter, which Vane had always found counterproductive. Small and quiet read as hiding. The performance of ease, poorly done, read as a kind of scream.

His name was Torben. He owed money to people who could afford her rates, which put the debt in a particular category. She'd had the file for nine days — seven establishing his habits, two waiting for the right moment, which was this one: a busy tavern, three companions who were casual acquaintances rather than allies, exits she'd mapped on the first visit.

She sat down across from him. She put her hands flat on the table.

He looked at her, and then at the hands, and then at her face, and then the fear-smell spiked sharply upward, almost sharp enough to taste.

"Torben," she said.

"I don't—"

"You do." She kept her voice at conversation level. There was no point in theater. "Your choices right now are: you come with me quietly and your friends have a normal evening, or you don't and we have a longer conversation." She paused. "I get paid either way."

He came with her quietly.

Outside, in the street, she handed him off to the client's man waiting at the corner and took her fee in a sealed envelope without counting it on the street, because counting money on the street was how you ended up with a shorter career than you'd planned. She'd count it upstairs.

The docks district smelled of its usual compound — brine and tar and the sweetness of rotting wood that had soaked in seawater for decades, and under everything a warm animal smell from the livestock pens two streets over. She had lived here six years. Her nose had mapped it so thoroughly that variations registered as information rather than sensation. A new ship in the harbor. Something dead in the alley off Cutter's Lane, probably a dog, two days gone. Rain coming, though the sky was still clear — something in the air pressure that came across as a particular flatness to the smell of the street.

She went upstairs.

---

Her rooms were two: a front room with a table, a chair, a locked chest, a window onto the street; a back room with a bed and a second locked chest and a lamp she kept turned low. The front room held the tools of the work — maps, contact lists in a shorthand nobody else could read, a small roll of oilcloth with restraints and lockpicks in it. The back room held the things she didn't want to think about while she was working.

She counted the fee. It was correct.

She put the envelope in the locked chest and sat at the table a while, doing nothing in particular. She could hold that for about ten minutes before the quiet of the evening started to press. Not loneliness — solitude she'd squared with years ago, for real and not for show. Something else. A specific absence. The sensation of a thing she was about to attend to.

From the back room's chest she took the small tin.

The wafers inside were pale, slightly iridescent, thinner than a fingernail. They smelled of almost nothing — a faint warmth, something floral that wasn't quite any flower she knew. This was one of the qualities she had always found unsettling about Bloom, in the detached professional way she found many things unsettling: that the substance which rewrote the inside of your skull for four hours had almost no smell at all. Everything had a smell. The absence was its own kind of statement.

She placed one wafer on her tongue.

---

It began in her chest. It always began there, a warmth that moved outward, and then the warmth became something more specific — an opening, the walls becoming less definitive, the boundaries of her own skin turning generous. Her smell broadened. Not sharper: broader. The copper-milk of fear went quiet and underneath it the city opened up, layer on layer, every person in every room doing their human chemical work — the cocktail of comfort and worry and hunger and desire and boredom and grief that was the smell of a city at night. She could not separate them cleanly, not like this; it was more like hearing a choir than hearing individual voices. The city, all at once, in full throat.

And then the further thing. The thing the Bloom-devotees called communion and she had never called anything, because naming it made it easier to need, and she had always tried to keep the needing at arm's length.

The sense of being continuous with all of it. Not dissolved — she was still herself, still Vane, still in a chair in a room above the docks — but connected at every edge to everything adjacent, and those things to everything adjacent to them, outward until the line between what was her and what was the city and what was Gaius itself became a matter of technicality. For the duration of the Bloom she understood, without thinking about it, why people called the Chrysalis a gift. It was the difference between looking at the world through a window and standing in it. She stood in it.

This lasted some portion of the night. She lost track of time in the usual way.

---

What she came back to was: the chair, the table, the low lamp, the window showing a sky gone from dark to the dark-gray that meant morning was coming. The city quieter. The smell of it returned to information rather than song.

And then the descent — never improved, no matter how many times she'd been through it: the walls returning, the boundaries re-establishing, the world stepping back behind the glass. For a few minutes she sat with it the way she sat with most unpleasant necessary things, which was without drama.

Then the inventory she always did after: the bark on her forearms, below the elbow in the early years and now at mid-bicep on the right side. She pressed her fingers against it. Cool, dense, the texture of old wood. It had spread another half-inch since last month, she thought — and then thought: you said that last month, and probably the month before. She had learned not to measure things she was not prepared to act on.

She needed another wafer in three days. Probably two and a half.

She went to bed. She slept four hours, dreamlessly.

---

In the morning: a letter under the door.

She knew Horst's seal — a pressed coin rather than wax, clean and businesslike, the mark of a man who found theatrical gestures unnecessary. She'd worked four contracts through Horst over the past two years. He was organized, he paid correctly, he communicated clearly. She had no information about who he worked for and had not sought any, because that was the kind of information that created obligations she hadn't agreed to.

She read the letter at the table with a cup of something that approximated tea if you weren't particular about what tea was supposed to taste like.

*Female. Young, approximately seventeen. Dark hair. No visible change. Last confirmed location: Sanctum Basin region, moving north. Do not harm — condition of contract. Deliver to the address below, Noble Quarter, New Plymouth. Standard discretion terms apply.*

Below this: a fee. She read the number twice.

She set the letter down. She finished the approximate tea. She thought about the fee.

Not an unusual brief, except in two ways. The first: the Noble Quarter address. Not a guardhouse, not a constable's office. A private address. The second: the instruction about visible change, or the absence of it. Most wanted notices described a person's change as a way of confirming identity — the copper skin, the reflective eyes, whatever was specific and visible and useful. This one described an absence of change as if the absence were the distinguishing feature.

She considered it a moment and then filed it under things that were the client's business rather than hers, which was the category that made the work manageable.

She wrote back to Horst: *Terms accepted. Advance as agreed. Will report when target located.*

Finding people was the one thing she had never needed to practice.

---

Cade's supply house was on the outer edge of the Crafting Quarter, in a building that appeared to sell fabric remnants and did, in the front half, do a modest legitimate trade in exactly that. The back half was reached through a door that looked like a storage cupboard and required a specific knock that Cade changed every few months, with the paranoid regularity of a man who understood that his business model had structural vulnerabilities.

She went in the afternoon, because the morning traffic was too thin to be anonymous in.

Cade was a small precise man of indeterminate age who wore his gray hair long and kept his hands very clean. He ran the Bloom trade in the docks district with the methodical attention of someone who had understood early that the substance was the least interesting part of the operation, and that supply chain and client management were where things were won or lost. She respected that about him while finding him personally unremarkable.

He had her usual behind the counter. He measured, he wrapped, he named a price that hadn't changed in the two years she'd been coming.

She was paying when she heard the two men in the back.

Not clearly. The wall between the shop and the back room was thick. Her hearing wasn't enhanced — but the nose had rebuilt the rest of her senses around it, and when it flagged something the rest of her leaned in. It flagged something now: the men in the back were stressed. The sour-flat smell of sustained stress, not acute fear — a problem that had been sitting a while.

She heard: "—still haven't replaced the Hartley woman—"

And: "—can't exactly run an advertisement—"

And, more quietly, after a pause: "—three months at minimum before the new one's producing at volume, if she survives the first month—"

She paid Cade. He folded the wrapping shut and pushed it across the counter. His hands were, as always, extremely clean.

The phrasing: *if she survives the first month.* She turned it over once. She knew, in the abstract professional way she knew many things, where Bloom came from. The information had arrived cleanly, without elaboration, and she had filed it under things that were the supply chain's business rather than hers.

She filed it there again now. Quick and clean.

She went back to her rooms and put the new supply in the tin and the tin in the chest and went to the window and looked at the street for a while, which was something she did when she was thinking and preferred not to be aware of it.

The Noble Quarter address. The girl with no visible change. The fee.

She had a job. Jobs had their own momentum, which was one of the things she valued about them.

She began planning the route north.

---

That evening she opened her maps and made notes in her shorthand. The notes were in cipher — her own, built over years — but the cipher was only part of it. Each entry carried something else: a faint chemical record of the moment she'd written it, laid down by her skin without intention, as involuntary as breath. The stress-smell of a stakeout at hour eleven. The cold-salt of a dockside morning. The metallic thread of a room where someone had been afraid. Anyone who cracked the cipher would still only have the words. The context lived in the margins, legible to a nose that could read it.

She had not told anyone this. It was not the kind of operational advantage you explained.

At the edge of her attention, last night's Bloom was already receding — not a craving, not yet. More like a room you've left that you are becoming aware of having left. She caught it because she'd had the files open twenty minutes and the connections between them were coming slowly, one at a time, each link costing conscious effort. This was how it went in the ordinary state: a patience tax, paid in full. She worked through it.

She had learned, early in her use, what the Bloom did to the work. At the height of it, with the files spread around her, she could feel the whole picture without reading a line — the scent-record of every note rising to meet her at once, weeks or months of observation compositing into something that arrived less like thought than like recognition. Connections her waking mind would take hours to build, given in a breath. She had broken three contracts open this way. Had walked into a room and known, from the smell of her own old notes, exactly where the gap in her map was.

She had told herself this was a tool like any other. She had been telling herself that for four years.

She noted the distance to Sanctum Basin. She noted the road conditions she'd heard from a carter that morning in the market. She noted that *no visible change* at seventeen was late — late enough to be notable, late enough that someone who understood what it meant would have two possible explanations, and one of those was rare enough to be worth a Noble Quarter fee.

She had heard the word *hollow* perhaps four times in her life. Once from an old woman in her building who'd had a neighbor, years back, and spoke of him the way you speak of a storm that passed through — with a kind of awed inconvenience. Once from a client who'd wanted someone found and wouldn't say why, and she'd declined that contract, because declined was sometimes the right answer. Once from Cade, obliquely, in a different context she hadn't fully understood — something about the eastern highlands, a place the dockside old-timers called *the hill that moves in the light*, spoken in the particular tone that meant the name was a warning rather than a description.

Once from herself, in the mirror, at fifteen, the morning after a night that had produced no warmth, no light, no sense of opening — nothing except the ordinary face she'd had the day before, and the day before that, and every day she could remember.

She had been wrong, in the end. The change had come three years later, in a direction she hadn't anticipated, and she had come to terms with it the only way that ever took: by finding use for what she had.

The smell of the city came up through the window. She sorted its components without meaning to, let them settle, and went back to the maps.

The girl was three weeks ahead of her. Less, if she was on foot.

She was probably on foot.

Vane pulled a fresh sheet toward her and started the math — distances, days, the carter's road conditions — her pen scratching it down in a tight private scrawl, figures and shorthand that would mean nothing to anyone who found them. Fast, and then faster.


## Chapter Five — *[Maren]*
### North Is the Safest Direction

---

On the first day she made two mistakes and one good decision.

The mistakes: she walked the road in the early morning, when the low light felt like cover and was in fact the thing that made her a dark shape against pale empty country; and she stopped to fill her water skin at a spot with no sightlines, so that when a rider came over the eastern rise she had nowhere to go but a clump of thorn-scrub. She crouched there twenty minutes. The rider passed without slowing. She came out with both forearms scratched and her left knee torn and the skin half full.

The good decision: she emptied the coal pouch at the first crossing.

Not the whole pouch — she kept the leather. The leather was useful, and it smelled like Delia's kitchen, and she was not going to stand in a stream examining what that meant. The charcoal and the wool-cloth she shook out into the current and watched go black and then gone. It had done its work. What it was now was evidence, and evidence was a thing she couldn't afford. Something set down in her chest as the water took it — the particular lightness of putting down a weight you've carried long enough to forget you were carrying it.

After that she walked by night and slept in the day.

The sleeping went badly at first. She'd spent her life in a bed with walls around it, and open ground was a different proposition in every way she hadn't thought to consider. The sounds were wrong. The light came through her eyelids at angles that had nothing to do with windows. She slept in snatches and woke tight and lay still until she'd worked out what had woken her, which was always wind, or a bird, or her own heart running ahead of the actual danger.

By the third night it came easier. By the fourth she woke with the false dawn and the alertness arrived as something close to useful. Her body was adjusting to the road without asking her. She was grateful, and then caught the gratitude and turned it over: her body was learning to run. Not the progress she'd have chosen at seventeen.

She kept a list of what she knew. The habit was Delia's — name what you've got, because a healer who doesn't know her stock is just a hopeful person with some weeds. She knew the road markers were good to within half a day, and that she didn't have to be on the road to use them. She knew watercress grew at the edge of any stream that ran cold and fast, and tasted of nearly nothing, and kept hunger from going sharp. She knew dew gathered on the north side of big stones, more than you'd expect. She knew the names and uses of most things that grew in plateau country, and a good many in the scrubland she was crossing now.

Four nights ago she'd thought of herself as helpless. She'd thought it for years without once turning to look at it. She ate the watercress and let the thought sit.

Early on, she tried a hamlet for bread — a well and a few houses where the road crossed a stream — partly for the bread and partly to learn whether she could stand among people and be no one.

The woman who sold her the loaf didn't overcharge and didn't quite look at her. She took the coin without letting their hands come near, set the bread on the board instead of passing it across, and stepped back before Maren had finished gathering it — the room you give a dog you haven't decided about. A man at the well stopped talking when she came near and took it up again once she'd gone by. No one was unkind. No one said anything. It was only that the air stayed a little wider around her than around anyone else, a space that opened and moved with her and closed up behind.

She'd glimpsed this done to other people her whole life and never once felt it land on her — the peddler with the brand on his thumb, the ones in the stories who quietly stopped being mentioned. At home she'd been furniture: known since before she could walk, felt and forgotten. Here she was a stranger, and the wrongness people couldn't name found her the way it had always found them.

She took the bread and went. She didn't buy from anyone else for a long time after. She told herself it was for the hiding. It was partly for the hiding.

---

On the third morning she made the worst mistake yet, and it came out of competence, not the lack of it.

She found a stream running north — fast, cold, clean, the kind Delia would have followed without a second thought, because a stream like that meant watercress and comfrey and the soft-stemmed violet that grew where the water pooled. Every piece of knowledge she had said the same thing: follow the water. Water was supply and direction both, the thread you followed through country you didn't know, because it always went somewhere.

She followed it three hours, foraging as she went, filling the skin twice. The stream bent east around a low ridge and she bent with it.

On the far side it opened into a flood-flat — treeless, grassless, a wide pale spread of gravel and standing water two hundred yards across before the scrub picked up again. She was fifty yards into it before she understood. Open ground in every direction. Nothing higher than her ankle. The ridge at her back cut off retreat; going around it again meant three hours lost. Ahead was two hundred yards of open flat in daylight.

She stood in it and felt, for the first time since Ashwick, plainly stupid. Not afraid — she'd been afraid since the first night. Stupid.

She crossed at a crouch that hid her from nothing, boots filling with water, the gravel shifting underfoot so that every step felt watched. The far side took twenty minutes and felt like more. She made the scrub and went flat and lay there ten minutes, breathing too hard, listening for hoofbeats that didn't come.

No one had seen her. Probably. She couldn't know.

She had followed the water because following the water was how you found things. She wasn't trying to find things. She was trying not to be found — the opposite problem, and she'd spent a morning solving the wrong one.

She backtracked the rest of the day to where the stream had turned, and went north overland after that, away from the water, and the day was gone. A whole day to a competence that turned out to be the wrong one. She ate the watercress she'd gathered that morning and it tasted like proof of her own miscalculation.

---

The patrol found the road before she did.

She came up on a rise at dusk on the fourth day and saw them below: three riders, and a fourth man on foot beside a farm cart, answering something she was too far off to hear. One rider sat a little apart, straight-backed in the way of someone used to being obeyed. Another carried a coil of rope on the saddle horn and a long-handled tool she didn't know but that had the look of something made for one job. The third sat easy, watching the road behind. Not travelers. People running a search, and unhurried about it, which meant they thought they had time.

She sat down in the scrub and her hands were shaking.

Not the managed kind. The real kind — started in the fingers, went up through the wrists, settled behind her sternum as a tremor in her breath. She pressed her palms flat to the ground the way she'd seen Delia steady a patient and told her body to be still. Her body didn't listen. Her vision had gone narrow and bright and her pulse was loud in her ears, and none of it was any use.

She made herself watch.

The farmer shook his head and pointed north with what looked like honest guessing rather than knowledge. The lead rider nodded once, said something to the others, and they moved on up the road without pressing the farmer further.
