She stayed down. The shaking took another two minutes and she let it — fighting it made it worse; Delia had taught her that about fear the way she'd taught her about fevers. You didn't suppress it. You sat with it until the body had done what it needed to. She sat with it. It passed. Her breathing came back. The bright narrow tunnel of her vision opened out again.

Then she waited until they were past the curve, and waited longer, and went wide around the road through a stretch of scrub that looked rough and was. Her ankle turned twice on the broken ground. She kept moving. By the time the road showed again in the lower country the moon was up and the riders were gone and her thorn-torn knee had stiffened into a problem for tomorrow.

Three riders. For one girl.

Nobody down there was asking whether she was worth three riders.

She thought about what her father had cost, and made herself stop, and walked.

---

The boy's cart was a quarter-mile short of a farmstead, and his turnips were in the road.

He was maybe ten, glaring at a wheel that had dropped into a rut and shed its load, with the focused outrage of someone who can see the shape of a problem and not yet the solution. The turnips were everywhere — big and very purple, the good kind that kept through winter. Someone had grown them with care.

She should not stop. She'd run the arithmetic in about two seconds: stopping meant being seen and remembered; the farmstead was close enough that the wife would clock a stranger; the road was open; the riders weren't so far ahead they couldn't turn back.

She stopped anyway.

"The wheel's not broken," she said. "Just caught."

The boy looked at her with the specific suspicion of a child who's learned that grown-ups offering help usually want something. "I can see that."

"Two hands on the back axle while I lift the front." She did it without waiting for him to agree. The cart shifted, the wheel came out of the rut, she set it down. "Now the turnips."

They picked up turnips in silence. He was quick about it, plainly working out whether she meant to pocket any. She didn't, and somewhere in the gathering he decided it, and by the time the cart was loaded he'd moved from suspicion to something more neutral.

"Where are you going?" he asked.

"North."

"That's not a place."

"No," she agreed.

He studied her a moment — road-worn, thorn-scratched, a pack that wasn't heavy enough to mean anything. "My mother makes a lot of soup," he said. "There's usually extra."

She should not go to the farmstead. She'd run that too, faster. She went anyway.

---

The farm wife was Betta — compact, capable, somewhere in her late forties, with the look of a woman who'd solved so many practical problems that solving them had gone invisible, a thing that ran in the background. She fed Maren soup without asking much, which was its own kind of asking; she was deciding something while she ladled. Maren ate and let herself be decided about. The soup was good and she hadn't had hot food in four days. Some sums were easy.

The boy was Corvin. He ate and watched Maren with the open intent of a child who's found something interesting and means to get information out of it.

"Why is your arm wrapped?" he asked.

"Corvin," said Betta.

"It's an old burn," Maren said. "Mostly healed."

She said it the way she'd said it a hundred times, and heard herself say it. There was nothing under the linen to protect anymore. No Rite to stand through. The deacon had already seen. She was already running, and she was still telling the burn story to a ten-year-old over soup.

"It's not a burn," she said.

Corvin looked at her with interest. Betta went still, the ladle stopped.

"There's nothing wrong with the arm. I just got used to keeping it covered."

She unwound the linen from her forearm and set it on the table. Her arm looked the way it had always looked. She folded the bandage once and put it in her pocket — linen was useful; her knee could use it. She didn't put it back on.

A pause. Betta started ladling again. "More soup?"

"Please," Maren said.

It was the most honest conversation she'd had in three years, and it had run about four sentences. She wasn't sure if that was encouraging or the other thing. She ate the second bowl, helped wash up after, and took the offer of the barn for the night with real gratitude.

---

Before she went out, Corvin found her at the door, holding something out palm-up with the slightly aggressive generosity of a child who has decided to give a gift and won't be talked out of it.

A small wooden bird. Rough, hand-carved — the knife marks still showing where he hadn't smoothed the wing joints, the tail a little off, the body blocky in the way of hands still learning their strength. But there was care in it. The head was tilted a little, as if the bird were listening, and he'd taken his time with that and got it right.

"I made it," he said — unnecessary, offered like a credential.

She took it. It sat light and warm from his hand in her palm. "It's good," she said, and meant it.

He shrugged with the elaborate unconcern of a boy who cared a great deal, and went back inside.

She put the bird in her pocket with the disc and went out to the barn.

---

The barn was warm the way places are when animals have lived in them a long time — a deep settled warmth with no fire in it, the banked heat of big bodies over years. Two cows and a donkey took her in with the incurious thoroughness of animals who'd watched a lot of things pass through a barn and found most of them dull.

She lay in the hay and counted what she had.

Money: maybe eight more days of lean spending, less if she needed a real bed or a meal the road didn't provide. Skills: herbs, basic wound care, strong legs, a working sense of what not to eat and how to move quiet. The knife, which was good. Delia's empty pouch. Her mother's comb, which had no use she was willing to look at.

The disc.

She took it out and turned it in the thin moonlight coming through the gap-boards. Dull metal, lighter than it looked, about two fingers across. One side smooth. The other had a shallow indentation she'd always taken for damage — a dent, a gouge from a drop — and three marks around the edge she'd always taken for decoration. She looked at the marks now with different attention.

Her great-aunt had pressed it on her after her mother died — Maren thirteen, too raw to ask anything — with only: don't lose this, don't show anyone, it's been in the family a long time. She'd kept it the way a grieving thirteen-year-old keeps a small metal thing: because it was easy to keep, and because in a family that had lost almost everything it was one thing that had survived.

She looked at the marks and thought: someone made this for a reason.

She'd never had that thought before. She didn't know what to do with it. She put the disc away, beside the boy's bird, and lay looking up at the dark for a while.

Through the wall the farmhouse made its small settling sounds. A window latch. Water in a basin. The creak of a chair. Then something off the pattern: a door — the back door, not the front — and Betta's voice, low, and another voice from outside, not a family voice, pitched to a register and then stopped.

Maren didn't breathe.

Betta again. Not the words but the shape of them — the thing she'd learned in four days to listen for. Careful. Controlled. A woman managing a conversation she hadn't wanted.

The outside voice said something. Not a question. A statement, quiet, which was its own kind of pressure.

Then Betta, one sentence, quiet. Then the door.

Then nothing.

Maren lay in the hay a long time. The cows breathed. The donkey shifted and settled. Whatever had been at the door was gone, or she just couldn't hear it, which wasn't the same.

Maybe she was building a threat out of a farmwife talking at her own back door. Maybe she'd been wrong about the deacon's quality of looking too.

She left before the false dawn, in the dark between the last moon and the first light. She took nothing that wasn't hers. She wanted to leave something — coins, the herbs from her pack, a note — and didn't, because anything left behind was a record of the way she'd gone, and she couldn't be sure about the voice at the door, and she wasn't going to trade sense for sentiment.

She was sorry to leave the warmth of the barn. She marked the pang of it, and marked that it was the only thing she was sorry about. Four days ago there'd have been more. She was running sums now. Whether that was good or bad could wait.

She chose north and walked into the dark.

---

Two hours on, in the first gray light, she came to a boundary marker.

She'd passed them all her life — squat stone pillars, waist-high, carved with the Chrysalis spiral and the name of the nearest temple district. You paid attention to them if you were on temple business or settling a claim or making a pilgrimage, and she had never been any of those.

She stopped at this one.

The spiral was worn smooth at the center where hands had laid on it over years. Below it, in formal temple script she'd been taught to read as a child: *In this territory, all who carry the gift are under the protection of the Chrysalis.* She'd read it before. It had never made her feel anything. It named a category she wasn't in, and she'd understood it the way you understand a rule that isn't about you.

She looked at it now.

*All who carry the gift.* She didn't carry the gift. That had been the fixed point she'd built herself around for three years — longer, if she was honest, since she'd begun to suspect at thirteen that she wouldn't open, and known for certain at sixteen. Hollow, she'd called it, because that was the word everyone used, and children take the words they're given even when they fit badly.

Hollow. Empty of the thing that was supposed to be in her.

She stood at the marker in the early cold and looked straight at it, maybe for the first time, instead of from the corner of the eye she'd always used to manage it.

She wasn't hollow. The hollow wasn't in her.

The hollow was in someone's plan for her.

*Three riders for one girl who doesn't have the change.*

*The deacon didn't look at Bram. He didn't look at Jory. He looked at my arm.*

*Her father had no change either. He left a long time before she was old enough to need the pouch — before anyone had a reason to look at him.*

Something the bloodline had wanted then, for whatever reason, and wanted still. She was a seventeen-year-old girl walking north across plateau country in the predawn cold, and she was a piece of something she couldn't yet see the shape of.

She took the disc out and turned it once. The marks caught the growing light and let it go.

She was going to have to find out what she was a piece of.

She put it away and walked. The sun came up ahead of her and a little to the right, which meant she was drifting east, and she corrected, and the road showed in the valley below and she took it, because paranoia had limits and her knee needed even ground.

North was the safest direction. It was also the only one she had.

She walked it.


## Chapter Six — *[Davan]*
### Confluence

---

The city announced itself by smell before it came into view.

Davan had grown up in a valley town that smelled of woodsmoke and river water and the sweetness of fermenting grain from the mill at the south end, and had thought of that as simply the smell of a place where people lived. The Confluence spent the last hour of his walk teaching him how small and particular that had been. A city built where three rivers met carried three sets of river-smell and the trade that ran on all of them — fish and wet wool and tallow and the sharp bite of a tannery somewhere upwind he hoped was far off. Under it, people. More people than he'd ever been near at once, their massed warmth reaching him almost as pressure.

He stopped on the ridge above the valley and looked.

Not the largest city on Thera — he'd learned that much on the road, where New Plymouth to the north was always named as the thing that made everywhere else look like what it was. This was a river-junction town grown prosperous and dense and tangled, buildings climbing the valley sides in an arrangement that suggested accumulated decision more than any plan. Three bridges. A temple on the eastern bank, older than the buildings around it, its stone darker and rougher than anything near it. A market square he could place by the quality of its noise, an active frequency riding over the general roar.

Three weeks from Amber Valley. The Confluence was on the way north and big enough to be no one in, the two things he wanted in a place right now. He had money for two or three lean days. After that, work, or keep moving, or both.

He went down into the city.

---

The market was everything he'd heard a market could be. He moved through it slow, not buying — he couldn't afford to buy — but taking it in with the attention three weeks of walking had made into a habit. A dozen sellers of dried fish alone. Three grades of lamp oil. A spice stall that reached him before he saw it, a wall of warm complicated smell that stopped him in the street.

In Amber Valley the changes had been familiar; he'd grown up with the same forty-odd people's mutations and watched them come on over years. Here he passed strangers wearing changes he had no reference for, and the not-recognizing settled in his chest as a kind of humidity, not painful, just there.

Then the woman selling river-eel two stalls down had a short sharp argument with a customer, and the spike of her anger came at him so specific and so placed that he turned toward it without meaning to, as if it had been a sound. He stood still a moment. The argument settled, the customer paid and went, the anger dropped away, and the pressure in his chest dropped with it.

He walked on, and didn't look back at the eel stall.

He'd been telling himself for two weeks that the bleed — he had no better word for it — would settle as the change matured. He had nothing to base that on. No idea if it would settle. No one to ask.

He was at the far end of the row, near the cloth-sellers, when he felt the other thing.

Not anger — he knew anger's spike now. Something lower and wider, a compound he had no name for. Unease, and under it something harder, with an edge. Contempt, maybe, but with fear stirred through it, the way some poisons worked by mixing two things harmless on their own. It came from several directions at once, converging.

He found the source fast, because the crowd was showing it to him. A gap had opened in the foot traffic by a dry-goods stall — not large, just the particular widening that happens when people move away from something without deciding to, the body's verdict landing before the mind's. In the gap, a man: maybe forty, thin, plain clothes, a canvas bag half-filled with purchases. He stood at the counter waiting to be served. The vendor served someone else. Then a third someone. The man waited, straight-backed, face neutral, hands still, giving no one anything to catch on — the bearing of a man long practiced at waiting like this.

The vendor ran out of other customers. She looked at the man, looked past him, and called instead to a woman browsing the next stall who hadn't been waiting at all.

The man said nothing. The gap around him widened. A woman with a child crossed to the far side of the row rather than pass behind him — casually, the way you step around a puddle, the small efficient avoidance of a thing you don't want your child near.

From three stalls off the compound feeling was clearer now. Not one person's, but many overlapping — the vendor's flat refusal, the mother's quick uneasy sum, and from the ring nearest the gap a low steady note of something Davan could only call wrongness aimed outward. As if the man's plain unremarkable presence were producing in the people around him a reaction that had nothing to do with anything he did and everything to do with something he wasn't. Something missing. Something that should have been there and wasn't — the baseline warmth, the thing that said *one of us* below the level of thought.

He wasn't showing a change. Davan looked for one — any of the dozen he'd seen in the last hour — and found nothing. The man looked simply, entirely human. Unchanged. And the crowd was answering that absence the way a body answers a splinter: local inflammation, a focused effort to push out the thing that didn't belong.

The man bought nothing. After a while he shouldered the bag, which already held purchases from other stalls, other vendors who'd apparently been willing, and walked on, looking at no one, the way Davan thought you'd walk after a lifetime of it — steadily, expecting nothing else.

What came off him as he passed was the worst of it. Not anger, not humiliation, not even the flat endurance Davan had braced for. Something quieter. A tiredness worn through tiredness and out the other side into something that worked like calm and wasn't.

Davan stood in the market and watched him go, and thought about the girl on the road ahead of him — dark-haired, seventeen, no change showing — and understood for the first time that what she was running from was not only the riders.

---

He found the temple in the late afternoon, by the practical logic of it — a temple district in a city this size would keep a travelers' hostel, and a senior cleric could be talked into housing a wandering young man willing to stack record-crates and carry water. He'd done it twice already on the road north.

He was crossing the outer court when he saw the Keeper.

Not close. The man stood across the court, speaking with two junior priests in the quiet decisive way of someone used to settling small problems before they grew — a quality Davan's father had, running his shop with the same economy. The Keeper was large, bearded, fifty or so, carrying the tiredness that comes from administration rather than labor. He didn't see Davan. He went inside.

Davan turned to the door, and the symbol above it.

Carved into the stone lintel in the old formal style, big enough to read from the courtyard. He'd looked at this symbol in some form his whole life — above the village shrine, on travelling priests' vestments, pressed into the wax of documents — and had always read the inner spiral as a spiral. Every Blooming in Amber Valley opened with the Recitation, *and the Three Witnesses moved through the dark, and they saw the world below*, and the spiral was the Chrysalis descending, the gift coming down into the Founders, the orderly beautiful story of a world handed to the people who deserved it.

He looked at it now and something in him answered — not his eyes, not his mind, but the part that had flinched from the eel-seller's anger. The symbol felt like something. Not sacred, not decorative. Lonely. The loneliness of a thing going round and round, held in place by something it couldn't leave and couldn't reach. Not a descent. A vigil.

He stood with the feeling of it moving through him until a junior priest asked if he needed help, and he said yes, and went to the practical business of trading a night's lodging for the record-crates.

He did not stop thinking about what he'd seen in the symbol.

---

He ate in the hostel with a dozen travelers and spoke to none of them at length — a family bound for a Blooming in New Plymouth, two merchants who talked only to each other, an old man who ate with fierce concentration and then fell asleep at the table. The food was plain and filling. Davan ate his and half of what the sleeping man left, without guilt; his body had been making reasonable demands for three weeks and getting most of them half-met.

After, he went down to the river.

The Confluence made the most sense from the bank. Three rivers arriving from three directions and merging not violently but with a kind of patient inevitability, the water from each taking its time to mix, so that in the right light you could still see the separate currents running alongside one another in the main channel, not yet the same thing. It was the kind of sight that made him want to carve — his version of wanting to think.

He worked the piece he'd carried for a week, a length of close-grained hardwood he'd found on the road, more resistant to the knife than pine, demanding more of him. He was making something he couldn't name. A shape that opened at one end and closed at the other. Something to do with a container, that wasn't one.

He sat until the light went, and then longer, because the river at night was its own thing.

The barge came in with the last of the twilight — a long flat-bottomed river-freighter heading north, a load of clay casks, four passengers on the forward deck. It tied up with the ease of a crew that had done it a thousand times.

He'd been thinking about the barge for two days. He had the fare if he didn't eat much before New Plymouth, and by every account the road north was duller than the river for getting where he was going. He watched the crew work, confirmed the decision he'd already made, and went to ask the captain about a fare.

---

The barge left at first light.

Davan stood at the bow and watched the Confluence shrink behind them and the northern valley open out, the river widening as the merged currents found their new course. The other passengers were aft: a merchant couple who'd brought their own food and wanted no company, and a woman alone, sitting on a folded blanket with her back to the cargo and her eyes shut.

Her name was Lera. She told him when she opened her eyes and found him looking, not annoyed, just direct, like someone for whom the conventions around privacy had gone mostly theoretical.

She was perhaps sixty, or older in ways that made the number less useful. The Chrysalis had been working on her a long time: her arms, below the rolled sleeves, had gone translucent at the wrists, the tendons showing under skin grown faintly luminous — not light exactly, the suggestion of it, the way certain river stones seem lit from inside when wet. The change had taken her hands fully — the fingers longer than they should be by a joint each, elegant and strange, the nails a dark iridescence. Her face was still her own, a face that suggested a lifetime of close attention and an easy relationship with what it had found. Her eyes had gone from brown to a gray-gold that caught the light like water over a copper basin, not yet the full silver of the furthest changes but on the way.

"You've been looking at me since the dock," she said. Not an accusation.

"I'm sorry."

"Don't be. I look at people too." She considered him with the same open attention. "You're a long way from home."

"How do you know that?"

"You watch the river like it's doing something new. People who grew up with this river don't watch it anymore."

He sat down on the deck — not because she'd asked but because it seemed the right answer to her manner, and she didn't say otherwise.

"Where are you going?" he asked.

"New Plymouth. The final Rite." She said it the way you'd say you were going to market. "I've been meaning to for some years. My daughter kept finding reasons to delay me." She seemed amused. "She's stopped trying."

He knew the final Rite — the ceremony for those in the last stages of the change, a formal witnessing of what it had made of a life. He looked at her arms again, briefly, and she allowed it.

"Does it hurt?" he asked. "The change, this far along."

"Not anymore." She looked at her own forearms with honest assessment. "The first years hurt. When it's working against what you expected, what you'd decided you should be. Then it stops hurting and starts just being what you are." A pause. "That's the theology. It's also my experience. I can only speak for myself."

They rode a while in silence. The river bent east around a long curve and the sun came off the water white for a moment.

"Can I ask you something," Davan said.

"You've been asking me things."

"Something I don't know how to ask politely."

She made a small gesture: ask it.

"The old stories. The Founders, the first people. The church says the change was given — that the moons witnessed it, that the Founders were chosen for this world." He stopped. "What if they weren't?"

"What if they were what instead?"

"Just people," he said. "Who came from somewhere and ended up here, and the world worked on them, and they made a story about it after, to explain what had happened."

He'd never said it aloud. He wasn't sure where it had been living, only that it had been, and the river and the morning and this transparent-armed woman with her directness had been the conditions it needed to come out.

She considered it long enough that he started to regret asking.

Then: "What would be the difference?"

"What?"

"The change is real. The transformation is real. The world entered them and made them into something more than they'd been, and it has gone on doing it with everyone who's carried the Chrysalis since." She wasn't arguing. She was reasoning alongside him. "Whether someone chose them for it or it was an accident of being in the wrong place — or the right one — what does that change about the thing that happened?"

He thought. "It changes what we're supposed to do with it."

"Does it?" She tilted her head. "I've always thought that part was ours either way. The story about where it came from doesn't tell me what to do with my arms. I worked that out myself, over fifty years." She looked at the water. "The story is a useful envelope. What's inside it is what matters."

"But if the story's wrong," he said. "Wrong in a way that leaves out something load-bearing."

"Plenty of useful things are wrong. A map is wrong — a simplification that gets you across the country without getting lost; the not-getting-lost is the point, not the map." A pause, something reconsidering its own edges. "Though I'll grant you — a map wrong enough to walk you off a cliff would be a different problem."

He sat with that.

"You've thought about this before," he said.

"Forty years. You have the advantage of being earlier in it."

The river moved. The merchant couple ate their packed food and ignored them. The morning grew into afternoon.

---

He slept on the deck under his coat, warm enough for the air and not for the sky — more stars than he'd expected, the moons all out and the cloudless dark giving him more of the space between them than he was used to. He lay on his back and looked up.

Three moons, all up at once — more often than their different cycles seemed to allow. He'd been tracking them for weeks without meaning to, the way you absorb anything regular in your surroundings; he could call when Swiftmoon would be full now without counting on his fingers. He wasn't sure why that felt like it mattered.

The warmth came first, the way it always did — before the light, a slow pulse that went with his breath. Then the light: a low blue rising in the skin of his forearms where they lay crossed on his chest. He'd seen this one in still water once or twice — the blue that wasn't fear and wasn't gladness, just the look of him with nothing to manage. He was learning to read it lately, the colors and what they went with.

He heard her shift.

Lera was sitting up on her blanket a few feet off, watching the light move in his skin. Not alarmed. The same open attention she'd given him all day.

"Sorry," he said. "I can't always control—"

"I know. I've watched others learn. It comes when it wants, at first."

He looked back up at the sky. After a moment she spoke again, not unkindly.

"Your mutation will become what you believe it is."

He thought about that. "The way your arms became what you believed."

"Eventually. It took time. The first years I believed they were frightening, and they frightened me. Then I believed they were strange, and I lived strangely a while." Something like amusement. "Then I spent a long time believing they were mine, and that seemed to do it."

"What do you believe about my mutation?"

"Wrong question," she said. "What do *you* believe about it?"

He looked at the light in his skin. Blue, steady, his breath moving through it. Not performing anything, not managing anything — just the body saying what was true in a quiet moment.

"I think it shows the truth," he said.

"Yes," she said, simply. As if he'd got something right.

He lay and watched the moons and let the light do what it did. Overhead the three bright shapes kept their slow arcs, patient and certain, circling something he couldn't see. He traced their paths against the dark until his eyes wanted to close.

He was almost asleep when the thought came back — the one he'd carried since the inn at Colter's Crossing, since the counting rhyme had surfaced and he'd pushed it down for not making sense. *Four fires in the sky, one came down to die, three stayed up to cry.* He'd been counting three every night for weeks. Three was what was there. Three was what everyone knew.

But the song said four, and the symbol over the temple door had felt like something circling what it couldn't leave, and Lera had said *I think it shows the truth*, and now, in the space between waking and sleep where things join without asking, the rhyme and the symbol and the old woman's certainty were fitting together in a way they hadn't before. Not a new thought. The same one, arriving with its whole weight for the first time — the way a word you've known all your life suddenly means what it says.

He tried to hold the shape of it — four, not three, one came down, the vigil in the stone — but it was the kind of thought that won't survive being looked at straight. By morning there was only the place where it had been: the knowledge that something had been there and wasn't. Not nothing. Not much better.

---

New Plymouth announced itself the way the Confluence had, by smell before sight, but where the Confluence had smelled like a market town this was something else: a city, a real one, the accumulated chemical fact of a hundred thousand lives lived close together for a very long time. It reached him a mile out and didn't let up.

The river widened. Buildings came up on the banks and didn't stop. The captain called orders and the crew moved to them. Davan stood at the bow and watched New Plymouth come at him.

Two things ruled the skyline: the temple in the east district, enormous and very old, and the palace on the northern rise — also enormous, newer, and far more concerned with being seen to be where it was. He'd grown up hearing about the palace the way you hear about things too large to have a personal relationship with, like the sea, or the high desert. From the river it looked built to be looked at from exactly this distance. What stayed with him wasn't the stone but what three hundred years of unbroken occupation meant — the leverage of a bloodline that had never once had to be replaced.

He thought about the Wound-maker. That was his grandmother's name for it, said with the weight of someone who took it for real and not story. She'd had it from her grandmother, who'd had it from someone who claimed a cousin had been in New Plymouth at the Stilling of the Brace — three hundred years back, more — when a river-lord marched on the city and the bloodline brought the thing out onto the palace steps and used it once, only once, and the river-lord's army stopped and then quietly went home, and the river-lord himself was not seen again. Even in Amber Valley the story had four versions. In two of them the weapon sang. In one it wept. In all of them it left a mark on the stone, black and clean as a burn, still there, that no one who carried the change could look at straight.

A relic, a royal secret, a warning, a children's tale, depending on who told it and why. He could hold it as all of those at once and none of them quite real.

Then the barge docked, and he said goodbye to Lera, who pressed his hand and said nothing more, and he went into the city with his pack and his knife and his carving and roughly enough money for three spare days, maybe four.

He walked an hour before he found a street he knew from his grandmother's descriptions, and by then he was just another man with a pack moving in the foot traffic, nobody's business.

He found a flophouse near the docks and paid for a bunk and lay in the dark with the noise of the city all around him and the impressions coming through the walls — the sad person two rooms over, the angry one downstairs, the child two floors up dreaming something vivid enough to reach him this far — and he pressed his hand flat to the wall by the bunk and tried to take it not as intrusion but as information.

He didn't sleep well.

By morning he'd decided to stay at least a week.


## Chapter Seven — *[Aldric]*
### The Investigator

---

He had been lying to people for three weeks and was finding it more sustainable than he'd expected.

This was not a comfortable thing to discover about himself. He had spent thirty years in a profession that treated honesty as both a moral foundation and a practical necessity — people came to the temple in distress, and distress required truth to be useful, and he had believed this with the uncomplicated conviction of someone who had never seriously tested it. He had heard a great many confessions in his career and had always found the lies people told themselves more damaging than the ones they told each other, and had said so, and had meant it.

And now here he was, lying with moderate fluency to a mason, to a junior brother who had asked why the east undercroft smelled of old air, to the Confluence's district warden who had inquired about unusual activity in the temple precinct following a noise complaint from the neighboring house. Each time he had said the appropriate thing in the appropriate tone and the lie had held, and afterward the flat discomfort had arrived the way providence used to — unbidden, familiar, carrying its own weight — except providence had been a thing he believed himself under, and this was the same weight turned the other way: the distance between what he preached and what he did.

The worst had been Petra.

Petra was the junior archivist — twenty-three, careful, devoted to the work in the way of someone who had not yet learned to separate devotion from expectation. She had come to him on the second week with a question about her own conduct: a discrepancy in the archive catalog, a gap where records should have been, and she was unsure whether to report it through the standard channels or raise it with him directly, and she wanted his guidance because — she said this, looking up at him with the trust a novice gives a confessor — he was the most honest man she knew.

He had given her the correct advice. He had told her that discrepancies should always be reported through proper channels, that the integrity of the archive depended on transparent accounting, that she should never allow convenience or uncertainty to compromise her professional obligations. Every word was doctrine — the same doctrine he had preached on Sundays to people who trusted him to mean it — and it came out of his mouth in a voice indistinguishable from the voice that had meant it last year, and three floors below him was a sealed room he had not reported, containing objects he had not cataloged, which he was visiting in secret at three-day intervals. She had thanked him and left, and he had sat at his desk for a long time and had not been able to determine whether this was hypocrisy or the specific cruelty of a man who still believed the principle he was in the process of violating. There was no catechism for the distinction. There should have been.

He had also been going back to the chamber every three nights.

This he had not lied to himself about, which he supposed was something.

---

The glass — he had come to think of it this way, because it was the nearest word he had for something that showed you things it had no light source to show — had shown him different things each visit. Not randomly. He had begun to believe there was something like responsiveness to it, a quality of attending to what he lingered on. When he pressed his fingers near the orbital arrangements and held them there, the image stayed and sharpened. When he lifted his hand and came back to a different area, a different image surfaced: not the stars now but something closer, a curved blue-green shape hanging in the dark that produced in him, each time, a recognition he had no basis for and could not dismiss.

He had a basis for it, actually. He had been declining to.

The shape was in the old astronomy texts. Not prominently — a detail, a diagram, one of the bodies depicted in charts of the known lights with a symbol beside it that had always been glossed as *the wandering one* or *the distant blue* and which he had never examined closely because it had not been relevant to any question he was pursuing. He had been pursuing questions of theology and administration and pastoral care, sufficient questions that did not, generally, require him to look very hard at old astronomy.

He had pulled the astronomy texts from the archive the previous week. He had looked at the diagram. He had looked at the shape on the glass.

He had put the texts back in exactly the position he'd found them.

He had gone to morning prayers and pinched the corridor lamp out of habit — already cold, had been cold for hours, he did it anyway — and stood in the chapel and said the words with the same attention he'd given them for thirty years. Whether the attention was the same or only looked the same from the inside, he could no longer say. The liturgy knew how to walk the mouth of a man whose mind had gone elsewhere. He had always warned novices of this. He had not expected to recognize it in himself.

---

The Prioress arrived on a Tuesday, a market day, and the timing meant that half the temple's staff were occupied with the increased pilgrim traffic and the other half were managing the administrative surge that market days produced. Her arrival was noticed by fewer people and with less ceremony than it might otherwise have been. Aldric suspected this was not an accident.

Her name was Prioress Crane. He had met her twice before — once at a regional convocation fifteen years ago, when she'd been a senior archivist and he'd been a recently-appointed Keeper still learning the administrative registers; once at a ceremony in New Plymouth that he'd attended as part of a temple delegation, at which she had been pointed out to him as someone of consequence whom he should know by sight. She was in her middle years, spare and precise in the way of someone who had decided long ago what was essential and had organized her person accordingly. She wore the Prioress's marks on her vestments without ostentation. Her change, if she had one, showed nowhere visible.

She arrived in his office with two junior priests in tow and the specific pleasantness of someone who had decided that pleasantness was the most efficient mode for this particular meeting.

"Keeper Voss," she said. "I hope the intrusion isn't—"

"Not at all," he said, which was the appropriate thing to say and was also completely untrue. "Please sit. I'll have tea brought."

She sat. He sent one of his own juniors for tea. The two priests she'd brought remained near the door in the practiced way of people whose function was presence rather than participation.

They spoke about the road conditions south of the Confluence, which had been poor since the autumn rains. They spoke about the Blooming numbers for the year — up slightly, a sign of a healthy community. They spoke about the pilgrimage traffic, increased following the death of a well-regarded traveling preacher whose route had passed through the Confluence and who had acquired, in death, the gravity that the living version of him had not quite managed.

She asked how he was finding the work.

"The usual richness," he said. "A temple at a junction sees everything eventually. I find it — sustaining." He paused with the right timing. "Occasionally exhausting."

"Of course." She accepted the tea that arrived and wrapped both hands around the cup in the gesture of someone who was cold, though the office was not cold. "I ask partly for administrative reasons and partly because I've heard you've been keeping unusual hours."

He met her eyes. "Seasonal administration," he said. "The pre-winter inventory runs long."

"The pre-winter inventory," she repeated, pleasantly.

"There's more to account for than it appears. Storage conditions in the undercroft in particular. I've been doing some of it myself rather than assign it to the junior brothers — they have enough to manage this time of year."

She nodded. She looked at him the way someone looks when they are deciding how much they already know versus how much they are being told. He held the eye contact because looking away was the wrong thing to do.

"I'll be here for some weeks," she said. "A doctrinal review — nothing alarming, standard circuit. The Confluence district is overdue." She smiled. "I hope we won't be in each other's way."

"The temple is yours, Prioress," he said. "I'll arrange suitable rooms."

After she left he sat at his desk for a while without doing anything. Then he reached toward the lamp and caught himself and lowered his hand and thought about what a doctrinal review meant in practice: access to the archive, access to the financial records, access to the correspondence logs, and the presence of a senior church official with the authority to request explanations for things that required explaining. He could walk to her rooms tomorrow and say it plainly — *there is a sealed chamber, I have not filed the report, there are five objects I cannot account for* — and she would listen, and she would be thorough, and she would file the appropriate reports through the appropriate channels, and the process would do what processes were built to do. He thought the word *confess* and replaced it with *report* and the replacement did not feel as different as it should have.

He thought about the sealed room two floors below him.

He thought about the note he'd found in the archive three weeks ago: *Restricted Collection, NP Archive, shelf 7-eastern. Access: Bloodline approval required.*

He thought about the letter to his former student at the New Plymouth archive — two weeks in composition, still unsent, because sending it would be the kind of action that could not be unsent. Some doors, once opened, rearranged the rooms on the other side.

He took out the letter. He read it. He revised two sentences that were more direct than they needed to be, replacing them with the oblique scholarly language of someone pursuing an innocent academic interest. He read it again.

He sealed it and put it with the outgoing correspondence.

Then he went and told the hostelmaster to prepare the east guest rooms for the Prioress's use, putting her on the opposite side of the temple from the undercroft — which was not why he chose those rooms, and which he declined to examine.

---

The weeks of Crane's presence established a new rhythm. She was everywhere and nowhere — present at morning prayers, absent for stretches of the day, reappearing at meals with the pleasant undemanding manner of a very good house guest, the kind who requires nothing and therefore cannot be anticipated. He began to sense where she had recently been by the alertness she left behind, a quality that lingered in a room the way important things tended to.

She was reviewing the archive. He knew this because Petra had mentioned it with the bright institutional neutrality of someone who had been told to be helpful and was being helpful in a way that communicated exactly how helpful she was being and nothing further. Crane was working through the eastern catalog. She had questions about certain acquisition dates. She was very thorough.

He had tried to stop.

He did not tell himself this often. To admit it was to admit that the attempt had been real and the failure had been real too, and the two of them together described a man he was not prepared to be.

He had stopped for eight days. He'd told himself the correct course was to file the blank discovery report through the proper channels and let the institution he'd served for thirty years do what institutions did with information. He had believed it. He had gone to prayers, pinched the cold corridor lamp, and felt — for the first time in weeks — the relief of a man who has decided to behave correctly.

The relief lasted four days. On the fifth he found himself at the top of the undercroft stairs at half past ten with no memory of having decided to go — he'd set a book down in his room and walked the length of the eastern corridor and down a flight of stairs while his mind was still on the next chapter, and here he was at the door, the cold coming up from below, his hand already reaching for the latch.

He went back to his room. He sat on the edge of his bed. He held his hands together in his lap and examined them as though they belonged to someone he was only beginning to know. A vigil, he thought, and the word arrived liturgical — the wrong language for a man drawn back to a sealed room against his own decision, and the only language he had. A compulsion that did not feel like compulsion. A question the body had already answered while the mind was still formulating its objections.

On the eighth day he went down, and did not pretend, this time, that it was a decision. He went because the not-going had begun to feel less like discipline and more like a man pressing his hands over his eyes and calling it dark — and because the light in the glass below was patient in the way of something that did not need his participation but was available for it, and the patience was worse than urgency.

He continued his nights.

He had been going below every three nights now, with a regularity he had not intended. The glass was showing him more. He had developed, over the weeks, what he thought of as a tentative method: he would sit with it in the dark for the first hour, letting his eyes adjust to the cold light it produced, learning what was available on this visit, and then in the second hour he would work more deliberately, returning to specific arrangements and staying with them. The structure had come to him without effort — darkness first, then focused attention, then the slow layered deepening — and he had not examined where it came from, in the way he had learned not to examine certain things about himself. The star chart had become more legible to him — not as an expert would read it, but he had begun to locate consistent features, recurring shapes, to orient himself within it.

The four containers sat on their shelf behind him each time, unchanging. He had tried the ridges on all of them over the second week — a thing he'd told himself he was only confirming — and the seams had not given, and the cold of them had gone into his hands and stayed there, and he had not tried again. He had thought of them the way he thought of a locked door in an occupied house: present, noted, someone else's business, and not, yet, his to force. Four. A number he caught himself counting once, against nothing, and put down.

There was one arrangement he kept returning to.

A burning point at the center — unmistakably a star, the kind that things moved around — and around it: several bodies in various orbits. Most were unremarkable, background detail only. Two or three were larger, closer to the center, with markings near them that meant nothing he could determine. But one — small, blue-green, third from the star — had more markings around it than the others. More notation. And, on this most recent visit, something he had not noticed before or which had not been visible before: four small shapes in tight orbit around it. Small. Regular. Arranged at different distances.

He counted them several times. Four.

He had grown up, as everyone had grown up, with three moons. The three Witnesses. *The Three Witnesses remain above us still. They do not leave. They have never left.* He had spoken those words at the start of every Blooming ceremony for thirty years, and they had always sounded like the bedrock kind of truth — the kind you don't argue with because it isn't making a claim, it is simply describing the world as it is.

He counted the shapes on the glass again. His lips moved.

Four.

The Recitation surfaced in his mind unbidden, the way liturgy always surfaced — *They do not leave. They have never left.* He had spoken the words ten thousand times. They had always meant faithfulness. The Witnesses, constant, devoted, keeping their vigil over the world they had gifted. He heard them now and the meaning shifted under him like a floor settling — *They do not leave.* Not faithfulness. Grief. The stubborn circling of something that would not abandon what it had lost. *They have never left* — not because they chose to stay, but because they could not bring themselves to go.

He sat back on his heels and looked at the blue-green shape for a long time. The cold worked through his knees. He did not move.

He was a careful man. Careful his entire adult life, with a robust appreciation for the ways in which the human mind found patterns when it very much wanted patterns to be there. He held all of this in one hand — the patient scholar, the institutional warning against zeal, the catechism of temperate reasoning he had recited to novices for twenty years. A witness, he thought, and then thought: no. A witness was a liturgical category, not an evidentiary one, and he was conducting — he was examining. He set the word down on the cold stone beside him and did not pick it up again.

In the other hand: the blue-green shape. The symbol in the doorway lintel that described an orbit. The dying woman's grip on his wrist, her silver eyes, the apologetic quality of her look. The note in the archive: *Restricted Collection. Bloodline approval required.*

He had not drawn the conclusion. He was aware of having declined to draw it, actively, for three weeks. Sitting on the cold stone floor of a sealed room beneath a temple, he understood that declining to draw a conclusion was not the same as the conclusion not being there.

He got up. His knees protested the way they had begun to protest all floor-level activities since he'd passed fifty, and he ignored this the way he ignored all indications that his body had opinions about what he asked of it. The glass held its own light. The four small shapes around the blue-green world. The one closest — the one that would be largest in the sky — had a quality he had not noticed before, or was only now in a position to notice: it was different from the others. Less regular in shape, as though something had happened to it.

He pinched the lamp out without thinking, which plunged the room into the cold faint illumination of the glass alone, and in that light he looked at the one irregular shape and thought: *damaged.*

Then he thought about what would have to happen to a moon to make it irregular.

He stood in the dark for a long time with the light of the glass on his face, and then he went upstairs and sat at his desk and wrote to Brennan, his old friend from seminary, now a scholar in Greenford. He had not been composing this one. It arrived in twenty minutes, unplanned — driven by the cold stone floor and the four small shapes and the thing he could no longer pretend not to see.

He did not write: *I think I know something terrible and wonderful and I need someone to tell me I'm wrong.* He wrote the scholarly equivalent, which was longer and meant the same thing. It was, he noticed sealing it, considerably less oblique than the careful letter already in the outgoing post.

He sealed it. He looked at it.

Then he did something he had not done in thirty years of correspondence: he checked the seal twice, pressing it with his thumb long after it had set, making sure of something already sure.

He put it with the outgoing post.

---

He was on his way back from the undercroft two nights later when footsteps came down the eastern corridor.

The quality of the step arrived before the source. Not a junior brother — too considered, moving with the quiet purposefulness of someone navigating a space they were still learning. He was at the top of the undercroft stairs with the door not yet shut behind him, and the footsteps were coming from the direction of the archive room, and they were heading toward him.

He shut the door quietly. He had perhaps fifteen seconds.

He moved to the record crates stacked against the eastern wall — a genuine storage project he'd been meaning to address since autumn — and lifted the lid of the nearest one as Prioress Crane came around the corridor's turn.

She stopped.

He looked up from the crate as if mildly surprised. He kept his hand inside the crate, resting on a stack of papers, because removing it abruptly would be wrong. "Prioress," he said. "I didn't hear you."

"I couldn't sleep," she said. "I was reviewing the acquisition ledgers." She looked at the crates, at him, at the corridor with its single lamp. "I didn't expect company."

"The pre-winter inventory," he said. "There's always more of it than I plan for."

She looked at the crate, the undercroft door behind him — closed, unremarkable, the kind of door that blended into a corridor of doors. Then at him again.

"I was about to head down," he said, and heard the fraction of a second too late that *down* was the wrong word, and moved past it without pausing. "The lower storage. Some of the climate records from the Brace period need re-housing. The moisture down there this autumn has been a concern." He replaced the crate lid. "I won't keep you if you're working."

"No," she said. She had not moved. "I was just finishing." She looked at the corridor one more time — not at him, at the space — and then she smiled. "The eastern wing has interesting architecture, doesn't it. The foundations look older than the rest of the building."

The sentence landed exactly as it was meant to.

"The temple has been built and rebuilt over several centuries," he said. "The foundations in this section do predate the current structure. If you're interested in the building history, I believe there's a survey in the archive from the last major renovation — the Keeper before me commissioned it."

"How thoughtful of you," she said. "I'll look for it." She turned with the unhurried manner of someone who has learned what she came to learn. "Good night, Keeper Voss."

"Good night, Prioress."

He waited until her footsteps had gone fully out of range before he allowed himself to move. He set the crate lid back in place with unnecessary care. He stood in the corridor for a moment with the lamp's thin light around him.

She already knew the foundations didn't match. She had been down this corridor before, possibly more than once, at hours when she expected to find it empty. The building history survey in the archive would not contain what she was looking for — he had already checked, three weeks ago — but she would look, and the looking would confirm what she suspected, and she was a patient woman who had not become Prioress by moving before she had what she needed.

She had not asked him directly. She was waiting for him to make a mistake, or to decide to trust her, and she appeared to be comfortable waiting for either. A fellow servant. That was the register she invited from him, and he was going to have to meet her as an adversary instead, which he had not taken vows to do.

He went to bed and lay awake until the early hours, when the sound of the archive room door closing travelled through the temple from the far side. Quiet, unhurried. Crane, working late. Or arriving early. Or neither — a door closing told you only that a door had closed, and he was becoming too alert to gaps in information to assume the rest.

He lay in the dark and pressed his thumb and forefinger together and found nothing to pinch and released them and looked at the ceiling.

Thirty years ago, when he was young and full of the dream of doing something historically significant, he could not have told you what he meant by that.

He was beginning to think the answer had not been waiting for him to want it.

He listened to the temple settle around him. Stone and timber, centuries of accumulated decision, the weight of a place where a great many people had come to find something they needed and had sometimes found it. He had served this place faithfully. He still believed in the service. He was less certain, with each passing night, what sanctum his service had been built over.

In the morning he was in the corridor before the dawn bell and the lamp outside the record room was cold and he pinched it anyway, thumb and forefinger, the familiar nothing of it, and walked on toward the chapel to say the words he had been saying for thirty years and meant and was not sure anymore in what direction.


## Chapter Eight — *[Vane]*
### Trail

---

She was five days behind when she left New Plymouth, farther back than she liked.

The delay had been unavoidable — two days sourcing the right road information, one acquiring gear for an extended northern route, another half-day on the paperwork a legitimate skip-retrieval earlier in the week had generated, because the docks constabulary had an appetite for documentation that bore no relationship to the actual work. She'd done all of it with efficient irritation and set out north on a clear morning with her pack and her maps and the working assumption that a seventeen-year-old on foot, rationing money, sleeping rough, was not moving as fast as she looked.

She moved fast. New Plymouth to the country around Stillwater was four or five days at her pace. Five days behind at departure meant the gap was closeable — it had to be, or the job was already done badly before it started.

The first three days produced nothing. No trace at waypoint inns, no reliable witness, the usual noise of rumor that gathered around any active search and had to be filtered for signal. She filtered. The bloodline's agents were ahead of her in some places and behind her in others, casting wide rather than following a trail. They didn't have one. The girl was not predictable. She was making good decisions about when to be seen and when not to be.

On the fourth day, at a crossroads village south of Stillwater, she found the kitchen.

---

The tavern-keeper confirmed a dark-haired girl had worked the kitchen three days, left abruptly, owed nothing and was owed a half-day's wages she hadn't collected. That last detail was interesting. People who left wages behind were either frightened into moving fast or principled about not taking what they hadn't earned. Both said something about the girl.

Vane stood in the kitchen doorway a moment before she went in.

It was occupied now by a different girl doing the morning prep, chopping roots with the mechanical rhythm of someone who'd done the task ten thousand times and expected to do it ten thousand more. She looked up when Vane appeared, then went back to the chopping with the practiced incuriosity of someone who'd learned that looking at strangers too long in a tavern kitchen led to conversations she hadn't chosen.

Vane was not interested in her. She was interested in the air.

Three days since the girl had been here — long enough that most of it had gone into the layered smell of a working kitchen, fat and smoke and old wood and the green note of herbs. But there were traces, if you knew them, and she did. Fear left a cold-metal signature that clung to surfaces for days; sustained terror under a performance of calm left its own compound, suppressed adrenaline beneath enforced steadiness, and she'd been reading that one for years. She found none of it. Nothing frightened.

The kitchen smelled of someone who had been alert. Watchful. Not afraid.

She stood there another moment and revised her working model of the girl.

Then she went out the window. Not because she needed to — the door was right there — but because she wanted to see the drop. A body's height to the ground, onto flagstones, awkward in the dark. She could smell the stone below, the faint iron trace of an old landing, recent enough to still carry something. A stumble on the left side. Recovered fast. She noted the stumble and went around to the yard.

---

The road north of the village had a section where the agents had stopped.

She found it an hour later — boot prints, the weight of horses standing a while, the residue of a conversation with someone on foot. A farmer, from the second set of prints. She looked at where he'd stood when he talked to them, and the direction his feet had pointed, and then walked fifty yards down the road to where a low embankment ran alongside a drainage ditch heavy with the dead scrub of late autumn.

She crouched at the embankment and looked at it.

The disturbed area was small — a narrow gap where someone had pushed through quickly, branches scratched at the height of a person crouching, the inner bank scuffed where a boot had slipped and caught. She stayed crouched a moment.

No fear-smell. The residue was hours old and faint, but she could work with faint. Alert. Watchful. The compound of a person holding very still while something dangerous moved nearby — heightened attention, suppressed breath, the low register of someone who'd decided to make themselves small and was doing it well. And under all of it, the thing she'd caught first in the kitchen and hadn't known what to do with.

Ordinary. Simply, completely ordinary. None of the thing she'd learned to read in infected people — that baseline warmth, present even in the barely-changed, that came from whatever the Chrysalis did to the body's basic workings.

It wasn't there. The girl smelled like a person and nothing else. The warmth the rest of them carried — the trace of an active organism in the body, ten thousand years of it bred into the baseline — was simply absent. She'd been tracking people long enough to have been reading that warmth without knowing she read it, using it in every assessment she made. Now that it was gone her nose kept going back to the absence, with the confused insistence of a tongue finding the gap where a tooth used to be.

She stood up and walked north.

---

She found the farmstead by the boy.

He was perhaps ten, coming back up the road with an empty cart and the faintly proprietary air of a child who has finished something he'd been trusted with and knows it. She smelled him before she reached him: woodsmoke, turnip earth, and something else — a warm particular note she'd learned over years to read as protectiveness. Recent. Aimed at someone specific, not general.

She slowed and let him come to her.

He assessed her with the frank efficiency of a child who spent time alone on roads and had developed views about strangers. She let him look — hands visible, posture without urgency — and watched his face decide, and her nose confirmed what his face said: cautious, not closed. He'd made a distinction between her and whatever had worried him before. She couldn't have said what put her in the better category, except that she wasn't on a horse and wasn't asking questions before he'd had a chance to weigh her. She put him in the middle category and walked on.

She asked about the road conditions north. He answered. They talked about the weather, briefly — two people who've established they're not a threat to each other and are deciding what else they might be.

She bought a turnip from him. He named an ambitious price and she paid it without negotiating, which landed as respect rather than naivety. The protectiveness-scent didn't fade and didn't sharpen.

She asked, putting the turnip in her pack, whether there was somewhere north she might find a meal and a barn for the night.

He thought about it. The scent shifted — a small complicated calculation. He mentioned a farmstead, his family's, his mother wouldn't mind; described the road without quite meeting her eyes.

He didn't mention the girl. She hadn't expected him to. She noted the shape of what he left out and walked on.

---

The farm wife's name was Betta. Late forties, compact and capable, hands that spoke to a lifetime of physical work and eyes that had decided something about Vane within three seconds of the door opening and were not revising it.

Vane smelled her at once: clean work-sweat and lanolin and something unresolved between throat and chest — a decision recently made and not yet settled into. Not fear. Something more deliberate than fear. The smell of a position being held.

"I'm looking for a young woman," Vane said. "Dark hair. She may have passed through a few days ago."

"A lot of people pass through," Betta said.

"This one would have been traveling alone. On foot."

"People do that also."

She was not going to be helpful. Vane had known it before the first sentence and her nose confirmed it now. She stepped back from the door slightly and waited.

Betta did not fill the silence — the mark of someone who'd understood that talking was usually the problem, not the solution. She held it longer than most. Vane gave her credit for it. No spike of fear, no anxious acceleration. Steady. The held position.

Eventually Vane said: "I'm not with the bloodline."

Something shifted in Betta's expression. Not much. A recalibration.

"She was here," Vane said. "She helped your boy with his cart. She slept in the barn. She left before dawn." A pause. "I'm not going to bring her back to anyone. I want to know which way she went."

What she didn't say: she'd already read the answer, in the kitchen and the ditch and the boy's careful omission. She knew the direction. What she was doing now was establishing whether Betta had a reason to lie that pointed at a reason to worry.

Betta looked at her a long moment. The scent held steady. The decision already made, just waiting to be spoken.

"North and east," Betta said finally. "She was heading for the road."

"Did she say anything? About where she was going?"

"No."

"Did she seem to know?"

Betta looked at a point on the doorpost past Vane's shoulder. "She seemed like she'd decided north was enough."

Vane nodded. She put two coins on the doorstep — not so many as to insult, not so few as to mean nothing. Betta looked at them and didn't move to take them.

"She didn't take anything that wasn't hers," Betta said. "In case that's something you need to know."

It was. "Thank you," Vane said.

She went north.

---

She was an hour past the farmstead when the world opened a register wider than she'd asked for.

Not the full experience, nothing so organized — just the leading edge of it, the walls going briefly generous. She stopped walking, because walking while this happened was unwise. She stood in the road and waited for it to pass, and while it passed she smelled, with perfect clarity, something that did not belong to this road or this countryside or this cold afternoon.

Copper, and something like flowers. Neither real. Both insistent.

Someone else's smell. The Bloom-giver's — the psychic contamination she'd been told about when she first started using, and dismissed at the time as something that happened to people less careful than her. She was, it turned out, not the exception she'd assumed. She was just further along the same road.

With the smell came a fragment of sensation: a ceiling. Low, stone, air that had not moved in a long time. The weight of being thoroughly and finally stationary, which was different from being still.

Then it was gone.

She stood in the road until she was sure the ground was the ground and the sky was the sky. It took a moment longer than it should have.

When she started walking again she missed the trail. The girl's scent had been riding just east of the road for a mile, a thin steady line she'd been reading without attention — and now it wasn't there. She backtracked thirty yards, fifty, eighty, and found nothing. On Bloom she'd have had all three layers at once, road and girl and weather, and walked it blind; off it, the layers didn't cooperate, and she had to assemble them by hand. She found the trail again six minutes later, where the girl had stepped into a dry drainage line and the scent had dropped below the hedge-smell. She'd known how to read a ditch like that for ten years. She hadn't registered it the first time past. Six minutes lost on a trail two days cold was not a mistake she could afford twice.

She took the wafer from her coat pocket. She'd taken to carrying one loose rather than going into her pack for the tin — a habit that had grown without her noticing, the way the wafer in the pocket had come before the tin and the tin had come before the question of whether she needed it. She held it between her fingers. The faint floral smell reached her. Almost nothing. The familiar absence that was its own kind of statement.

She thought about what she kept finding in the girl's traces: that same almost-nothing. No baseline warmth, no signature, just a person moving through the world and leaving the ordinary record of one. An absence her nose couldn't stop returning to.

Two absences. One in her pocket. One she was following north. She noted the parallel and put the wafer away without using it.

She made a note in her shorthand when she stopped for water — three words and a symbol, the kind of entry that would smell, when she read it later, of this road and this afternoon and a thought she hadn't finished having.

---

That night, in the woodcutter's shelter, she took a wafer.

Not because she needed it — she was on schedule, two and a half days, she could have waited. Because the files were spread around her on the floor and the connections were coming one at a time, each link requiring the conscious assembly that was, in the ordinary state, the cost of the work. She could do it that way. She'd done it that way for years before the Bloom. It was simply slower, and slower was not currently acceptable.

The wafer dissolved. The warmth moved outward from her chest. And then the files opened.

Not the paper — the paper didn't move. But the scent-record of every note rose to meet her at once, weeks of observation compositing into a single layered image that arrived not as thought but as recognition. The kitchen south of Stillwater: alert, watchful, the girl's residue sitting clean and competent in the fat-smoke of the cooking space. The ditch embankment: the stumble, the recovery, the left-side favor she'd been tracking since. The boy on the road: his protectiveness aimed at something behind him and to the south, the direction he hadn't pointed when he mentioned the farmstead. The farmwife's held position, the chemical signature of a decision made before Vane arrived. All of it at once, every piece in place, the picture assembling without effort — a girl moving north through a corridor of people who had quietly, without coordinating, decided to let her pass.

She saw, with the Bloom's compound clarity, what would have taken her another two days to assemble in the ordinary state: the girl was not alone. Not in the way of companions — she traveled solo, that was clear. But the country itself was cooperating. Small acts of shelter, small acts of concealment, the organic network of people who recognized something in her passage and answered it without being asked. The boy. The farmwife. Probably others she hadn't found.

The picture held perhaps an hour. She wrote through it with a fluency that had nothing to do with speed and everything to do with the absence of friction — each note landing in its right place, each connection confirmed and cross-referenced, the whole architecture of the search laid out in her shorthand with a precision she could feel in her hands as rightness rather than effort.

Then the hour ended, and the world stepped back behind the glass, and the files were paper again, and the connections she'd made were still there but had to be held now rather than simply seen. She sat in the dark and breathed through the descent — the walls returning, the boundaries re-establishing, the flatness of a world experienced through one register after being, briefly, experienced through all of them.

She checked her forearms. The bark on the right had advanced perhaps a quarter-inch past the mark she used for reference — the small scar from a rope-burn two years ago, which the bark had reached last month and was now past. She pressed her thumb to the leading edge. Dense, cool, the texture of old heartwood. Not painful. Just there, the way it was always there, one more thing the Bloom took in exchange for what it gave.

She put the files away and lay on her back and did not think about the exchange. She was out before first light.

---

Toward evening she came over a rise and saw two of the bloodline's agents on the road below, heading south at a pace that said destination, not search. They'd worked this section and found nothing. Whatever Betta had decided to tell them would have been less than she'd told Vane, or nothing at all. She waited on the rise until they were gone, then came down to the road.

She found a hay-barn at full dark — old, one wall open to the field, sound enough to keep the wind out. She ate from her pack and checked her maps by lamplight. Two days behind, probably less. The girl's gait had been uneven since the ditch — favoring one side on rough ground, not badly but consistently. It would cost her on anything uneven.

She lay on her back and looked at the dark ceiling.

She had a job. The job had its own momentum, one of the things she valued about jobs. Simple machines: input, process, output. You located the target and delivered the target and collected the fee and the job closed and you moved to the next. The mechanism was clean. She'd run it for ten years without serious trouble.

The trouble she was having now she named precisely: a working assumption had turned out wrong, and she hadn't yet replaced it with a better one. She'd assumed the girl would smell like someone in trouble. People in trouble smelled like trouble. The girl smelled like someone making decisions, and the decisions were, by the evidence, mostly correct.

She thought about the Noble Quarter address. The size of the fee. The instruction: *do not harm.* Which implied harm was a concern, which implied whoever waited at that address understood what they were asking for and had put in the caveat deliberately.

She thought about what waited at a Noble Quarter address for a girl with no change.

The Bloom-giver's ceiling rose briefly in her memory, stone and low and still. She pressed it back down.

She put her hand in her coat pocket and felt the wafer's small weight and took her hand away from it and lay still.

At her current pace she'd reach the road north of the girl's likely position by morning. The ankle she'd read in the ditch-scruff would slow the girl on anything rough. She'd already pulled ahead; she knew it by the quality of the traces, the way the evidence kept arriving fresher without closing on the source. She was ahead. She was ahead and had not decided what to do with that yet.

She was not sure in which direction the job's momentum was supposed to carry her.

She would figure it out in the morning.

---


## Chapter Nine — *[Maren]*
### The Hollow

---

The ankle announced itself two hours north of Betta's farmstead.

Not dramatically. A wrong step on a stone hidden under leaf-duff, a small lateral give that shouldn't have given, and then the specific hot ache of something that had been borderline for days and had now arrived at a decision. She stopped and stood on one foot and breathed through her nose and waited for the ache to report on its own severity.

Moderate. Not a break — she'd seen breaks, set two of them under Delia's instruction, and this didn't have that quality. A sprain, probably the same joint she'd rolled two nights back getting out a tavern window in the dark — three days of kitchen work for a bed, a good arrangement until the barman started asking questions she didn't like and she'd left by the nearest exit that wasn't the front door. She'd walked hard on it since. She could continue. She would continue more carefully.

She cut a length of her spare shirt-linen and wrapped the joint tight, which helped, and found a stick at the right height and density for a staff, which helped more. Then she did the mathematics of her situation, which she had been avoiding and could no longer reasonably avoid.

Food: half a portion of dried herbs that weren't particularly edible, one withered apple Betta had pressed on her at the door, and the turnip from Corvin's cart that she'd tried to pay for and he'd refused, pressing the coin back into her hand with the brief finality of someone who has already decided. Her hands had been doing things lately without waiting for the rest of her to catch up.

Money: not enough for an inn, probably enough for one more meal if she found somewhere to buy one.

The ankle: now a factor.

She looked at the road below her, the one she'd been paralleling from a safe distance, and did the final calculation: she needed to cover ground faster than she currently could off-road. That meant the road. That meant being visible. That meant trusting she looked like what she was — a road-worn girl with a walking stick and legitimate reasons to be going somewhere, which described a great many people on any road at any time.

She came down to the road.

---

She covered four hours before the ankle became something grim persistence and a walking stick could no longer manage. Not suddenly — it wound down, the hot ache climbing from the joint into the whole lower leg, the staff turning from supplement to support. She was listing. She caught herself listing and corrected and caught herself again ten minutes later.

The woodcutter's shelter appeared on the left as the light was going — old, low-roofed, the door off one hinge and wedged open with a stone. She went in. It smelled of old wood and mouse and the cold specific smell of a space that had not had a fire in months. A pile of split wood in the corner, dry despite the season because the roof, at least, was sound.

She sat on a crate and unwrapped the ankle and looked at it.

Swollen. More than she'd thought, because she'd been not-looking with some intention. The joint puffy, the bruising started — not dramatic but present, a yellowish spread at the edges that would be properly purple by tomorrow. She wrapped it again, tighter, and propped it on the crate's corner, which helped the throbbing and left her in an awkward position to sleep in.

She ate the turnip. She saved the apple.

She built a fire in the small hearth — which took some creative use of the broken hinge as a strike-surface and cost her two burned fingers before the tinder caught — and sat in its small circle of warmth and thought about what she was going to do.

Immediately: nothing. The ankle needed a day, better two. Moving on it tonight or in the morning was the kind of decision that turned a moderate injury into a real one, and a real one into a problem that ended badly. She'd seen it with farmhands in Ashwick, the ones who worked through the first hurt and were laid up a month for it. She was not going to make that mistake because she was in a hurry.

She was in a hurry.

She stayed.

---

The first day was manageable.
