She slept heavily, which she hadn't done in two weeks, and woke with the ankle complaining less than expected, and spent the day in reasonable occupation: tending the fire, making a poultice for the joint out of the herbs she couldn't eat, taking stock of the shelter, cleaning and sharpening her knife on a flat stone she found by the door.

She did not think about anything in particular. This was the skill again, the one she'd been working on for three years. She deployed it.

She ate the apple in the afternoon, in small sections, making it last. It was floury and slightly fermented, weeks past its best, and it was the best thing she'd tasted since Betta's soup.

She slept again before dark.

The second day was harder.

---

The food was gone. The ankle was better — walkable but not comfortably, not off-road, probably fine on even ground if she was sensible. The firewood was low. The shelter held the cold the way a space does when it has absorbed months of it and won't warm for a short stay. She was hungry in a persistent low-level way.

Not dire. She'd been hungry before. She could go three or four days before it became a physical problem rather than a discomfort, and she was at discomfort. The road was an hour off. There was almost certainly a waypoint inn within half a day. She had coin for a meal.

She knew all of this and none of it helped, because the hunger wasn't the problem. The hunger was just what moved into the space where her defenses usually lived. With the defenses down, other things moved in.

She sat by the dying fire and let them.

---

She thought about her mother.

Not the death — she'd thought about that enough; it had its shape, she knew its angles. The other parts. Her mother braiding her hair with the impersonal efficiency of someone completing a task, not unkind and not tender, just thorough. The smell of her: woodsmoke and the clean soap she made from ash and rendered fat and a handful of dried lavender from the kitchen garden. The way she'd said Maren's name when she was displeased — three syllables, clipped — and the way she'd said it when she wasn't, the same three syllables landing completely differently.

Her mother had known.

About the bandage, the pouch, the performance. She must have. She was not a stupid woman, and she had watched Maren closely in the years after her husband left, the close watching of someone monitoring a situation they have already assessed and decided they cannot change. She had never said anything. She had let the deception stand, hadn't helped with it and hadn't undermined it, and had died without telling Maren what she knew.

Maren had always resented it as a kind of abandonment. Sitting with it now, in a cold shelter with a bad ankle and no food, it felt different. Not a choice to abandon. A choice not to add weight to something already heavy. Her mother had looked at the situation and decided she couldn't make it easier and could certainly make it harder, and had elected to do neither. That was, in its way, a thing that took a certain amount of love.

She let it sit a while.

Then she thought about her father, which was where it got complicated.

She had the revision going now, the one that had started at the boundary stone. Not the man who left but the man who ran. Same act, different story. He had been what she was — the thing without the gift. He had managed it the way she'd managed it: the careful arm, the performance, the watching of a deacon's eyes for the wrong kind of attention. He had done it longer than she had. He had known what it meant when the attention came, and he had gone in the night without waking her — the choice of a man with very little time, using what he had to make sure she didn't know he was gone until it was too late to follow.

She thought: *he left me.*

She thought: *he ran so they'd follow him and not stay to look for me.*

Both true. She sat with both.

The fire went to embers. She had no wood left to build it back.

---

In the afternoon she did something she hadn't consciously decided to do: she took the disc from her pocket and sat with it in the gray light from the door and looked at it with the focused attention she'd been giving everything lately, the attention that had recently started producing actual information.

The markings she'd always taken for decoration. Three of them around the edge — and now that she was looking at them rather than past them, they weren't decorative in character. Too regular, too precisely placed, the lines too clean for ornament. The symbol on the main face resolved the same way up close: not a spiral. A set of nested arcs, four of them, struck from a single center — the innermost tight, the next two stepping outward at even intervals, the fourth riding wider and slightly off-true, as though whatever had drawn them had let the last one drift. Along each arc, a small raised point. A geometry, not a picture. Something that could be copied, carved, quoted. The central indentation she'd taken for damage was not a dent but a recess, shaped too deliberately for anything accidental. It was made to receive something. A key, maybe. A finger. Something specific.

She pressed her thumb into it.

Nothing happened.

She tried each finger in turn, which felt slightly ridiculous. Nothing happened to any of them.

She turned the disc over and looked at the smooth side. Nothing there. She tilted it toward the light and the metal did something she hadn't noticed before — a faint iridescence at certain angles, not random, a pattern that appeared and vanished as she moved it. Not decoration. Structure, something in the metal itself.

And as she held it tilted, a thin ring of color came up at the disc's outer edge — faint, bluish, gone when she laid it flat, back when she tilted it. Not a reflection. The color came from inside the metal, or from the edge of it, a glow too faint for full daylight but visible in the shelter's gray half-light. The vibration was there too — stronger than on the road, a low steady pulse she could feel in her fingertips, like something ticking over very slowly. Not mechanical. Organic, almost. The rhythm of a thing that was not inert and never had been.

She held it a long time and the color did not change and the pulse did not quicken and she could not tell whether it was answering her or simply doing what it did regardless, the way a clock ticks whether or not anyone is listening.

She didn't know what any of it meant. She knew how to identify plants and dress wounds and set a bone and build a fire and walk very long distances without complaining. She did not know how to read a metal disc that had been in her family for generations with no explanation but *don't lose this, don't show anyone.*

Her father had known how to fix small precise things. He'd had patience for mechanisms, for the particular coaxing-into-place that small broken things wanted. If this was a mechanism, he would have understood it better than she did.

He wasn't here.

She put the disc away. She turned over Corvin's whittled bird in her other hand — the coin refusal at the cart, the bird at the door, collapsed in her memory into one gesture, which seemed right. A small thing, wings tucked, smooth where his hands had worked the wood down to the essential shape. She'd been carrying it because it had a quality of intention to it that she found, obscurely, steadying.

She put it away too and sat with the cold and the hunger and the feeling she was starting to recognize as the specific weight of a question she didn't have the tools to answer. She'd been carrying it since childhood without knowing that's what it was.

The light shifted. Outside, the road was quiet — no horses, no voices, no sign that anyone was working this stretch of country. She couldn't tell if that was safety or just the absence of evidence.

She stood and tested the ankle. Better. Not good. Better.

She thought: *I have been calling myself hollow since I was fourteen, and hollow means empty, and I don't feel empty. I feel like a person who is cold and hungry and has a bad ankle and doesn't know what the disc is and can't answer the most important questions about her own life — and all of that is very full. Hollow is wrong.*

The old eastern songs — the Irongate kind, the ones the traveling singers brought through Ashwick every few years — had a different word. *Unblooded.* And the songs said the Unblooded could touch things the changed ones couldn't. The old things. The god's old bones and weapons, the songs said — though they said it casually, the way songs said everything, as if the fact were decoration and not the load-bearing wall it might actually be. She thought about the disc in her pocket: cold metal that had never burned her, that had sat in her father's hollow hands and his father's before that, patient and inert and waiting for the right fingers. The Recitation called her kind servants. The songs called them keepers of old things. The deacon had called her hollow, and the bloodline had asked to be told.

Two words. *Hollow* said empty — a gap where something should have been. *Unblooded* said it had never been there at all. The second was closer. She was beginning to suspect the choice of which word to use was not an accident.

Whoever had decided she was hollow was wrong, or lying, or both. And being wrong or lying about that meant she should be a great deal more skeptical about whatever else they'd decided about her.

She wanted to know who decided it, and why.

Not a new thought. She'd had it at the boundary stone. What was new was that it was the only thought left, now that the food was gone and the fire was out — a question that had been waiting for her to go quiet enough to hear it.

She put on her coat and her pack and took up the walking stick and went to the door.

The road was empty both ways. The afternoon light was pale and flat, making everything look equally far off. She stepped out and tested the ankle on even ground — manageable — and turned north, because north was still the only direction she had, and a question wanted movement toward an answer.

She walked.

The road was even and the ankle held and the light lasted longer than she'd expected, the sky doing the thing it did in open country, the sunset hanging in the west long after it had any business to. She watched it go from pale gold to a flat gray-orange to nothing, and kept her eyes on the road, and did not think about the disc or her father or the word hollow — just the road, and the next step, and the one after that.

The question was still there. It would be there in the morning.

---


## Chapter Ten — *[Maren / Vane]*
### The Wrong House

---

She almost walked past her.

The woman was off the road's edge, in the ditch-grass where the ground dropped slightly before leveling into a field, and in the gray morning light she was easy to mistake for something fallen from a cart — a bundle, a roll of canvas, something inert. Maren was two steps past before the something inert made a sound.

She stopped. She turned back.

The woman was perhaps mid-thirties, well-made gear, a pack that spoke to someone who traveled regularly and knew what to bring. She was on her side in the grass with her knees drawn up and her hands pressed flat to the ground, pressing the way you do when you need to know whether the ground is still doing what ground is supposed to do. Her eyes were open. She was breathing fast and shallow.

Maren looked at her from the road's edge. The woman had been moving faster — that much was plain from the gear and the posture of a body pushed hard. She'd been ahead. No one had passed Maren in the night, which meant this person had gotten out front some time ago and had simply been here when Maren arrived. She looked up and down the road, empty both ways, and back at the woman.

"What's wrong with you," she said. Not unkindly. Just the first question.

The woman didn't answer. Her hands pressed harder. Her jaw was tight and her eyes were tracking something Maren couldn't see — not the sky, not the field, something with no location in the physical world. Her bark-patched forearms were rigid, and the bark itself looked wrong, darker than it should have been, the edges raised and angry where they met skin, as if whatever lived in it had been pulling and the thing that usually held it in check had stopped holding. A fine tremor ran through her shoulders in waves — not shivering, something deeper, the body arguing with itself below the voluntary.

She smelled of copper and something faintly floral that wasn't quite any flower Maren knew. Under it: sweat, and the sour-flat note of a body that had been expecting something it hadn't received.

Maren came down off the road.

---

She'd seen this before, or something like it. Not Bloom — she hadn't known what Bloom was two weeks ago, wouldn't have known the name. But she'd seen the dissociated states the most extreme changes could produce, the ones where the mutation pushed into territory the mind struggled to stay present through. Delia had treated two people like this over the years, and what she'd done was specific and practical: cut the sensory noise, give the body something real to hold, wait.

She set her pack down. She sat in the grass beside the woman, close but not touching, and spoke in the low even tone Delia used — not soothing exactly, more a rope thrown into water. A voice at one end and something solid at the other.

"You're in a field," she said. "North road, about a half-day south of Stillwater. It's morning. The ground is solid."

The woman's breathing didn't slow.

"You're in a field," Maren said again. "The ground is solid. You can feel it."

A long moment. Then, by degrees, some of the rigidity left the woman's hands. Not all of it. Enough.

Maren reached into her pack and found Corvin's whittled bird — the small carved thing he'd pressed into her palm the same moment he'd refused her coin, wings tucked close, the knife marks still rough at the joints, the body warm from her pocket. She pressed it into the woman's hand.

The woman's fingers closed around it, slowly and then with purpose. She ran her thumb along the carved surface the way you test a thing for realness. Her breathing began to come down.

Maren kept talking — the field, the road, the morning, the solid ground — until the woman's eyes focused and came back to the world and found her.

---

They looked at each other.

Maren did what she'd learned to do when she didn't know what she was looking at: kept her face neutral and gathered information.

The woman was collecting herself with the methodical ease of someone who'd done it before and had a procedure. She pressed both palms to the ground, sat up, tested her own steadiness before she tried speaking. Her eyes were alert and a little too bright — the look of someone whose body was still running after the rest of her had stopped.

She looked at Maren a beat longer than the first look, and something crossed her face, quickly managed and then gone. Recognition, or something that lived in the same neighborhood.

The back of Maren's neck prickled.

"Thank you," the woman said. Level. You would not have known, from the voice, that she'd been on her hands and knees in ditch-grass thirty seconds ago. But her hands were still shaking — a fine persistent tremor controlled at the wrist and not at the fingers — and the bark on her forearms had not gone back to the color it had been. Whatever had been happening to her was not over. It had only become something she could do standing up.

"What happened?" Maren asked.

A pause. The briefest possible pause, which was itself an answer. "Episodes," the woman said. "I get them sometimes. They pass."

She was lying. Not about the episodes — those were real. About the *sometimes*, and the *pass*. Maren had watched Delia treat people whose bodies were fighting their own changes, and it had looked like this: a body negotiating with itself, the terms recently shifted and the settlement not yet reached. She noted it without comment.

The woman looked down at the bird in her hand. She turned it over once, closed her fingers around it again, held it, and her breathing came the rest of the way down.

She stood cleanly, without asking for help or signaling she needed it, picked up her pack, and brushed the grass from her coat with the brisk efficiency of someone closing a topic.

Maren stood too.

They were on the road's edge, two feet apart, the morning quiet around them, and the prickle at the back of Maren's neck had not gone away.

"You're going north," the woman said. Not a question.

"Yes."

"Stillwater?"

"Past it."

The woman nodded once. She had the quality, Maren thought, of someone very good at waiting — not patient exactly, more a mechanism coiled and still and able to stop being still at any moment it chose. She also smelled things. Maren couldn't have said how she knew it — something in the way the woman's attention moved, slightly ahead of where her eyes were pointed.

"You're the one they're looking for," the woman said.

Maren's hand moved toward her knife and stopped. She made it stop. The woman hadn't moved, hadn't reached for anything, stood with her hands visible and her posture without aggression. She was simply saying a thing that was true.

"Yes," Maren said, because denying it was wasted effort and she was tired of wasted effort.

"You could have walked past me," the woman said.

"I could have."

"Most people would have."

Maren said nothing. It was true and didn't require her.

"I've been following you for nine days," the woman said. "You're better at this than you should be."

"You're the one who ended up in a ditch."

The woman tipped her head, one small concession. "Yes," she said. "I am."

A cart appeared on the road to the south, unhurried, a farmer and a load of timber. They both watched it come and pass without moving or speaking. The farmer glanced at them — two women at the road's edge in the morning, nothing alarming — and went on.

When the cart had rounded the bend the woman said: "My name is Vane. I was contracted to find you and deliver you to a private address in the Noble Quarter."

Maren looked at her steadily. "Was."

"The contract is still open." A pause. "I'm telling you what I know."

"Why?"

Vane looked at her. "Because you sat in a ditch with a stranger and talked her back into herself, and most people wouldn't have, and I don't work well with wrong information about the people I'm dealing with." A pause. "And because the Noble Quarter is not where you go when the bloodline wants someone questioned."

The prickle at Maren's neck spread.

"Then where do you go," she said, though she thought she knew.

Vane said nothing. She didn't need to.

---

They stood in the road for a moment that lasted long enough to be a kind of conversation.

Maren thought about the three riders. The farmhouse door at night. Delia crying through a wall. Her father, gone before dawn, never coming back. She thought about the Noble Quarter address — private, a door that didn't open back out.

"How many are ahead of me?" she said.

"I counted three on the road south of here. There will be more in the city. The contract came through a handler who works for the bloodline's domestic office." Vane gave the information the way a person hands over something they've decided to hand over — fully, no performance. "They've been searching wide. They don't have a trail."

"You do."

"I did." Vane looked at her with that compound attention. "You don't smell like what I expected."

Maren blinked. "What did you expect?"

"Something frightened." A pause. "You're not."

"I'm frightened," Maren said. "I'm just also angry."

For a second Vane's face did something it plainly didn't do often — it almost smiled, and the almost changed her whole face, and then it was gone.

"I'm going to walk north," Vane said. "I need to resupply in Stillwater. I'm going to think about some things." She held out the whittled bird.

Maren looked at it. "Keep it," she said.

Vane looked at the small carved bird in her own hand a moment. She pocketed it without comment.

"If you're heading for New Plymouth," Vane said, "don't use the east gate. The guards change at midday and the midday shift has a specific brief." She said it in the tone of someone passing on operational information. Nothing warmer than that. "There's a river crossing two miles west of the east gate. No toll. The guards are interested in the gate traffic, not the ford."

She walked north without waiting for a response.

Maren stood in the road and watched her go.

She did not know what to make of it. She couldn't work out what Vane was doing, or why, and she was used to being able to work things out.

She thought about what Delia had said once, about the best healers she'd known: that they weren't warm, exactly, but they were present in a way that came to the same thing in the end.

She walked north.

She kept Vane in the edge of her vision until the road curved and took her out of it, and noticed she was doing it and kept doing it anyway, because peripheral vision was information and she was not going to stop gathering information now.

Ahead: Stillwater, and whatever was in it. New Plymouth past that, enormous and unknown, a city she'd never seen, the Noble Quarter with its private address and its door.

North was the safest direction. It was also, increasingly, the most complicated one.

She kept walking, and the morning opened around her, and the moons — all three still up, pale and fading in the growing light — were still there.

She could see them at the edge of her vision, and that was enough.

She had road to cover.


## Chapter Eleven — *[Vane]*
### Uncontracted

---

She walked north for an hour before she stopped walking.

Not because she'd reached anywhere. Because the decision she'd been treating as unmade was already made, and walking away from it was theater she was performing for herself.

She stood in the road.

She put her hand in her coat pocket and found the wafer and held it between her fingers without taking it out. Four left in the tin. At her rate — one every two and a half days, sometimes two — that was nine days, possibly ten. After that the episodes would come faster and stay longer and the bark would spread without the Bloom to hold it in check, and the spreading was not the problem. The problem was her senses when the Bloom receded — the world going from choir to silence, and the silence so complete it felt like a kind of deafness. She'd been through partial withdrawal once, two years back, when Cade's supply was interrupted for a week. She'd spent the last three days of it on the floor of her room with her hands flat to the boards, unable to identify the smell of her own skin. Not functional. Not a person who could walk a road or find a girl or make a decision more complex than whether to breathe.

Nine days. Possibly ten.

The girl — Maren; she had a name now, Vane had pulled it from one of Horst's updates two weeks ago and had been not-using it with some deliberateness, because names were a kind of weight — was a mile south. Walking north. Probably watching Vane's back until the road curved. Possibly already moving faster, taking the opportunity, because she was the kind of person who took opportunities.

Vane thought about the whittled bird in her coat pocket, next to the wafer.

Then she thought about the Noble Quarter address and the specific thing that happened at Noble Quarter addresses to people with no change. Then she thought about the Bloom-giver's ceiling, which she'd been thinking about at intervals for two weeks, filing each time, and which would not stay filed.

She had a contract. With Horst, who worked the bloodline's domestic office — the field arm, the one that ran contractors and sent updates by hand-disc glyph rather than courier, which was how she'd known within four hours that the target had moved — who wanted a girl delivered to a private address and had paid an advance she'd already spent on gear and provisions, which meant she owed the work or a refund she didn't have. Completing the contract meant returning to New Plymouth. Returning to New Plymouth meant Cade's back room and a full tin and the withdrawal clock reset to zero. Walking away meant nine days, and then the floor, and then whatever came after the floor.

She'd walked away from contracts before. Not for this reason. Not with a countdown in her coat pocket.

She turned around and walked south.

---

She found Maren where she expected to — moving north at a solid pace, not running, not dawdling, the walk of someone who'd made a practice of covering ground without wasting energy. Maren's pace didn't break at thirty yards; she'd already clocked Vane coming and adjusted her posture, alert but not frightened. The knife didn't come out. Another data point in the ongoing revision of Vane's working model.

She fell into step beside her.

They walked a moment without speaking. Maren smelled of road and dried herbs and something angular that was not fear but worked hard in the same neighborhood.

"I'm going to Stillwater," Vane said. "I have a supply contact there."

"All right," Maren said.

"It's faster together. And safer."

"I know."

"You don't trust me."

"No," Maren said. "But I trust that you turned around, and I'm going the same direction, and the road has three of the bloodline's people on it between here and Stillwater." She paused. "So."

It was, in its way, the most accurate possible read of their situation. Vane had no productive response to accuracy.

They walked.

---

She set the ankle at midday, when they stopped at a stream.

Maren had been managing it well — the right staff, the right weight, not favoring it more than necessary — but managing was not treatment. Vane had the supplies: a strip of proper support bandage, a small pot of drawing salve meant for her own bark-patches and equally good on an inflamed joint.

Maren sat on a rock with her boot off and watched Vane work with the expression of someone who hadn't expected this and was deciding what to do with not having expected it. She said nothing, which Vane appreciated. People who talked while you worked their injuries generally made the work harder.

"You know what you're doing," Maren said, when she was done.

"I've had to be functional in rough situations. You learn."

"Delia taught me to set a bone," Maren said. "I've treated worse than this. I should have done it better myself two days ago."

The first time she'd said the name. Vane kept it without comment. The herb woman, presumably — the smell of the kitchen on her, the knowledge in the grounding technique, the herb selection in her pack. Someone had trained her, and trained her well.

"She know you're running?" Vane asked.

Maren looked at the stream. "Yes."

"She tell them?"

A pause. "I don't know." Another. "I don't think so. I think she tried not to."

The distinction was exact, and honest. Vane gave her credit for not defending the woman with more certainty than she had.

She shared her food — dried meat, hard biscuit, a small block of dense seed-cake that was not enjoyable but was caloric. Maren ate without excessive gratitude, which Vane found correct. You ate because you were hungry. You said thank you once and meant it and moved on.

"Why are you doing this?" Maren asked.

Vane had expected the question and had no answer she was satisfied with. "I told you. I don't work well with incorrect information."

"That's a reason to release a contract. It's not a reason to walk back."

Also accurate. Vane looked at the stream a moment.

"The address in the Noble Quarter," she said. "I looked into who owns the building. It's held through three separate property arrangements that nominally belong to different people." A pause. "They all trace back to the bloodline's domestic office."

"You already knew that."

"Knowing it from the contract and knowing it from the property records are different kinds of knowing." A pause. "There are other buildings held the same way. I've tracked people to two of them. Those contracts closed. I collected the fee and moved on."

She stopped.

Maren was watching her — not with the wariness she'd worn all morning. Something more direct.

"What happened to those people?" Maren said.

Vane said nothing. She had an answer, and it lived in the same category as the Bloom-giver's ceiling: the things she'd been filing for years that had run out of room.

"All right," Maren said, in the tone of someone who already knew the answer and had asked to see what would be done with it.

They packed up and walked north.

---

An hour into the afternoon, walking a half-step behind Maren on a section of road too narrow for two abreast, Vane caught it.

Not something new. Something she'd been smelling since the ditch-grass and hadn't placed, because placing it required a comparison she hadn't been willing to make. Maren's trace — the ordinary human smell of her, road-sweat and dried herbs and the angular not-quite-fear — carried under it that quality of absence Vane had been tracking for days. No baseline warmth. No Chrysalis signature. Just a person moving through the world with nothing extra in her chemistry, the biological blank Vane's nose kept returning to because it matched no template she'd built in a decade of reading people.

The wafer in her coat pocket had the same absence.

She'd known it the way she knew a great many things about the supply chain — abstractly, in the register of logistics rather than consequence. She knew where Bloom came from. She knew the production required a specific kind of person. She'd heard the phrase in Cade's back room, spoken with the flat logistics of inventory: *if she survives the first month.* She'd heard it and filed it, and the filing had held for four years, because filing was what she did with things that would interfere with the work if she looked at them straight.

She was looking at one now. Walking a half-step ahead, favoring her left ankle, smelling of fennel and road dust and the particular clean absence Vane had been putting on her tongue every two and a half days for four years.

The wafer's faint floral note — the one that wasn't quite any flower. She understood now, with the slow horrible clarity of a connection arriving whole, what it was. Not floral. Residual. The ghost of a person's chemistry, stripped and processed and pressed thin, carried forward the way a scent clings to a room long after the person has gone.

She had been consuming this. She had been tracking a girl whose kind she had been consuming — and the nose that tracked her, the compound attention, the operational edge she'd used to break three contracts open, had been built wafer by wafer on exactly this.

The compartment did not hold. It opened the way a sealed room opens when the mortar fails — not dramatically, just a seam widening in the wall she'd built between the knowing and the feeling, and through it came everything she'd filed for four years, all at once. She stood very still on the road a moment and breathed through it, the way she breathed through everything.

Maren turned back. "Are you all right?"

"Fine," Vane said. Level. You would not have known, from the voice, that anything had happened — a skill she'd practiced longer than the Bloom. "The road's uneven through here. Watch the ruts."

She walked on. The wafer in her pocket had a weight it hadn't had before. Not heavier. Different.

She did not take it out. She did not throw it away.

She kept walking, and the afternoon was long, and the girl ahead of her smelled of nothing that should have been familiar, and was.

Near the second bend before the lake — the road narrowing between two low hedges that carried the sour grain of a recent cattle drive — Vane registered, four strides after passing him, that there'd been a man on the far side of the eastern hedge. Standing, not walking. She'd read the stillness as an animal and kept going on the reading, because animals were background and she was in a hurry. The correction came one breath late: the stillness had been a man, the posture watching rather than grazing, the sourness a hedge-tender who hadn't washed in three days. Not bloodline. Not anyone to be concerned about. But she'd read him as not-a-person, and read him that way because the Bloom on a fresh wafer would have handed her all three layers at once and told her which mattered, and without it she'd picked the wrong one first. She did not stop. She logged the miscalibration, the way she'd logged the six-minute lapse in the drainage line that week, and adjusted nothing in her gait.

---

Stillwater was a lake town, modest and well-organized — a place that had done the same things for a long time and built a particular efficiency around them. Fishing boats on the water. A processing district that smelled of it. A market, small but stocked for local need rather than trade. Vane's supply contact was a woman named Ori who ran a general-goods house near the dock and asked no questions in exchange for a modest premium on her prices, an arrangement Vane had always found reasonable.

While Vane resupplied, Maren went looking for her uncle.

She came back an hour later. Vane didn't need to ask. She could smell it from ten feet — the flat specific quality of someone who has just learned a thing they already feared, and found the fearing had not, in fact, prepared them.

"Two years ago," Maren said. "Fever."

"His wife?"

"Ulla. She's still at the house." She stopped. "She'd been visited. A month ago, someone came asking about me. She didn't tell them anything." A pause. "She said she didn't know anything to tell. She didn't know I'd be coming."

A month ago meant the bloodline had been building the net before the deacon ever went to Ashwick — before Maren ran, before anyone could know she'd run north. They'd been triangulating refuges in advance.

Vane kept the thought to herself. Maren was already working the same calculation from the look on her face and arriving at the same place, and saying it aloud would not help.

"She gave me this," Maren said. She held up a coat — heavier than her current one, properly weatherproofed, sized close enough to work. "And this."

The second thing was a letter. Small, folded twice, the paper gone soft with age and handling, the wax broken and re-pressed at some point by someone other than the sender.

"From my uncle," Maren said. "Ulla said he'd kept it for years — told her if Maren or Maren's father ever came looking, she should pass it on." She looked at it. "It's been opened."

"By Ulla?"

"She said not. She said it arrived that way."

Someone had read the letter before it reached the uncle, or after. The bloodline's agents, maybe, on the triangulation visit. Or someone further back.

"Have you read it?" Vane said.

"Not yet."

"Do you want to be alone?"

Maren looked at her with a slightly startled quality, as if the question wasn't what she'd expected from this direction. Then she shook her head.

She unfolded the letter. Vane watched her face rather than the letter and read it from there — the small deliberate movements of someone going through something dense, line by line, taking in more than she'd expected in a different shape than she'd expected.

When she looked up, her jaw had set and released, and her eyes went past Vane to the middle distance and came back.

"My father came through Stillwater five years ago," Maren said. "Heading east, traveling fast, didn't stop more than a day." She paused. "He said he was going to the hill. That there was something there he had to see before they caught him."

Vane was still.

"He told my uncle the disc would open it. Whatever it was." Maren folded the letter again, with some care. "He said it once and then he left, and my uncle never saw him again."

The hill. East of the main road, in the foothills before Irongate country. The name Cade had mentioned, that the dockside old-timers spoke of in the particular tone that meant a name was a warning rather than a description. She'd filed it then the way she filed everything outside an active contract. It belonged to one now.

She looked at Maren.

Maren was looking back with the expression of someone who'd just received, from an unexpected direction, a piece of something they hadn't known they were looking for, and hadn't yet decided what to do with.

She had the disc in her hand — taken from her pocket without Vane noticing, turning it over with the automatic gesture of someone who'd carried a thing for years and reached for it in moments of weight. The metal caught the afternoon light flatly. But at the edge, in the angle between her thumb and the light, the thin blue ring was there again, brighter than it had been in the shelter. Pulsing. She tilted the disc and the ring moved with it, consistent, not a reflection. The vibration in her fingers was steady and warm.

Vane looked at it. She thought about the Noble Quarter address, the property arrangements, the two contracts she'd closed and not looked behind. She thought about the tin in her pack and the wafer in it and the ceiling made of stone.

She thought about the boy who'd whittled the bird now in her coat pocket, and the girl who'd carried it without knowing why, and the woman she'd followed north for nine days who smelled like a person doing something correctly even when she didn't know what she was doing.

The bird was a practical thing. A reminder of Maren's steadiness in the ditch, the quick competence of her hands, the way she'd kept moving when moving was the only right answer. Not mystical. Evidence.

This was the moment where a professional made a clear assessment and acted on it.

She made one.

"The ford crossing I told you about," she said. "It comes out on the river road, two miles west of the New Plymouth east gate. From there the Temple District is reachable without the main checkpoints." A pause. "And I know someone near the docks who arranges rooms and papers for people who need them. No questions."

Maren looked at her steadily. "You're coming to New Plymouth."

"I need to resupply," Vane said. "And I need to understand some things about the contract I'm walking away from before I decide what to do about it."

"That's not the reason."

"No," Vane said. "It isn't." She put the salve back in her pack and shouldered it. "The reason is your father went east five years ago to see something, and whoever had his letter has known about it for five years, and they've been looking for *you* specifically — not just for someone uninfected, for you, with your name. Which means they know about the disc."

Maren went very still.

"I have a nose," Vane said. "And I'm very good at finding things. And I think you're going to need someone who's very good at finding things before this is over." She looked north, toward the road. "Also your ankle is going to be giving you trouble by nightfall, and someone should be there when it does."

She walked north and didn't wait to see if Maren would follow.

She heard her fall into step within thirty seconds.

She did not let it show on her face.

But she noted it, the way she noted everything — took it in with the scent of this afternoon, this town, this road, and the quality of the decision she'd just made for reasons she understood with her nose better than with any other part of herself.

It smelled, she thought, like a job in which the agreement and the actual work had stopped being the same thing.

She walked anyway.


## Chapter Twelve — *[Davan]*
### New Plymouth

---

He found work on the third day, later than he'd hoped and earlier than he'd feared.

The docks district ran on casual labor the way a mill ran on water — constantly, without much concern for where it came from, as long as it arrived. He spent two days learning the rhythms of it before he approached the right foreman at the right time, a technique his father had taught him: watch how a system works before you try to enter it, because every system has a current and swimming against it is wasted effort. The foreman's name was Jesset, a large weathered man whose change had given him an additional joint in each finger, making his grip extraordinary and his handshake memorable. He hired Davan for three days' unloading at a fair rate and told him where to be at dawn.

The work was straightforward and physical and occupied exactly the right amount of his mind — most of it, not all. The part that wasn't occupied watched the docks.

New Plymouth's waterfront was a city's worth of information compressed into a quarter-mile of dock and the streets behind it. He'd grown up in a town where he knew everyone's business and everyone knew his, the social geography legible in ten minutes. This was different in kind. In three days of unloading crates he took in: seven distinct neighborhoods visible from the dock, each with its own quality of foot traffic and its own smell; at least four languages, two he couldn't place; a shadow economy operating openly enough that he could see its outlines without reading its language; and a stratum of people who moved through the docks district without being of it — clean clothes, the purposeful walk of people passing through rather than arriving.

He kept all of it in the back of his mind, available for later.

Pip he met on the fourth day, when a crate slipped on a wet plank and Davan caught it and Pip caught Davan, because the crate's momentum would have taken him over the edge. They were briefly a chain of two people keeping each other upright on a wet dock, and then they weren't, and Pip said something profane and specific about wet dock planks, and Davan agreed, and they ate lunch together.

Pip was twenty-three, born in the docks district, with the expertise of someone who'd grown up in a place and paid attention. His change had come at sixteen as an ability to find things — not by magic, he was careful to say, not like a bloodhound, more a persistent accurate intuition about where a thing was likely to be given everything he knew about the situation. He'd lost three months' wages at cards before he understood it didn't extend to predicting people's choices, and made it back once he'd stopped trying and used it for what it was good for: finding things lost, misplaced, or hidden by people who didn't know him well enough to account for him.

He knew New Plymouth the way a good river pilot knew a stretch of water — not by map but by attention, by having been wrong enough times to learn where the current actually ran.

---

The flophouse was better than the stable had been — not high praise — but Davan was sleeping well and eating adequately and had a functional daily life by the end of the first week. Work at dawn when there was work. The market at midday for cheap food. The evenings his own.

He spent them carving and watching and thinking about the symbol.

He hadn't been able to stop thinking about it since the temple doorway at the Confluence — the inner orbit he'd seen and couldn't unsee. He'd been looking for it here, in the temples and shrines and official buildings, finding it on every lintel, reading it every time the way he'd read it that afternoon in the courtyard: not a spiral. The geometry of a circular path.

He carved it into wood. He dropped the pieces in the river. He carved it again.

"You do that a lot," Pip said one evening. They were sitting on the dock with their feet over the water, Pip eating something that had been sold to him as a meat pie and which he was revising his opinion of. "The carving."

"When I'm working something out."

"What are you working out?"

Davan looked at the symbol in the wood. "I don't know yet. That's why I'm carving it."

Pip looked at it. He had the quality Davan had noticed early — a visual attention that landed on specifics rather than impressions. "That's the Chrysalis mark."

"Part of it. The inner part."

"What about it?"

Davan considered how to say it, which was the usual difficulty. He'd been thinking it for weeks and it hadn't resolved into language. "It doesn't look like the rest of the symbol. It's older, scholars think. Absorbed from something that existed before the Chrysalis theology."

Pip looked some more. "Looks like something going around something."

"Yes," Davan said. "That's what I think too."

They sat with it a while. Below them the river moved with its usual indifference. Three moons were up, their combined light making silver of the water.

"There's a man," Pip said eventually, "who collects things. Edvar. Lives in the Crafting Quarter, sells maps, but that's not really what he does." He looked at the symbol again. "He has old things. Things that don't fit anywhere. Might be worth talking to." A pause. "He's particular about who he talks to. I'll put in a word."

---

A week passed before Pip said anything more about Edvar, and another three days before he mentioned, with studied casualness, that Edvar would see him Thursday afternoon if he wanted.

Davan understood from this that something had happened in the interval — that Pip had done some form of work on his behalf, beyond a casual word, and that Edvar had weighed the report and reached a provisional conclusion. He was being given an appointment, which was different from being welcomed, and the distinction felt important.

He went on Thursday.

---

Edvar's map shop was narrow and deep, scrolls in racks along both walls, the smell of old paper and the mineral scent of the inks used in serious cartographic work. The maps were extraordinary — Davan lingered on one, a coastal survey of the northern reaches that showed features he'd never seen documented, fjords and headlands drawn with the precision of someone who had been there and looked.

Edvar was sixty or so, lean in the way of someone for whom food had always been an afterthought, with a scholar's hands and a merchant's eyes — someone deeply interested in things who had learned to be careful about showing it. He came out from behind a worktable when Davan entered, took him in with a brief thorough attention, and said: "Pip speaks well of you."

"He's been generous," Davan said.

"He's accurate, which is different." Edvar looked at the carving Davan had set on the counter without quite deciding to. "He said you'd been working this form for weeks."

"I saw it differently in a temple doorway. I've been trying to work out what I saw."

Edvar picked up the carving. He turned it in his hands with the specific handling of someone not looking at an object so much as reading it — the pressure points, the grain direction, the way the form had arrived through the wood rather than been imposed on it. He set it down.

"The inner form," he said. "You've separated it from the Chrysalis surround."

"The surround felt like it had been added later. Like two things that had been joined."

Edvar looked at him with a closer, more deliberate attention than before. "Most people who notice that decide they've made an error and correct back to the standard reading."

"It didn't feel like an error."

"No," Edvar said. "It isn't one." He was quiet a moment, his hands still on the worktable, deciding. Then he went to a rack at the back and spent some time there before returning with a scroll, unrolling it on the table.

A map — old, the paper brown at the edges, the ink a faded reddish-brown rather than black. It showed a section of the eastern highlands in considerable detail, the kind that only came from having walked the ground and measured it. In the margin, in a small precise hand, a notation: the inner orbit symbol, isolated, with a number beside it.

"Four," Davan said.

"Four," Edvar agreed.

Davan looked at the map more carefully. He could feel the land taking shape in his mind the way warmth spreads from a fire — the northern coast where New Plymouth sat, a river system running south and branching toward the Confluence, the glint of the lake at Stillwater marked below. And east: foothills rolling toward Irongate country, the highlands rising in stages, the ground going rougher and less traveled the further you went. If the legends put something in those eastern foothills, it would be five or six days' travel from where he stood. He traced the distance with his eye and felt the scale of it settle into him, solid and real.

"What does it mean?" he asked, pulling his attention back to the margin. "The four."

Edvar rolled the map up with deliberate care and returned it to its rack. He came back and stood with his hands on the worktable and looked at Davan a moment.

"I don't know yet," he said. "I've been working on it for twenty years." A pause. "Come back when you've been here longer. There are things I show to people I know. You're someone Pip vouched for on a Thursday." He said it without unkindness. "That's a start, not an arrival."

Davan looked at the rack where the map had gone. Then at Edvar. "Fair," he said.

He thanked him and left. Outside, in the Crafting Quarter's compound of sawdust and metal and oil, the afternoon pressed in around him, matter-of-fact and indifferent.

He'd been assessed and found not-yet-sufficient, which was a different thing from being turned away. The map had been shown — just the margin, just the notation, nothing more — as a reciprocal gesture: *I'll show you I have something if you show me you understand why that matters.* A precise social transaction, and he respected it and found it clarifying. He was not in Amber Valley, where trust was ambient. He was somewhere that required trust to be built, specifically, over time, the way you built anything worth having.

He would come back.

He walked down toward the river.

---

He carved on the docks that evening, feet over the water, working the piece he'd carried since before the Confluence — the hardwood shape that had been resolving itself for weeks, an abstract curve with something intentional to it he hadn't yet named.

He'd been there longer than he meant to be. The light was going and his hands were cold and the detail he'd been working — an edge that wanted to be a curve and kept arriving as an angle — wasn't cooperating. He put the knife away. He put the wood in his pocket. He sat a moment looking at the river, gathering the momentum to do what he'd already decided.

He almost left. He was halfway to standing when his hand found a piece of driftwood on the dock beside him — soft, water-worn, the kind that took a blade with almost no resistance — and the grain said *one more*, and he sat back down and started the symbol again, the inner orbit, quick and clean in the soft wood. A two-minute carving. An afterthought.

He was finishing it when a footfall came down the dock — a different weight and rhythm from the fisherman, lighter, checking its footing. His knife slowed in the wood without his deciding it.

Not close — a piling over, maybe six feet. This was the quiet end of the dock, past the loading bays, past the noise — the kind of spot the dock workers didn't use because there was nothing to load and travelers didn't use because there was nothing to see. The people who came here came because they wanted the water without the waterfront, and in two weeks of evening carving he'd noticed the same handful of faces: a fisherman who drank alone, an old woman who fed the river birds, the occasional couple wanting privacy. People at the edge of things.

A girl stood at the dock's end — not sitting, standing, in the posture of someone who had come this far and was deciding whether to stay. Dark-haired, perhaps his age or a little younger, road-worn in the way he recognized from his own weeks of walking. Her eyes moved from water to dock to him and back, the quick triangulation of someone deciding whether a space was safe enough to occupy. She stood like that long enough to be a decision being made.

Then she sat. One piling over, feet above the water, eyes on the middle distance. She had a quality he'd learned to recognize in himself in mirrors: the alert watchfulness of someone who'd been moving through uncertain country and hadn't entirely stopped, even sitting still.

Something else, too. Not a wrongness exactly — more like hearing a familiar song with a note missing, or sitting near a fire that gave no heat. The warmth was expected and absent, and the absence was its own kind of presence. The feeling sharpened, and he looked away.

She wasn't looking at him. She'd fixed on the driftwood beside him.

It lay where he'd set it — he'd been carving the symbol again without noticing, the way it appeared in whatever he was working when his attention was elsewhere, the way certain words appeared in his speech when he was tired. The inner orbit, clean and precise, in a piece of soft driftwood off the dock.

She was looking at it the way you look at something you've seen before and weren't expecting to see again.

For a moment he thought she'd leave. She shifted her weight forward — the beginning of standing, the gathering motion of someone who has seen a thing she didn't expect and whose first instinct is to withdraw. He held still and let it happen, because anything he did right now would be wrong. She had to decide.

She settled back.

The feeling sharpened, and the skin-light came up. Not bright. A low blue glow along the insides of his wrists, faint enough that the dock's last light nearly hid it, warm under the cuffs of his coat before it reached the eye. It came the way breath comes — not decided, not performed, the body answering a question the mind had not yet let itself ask. He pressed his palms flat to his thighs without thinking, which did nothing. The light stayed. The girl six feet away didn't seem to notice, or didn't let him see that she had. His own body had known her before he did.

The weight of the pine carving in his coat pocket found him slowly — the small figure he'd made on the road north without deciding to, the one with the arm that wouldn't hang right. He hadn't looked at it closely since. He didn't need to. The girl sitting six feet away was holding her left arm the same way — close, guarded, tucked against the ribs with the specific care of someone who'd carried that posture so long it had stopped being a choice and become simply how she sat. His hands had carved that gesture weeks ago, working from something he hadn't understood, and now it sat beside him on a dock in the fading light. A strong reluctance came over him — not because the carving was frightening, but because looking at it would mean acknowledging a thing his hands had known before the rest of him was ready. He wasn't ready.

He waited.

A long time. Long enough that the light moved and the dock's shadows shifted and a barge passed in the main channel, and neither of them spoke or looked at each other. She was deciding something. He could feel the shape of it in the way her attention kept returning to the driftwood symbol and leaving it and returning — the rhythm of someone testing whether a risk was worth taking.

Then she reached into her coat and took out a small metal disc. Her hand hesitated — the briefest pause at the coat's edge, the last moment before a thing done couldn't be undone. She didn't offer it to him. She angled it toward the fading light so the edge caught, turning it just slightly, the gesture deliberate. From six feet away, in the last gray light, there was something on the edge — a marking, a form pressed or cut into the material — but the angle and the dusk gave him only the suggestion of it. Not the shape. The fact of it.

He held himself still.

Neither of them said anything.

Somewhere further down the dock, at the edge of his vision, a figure leaned against a mooring post — a woman, older, leaning in a way that said she'd chosen the spot deliberately and had no interest in being seen. He had the sense she'd been there longer than he'd realized. She wasn't looking at him. She was looking at the river.

He turned back to the girl with the disc.

"I'm Davan," he said.

A pause.

"Maren," she said.

They sat in the growing dark and said nothing for a while, and the river moved below them, and the first moon came up over the eastern ridge, and the woman down the dock remained precisely as still as she'd been.

He did not take the pine carving out of his pocket.


## Chapter Thirteen — *[Aldric]*
### What the Restricted Collection Restricts

---

Brennan's letter arrived on a Thursday, tucked between a supply invoice and a pastoral circular from the New Plymouth office. Aldric identified it by the handwriting — Brennan's cramped leftward script, unchanged since seminary — and set it aside until he had worked through his afternoon correspondence first, aware the whole time of its patient weight at the corner of the desk.

He opened it.

---

Aldric had sent his observation. Brennan had sent back a storm.

The letter was eight pages. The first four were what he'd asked for: the Fathers' Boats tradition, its variants, its geographical distribution, its age. What Brennan had found went considerably deeper than the two pages in the Confluence archive. The tradition was old — older than the standard Chrysalis theology by a margin suggesting it predated the religion that had absorbed it. In the oldest versions, the boats were not metaphorical. They were specific: four of them, arriving from elsewhere, three of which remained. The fourth was described variously as fallen, broken, gone into the ground.

Aldric set the page down and pressed his fingers to the bridge of his nose. He was hearing, with a clarity that felt almost physical, the voice of every child in every temple yard he'd ever walked through: *four fires in the sky, one came down to die, three stayed up to cry.* A skipping rhyme. Considered by the Temple to be harmless folk tradition. He had considered it harmless himself, for thirty years, the way you consider wallpaper harmless — it was simply there, too familiar to examine. The children had been singing the count the theology had quietly revised, and no one had noticed because no one listens to what children are singing.

And underneath the rhyme, the Recitation: *And the First Light came down from the Witnesses and touched the soil of Gaius.* Came down. He had spoken the line ten thousand times and had always read *came down* as blessing — light descending, the sacred gift. He read it now and the word *down* had a different gravity to it. Things that came down did not always come down on purpose. The liturgy had been telling him for thirty years. He had been repeating it without hearing it.

He picked the page back up. On what the fall had meant — what it had done to the world, or left in it — the oldest versions did not agree, and Brennan did not pretend to reconcile them. Some spoke of a blight, some of a blessing, some said only that the light had come down and the world was not the same after. *The tradition is certain that it fell,* Brennan had written. *It is not certain — or will not say — what fell with it.*

What fell with it.

Aldric pinched the desk lamp out of habit — still burning, had been burning for an hour, he pinched it anyway and sat in the dimmer light before he caught himself and relit it with a taper.

He read the next four pages.

---

Brennan had a source he hadn't named in prior correspondence, a contact in the New Plymouth archive who had, under circumstances Brennan declined to specify, seen portions of the Restricted Collection. Not the full collection. A fraction of it. But a specific fraction: the material relating to what Brennan called, in the guarded language of a man who knew his letters were read by people other than their intended recipient, *the old cartography*.

The old cartography, as Brennan described it, showed Gaius from an angle conventional Gaian cartography did not use. The angle from above — further above than any hill or tower could account for. The shape of the continent from outside the continent. In several versions, in the borders and margins, a notation recurring across multiple maps and multiple hands.

The notation used a symbol Brennan had reproduced in the margin of his letter. Aldric looked at it.

The inner orbit. The symbol from the temple doorway, isolated from the Chrysalis surround, the thing that wasn't quite a spiral.

Beside it, in each map, a number. Always one of three: three, four, or one.

Brennan's interpretation was that the notation was a count. Of something in a fixed position relative to the world. Something counted from the position the maps had been drawn from.

Three, four, or one.

*Three moons,* Aldric thought. *And the maps that count four are the old ones.* He did not finish the arithmetic. He had become practiced, these past weeks, at not finishing it.

He read on.

---

Brennan's contact had seen something else in the restricted materials, tucked into the cartographic discussion as though it were a footnote rather than what it clearly was: another piece of a pattern.

*Among the catalogued objects from what the archive's internal notation calls the Founding period,* Brennan wrote, *my source identified three distinct categories of material. The first corresponds to what you described finding — containers of the same unidentified gray material, sealed, apparently inert. The archive lists several of these, all recovered from the eastern highlands over the past two centuries. The second category is objects of navigation or display — instruments whose purpose is not entirely clear, but whose construction matches the containers in material and apparent age. Something in your description of the glass face in the sealed room suggests it may belong to this category, though I would want to examine it before saying so with confidence.*

*The third category is more difficult. The archive notation refers to it obliquely as "the Voice" or "instrument of authority." From what my source could gather, it is a weapon. It appears once in any confirmed historical account: the Stilling of the Brace, three hundred years ago, when the bloodline suppressed the Brace uprising and left a mark on the palace steps that has never been explained and that people with the Chrysalis change cannot look at directly. The archive materials suggest the weapon was not manufactured by the bloodline but recovered — a Founding-era artifact they had learned to operate.*

*The qualification attached to all three categories: the annotation "for unchanged hands" appears in the margin of the relevant catalog page, in a hand that is not the archivist's. It is the kind of note written when someone is recording not just what a thing is, but what it requires.*

*If the containers, the navigation objects, and the weapon were all built for and operated by people who carry no Chrysalis change, then the bloodline's claim to be the only such people is the foundation of their authority — and the weapon at the Stilling is the most visible proof they have ever offered of that authority's physical reality.*

*I think the container you found, the glass you found, and the weapon in the palace vaults may all be from the same original collection. I do not know what this means for anything. But it seems to me that you should know it.*

Aldric set the page down.

He was a careful man, and careful men did not leap from fragments to conclusions. But three categories of artifacts with the same annotation, in the same archive, pointing toward the same history — that was not a fragment. That was a shape.

He read the final page.

---

*I have to tell you something that will sound like the product of an old man's anxiety,* Brennan wrote, *but take it seriously. I have been asking about the Restricted Collection for six months. In the last two I have been aware that someone has been asking about me asking. Not a scholar, not a librarian. Someone with access to the kind of information about my movements that requires a well-connected patron or a formal arrangement with the bloodline's domestic office.*

*This territory has people in it who are watching for exactly this kind of looking.*

*I am also telling you that I think you should come to Greenford. Not by letter. Some of what I have cannot be written down. There is a man here, a surveyor who worked the eastern highlands forty years ago and has not discussed what he found there since, and I think he will talk to you in a way he won't talk to me, because you are a Keeper and I am merely inconvenient.*

*Come carefully. Tell no one you are coming, including in correspondence.*

*I remain, as always, your poorly-advised friend,*
*B.*

---

Aldric folded the letter and held it in his hands and thought about what it meant.

Thirty years of carefulness had served him — a major waypoint temple, a Keeper's rank, the respect of his peers and superiors — and had served the temple, and had served, he had always assumed, the god he had given the whole of his adult life to. He believed in process. He believed in working through the appropriate channels, even when the appropriate channels were slow, because the channels existed for reasons and the reasons generally remained valid even when individual situations made them inconvenient. He had thought of channels as *providence* once, reaching for the word and catching himself — *procedure*, he had corrected, *procedure* — and the fact that the correction had been necessary had stayed with him in the way small mis-steps did.

Sitting at his desk in the late afternoon with Brennan's letter in his hands, he understood that he was reasoning around it the way he'd been reasoning around the sealed room and the face and the symbol above the doorway. Making orbital loops. Not arriving.

He thought about Prioress Crane.

She had been at the temple for five weeks. She had finished her review of the eastern archive catalog and had moved to the financial records, a normal thing for a doctrinal review to do, requiring her to spend several afternoons in the records room two floors above the undercroft. During those afternoons, Aldric had found reasons to be present in the eastern corridor, the one with the stairs, without being obvious about it. He was not sure he'd been successful at the not-obvious part.

She had, three days ago, asked him a question about the history of the temple's eastern wing. A casual question, architecturally framed, the kind arising naturally from reviewing records of building work. He had answered it accurately and completely, providing the date of the eastern expansion and the name of the Keeper who had commissioned it and the surviving records of the construction. He had not mentioned the undercroft. She had not asked about the undercroft.

The question had not been about the undercroft.

He had spent three days wondering why she hadn't asked about the undercroft.

He took out his journal and found the page with the note he'd made weeks ago: *Restricted Collection, NP Archive, shelf 7-eastern. Access: Bloodline approval required.* He looked at it. He thought about what Brennan had written: *someone with access to the kind of information about my movements requiring either a very well-connected patron or a formal arrangement with the bloodline's domestic office.*

The church and the bloodline were separate. This was doctrine, history, the settled arrangement of the world. The church administered the spiritual life of Gaius; the bloodline administered its temporal governance; the two collaborated on matters of mutual concern and otherwise respected their respective territories. This was what Aldric had understood his entire career, and it was, he was slowly understanding, a description of a relationship that had been true once and had not been true for some time.

The Restricted Collection required bloodline approval for access.

The Prioress's review had been initiated by the New Plymouth office.

The face in the sealed room below him. The fourth shape around the blue-green world. A notation on old maps counting something from above, and the annotation in someone else's hand: *for unchanged hands.* A weapon used once at the Stilling of the Brace, leaving a mark on the palace steps that people with the Chrysalis cannot look at directly. The pieces arrived the way catechism used to — in an order he hadn't imposed and couldn't dismiss. He did not assemble them. He was no longer sure whether he was choosing not to, or simply could not yet see the figure they made.

He thought: I have been reasoning around this for two months because to arrive at the conclusion is to be in a situation with no institutional remedy. The institution is part of the situation.

The thought had the quiet of a chapel after the last bell. He let it stay. There was no liturgy for this — no rite, no rubric, no page in any book he had studied that would tell him what a Keeper did when the Keeper's work had been erected over wreckage.

Then Crane, who had not been a person to him for weeks — only an adversary navigating the same corridors, a function he could work against — arrived in his mind as what she was. A Prioress who had spent her career in service to the same principles he had served, who had risen through the same combination of competence and dedication and willingness to do unglamorous work that had brought him to his. She believed in the institution. He believed in the institution. They disagreed, if they disagreed at all, only about what the institution was for.

He could tell her.

The thought arrived with a clarity that surprised him, because it was not new — it had been sitting at the edge of his thinking for weeks, the obvious correct action he had been declining to take. He could walk to her rooms tonight. He could sit across from her with the tea she always had warming and say: *There is a sealed room beneath the east wing, and in it are objects that predate the temple, and one of them shows me things I cannot explain, and I have been lying to you about all of this, and I am asking for your help.*

She would listen. She would be thorough. She would file the appropriate reports through the appropriate channels, because she was a woman who believed in channels, and the reports would travel upward through the system the way prayers were supposed to travel — regional office, New Plymouth archive, the High Keeper's desk — and they would arrive, eventually, at the same place all such reports arrived: a decision made by people with the authority to decide, in consultation with the bloodline's historical commission, what to do with objects recovered from sealed rooms in old temples.

*The Restricted Collection, NP Archive, shelf 7-eastern. Access: Bloodline approval required.*

The question he had been carrying — the question Sella had pressed into his wrist with her translucent grip, the question the children had been singing in the temple yard — would be contained. Not answered. Contained.

He had served the institution for thirty years. He trusted it the way he trusted a well-built wall — completely, until you found the sealed room behind it.

He did not pinch the lamp. He held his thumb and forefinger still above his knee, pressed together, finding nothing, and he looked at them and uncurled them deliberately and laid his hand flat on the desk.

He took out a clean sheet and began to write a letter to his second, Brother Cael, explaining that he would be traveling to Greenford for a scholarly consultation and expected to be absent for two weeks, that Cael should manage the daily operations in his absence, that the Prioress should be afforded every courtesy and any assistance she required. He wrote it in the pleasant administrative tone of a Keeper planning a routine journey, because that was the correct tone and because Cael was a good man and a reliable administrator and had no part in any of this, and Aldric wanted to keep it that way.

He sealed the letter to Cael and walked it to the administrative block personally. The corridors were quiet at that hour — dinner service in the refectory, most of the brothers occupied. He was gone perhaps eight minutes.

When he came back the door to his rooms was ajar.

He stopped in the corridor. He had not left it ajar. He was the kind of man who did not leave doors ajar, and had not been the kind for thirty years, and the habit did not fail when he was distracted. He pushed it open.

Prioress Crane was standing at his window with her back to the door and her hands folded at her waist, the posture of someone who had been looking at the view and had decided, a moment before he arrived, to stop. His reading chair had been moved — not dramatically; a hand's width, so the angle of its back opened a clear sightline to the desk where it had previously not. The desk lamp had been turned on its base so its shade faced the door. His reading lamp sat two inches forward on the shelf from where it lived. None of this would have been visible to a casual eye. All of it was visible to his.

She turned when he came in. Her face did the small pleasant thing it always did.

"Keeper Voss," she said. "I was told you had been called to the administrative block and would return shortly. I thought I would wait. I hope you don't mind. The draft in the eastern corridor is worse than I remembered."

"Prioress." He did not walk further into the room. He stood in the doorway and let the distance between them remain what she had chosen it to be. "Is there something I can help you with?"

"Nothing that won't keep. I was curious whether the Confluence light from this window was as good as I remembered from my novitiate. It is." She smiled. "Safe travels tomorrow. We can speak when you return." She came toward the door and past him — close enough that he smelled the plain soap she used, close enough that she would have smelled whatever he was smelling of himself — and continued down the corridor at her unhurried pace.

He did not move until her footsteps had gone fully out of range.

Then he stood in his own room with the chair wrong and the lamps wrong and a silence in the furniture that said several things at once: that she had chosen to be seen, that what she had chosen to be seen doing was *waiting*, and that *waiting* was the word she had picked for the public record and was inviting him to accept.

He was not going to accept it, and she knew he was not going to accept it, and her face as she left had known he would know. She had shown him what she could do, and how she would describe having done it — and the description was the thing the institution was built to receive.

He sat and thought about whether there was anything in his rooms, in his desk, in the undercroft, that he needed to address before he left.

He went to his desk and opened the drawer where he kept the journal. It was there. It was where it always was — left side, spine toward the back, the pencil beside it as a bookmark at the current page. He looked at this for a moment.

The pencil was on the wrong side.

He kept the pencil to the left of the journal's spine, always, the habit of a left-leaning writer who reached for his tools in a specific order established over twenty years. It was on the right. He stood at the open drawer and tried to remember the last time he'd written in it — two nights ago, the entry about Greenford, the oblique shorthand. He had put the pencil back. He always put the pencil back in the same place, because the habit was below the level of decision and did not require his attention.

He picked up the journal and opened it to the current page. The entry was there. Nothing had been added. Nothing had been removed. He turned back several pages and found the oblique shorthand intact, the cipher undisturbed. He could not tell whether someone had read it. The shorthand was his own, and reading it required either knowing the cipher or being very patient with the kind of substitution patterns he'd used, and Crane was — he thought about Crane — very patient.

He could not be certain — not about the pencil alone. A pencil on the wrong side of a journal was not evidence. It was a pencil on the wrong side of a journal. He might have moved it himself without noticing. He might have been careless. But the chair had been moved, and the lamps had been moved, and a Prioress had chosen to be seen standing at his window without a stated reason, and the pencil was the fourth thing and not the first. She was accumulating rather than confronting, which was considerably worse.

He stood at the drawer for a long time.

The face could not be moved. The sealed room could be re-sealed, but Dolan knew it existed, and Crane was thorough, and a re-sealed room found by a thorough person was considerably more interesting than an unsealed room found before the finding. He thought about this and decided to leave it as it was. If she was going to find it, she would find it, and what she'd find was a room with artifacts she had no more context for than he'd had eight weeks ago.

The four on the shelf. He thought of them in the private numbering he'd developed without deciding to: the first, the second, the squared-off one, the one smaller than the others. Names he had not told anyone, because you did not give names to things you were pretending not to have found. He could file a discovery report tonight — the form was still in his desk, still blank below the third line — and the four would be catalogued and transported and assessed by people with the authority to assess. That was still the correct action. It had been the correct action eight weeks ago. It was the specific thing he might, if he had moved fast enough, still have been forgiven for not doing. Eight weeks was past the window where not-reporting could be called a delay. It was now a decision. He understood that clearly tonight — the way a man understands, standing in water, that he is no longer on the bank.

He thought about Brennan's letter. He could not keep it in his rooms — not now, not with a pencil on the wrong side and a question he could not answer. He could not destroy it, both for the information it contained and because he was not, yet, the kind of man who destroyed correspondence, and he was not entirely sure he wanted to become that man. He would carry it.

He opened the journal to the current page and wrote, in his oblique shorthand:

*Going east to find out what I don't know. The not-knowing has become a different kind of problem than not-knowing usually is. I leave the room below as it is. I leave the light to face what comes. I leave with less certainty than I arrived with, which is the condition I was taught to seek and turns out to be considerably less comfortable in practice than in theory.*

He paused. Then he wrote:

*The symbol above the east door describes an orbit. I have counted four bodies in it. I am going to talk to a man who walked the eastern highlands forty years ago and found something he has not spoken about since. I am a Keeper of this temple and I have served in honesty for thirty years, and I am aware that what I am about to do requires me to define honesty more broadly than I have previously found necessary.*

*I believe the definition can bear the expansion.*

*I hope I am right about this.*

He closed the journal.

---

He left at first light on a Saturday, the beginning of his nominal discretionary period — two days per month that were his own, by the ancient custom of his order, so rarely used in his career that Cael looked faintly surprised when Aldric mentioned them. He told Cael the Greenford consultation had been long planned and he'd simply not mentioned it. Cael accepted this with the professional incuriosity of a man who had learned that Keepers occasionally had business that was Keepers' business.
