He passed Prioress Crane in the eastern corridor on his way to the stable. She was walking toward the archive with a folder of documents under one arm, already in her working posture, the precise organized motion of a woman with a full day ahead of her.

"Keeper Voss," she said. Pleasantly.

"Prioress." He kept his pace even. "I'll be in Greenford until the middle of the month. Brother Cael has everything in hand."

"Of course." She looked at him with the expression she always wore, the one giving away nothing and suggesting, by its very completeness, that it was giving away nothing deliberately. "Safe travels."

"Thank you," he said.

He walked to the stable and readied his horse, old and amiable and with an excellent temperament for long roads, and he went east toward Greenford with Brennan's letter in his coat and the journal in his pack and the symbol from the doorway lintel in the back of his mind, orbiting something he couldn't see yet but was riding toward.

Behind him, at the end of the corridor, Prioress Crane watched him go.

He did not look back. He had always found it more useful to know which direction you were heading than what you were leaving.

The four in the chamber he had left sealed. He had not opened them because he had not known what opening would do, and he had not known whether the not-knowing was prudence or cowardice or a third thing he did not yet have a name for, and he had not been willing, on the eve of leaving, to decide.

The road east was cold and clear, and the moons were down, and the sky before him was the pale gray of early morning promising either a good traveling day or rain before noon, and couldn't yet be told which.

He rode into it.


## Chapter Fourteen — *[Maren]*
### Stillness as a Skill

---

Vane did not want her leaving the room.

She hadn't said it directly — she rarely said anything directly she could say through arrangement instead — but the shape of it was clear from the first morning: the silent inventory of the room's exits, the window assessment, the instructions about the sound to make in the alley before coming up. The room was defensible. The city was not. The former was preferable for as long as they could manage it.

Maren pushed back on the second morning.

"I need to know the city," she said. "I can't run from somewhere I don't know."

Vane looked at her a long moment. "Two hours. Morning, when the docks are busy. Direct route to the market and back. Nothing requiring someone to look at you twice."

"That's not knowing the city."

"It's a start." She said it with the finality that meant the conversation was over, and Maren recognized the finality and decided the second morning was not the moment to test it.

She tested it on the fourth morning, returning an hour late by a different route, and Vane was at the window when she came into the alley, and the look on her face didn't need words. They sat in the room a while without speaking, and then Vane said: "Tell me the route you took and everything you noticed," and Maren told her, in full, and by the end the look had shifted — still watchful, but with something behind it that wasn't quite satisfaction and wasn't quite relief and was probably both.

After that, two hours became three, and the direct route became Maren's own route, provided she could account for it afterward.

This was how Vane gave ground — not by conceding but by incorporating.

---

The room was tolerable. Small — one bed, one table, one lamp, the window onto the alley — but clean, and the landlady Tova had provided an extra blanket without being asked, which suggested some experience with people who needed rooms and discretion at once. The river smell came through the window at all hours. Maren stopped noticing it by the third day.

They slept in shifts the first two nights, until Vane had established to her own satisfaction that the room was not known to anyone who mattered. Then they slept normally — Vane in the way of someone trained to wake fast, Maren lying in the dark thinking until exhaustion made the decision for her.

She thought about the disc. She thought about what her uncle had told Ulla before the fever took him — the one thing her father had said the night he left: *Going to the hill. The disc will open it.* He'd said it once and kept moving. He'd known enough to go looking, known what the key was, known, presumably, what it meant that someone might be looking for him.

She thought about what Vane had said on the road: *they've been looking for you specifically, with your name, which means they know about the disc.* They knew, and had known long enough to anticipate her coming north. Her father had gone east with it five years ago and hadn't come back. She didn't know if that meant he'd failed or succeeded or been caught, and not knowing was the kind of gap she couldn't fill by thinking harder, so she stopped trying.

She had the disc in her hand without remembering reaching for it. She turned it over. *The disc will open it.* She'd been reading the sentence like the instruction to a lock — the lock a door in a hillside, the seal on a stone, a way in. But her father had been gone five years and nobody had found him, and a man who walked into a hill and didn't walk out was a man who was somewhere in that hill, or at its foot, or in the ground it sat on. She'd held the disc four years the way you hold a letter you haven't answered. She understood, sitting on the bed with the river smell coming in, that she'd also been holding it the way you hold a marker for a grave you weren't allowed to stand at. It was warm against her palm — warmer than her own hand — and she let herself read the warmth as her father's, preserved: a thing that would tell her where he'd fallen, that would let her stand in the place at last and say his name aloud. *The disc will open it.* Open the ground. Give back the body no one had returned. It was a reading, and she tested it, and it held — the first one that let her want to carry the disc instead of dread it. She went to sleep with it against her ribs. It felt like a hand she couldn't quite reach.

She thought about the young man at the docks instead.

She had not mentioned him to Vane. Not quite protectiveness, not quite caution — more like not wanting to give a thing away before she understood its value. He'd had a symbol she recognized, the same form as the edge of the disc, or close to it. He'd been carving it. She didn't know what it meant that someone else had found the same shape on his own. It could mean anything — he'd seen it in a temple doorway and it had stuck, the way shapes stuck, and the resemblance was coincidence or family. She wasn't sure. She wasn't sure of much lately, and the not-being-sure had stopped feeling like weakness and started feeling like the only honest position available.

---

The square at the top of Carter's Rise had a public well and a flower-market and, that morning, a crowd doing the thing crowds did when they had found something to be certain about together.

Maren had come up the Rise on her own route — accounted-for, Vane's word, a path she could walk back in full afterward — and she'd meant to cross the square and take the north stair down to the tannery streets. She did not cross it. The crowd had closed the square like a fist.

At the center of it a man stood with his back to the well. Forties, a porter by his apron, his cart abandoned at the edge with its load half-spilled. A woman was pointing at him and saying the word — *hollow* — saying it the way you said a thing you'd decided was true and found, in the deciding, a kind of relief. He owed her money, or he'd shorted her, or he'd looked at her wrong; the grievance underneath was too small to hear over the word laid on top of it. The word was bigger than the grievance. The word was always bigger.

Maren stopped at the edge of the crowd, because moving against it would have been a current going the wrong way, and a current going the wrong way is a thing people remember. She kept her left hand in her coat pocket, loose, unhurried, the way you keep a hand warm. She made her face into the face of someone mildly curious about a disturbance that did not concern her. Three years of practice answered the call without being asked.

A deacon came. Not robed for ceremony — a working deacon, a satchel, the bored competence of a man who did this often enough that it had stopped being holy. He did not ask the porter anything. He took the man's hand the way you take a horse's hoof, turned it to the failing light, and bent close over the skin.

The crowd leaned. Maren leaned with it, because not-leaning was its own signal.

What the deacon was looking for showed differently in everyone, and it showed in everyone. On the porter it was small and slow to find: a dullness in the skin at the wrist, a sheen that wasn't sweat, the faint standing sign of a body the Chrysalis had written even one line into. On another man it might be a flush of color, or a thread of light beneath the nails, or something with no name that only a deacon's eye had been drilled across. It was never the same twice. It was only, ever, there. The deacon found it without trouble, grunted, and let the hand go — and the certainty went out of the square all at once, like water from a cracked basin. The woman who had pointed was already not looking at anyone. The porter said nothing, because there was nothing to say that would un-hold the hand in front of his neighbors, and he went to his cart and began picking his spilled load out of the gutter, and the crowd remembered the errands it had.

Maren crossed the square. North stair, tannery streets, the route as planned, her pace exactly what it had been.

She catalogued it without deciding to. How fast it had assembled. How little it had needed — a debt, a word, a pointed finger. How the deacon had carried the whole apparatus in his own trained eye, casual as a man checking fruit for ripeness, because the city kept that knowing close to hand the way it kept its weights and measures: a routine confirming of what a person was. The porter's skin had given the deacon something, and he'd walked away shaken. Hers would give nothing. Bend a lamp to her wrist and there would be no dullness, no sheen, no sign at any depth — the clean blank no coal could counterfeit under an eye that knew what it was hunting. Once she'd carried charcoal and wool to smudge a palm dark for the length of a glance — enough for a deacon working down a Rite-day line, never enough for this. She'd shaken it into a stream her first day north, because charcoal on a hollow wasn't a disguise. It was a confession waiting to be found. She had survived three years not by passing the test but by never once being made to take it.

She did not run. Running was a current the wrong way. But she understood, going down the tannery stair, why Vane counted exits.

---

On the fifth morning, Maren went out for her circuit — market, then a swing through the side streets near the Temple District before heading back. The market was busy, the foot traffic thick enough that she moved through it easily, one face among dozens.

She was at a stall selling dried fish when she saw them.

Two men, working the market in the way that wasn't shopping. One at the far end of the row, the other closer. The spacing you used covering ground systematically — not looking for goods but for faces. Their clothes were unremarkable, which was the tell: too unremarkable, too deliberately nothing, the studied plainness of men who wanted to be invisible in a specific way. She'd watched Vane move through a crowd long enough to know what that particular not-being-there looked like from outside.

The closer one stopped at a vegetable stall. She heard him ask a question. The vendor shook her head. He asked again, and Maren caught the shape of it — *young woman, dark-haired, traveling alone* — and was already turning, already moving, slipping between two women with loaded baskets into the gap between stalls. A side street. Then another. She kept her pace even, her hands loose, and didn't look back until she'd put three corners between herself and the row.

Nothing behind her. She walked the long way back.

---

She came into the alley with the correct signal and up the stairs and stood in the middle of the room.

"Market row, eastern end," she said. "Two men, working a spread pattern. Dressed plain, too plain. The closer one was asking a vendor — dark-haired young woman, traveling alone. Asking north from the river end, moving east. The other held position at the far end. Both in gray-brown coats, belt knives not visible but the shoulder posture wrong for unarmed." She paused. "The vendor he asked didn't react like she knew anything. He moved on."

Vane watched her with the attentive stillness she wore when she was processing.

"Which direction were they moving?"

"North from the river end when I left. Into the market. If they're systematic they'll work east and then north by noon."

"How far from the Merchant Quarter?"

"Four streets, maybe five." Maren sat down. "He's deploying in a grid."

"Yes," Vane said. Quiet a moment. Then: "Good work. That's well-observed."

Maren said nothing. She was thinking about the timing — how many days before a grid like that swept this far.

---

Vane sat across the table and was quiet a longer moment, and Maren recognized the quality of it: thinking already done, now being sorted into the right order for presentation.

"I'm going to make a report to the dock constabulary," Vane said. "About the back room at Cade's supply house."

Maren looked at her. She knew what the back room was — Vane had mentioned it once, obliquely, explaining the city's Bloom-trade geography. "That's the Bloom supply operation."

"Yes."

"If you report it, the constabulary shuts it down."

"Or investigates. Same effect." Vane's voice was level, clinical. "Horst's agents can search a city where the constabulary is looking the other way. They can't search one where the constabulary is suddenly interested in the docks. An investigation into the Bloom trade draws official attention to every transaction, every movement, every face in the area. It makes quiet operations expensive."

Maren turned it over. The logic was sound. Horst's men in their too-plain coats, working a grid — they needed invisibility. Put the constabulary in the docks district and the invisibility was gone.

"Why does the constabulary care about a report from you?"

"Because I know what's in the back room," Vane said. "And who uses it. And that the Bloom trade and the bloodline are connected." A pause, shorter than the ones she usually allowed herself. "They use hollow people to produce it. I overheard it. If I make that connection visible to the constabulary, Horst has a contamination problem instead of a search."

The practical geometry was clear. What Maren was looking at was something else.

The bark on Vane's arms, beneath her sleeves — Maren had caught a glimpse that morning when Vane rolled them to wash, and the patches had spread since the road, the edges angrier, the dark grain climbing past the elbow on her right arm now. The supply tin in her pack that Maren had never seen her open but had seen her check — twice a day, the same brief precise motion, the way you check a wound you're watching for change. The episodes on the road had not stayed manageable. Maren had woken two nights ago to Vane sitting on the floor with her palms flat and her eyes unfocused, breathing in the counted pattern she used when the world was doing something she hadn't asked it to. It had lasted twenty minutes. When it passed she'd gone back to the table and the maps as if nothing had happened, and neither of them had spoken of it.

The Bloom kept those episodes short. Burning the supply meant no refill. It meant whatever was left in the tin — Maren didn't know how much, but from the frequency of the checking she suspected not much — was all Vane had between herself and the full withdrawal, and Maren had seen a preview of the full withdrawal in a roadside ditch, and it had not looked survivable as a sustained condition.

"That's your supply," Maren said.

Vane's expression didn't change. "Yes."

"You're burning your own supply to pull them off me."

"I'm creating a tactical diversion using an available resource." She said it with complete evenness, and said nothing else. But her hands on the table were not quite still — the fine tremor Maren had first seen in the ditch, present now at the fingertips when she wasn't gripping something. It came and went. It was coming more than it went.

Maren looked at those hands. She thought about the tremor and the bark and the tin Vane had checked and then stopped checking, and the copper-floral smell on the wafers, and what it meant that something with that smell was made from a person. She thought about the cost being laid on the table between them, factual and unarguable, and the way Vane was not going to let her say anything significant about it — and the way that refusal was itself a statement about what the cost meant to her.

"All right," Maren said.

Vane nodded once, already moving on. "We make the report tomorrow morning. After that we have two days before the constabulary presence is established enough to give us cover. We use those two days."

"For what?"

"Planning the route east." She pulled the maps across the table.

---

On the sixth day, Vane came back from her morning round earlier than usual.

Maren read it before she spoke — the quality of the entry, the way she set the pack down, the economy of her movements tightening when something needed managing. And something newer: a hesitation at the threshold, brief, almost invisible, the way someone pauses when they're not certain the room will stay where it is. Vane had made the report two days ago. Maren had not asked about the tin since. She didn't need to. The checking had gone from twice a day to not at all, which meant it was either empty or Vane had decided to stop looking at what was left.

"Horst is in the city," Vane said.

She sat and told Maren what she knew: the Merchant Quarter, a rented house, two men. Arrived two days ago by the river road. In the city himself, which meant the contract's timeline had become a concern at the level of someone who cared about timelines.

"He thinks I'm still running the search," Vane said. "He doesn't know I've walked away. As far as he's aware, I'm close."

"Are you? Close?"

Vane looked at her with the flat expression that meant the question had an obvious answer. "I'm in the same room as you."

Maren thought about the geometry: Horst in the Merchant Quarter, his men ranging, the bloodline's existing search converging on New Plymouth from the south. The room, defensible, four districts from the Merchant Quarter.

"How long before it stops being four districts?"

"Days. Less if he's systematic." Vane's eyes went to the maps, then back. "We need to move before he finds us. But we can't move until we know where we're moving toward." A pause. "The hill. Your father's letter says the disc opens it. We need more than that before we walk into open country with the bloodline behind us."

"How do we get more?"

Vane was quiet a moment — the quiet that meant she'd been thinking about this before the conversation and was deciding how to present it.

"There was someone at the dock the other night," she said. "When you were sitting at the water."

Maren went still.

"The young man. Carving." Vane said it evenly. "He had the symbol. The inner orbit — same form as the disc's edge. He'd been carving it, more than once, from what I saw. Not as decoration. The way someone works something out."

"You were watching."

"I was there." A pause. "I stayed further back than I might have. You seemed to be handling it."

She had not described what Maren had done with her hands at the dock's end. Maren noted that, and didn't ask.

Maren thought about the dock, the fading light, the symbol in soft driftwood. She thought about the way his attention had moved — slowly, gathering, the way someone listened who was used to things telling him things in their own time.

"He's not with the bloodline," she said. Not a question.

"No." Vane said it with the certainty of her nose behind it. "He's not from the city either. He came in from the south, same as you, probably within a week of when you arrived. He's been working the docks." She looked at Maren steadily. "He came to New Plymouth the same way you did — without a plan except north."

Maren sat with this.

"He had the symbol," she said. "He might know what it is. Or where it comes from."

"Or he might know nothing more than you do, and be carving it for the same reason you carry the disc — because it won't leave him alone."

Vane said nothing, which was its own answer.

The disc pulsed in her pocket — the low warm vibration it had been giving more often since they'd reached the city, as if proximity to something had sharpened whatever was happening inside it. She hadn't told Vane about the blue ring or the pulsing. Not because she was hiding it. Because she didn't know what to say about a piece of metal that seemed to be waking up, and wasn't sure *waking up* was right, and wasn't sure any description she had was.

She thought about her father's letter, the hill to the east. She thought about someone else in the same city who had found a similar shape — or the same shape; she couldn't be certain from one glimpse on a dock in fading light. She was building connections out of thin material and knew it. A symbol carved in driftwood could mean a dozen things with nothing to do with a disc passed down through three generations of hollow women.

But it could also mean one thing. And the one thing was worth finding out.

"I should talk to him," she said.

"Yes," Vane said. "But not yet."

"Why not yet?"

"Because Horst arrived yesterday and you haven't looked at how that changes the dock as a location." Evenly. "Three nights ago it was a reasonable place to sit. Today it's exposed, high-traffic, hard to exit quickly. We find a better place first. Then you talk to him."

Maren looked at her. "You've already found the better place."

Something in Vane's face gave slightly, the way a locked thing gives when the right pressure is applied. "There's a cooperage on the river road, two streets west of the dock. It has a yard. The yard has a gate on the far side opening onto a service alley. The alley runs north." A pause. "The man who owns it doesn't ask questions if you buy something first."

Maren thought about the operational precision of it — the pre-surveyed exit, the cover purchase, the route mapped. Then she thought about the young man at the dock, the slow gathering attention, the symbol in the driftwood.

"How do I find him?"

"He goes to the dock the same time most evenings. I'll tell you when the pattern's safe to use again." Vane met her eyes. "Two more days. Maybe three. Then you talk to him." A pause. "In the meantime — longer route tomorrow. Try the cartographer on the Inkwalk, ask about the eastern roads. Return by the river."

It was not how Maren would have done it. She'd have gone back to the dock tonight, found him, had the conversation. The urgency of having found a piece she didn't have felt like something that shouldn't wait.

She thought about Vane walking back toward her on the road south of Stillwater, the decision already made, the reasons given in the right order. She thought about the door in the night at Betta's farmstead, and the ditch-grass beside the road, and Horst now four districts away and narrowing.

"Two days," she said.

"Maybe three."

She turned to the window. The alley below was empty, the city going about its business in the middle distance, indifferent and enormous and full of things she didn't know yet.

Patience was a skill like any other. She was learning it late. Vane had been doing this ten years and was still alive, and the patience she was asking for was the same patience she'd shown on the road south of Stillwater, and that had been the right call.

She sat down at the table.

"Tell me what you know about the city's eastern roads," she said. "If we're going to the hill, I want to understand what's between here and there."

Vane looked at her a moment. Then she pulled the maps across the table.

The eastern route was rough. Vane traced it without sentiment: five or six days on foot through increasingly difficult country before you reached anything close to the hill country, the terrain rising into foothills that went genuinely wild before Irongate territory began. The eastern gate was watched, had been for weeks by her assessment. The ford two miles west of it was the way out — less trafficked, no permanent post.

They worked until the lamp needed oil, and then Vane refilled it and they worked some more, and outside the city moved through its evening and the river smell came through the window and the moons rose one by one over the eastern roofline, and neither of them mentioned what they were preparing for except in the practical language of distances and road conditions and the eastern gate and the ford two miles west of it.

It was enough. For now, it was enough.


## Chapter Fifteen — *[Davan]*
### The Cooperage Basement

---

He went back to Edvar the following Thursday, and the Thursday after that.

The second visit he brought a piece he'd been working — not to show off, not as payment, but because he'd learned from watching his father that the best way to establish yourself with a craftsman was to bring your own work and let it speak without commentary. Edvar looked at it a long time without speaking, turning it in his hands the way he'd turned Davan's first carving, then set it on the worktable and said: "You trained under someone."

"My father. He made furniture."

"The grain discipline is a furniture-maker's discipline." Edvar handed the piece back. "What do you know about pre-Chrysalis material culture?"

A test framed as a question. Davan told him what he knew: the standard temple account of the Founding, the Fathers' Boats tradition from the river communities, the way the inner orbit symbol predated the Chrysalis iconography by a margin scholars acknowledged without examining closely. He told it plainly, without elaboration, and stopped when he was done.

Edvar looked at him. "What do you think the symbol describes?"

"Something in orbit," Davan said. "Around something fixed. I think it's counting."

"Counting what?"

"I don't know yet."

Edvar nodded slowly, the nod of someone absorbing an answer that was right as far as it went. He showed Davan nothing else that visit. But when Davan left, he said: "Thursday after next. Come in the afternoon."

On the third Thursday, Edvar showed him two maps. Both old, both with the margin notation. Both with different numbers beside the symbol — one with three, one with four.

"Different dates?" Davan asked.

"Different sources," Edvar said. "Possibly different dates. I can't be certain." He looked at the maps. "The one with four is older. Significantly, I think. The one with three was made — I believe — after something changed."

"What changed?"

Edvar rolled the maps and returned them to their rack. "That," he said, "is the question I've spent twenty years on." He turned back to Davan. "I'm going to take you somewhere Thursday evening. After you come here."

He spread the eastern highlands map flat one more time before rolling it, and Davan looked at it with fresh attention — the distance from New Plymouth's northern coast to the foothills, the river system as a kind of measuring line, the contour lines thickening toward Irongate country. Five or six days' hard travel, he thought; he'd estimated it from Edvar's first map and found he'd been right. The hill, wherever it sat in those foothills, was five or six days from where he stood, and the path to it went rougher, remoter, the kind of country that asked something of you before it showed you anything. He filed it not as a fact but as a felt distance — something in the body already accounting for the road before the mind had.

---

The cooperage was two streets from the river, on a lane that smelled of oak shavings and vinegar and the sweetness of new wood soaking in water. A working building, honest about what it was, the sign above the door plain and businesslike. Davan had walked past it three times without paying it attention, which he suspected was the point.

Pip met him at the corner. He'd said nothing specific about what they were doing — only that Edvar had decided, and that Davan should buy a hoop-set at the front counter before going through to the back. Davan bought the hoop-set, which he had no use for, and the cooper's apprentice took his coin and nodded him through a door at the back without looking at him directly, its own kind of communication.

Down a set of stairs. A low-ceilinged room — stone floor, damp where the wall met the ground, the cooperage's vinegar-and-oak coming down from the shop above but mixing with something older underneath, the mineral cold of a space that had been below ground a long time and settled into its own climate. Two lamps on the table, not enough for the corners, so the room's edges dissolved into a dimness that made it feel smaller than it was. A table and seven chairs, five occupied. Pip behind him. Edvar already seated, which surprised him — he hadn't seen Edvar leave the shop.

He sat against the wall, his back to the cold stone, and felt the room's temperature settle through his coat. He was new here.

---

The woman who ran the meeting was perhaps fifty, with the aggressive precision of someone who had spent years being the most informed person in a room and long since stopped apologizing for it. Her name was Renner. She'd been a temple archivist for twenty years before leaving — or being asked to leave, a distinction she considered a matter of framing rather than fact — after spending too long in the eastern catalog asking the wrong questions about the Restricted Collection.

She opened by challenging the cartographer across the table before anyone had fully settled.

"You've revised your date on the Irongate inscription again," she said. Not a question.

The cartographer — young, sharp-featured, ink stains on his right hand there probably since apprenticeship — looked up with the expression of someone expecting it. "The stratigraphy supports a pre-Chrysalis date. I've said that for two years."

"The stratigraphy supports ambiguity. That's different from support."

"Ambiguity that falls within the pre-Chrysalis window."

"Ambiguity that *could* fall within—"

"Renner." This from the tanner, a square-built man named Corvel with the deliberate speech of someone who'd learned to measure words in public and hadn't fully released the habit in private. "We've had this argument."

"We've had it because it matters," Renner said, without heat. She had the quality of someone who'd long ago separated argument from animosity and found the separation useful. She leaned forward, both forearms on the table, and the nearer lamp caught the lines of her face in a way that made her look like someone who'd been having arguments in low-ceilinged rooms for a very long time and intended to continue. "If the inscription is pre-Chrysalis, the symbol predates the Founding. If it's contemporary, the symbol was only absorbed into Chrysalis iconography early. Those are different claims."

"They both say the symbol existed before the current theology made it mean what it means," the cartographer said. He had not leaned forward — one hand flat on the table, the other turning a stylus he wasn't using, the habit of someone who thought with objects in his hands.

"They say very different things about what the symbol was originally for."

Somewhere above them the cooperage floor creaked — a worker shifting weight, or the building settling, or both — and the sound came through the stone the way any noise reached a room under something else: muffled, filtered, slower than it should have been. Nobody looked up. They'd met here long enough that the building's sounds were furniture.

Davan sat against the wall and listened. Three minutes in he had the shape of it, or the beginning of the shape — an argument running a long time. On the surface, dates. Stratigraphy. The cartographer's timeline against Renner's. Underneath it, something he didn't have yet, something about what the symbol was for before it was found. He wasn't sure yet whether *astronomical* and *theological* were the right words for the two sides, or whether they were the words this room had settled on and he was borrowing them.

He kept his mouth shut and paid attention.

---

The woman who spoke next had not introduced herself. Younger than Renner by perhaps twenty years, with the particular watchfulness of someone who spent more time observing than speaking and had learned from both. She had a quality Davan associated with people who worked with their hands — a directness of gaze, an assessment that moved from the face to the hands and back. She'd come in after him and sat at the far end, saying nothing for the first quarter-hour while Renner and the cartographer worked through their argument.

When she spoke, it was to Corvel. "You brought the container."

"I always bring the container," Corvel said. He produced it from his coat and set it on the table with a care that was habitual rather than dramatic — the practiced handling of something he'd carried for years without fully understanding, which was its own form of respect.

Smooth, gray, the length of a forearm, sealed, a material nobody had ever identified. In the lamplight it had no sheen; it absorbed the light rather than reflecting it, which gave it the quality of a hole in the table's surface — present, solid, somehow refusing to participate in the room's illumination.

The cartographer took it first, turned it twice with professional attention, passed it to Renner. She held it the way she held everything — assessment rather than reverence, both hands, testing the weight. She ran her thumbnail along the seam and set it down and pushed it to Edvar, who touched it briefly, nodded, slid it on. When it reached Davan he held it and felt again what he'd felt at first sight of it across the table — not quite emotion, not quite sensation, something in the register between. A proximity. Something inside that was not inert in the way sealed objects usually were. His skin-light stirred faintly under his coat sleeves. He turned slightly toward the wall.

He passed it to his left, to the younger woman at the far end, who took it with both hands and did not pass it on.

"The seam," she said — a glassblower, Davan would learn later, which explained both the hand-calluses and the quality of her assessment. "It's not a joint. No adhesive, no weld. It's more like the material itself closes."

"We've established that," Renner said.

"I know. I'm saying it again because it's relevant to whether the closure is mechanical or material." She turned the container in her hands. "If it's mechanical, there's a key or a sequence. If it's material — if the substance itself makes the seal — then it might respond to something the material recognizes rather than something that manipulates it physically."

"Such as."

"I don't know. Something that belongs to whoever made it. Or whoever it was made for."

A small silence fell. Not the silence of agreement — more the silence of people who've heard something bump against a question they've been not-quite-asking.

"The bloodline," the cartographer said.

"Or the hollow," Renner said.

The word landed differently in this room than it did in the street. In the street it was a slur, or close to one — lowered voice, a condition to be pitied. Renner said it with the flat precision of a term she'd examined from every angle and decided to keep using because the alternatives were worse.

"The Restricted Collection footnotes," she said, in the tone of an argument made before, "refer consistently to a capacity belonging to unchanged hands. The ability to interact with —" she paused, choosing words with the precision of someone who knew how imprecision had cost them before "— objects of the Founding period. The bloodline has always claimed that capacity as theirs. Exclusively theirs. Their authority rests on being the only unchanged people.

"And if the phrase was ever literal — if *unchanged hands* means what the words say — then what these objects respond to is the trait. Not the bloodline. Not descent. Not any particular person. Anyone whose hands have not been written on by the Chrysalis should be able to hold one of these and have it respond." She tapped the table once, flat-palmed — a woodworker's gesture, testing for a hollow. "That is what the footnotes describe. A property of the material in conversation with a property of the body. The bloodline has spent three hundred years presenting a key as a personal attribute, and the correction, when it comes, will be that the key is the absence of the change, distributed wherever the absence happens to fall."

"Those are the same bloodline's footnotes," the glassblower said.

"Which is relevant but not disqualifying."

"It's extremely relevant. You're using their account of what they can do as evidence for what they can do."

"I'm using their account as evidence for what they *believed* they could do, which tells us something about the objects even if it tells us nothing reliable about the bloodline."

Renner set her hands flat on the table — deliberately, the gesture of someone about to lay something out — and looked at the container where it had come to rest in front of her. The nearer lamp guttered once in a draft from the stairwell and the shadows shifted and resettled, and in that brief rearrangement the container seemed to darken further, as though it had drawn the flickering into itself.

"Consider what authority actually rests on. Not the genealogy. Not the doctrine. The physical reality." Around the table the quality of attention changed — the cartographer's stylus went still, Corvel's hands came together on the table's edge. "The bloodline's authority has one moment of absolute demonstration: the Stilling of the Brace, three hundred years ago. A weapon used once, recovered from the Founding period, that left a mark on the palace steps people with the Chrysalis cannot look at directly. That event is the capstone of their claim. And that weapon, the container Corvel carries, the navigation instruments in the restricted archive — they may all be the same category of object. Made of the same material. Designed for the same hands."

"Unchanged hands," Corvel said quietly. He was looking at his own hands on the table as he said it.

"Yes. The bloodline's authority rests on being the only unchanged people. Whatever the Chrysalis is, they don't carry it — and so they can operate the objects, and no one else can." She looked at the glassblower. "That's the argument."

The glassblower — Asha — was quiet a moment. She still had the container in front of her, one hand resting on it, and she did not take the hand away. The lamp threw her shadow long against the stone.

Then: "The hollow."

"What about them?"

"They're also —" She stopped. She turned the container in her hands one more time — slowly, with the attention of someone who worked material for a living and understood what it meant to hold something that refused to behave like anything she'd handled. She set it in the center of the table, where both lamps caught it equally and it absorbed them equally. "If the qualification is absence of change. Not bloodline descent. Not genealogy. Just — unchanged hands."

She didn't finish the sentence. She didn't need to. The shape of it was in the room.

Renner's hands came off the table. "That's a very long reach from a very short piece of evidence."

"I'm not reaching. I'm following the logic you laid out."

"The logic I laid out was about the objects and the bloodline's claim to operate them. You're extending it to people we know almost nothing about." Renner's voice had not changed in volume or speed, but it had acquired the density of someone who has seen an idea go too far and is pulling it back by the reins. "We have no evidence — none — that a hollow person has ever interacted with a Founding-era object. We have the bloodline's claim, their demonstration at the Stilling, and three hundred years of them being the only unchanged people anyone thought to test."

"Because no one has tested the hollow," Asha said.

"Because no one has had reason to. And 'no one has disproved it' is not evidence. It's a gap." Renner looked around the table. "I am not going to sit in a basement and build a theory on a gap. That is exactly how the bloodline built theirs."

The silence after was not agreement. It was the silence of people who have heard something land and aren't sure yet whether it hit the ground or went through it.

Davan's skin-light pulsed once under his sleeves, amber, before he could press his forearms to his thighs and breathe it down. The light subsided. He didn't think anyone had seen; he checked the shadows on the stone beside him and found them still.

He looked at the container on the table. He thought about the proximity-sense he'd felt holding it — the warmth that wasn't heat, the reach of something inside toward something outside. He thought about the girl on the dock with her disc, and the eastern foothills, and the five or six days between here and whatever was there.

He kept his mouth shut. He was new here. But the gap Renner had named was sitting in the room like a green board left to season — the kind that holds its tension in the grain, that you can feel before you touch it, that will eventually move. He could feel it. It was warm.

---

Afterward, in the lane outside, Pip fell into step beside him.

"Well?" Pip said.

"The container," Davan said. "Corvel's had it how long?"

"Eight years. He brought it to the group eight years ago, when he found it."

"Has anything ever happened when someone touched it? Any response?"

Pip looked at him sideways. "Not that anyone's mentioned. Why?"

Davan thought about the proximity-sense, the not-quite-warmth. He wasn't sure how to describe it without describing his mutation, and he wasn't ready for that with people he'd spent three hours beside. "Just a thought," he said.

Pip accepted this with the equanimity of someone who'd learned to take half-answers from people who'd eventually give him the rest. "What did you make of the argument? Renner and Asha."

"The glassblower's Asha?"

"Yes."

"I think Renner's right that we can't build on a gap." Davan turned it over. "And I think Asha's right that the gap is there." He paused. His skin-light stirred under his coat — amber, edged with something bluer — and he pressed his wrist against his hip until it went quiet. "The thing I keep coming back to is the number changing. Four to three. Something happened. Whoever made the old map knew it had, and recorded it in the margin as a count."

"That's Renner's position," Pip said. "The number's astronomical. Something changed in the sky."

"And the cartographer thinks it's theological? A count of something in the Chrysalis framework?"

"He thinks it could be either, and that treating it as definitely astronomical is overclaiming."

"He's wrong about that." Davan said it without thinking, then considered whether he believed it and found that he did. "It's astronomical. I've been watching the moons for months. The symbol is the same geometry as the path of the moons. It's not a coincidence."

Pip was quiet a moment. "Renner will want to hear that. She's been arguing it four years off the maps. She'd appreciate someone arriving at it independently." A pause. "Though she'll also want to know why you're certain, and 'I've been watching the moons' is not going to satisfy her."

"I know." Davan looked at the lane, the lamplight, the cooperage's shadow behind them. "I have something else. But I need to think about whether I'm ready to bring it."

Pip nodded slowly. He knew what to press and what to leave, and he left it.

"There's something else," Pip said after a moment. He stopped walking. "Edvar told me before you came tonight. Someone came to his shop last week. A woman. Alone, direct, not from the city. She asked about old maps of the eastern highlands."

Davan went still.

"He didn't show her anything. She wasn't someone he knew." Pip met his eyes. "He described her. Dark hair. Road-worn. Young." A pause. "She had something in her coat pocket she kept touching. He couldn't see what."

"He said she knew what she was asking," Pip went on. "Not browsing. Already had a piece and was looking for the next one." Another pause. "She bought a map of the eastern roads north of the river fork. Paid with a Stillwater coin."

Davan said nothing.

"I think she's connected to whatever you're connected to."

Davan thought about the dock. The fading light. The disc angled to catch it, and on the edge of it something — a marking, a form he couldn't resolve from six feet away in that light, but the gesture clear: she had shown him something, and the showing had been the gesture of the driftwood — she had known the shape the moment she saw it. The way she'd held her arm — close, guarded, exactly the posture his hands had carved into the pine figure weeks before he met her. The carving in his coat pocket he'd been circling for weeks, the one with the arm that wouldn't hang right, that he still hadn't fully looked at since the dock — because looking at it meant reckoning with the fact that his hands had known something about her before the rest of him was ready.

He was done waiting to be ready.

"I don't want her address," he said.

Pip looked at him. "I wasn't going to offer it."

"Good." He thought a moment. "She went to Edvar without anyone setting it up. She's running her own search. If I show up at her door, I'm everything she's been avoiding." He turned the carving over in his coat pocket, feeling the shape of it through the cloth. "Set something up through the cooperage. A time, a place, a gate she can leave through if she wants. And send her something she'll recognize."

"What?"

He took the pine carving out and looked at it in the lamplight a long moment. The small figure, head tilted as if listening, one arm held close to the body in the guarded posture his hands had insisted on and he'd never been able to correct. He'd tried to fix the arm three times on the road north and it had come back each time, and now he knew why — because it was right, because somewhere ahead of him on the road a girl had been holding her arm exactly like this for three years, and his hands had found the shape of that gesture before the rest of him had any reason to know it existed.

He had no explanation for this. He was done looking for one.

He held it out to Pip.

"She has the disc," he said. "If she looks at this she'll either understand or she won't. Either way we'll know something useful."

Pip took it and looked at it and looked at Davan and looked at it again. He didn't ask.

"Thursday," Pip said.

"Thursday," Davan agreed.

He walked back toward the flophouse with his hands in his empty pockets, and the emptiness of the one pocket felt right — not a loss but a release, a breath let out after holding it. He thought about the argument he'd sat through, the positions staked out over years, the number changing from four to three — and the gap where the fourth had been, the thing nobody in the room had said out loud but everyone had been thinking around — and the thing in Corvel's container reaching toward him when he held it, and the gap Renner had refused to fill and Asha had refused to leave alone.

He didn't know yet. He was beginning to believe that not knowing yet was a condition, not a conclusion. You moved through it. The rest came.


## Chapter Sixteen — *[Maren]*
### Thursday

---

The carving arrived in a cloth bag with no note.

Pip's runner — a boy of perhaps twelve, quick-footed and professionally incurious — brought it to the alley entrance in the early afternoon and gave the signal Vane had established the week before: two knocks, pause, one knock. Maren came down. The boy handed her the bag and was gone before she'd untied the cord.

She brought it upstairs and set it on the table and opened the cloth.

Small. Pine. A figure — a person, not an animal, not a symbol. Head tilted slightly, as if listening to something just past the edge of hearing. The carving had the quality of something arrived at rather than imposed — the knife marks confident but not forced, following the wood's grain rather than fighting it. The kind of work her mother would have called *honest*, meaning it didn't pretend to be more than what it was.

One arm hung naturally. The other was held close to the body. Tucked in, guarded, the elbow drawn toward the ribs in a posture so specific that Maren's hand went to her own arm before she understood why.

She held the carving in both hands and did not say anything for a long time.

She knew the posture. She'd been holding her arm like that for three years — the arm with no coal-dark palm, the arm she'd learned to keep close and angled away, the arm that could end her if a stranger saw the wrong thing at the wrong moment. She'd stopped thinking of it as a conscious choice somewhere in the second year. It had become architecture. The way she stood, the way she sat, the way she moved through rooms — all of it organized around that one arm's proximity to her body.

Someone had carved it. Someone had carved it into pine before meeting her, because the wood had the faded amber of a piece worked weeks ago, not days, and the surface had been handled enough to develop the slight sheen that came from being carried in a pocket and turned over by restless fingers. He'd carved this on the road, before the dock, before the symbol in the driftwood, before any of it.

His hands had known her posture before his eyes had seen it.

She sat with this. She turned the figure slowly, feeling the grain under her thumbs, the slight roughness where the knife had hesitated at the junction of the tucked arm and the body — as if even the blade had paused at that particular gesture and considered what it meant.

The disc pulsed once in her coat pocket. The low warm vibration she'd been feeling with increasing frequency, the metal doing something it had no business doing, responding to proximity or alignment or whatever physics governed ten-thousand-year-old mechanisms that were supposed to be inert. She pressed her hand against the pocket and the pulsing settled.

She looked at the carving again.

She thought: *he knows what I am.* Not the details — not the disc, not the hill, not the letter from a dead uncle about a father who'd walked east and hadn't come back. But the shape of it. The condition of being something you couldn't show. He'd found it in the wood before he'd found it in her, and he'd sent it to her without explanation because the carving was the explanation.

She set it on the table next to the disc.

Two objects. One she'd been given by a father who'd left. One she'd been given by a stranger who'd stayed in the same city. Both asked her to go somewhere.

---

Vane came back twenty minutes later. Her eyes found the carving on the table before she'd fully crossed the threshold — entry, objects, exits in one sweep, and then the carving held her attention with the particular stillness she wore when something had earned it.

"Who sent it?" she said.

"The young man from the dock."

Vane picked it up without asking. She held it with both hands, testing the weight, brought it close to her face. Smelling it. Reading the carving the way she read everything else: through chemistry.

"Pine. Old cut — four weeks, maybe five. He's been handling it constantly." She turned it again. "The knife work is trained. Not professional, but someone taught him." She set it down. "He sent this as an introduction."

"Yes."

"Bold." Vane's eyes were on the carving but her attention was somewhere past it, in the place where she calculated risk. "He's telling you he's been thinking about you. That's either honest or it's a very good opening move."

Maren looked at the carving on the table, the tucked arm, and said nothing. Vane hadn't mentioned the arm. She'd read the wood and the scent and the sender's intent, and she'd missed the thing that mattered most — or rather, she'd never had reason to notice it, because Vane had never watched Maren the way a carver watches a subject. She knew the smell of Maren's fear and the chemical signature of her alertness. She did not know the specific geometry of how Maren held her body when she was protecting the arm that could get her killed.

This was not a thing Maren was going to explain. Not yet. The carving sat on the table between them and it held a recognition that belonged to Maren and to the person who'd made it, and sharing it with Vane would turn it into information, and information was what Vane did with everything, and some things should be allowed to stay what they were for a while before they became useful.

"There's a message in the bag," Vane said. She hadn't looked in the bag. She'd smelled it — a faint chemical trace, ink on cloth, the particular sharpness of dock-district lamp oil. "Inside the cord."

Maren untwisted the cord. A strip of paper, folded once. Two words and a time: *Thursday. Afternoon.*

"The cooperage," Maren said.

"Probably." Vane sat down. "I'll walk the building tomorrow morning. The yard, the surrounding streets, whatever exits there are. I need to see it before you go in."

"All right."

"Not *all right* like you're humoring me. All right like you understand that I can't keep you safe in a place I haven't seen."

Maren looked at her. "All right," she said again, differently.

---

Vane came back from the cooperage the next morning with dirt on her boots and the particular expression she wore when a location had met her standards, which was to say: no expression at all, but an absence of the tension that meant *bad.*

"The yard is workable," she said. She sat down and drew on the back of the eastern road map with a stub of charcoal — quick lines, the spatial shorthand of someone who thought in exits and sight lines. "Bounded on three sides by the building and storage sheds. The fourth side is a wooden fence — slat construction, good visibility through. There's a gate on the far side. The gate opens onto a service alley running north to the river road. Three minutes at a walk, less if you run. Low wall at the midpoint, climbable."

Maren looked at the sketch. "You want to be in the alley."

"At the gate. I can see through the slats without being in the yard. If something goes wrong you come to me, not the other way around." She paused. "I want a signal. Something you can do without him noticing."

"Such as."

"You'll have the carving. Set it on the table at the start. If you pick it up and put it in your pocket, I come in."

Maren nodded. The operational architecture of this was familiar now — familiar as the room's dimensions and the alley's geography and the specific cadence of Vane's morning rounds. She'd spent eight days inside the structure of Vane's caution and had learned to read it the way you read weather: not as obstruction but as information about the conditions you were operating in.

"He's not dangerous," Vane said. "His smell is curiosity and something I can't place — a warmth that doesn't match anything in my reference set. Not threat. Not deception." She paused. "But I don't know him, and I don't know who knows him, and someone sending you a portrait of yourself made before you met is not a thing I can file and move on from."

Maren set her jaw and catalogued the argument the way she catalogued exits — noted, filed, not available for debate. "I'm going," she said.

"I know you're going. I knew you were going before you opened the bag." Vane looked at the carving on the table. "That's not the question. The question is whether you go prepared."

"I'm always prepared."

"You carry a knife on your left hip and you've never once adjusted for a draw." Vane said it without emphasis. A fact, laid alongside the other facts, but this one landed differently. "You carry it the way someone carries a tool. Not the way someone carries something they might need in the next three seconds."

Maren said nothing. The knife was her mother's — a good blade, well-balanced, the handle worn smooth by years of kitchen work before it became the thing Maren put on her belt when she left Ashwick. She'd thought of it as a knife. Not as a weapon. The distinction, apparently, was visible to someone who'd spent a decade making the distinction matter.

Vane stood up. "Show me where it sits."

Maren stood. She touched the knife at her hip — the familiar position, riding just behind the bone.

"Too far back," Vane said. "You'd have to reach behind yourself. That's a half-second you don't have if someone's in front of you." She came around the table and stood beside Maren, not touching, and pointed to a spot two inches forward on the belt. "Here. Just ahead of the hip bone. Your hand falls to it without your elbow having to move."

Maren shifted the knife. The new position felt wrong — too prominent, too accessible, too much like a statement about what she expected from the world.

"It feels exposed," she said.

"Good. That means you know it's there." Vane stepped back. "Draw it."

Maren drew. She drew the way she'd always drawn — reaching, gripping, pulling — and by the time the blade cleared the sheath Vane was looking at her with the flat assessment of someone watching a thing done incorrectly.

"Your thumb goes to the pommel first," Vane said. "Contact before you grip. You're finding it, then drawing it. Two motions instead of one." She took the knife from Maren's hand, replaced it in the sheath, and drew it in a single motion so fast Maren registered the blade's position before she registered the movement that put it there. "One motion. The hand knows where it is because you've already told it where it is. You set it in the right place on your belt so your hand doesn't have to search."

She handed the knife back.

"Again."

Maren drew. Better — faster — but her wrist turned at the wrong angle and the blade came up pointing inward.

"You're pulling it to yourself. Pull it away. The edge goes out. Your body is behind the blade, not in front of it."

Again. And again. Ten minutes of the same motion, the same correction, Vane adjusting Maren's grip twice and her elbow once, each adjustment delivered with the economy of someone who had been taught this way and saw no reason to teach it differently. No warmth in the instruction, no encouragement. Just the information and the expectation that the information would be used.

Maren's hand began to learn the new position. By the eighth draw, the motion was cleaner — not fast, not graceful, but correct, the blade arriving where it should in the orientation it should, her body behind it.

"Better," Vane said. It was the first word of evaluation she'd offered, and it arrived without inflection, a measurement rather than a compliment.

"I want you to practice that every morning," she said. She sat back down at the table and her hands went flat — the thinking-already-done gesture. "Twenty draws. When you can do it without looking at your hand, we'll work on the next thing."

She did not say what the next thing was. Maren suspected this was deliberate.

"Take the disc on Thursday," Vane said, returning to the cooperage as if the knife lesson had been a parenthetical. "If he knows the symbol, show him. If he doesn't, you've lost nothing. But don't show him the letter."

"Why not the letter?"

"Because the letter is the destination. The disc is the question. Give him the question first and see what he does with it." She tapped the charcoal sketch of the cooperage yard. "Anyone can follow a destination. You want to know if he's asking the same question."

---

The cooperage yard was smaller than Maren had expected — a working space, not a public one, bounded on three sides by the cooperage building and its storage sheds and on the fourth by the slatted fence Vane had drawn. Stacked barrel hoops leaned against the near wall. The ground was packed earth, darkened in patches where water had been used and dried unevenly. The smell was oak and vinegar and the faint sweetness of new wood soaking, and underneath it the river, two streets away, doing what rivers did.

She'd come through the front with a purchased hoop-set she had no use for, and the apprentice had nodded her through to the yard with practiced non-attention. She thought about the infrastructure of discretion, the way the cooperage functioned as a kind of lock, the purchase as the key, the apprentice as the mechanism that turned without caring what it let through.

The yard was empty. She was early.

She sat on an upturned barrel near the center and set the hoop-set beside her and waited. The afternoon was cool, the light coming in at the low angle that meant the season was turning toward the shorter days. She could hear the cooperage workers inside — the rhythmic knock of mallets on hoops, the creak of staves being bent, the ordinary sounds of people making things. Above the roofline, the sky was the gray-blue of a city afternoon, the color of air that had been breathed by too many people and was making the best of it.

She checked the gate. Closed but not latched. The service alley beyond it was visible through the slats — narrow, running north, the low wall at the midpoint exactly where Vane had sketched it.

She'd watched Vane leave the room an hour before the meeting, and the tremor in her hands had been worse today, and neither of them had said anything about it.

She waited. The knife sat two inches forward on her belt. She'd practiced the draw that morning, twenty times as instructed, and the new position was becoming less foreign — not natural yet, but no longer the wrong place, more like a place she hadn't known was right.

The yard door opened.

---

He was taller than she remembered from the dock. Or she'd been sitting then and the angle had compressed him, or the fading light had done something to scale, or memory had simply stored him at a size that fit the moment rather than the fact. He came into the yard and stopped just past the threshold and looked at her, and then his eyes moved from her to the yard to the gate and back to her, gathering information the way she gathered it, cataloguing without rushing. He was carrying a satchel over one shoulder, and his hands, when he set the satchel down on a barrel, moved with the confidence of someone who worked with them for a living. There was a small knife-cut on his left thumb, the kind you got from carving and not minding where the blade went.

He didn't speak immediately. He stood in the doorway and let the yard settle around him — the sound of it, the smell of it, her in it. Maren had arrived early to do the same thing from the other side. She let him finish.

"Maren," he said.

"Davan." She'd remembered his name the way she remembered the symbol on the disc's edge — completely, without effort, filed under *essential* before she'd had the chance to evaluate whether it was.

He sat on the barrel across from her. The space between them was perhaps four feet — close enough for conversation, far enough for a decision. He didn't rush to fill it with words. She'd spent three years around people who talked to cover silence, and the ones who didn't were the ones she'd learned to pay attention to.

"You got the carving," he said.

"Yes." She took it from her pocket and set it on the barrel between them. The pine figure, head tilted, one arm close. "You carved this before we met."

It wasn't a question. He looked at it and then at her and something worked through his expression — not embarrassment, not pride. He was reckoning with the fact that a thing he'd made had been understood, and the understanding was both welcome and slightly disorienting.

"On the road north," he said. "Weeks before the dock. My hands kept making that shape." He paused. "I tried to fix the arm three times. It came back."

Maren looked at the carving's tucked arm. She did not look at her own.

"Why did it come back?" she said.

"I don't know." He said it plainly, without the performance of mystery. "My hands find things before I do sometimes. They found you."

She sat with this. It should have been unsettling — someone carving her gesture into wood before they'd met, the intimacy of being known by a stranger's hands. It wasn't unsettling. It was the same quality she'd felt at the dock when he'd shown her the symbol in the driftwood — a piece fitting against a piece, the satisfying rightness of a joint cut true.

"You have the symbol," she said. "The inner orbit. You've been carving it."

"For months. Before I knew what it was." He leaned forward slightly, his forearms on his knees. "I've been watching the moons. The geometry matches — the path they take, the relationship between them. The symbol is counting something in orbit."

Maren took the disc from her coat. She held it in her palm for a moment — the weight of it, the warmth, the faint pulse she could feel through her skin like a second heartbeat running at a frequency slightly off from her own. Then she set it on the barrel next to the carving.

Davan looked at it. His hands moved toward it and stopped — not hesitation exactly, more like the pause of someone approaching something they've been moving toward for a long time and wanting to be present for the moment of arrival rather than rushing through it.

"May I?" he said.

She nodded.

He picked it up. He turned it slowly in his fingers, the way he'd turned the driftwood at the dock, with the attention of someone who read objects the way other people read faces. His thumb found the symbol on the edge and traced it, and he went still in the way of someone who has just heard something very clearly in a room they thought was silent.

"It's warm," he said. "Not — not temperature. Something else."

"I know."

He looked up at her. "You feel it too?"

"It pulses." She said it matter-of-factly, the way she said most things that mattered — without drama, without invitation to elaborate, just the fact laid down like a stone in a wall. "It's been doing it more since I got to the city. Stronger. More frequent."

He held the disc another moment, then set it back on the barrel between them with the care of someone returning something borrowed.

"Tell me what you know," he said.

---

She told him.

Not everything. She told him what Vane had coached her to offer: the disc, the symbol, the fact of being hollow, the fact of being hunted. She told him what the disc was — or what she thought it was, a key to something in the eastern hills, a thing her father had known about and gone looking for and not come back from. She told him about the hill. She did not tell him about the letter or her father's name or the geography of the route east. She gave him the question, as Vane had said. Not the destination.

He listened completely, without interrupting, in the slow thorough way of someone who needed to walk all the way around a thing before he responded to it. When she mentioned being hollow his expression didn't change, but his hands went still on his knees — not pulling away, not reaching forward. Just still, as though something large needed absorbing before any part of him could move.

When she finished, he was quiet for a while.

"Four," he said.

She waited.

"The maps in the cooperage — the old ones, with the margin notation. One has three beside the symbol. One has four. The one with four is older." He looked at the disc on the barrel. "Something changed from four to three. The symbol is the same geometry as the moons' orbit. Three moons."

"And four is what it was before," Maren said.

"Yes." He met her eyes, and for a moment it looked like he might follow the count somewhere — name the thing that turns four into three. He didn't. "I keep getting to the edge of it," he said. "And stopping."

The disc pulsed once between them on the barrel. A low warm throb she felt through the wood, and from the way Davan's eyes moved to it she knew he'd felt it too.

"There's more," he said. "There's a group. People who've been working on this — the symbol, the margin notation, the question of what changed. A former archivist, a cartographer, a glassblower, a tanner who has a sealed container made of the same kind of material as —" He stopped. He looked at the disc again. "As that. Or close to it. The same refusal to behave like anything anyone's handled before."

"How long has the group been meeting?"

"Years. Before I arrived." He paused on this — a weight in the pause that she read correctly, the weight of someone acknowledging he was new to a thing he was describing. "They've been circling the same question from different angles. Whether the symbol is astronomical. Whether the number counts something in the sky. Whether the bloodline's account of what they can and can't do is actually what it claims to be." He turned the carving on the barrel, a half-rotation, a habitual gesture — something his hands did while his mind worked. "There's an argument that keeps coming up. The bloodline says only they can interact with Founding-era objects. Only unchanged hands. They've held that position for as long as anyone can remember, and no one's challenged it."

"Because no one would," Maren said. "Who's going to walk up to the bloodline and say *let me try?*"

"That's exactly the argument. Whether no one's challenged it because it's true, or because the conditions for testing it have never existed." He was quiet a moment. "I sat in the meeting and listened to them go back and forth, and the thing that stayed with me afterward wasn't the argument. It was the gap underneath it. This question nobody can answer because nobody has ever been in a position to try."

He didn't say the rest. He didn't look at her hands. He didn't connect the gap to the girl sitting across from him who had no Chrysalis and a disc that pulsed when she held it. The implication sat in the yard between them, and neither of them reached for it.

The afternoon was turning. The light in the yard had shifted, the cooperage's shadow lengthening across the packed earth, the barrel hoops on the far wall catching the last direct sun and throwing thin curved shadows like the ribs of something opened up. Inside, the mallet-sounds had slowed — the workers finishing for the day, the rhythm of making things winding down into the rhythm of putting things away.

"The hill," she said. "My father went east to find it. The disc opens something there. I don't know what."

"There's a mapmaker — Edvar — who's been working the eastern highlands question for twenty years. He has things I haven't been shown yet. I think there's more he's waiting to share." Davan turned the carving again. "The group meets on Thursdays. I could talk to Edvar. Or Pip — he's the one who arranged this. See if they'd be willing to hear what you have."

"You can't promise that."

"No." He said it without defensiveness. "I've been to one meeting. I'm not in a position to bring anyone in. But what you're carrying —" He looked at the disc. "They've been working around the edges of this for years. You have a piece they don't have. That counts for something, with the right people."

Maren thought about this. The honesty of it — the admission that he couldn't deliver what he hadn't earned, that his position in the group was provisional and new — told her more about him than the carving had. Anyone could make a promise. Fewer people could accurately describe the limits of what they could offer.

"Talk to them," she said. "If they're willing, I'll come."

---

The yard was in shadow by the time they finished. They had talked for the better part of two hours — not all of it the question, not all of it the hill. Some of it was simpler. Where he'd come from, where she'd come from. The road north and what it had cost them both. He told her about Amber Valley, and the Chrysalis Rite, and the light that lived under his skin — he said it plainly, the way you'd describe a weather pattern, something that happened to you that you were still learning to read. She told him about Ashwick, and the coal pouch, and the three years of performing a version of herself that had nearly become the only version. They traded these things the way you traded provisions on the road — not generously, not grudgingly, but with the practicality of two people who understood that shared information was shared weight.

He didn't ask about Vane. She appreciated this. It meant he either knew Vane was there and had decided not to make it a thing, or he didn't know and the not-knowing was beside the point.

She stood. He stood.

"The carving," he said.

She took it from her pocket and held it up. The pine figure, head tilted, one arm drawn in tight. Her arm.

"I'm keeping it," she said.

Something eased in his face — a tension she hadn't known was there releasing, the way a held breath changes a face and only becomes visible in the letting go. "Good," he said.

He picked up his satchel and slung it over his shoulder, and the movement was easy and unhurried — not slow, not passive, but paced.

He went to the yard door and opened it, and the cooperage's interior was dim behind him, the workers gone, the mallets silent. He turned back once, not to say anything, just to look — the yard and the barrels and the woman with the disc and the late afternoon light.

Then he looked past her. Toward the gate.

Maren turned.

Vane was there. Not through the gate — at it, visible through the slats, her shoulder against the fence post in the posture of someone who had been standing still for a long time and was very good at it. She wasn't looking at Maren. She was looking at Davan.

It was not a threat assessment — Maren knew what those looked like from Vane, the compound nose-and-eyes sweep that filed a person under *categories* in the first three seconds. This was something else. Vane looking at someone and taking the time to look, which was a thing she almost never did, because time spent looking was time not spent calculating, and Vane had built her life on the calculation. But she was looking. At the way he stood in the doorway. At his hands, which were still.

Davan looked back. He held the look the way he held everything — steadily, without force, letting it be what it was. Then the faintest warmth moved under the skin of his wrists, visible in the late light, and he didn't hide it. He let it be visible the way he'd let the carving be visible — here is what I am, do with it what you will.

Vane nodded. Once. The smallest possible movement, an acknowledgment Maren couldn't fully read — not approval, not dismissal, something more private than either, something that belonged to whatever Vane's nose was telling her about the person in the doorway.

Davan returned the nod. Then he turned and went through the cooperage door and was gone.

---

Maren stood in the yard a moment after he left. The disc was warm in her pocket. The carving was warm in her other pocket. She felt, for the first time since leaving Ashwick, like something had been added rather than subtracted — not safety, not certainty, but company, the weight of someone else carrying a piece of the same question.

She went to the gate. Vane had already started walking, moving north along the service alley with the unhurried gait of someone who had clocked every exit between here and the room and found them acceptable. Maren fell into step beside her.

They walked half a block in silence.

"Well?" Vane said.

"He's going to talk to his people. See if they'll hear me."

"Not certain, then."

"No. He's new to them. He said so."

Vane was quiet for a few strides. Her hands were in her coat pockets — hiding the tremor, or warming them, or both. The bark on her right arm was visible where her sleeve had ridden up, the dark wood-grain climbing past the elbow, the edges angrier than yesterday. She'd stood at that fence two hours without moving, and the cost of that was probably something Maren would not be told about and would notice anyway.

"That's honest," Vane said. It was not a compliment. It was a filed observation — *he described his position accurately rather than inflating it* — entered into whatever ledger she kept for people whose behavior she was tracking.

"He has pieces I don't have," Maren said. "A mapmaker with something he calls a face — a flat surface that answers to a touch the way the disc does. The young man hasn't been shown what it holds yet."

Vane's stride didn't change, but she turned her head slightly — the fraction of movement that meant her nose was working, pulling at the air as if the information itself had a scent.

"Like the disc," Vane said.

"And the old maps count four. And now there are three."

They walked in silence for a while. The evening was turning cool and the city was settling into its nighttime rhythms and the moons were rising over the eastern roofline, one by one, in their slow patient arcs. Three of them. Maren counted, as she'd been counting since the dock, since the road, since the first night she'd left Ashwick and looked up and seen three lights in the sky and felt something she couldn't name pulling at the center of her chest.

Three moons. And the number used to be four.

"The group argues about whether the bloodline's claim to the old objects is real," Maren said. "Whether only unchanged hands can work them, or whether that's just never been tested."

Vane said nothing for several steps. When she spoke, her voice was level and guarded in the way it got when she was managing something that had gotten past the professional register and into the personal one. "That's not an academic question for you."

"No."

"No," Vane agreed. She didn't say anything else about it. They turned the corner onto the river road and the smell of the water came up to meet them, and Maren put her hand in her pocket and felt the carving and the disc side by side, pine and metal, and the disc pulsed once — warm, certain, reaching — and the carving sat beside it, wooden and still and carved in the shape of her own guarded arm by hands that had known her before she'd known them.

She did not look up at the moons.

Not yet.


## Chapter Seventeen — *[Aldric]*
### The Surveyor

---

Greenford was smaller than he'd expected. Not in the way of a village — it had weight, it had infrastructure, the stone bridges over the river spoke of prosperity and civic will — but in the way of a place that had decided what it was long ago and had not been asked to reconsider. The university district sat above the river on a gentle rise, its buildings the same gray-brown stone as the bridges, and the market square below it was quiet in the late afternoon, the stalls closing, the day's commerce folding itself away with the practiced efficiency of a town that did not stay up late.

Aldric dismounted in the square and led his horse to a public trough. His back ached from four days in the saddle — he was not a young man, and the road east from the Confluence had been less forgiving than he remembered, the last stretch through the river valley rutted and muddy from recent rain.
