He had slept at waypoint inns and eaten what was offered and spoken to no one about anything that mattered, and the discipline of this had cost him less than he'd feared — which troubled him. The lying was becoming easier.

He found Brennan's house on the second street above the square, a narrow-fronted building with a blue door and a window box containing herbs that had not been trimmed in some time. The door was answered before he knocked — Brennan had been watching from the upstairs window, apparently, which was either vigilance or the anxious habit of a man who had been waiting for someone to arrive and could not stop checking whether they had.

---

Brennan was shorter than Aldric had imagined. Twenty years of correspondence produced a mental image, and Aldric's image had been wrong in the specific way that handwriting misled — Brennan's dense leftward script suggested a larger frame, a man who took up space the way his sentences did. The actual Brennan was compact, sharp-featured, with close-cropped gray hair and the quick precise movements of someone who had spent decades in rooms full of fragile things and had learned to navigate without disturbing them.

His hands, when he gripped Aldric's in greeting, were dry and strong and slightly ink-stained at the fingertips. His eyes moved over Aldric's face with the frank assessment of a man who had been reading objects for thirty years and read people the same way — not rudely, but thoroughly, looking for the information the surface carried.

"You look tired," Brennan said.

"I am tired."

"Good. Tired men don't waste time with preamble." He stepped aside and gestured Aldric through. "I'll put water on. Your horse?"

"Trough in the square."

"The ostler two doors down will stable her for a half-mark a night. I'll send the boy." He was already moving through the narrow hallway, talking over his shoulder. "You brought the letter?"

"In my coat."

"Keep it there for now. I have things to show you that came after."

---

The house was what Aldric had expected from a man who studied material culture — which was to say, it was full of materials. Books occupied every horizontal surface and several vertical ones, stacked in arrangements that suggested a filing system comprehensible only to its architect. Maps hung on the walls of the main room — not framed, pinned, with annotations in Brennan's cramped hand trailing off the edges onto the plaster. A long worktable ran beneath the window, and on it lay a sequence of documents arranged in what appeared to be chronological order, each weighted at the corners with small smooth stones. The room smelled of hot wax and old vellum, and beneath that the faint mineral tang of the ink Brennan used — a specific scholar's scent Aldric had not met in years.

He stood in the doorway.

The low persistent unease that had been his companion on the road lifted, for the first time since the Confluence. Recognition. This was the room of a man who had been working on a question for a long time, and the room itself was the visible record of that work — the strata of inquiry, layer on layer, each map and note and weighted document representing a moment when something had been found or tested or discarded. He had a version of this room in his own mind. He had never seen it made physical before.

Brennan set a kettle on the stove and came back to the worktable and stood beside it with his hands flat on the surface — the way Aldric himself stood before the difficult. Grounding in the physical first.

"Sit down," Brennan said. "This will take the evening."

---

The twelve maps were arranged in two rows of six. Brennan had acquired them over a span of fifteen years, from sources he described with the vagueness of a man who had cultivated relationships that depended on discretion. Some were copies — hand-traced from originals he'd been permitted to examine under conditions that precluded removal. Others were originals, purchased or traded for or, in one case, rescued from a condemned university storage room three days before its contents were burned as a fire precaution.

All twelve showed the same thing.

"The notation appears in the margins of maps drawn from above," Brennan said. He was standing at the head of the table, pointing without touching, the scholar's instinct to preserve. "Not every such map — roughly one in four, from what I've been able to survey. But when it appears, it's always the same symbol, always in the margin, and always accompanied by a number."

Aldric leaned forward. The symbol was the inner orbit — the geometry he had been looking at for months, on the temple doorway, on the face in the sealed room, in the patterns his own thinking had begun to trace. In each map's margin it was rendered slightly differently — different hands, different implements, different degrees of haste — but the form was consistent. A path around a center. Something in motion around something fixed.

"The numbers," Aldric said.

"Three or four. In the oldest maps, four. In the most recent, three." Brennan moved along the table, touching the edge of each map's protective cover. "The transition happens here." He indicated a map roughly two-thirds of the way along the sequence. "This is the oldest map I have that shows three. The dating is contested — I'd put it at roughly nine thousand years, give or take a margin that would make a rigorous scholar uncomfortable. The maps showing four are all older than this. Every map showing three is younger."

Aldric looked at the transition point. A map drawn on something that was not quite paper — thicker, with a faint translucence, the material itself old in a way that paper did not get old. The symbol in its margin was drawn in a steadier hand than most, the number beside it a clear and careful three.

"Something changed," Aldric said.

"Something changed." Brennan looked at him. "In the sky. Between the date of the last four and the date of the first three. Something that was being counted went from four to three, and everyone who drew these maps afterward recorded the new number."

Aldric thought about the face in the undercroft. The blue-green world. The four shapes in orbit. One irregular. Damaged.

He did not say any of this. He looked at the maps and he thought it, and Brennan let him arrive on his own.

"You've verified the dating?" Aldric said.

"As far as I'm able. The material composition is consistent within groups — the older maps use a substrate that matches Founding-era artifacts, the same gray material as the sealed containers. The younger ones are conventional paper or vellum. There's no clean way to assign absolute dates, but the relative sequence is solid. Four comes before three. Something that was counted went down by one."

Aldric reached toward the oldest map and stopped. "May I?"

"Carefully."

He lifted the protective cover. The map beneath was drawn on the gray material — smooth, cool, with the density of something manufactured by a process that no longer existed. The coastline it described was recognizably Gaius, but from an angle he had never seen applied to a map before: the full shape of the continent, rendered as if the cartographer had been standing very far above and looking down. Features he knew as local — the river delta at New Plymouth, the ridge country east of the Confluence — appeared as small details in a larger form, each a single stroke in a shape he had never stood far enough above to see.

In the lower margin, the symbol. Beside it, in a hand that was neither hurried nor decorative: four.

He set the cover back.

---

They ate a simple meal at the cleared end of the worktable — bread, hard cheese, a preserved fish that Brennan apologized for and then ate most of. Brennan talked as he ate, the way scholars talked, without clear boundaries between the professional and the personal, the question and the life organized around the question.

He had come to Greenford thirty years ago and stayed because the university's collection of pre-Chrysalis materials was better than anyone outside the institution understood. "It's a backwater archive," he said, "which is the best kind of archive, because it means the people who manage what gets remembered and what gets forgotten haven't paid attention to it in centuries." The first map with the notation had come to him twelve years ago. He had spent six years thinking he was wrong about what it meant, and four more becoming certain he wasn't. "Then I wrote to you."

"Because of who's watching," Aldric said.

Brennan set down his bread. His expression shifted — the scholarly animation folding back, the quicker wariness underneath showing through. "Yes."

"Your letter said someone with bloodline connections."

"Someone with access to my schedule, my correspondence log at the university office, and my borrowing records at three separate archives. That's not a curious colleague. That's a structured inquiry." He picked a crumb off the table, the small fastidious gesture of a man who did not like disorder. "I noticed it six months ago. It's possible it began before that and I simply wasn't watching for it."

Aldric thought about Crane. The pencil on the wrong side of the journal.

"I'm being watched as well," he said.

He told Brennan about Crane — not everything, not the sealed room or the face, but the review, the architectural questions, the pointed absence of certain other questions, the pencil. Brennan listened the way Aldric had expected him to listen: completely, without interruption, his hands still on the table, the full attention of a man accustomed to reading the evidence before forming the interpretation.

"The pencil could be nothing," Brennan said.

"Yes."

"But the review's timing and the scope of its mandate are not nothing."

"No."

Brennan was quiet for a moment. He looked at the maps on his worktable — the patient sequence of twelve perspectives on the same count, the oldest recording four, the youngest three, the transition between them marking a change that had been visible from above for nearly ten thousand years and invisible from below for exactly as long.

"I should tell you about the surveyor," he said.

---

His name was Reth. Brennan said it with particular care, the way you handled a name that had been entrusted to you under conditions. Reth had been a surveyor for the eastern territorial commission — government work, methodical, well-compensated. He had walked the eastern highlands mapping terrain for road planning and mineral assessment. He had done this for fifteen years and retired early and had not spoken about why.

Brennan had found him through the university's geological records — Reth's survey data had been filed, as was required, and Brennan had been reviewing highland topography in connection with the margin notation's implications when he'd noticed an anomaly in one of Reth's terrain reports. A formation described in language that was technically correct and emotionally absent in a way that drew attention to itself — the over-careful blankness of a man writing around something he would not name.

"He described a ridge formation east of the highland plateau as 'an exposed mineral deposit of uncertain composition, approximately four hundred meters in lateral extent, partially obscured by vegetation and sediment accumulation,'" Brennan said. He was quoting from memory, the exact language clear in his voice. "There is nothing wrong with this description. Every word is accurate. And it tells you absolutely nothing about what he actually found."

"You contacted him."

"I wrote. He didn't respond. I wrote again. He didn't respond. I went to his house — he lives in the north end of the city, a quiet street, keeps to himself. He opened the door and looked at me the way you look at someone who has found the thing you've been pretending doesn't exist." Brennan paused. "He said no. He said he'd filed his report and the report was the end of it. I asked him why a mineral deposit required such careful language. He told me to leave."

"But he's agreed to see me."

"He has." Brennan leaned back. "When I told him a Keeper from the Confluence had traveled to Greenford to ask about what he'd found in the eastern highlands, something changed in his expression. Not softening — Reth doesn't soften. But the refusal shifted. He said: 'a Keeper?' And I said yes. And he was quiet for a while and then he said: 'Bring him. Not you. Him.'"

"Why?"

"Because you're an institution. To Reth, that means something. He filed his report with an institution — the territorial commission — and the commission filed it and forgot it. That was the agreement he made with himself: he'd done his duty, he'd reported what he'd found, and the rest was someone else's responsibility. But no one ever came. Forty years and no one followed up, no one asked, no one knocked on his door until I did, and I'm a scholar, which is the wrong kind of authority." Brennan looked at Aldric. "You're a Keeper. That's the right kind. He wants to tell someone who has the weight to do something with what he tells them."

Aldric considered this. The weight of it, the specific expectation being placed on a title he had carried for decades and was only now discovering the true dimensions of.

"When?" he said.

"Tomorrow morning. His house, not mine. He won't come here — too many books, he said, and I think he meant it literally. He doesn't trust rooms full of questions. He trusts rooms where people live."

---

Reth's house was on the north end, as Brennan had said — a stone cottage with a well-kept yard and a gate that opened silently on oiled hinges, the small signal of a man who maintained what was his. Aldric went alone. Brennan stood at the corner of the street and watched him walk the rest of the way, as agreed — present but not participating, a condition Reth had specified.

The door opened before he reached it. Reth was old — older than Aldric had pictured from Brennan's description, though it was hard to say by how much. His face had the weathered compression of a man who had spent decades outdoors reading terrain, the skin tightened over the bones by sun and wind into something like a geological feature itself. His hands were thick, scarred across the knuckles, resting at his sides with the stillness of someone who had learned to stand in one place for a long time and not mind it. His mutation was subtle — a faint iridescence to his skin in the morning light, the kind of change you might not notice indoors.

He looked at Aldric for a long time without speaking. Not the way Brennan read — for information and implication — but the way a surveyor read terrain, for what the surface told you about what was underneath.

"Keeper Voss," Reth said.

"Surveyor Reth."

Reth inclined his head, a small formal motion. "Nobody's called me that in twenty years." He stepped aside. "Come in. I'll say what I have to say and you'll leave and that will be the end of my part in it."

---

The house was sparse and orderly — a man living alone, keeping only what he needed, every object in its place with the discipline of someone who had spent professional years working from organized field kits. The air was clean and stone-cold, nothing domestic in it: no baking, no tallow, no sign that anyone ate here more than they needed to. A single lamp burned on the table, turned low. Aldric's fingers found each other before he could stop them — the pinch, the habitual reach for a flame that wasn't his to manage — and he pressed them flat against his thigh instead.

Reth sat across the table and placed his hands on the surface, palms down, and began.

"I walked the eastern highlands for the territorial commission. Forty-one years ago. The commission was mapping potential road routes through the highland plateau — trade access to the eastern marches, a thing that gets proposed every generation and never built. I was the third surveyor assigned to the eastern section, which included the foothills below the plateau and the broken country east of the tree line."

He spoke without inflection, the measured cadence of a report being delivered to an authority. This was, Aldric understood, what Reth had been waiting forty years to do: give his report to someone who would receive it.

"The terrain east of the tree line is rough. Erosion gullies, exposed rock, heavy scrub vegetation. Not difficult to walk but difficult to map accurately — the surface is deceptive. What looks like a ridge from one angle resolves into a different formation from another. I spent three weeks in that section, working methodically, marking stations, taking measurements."

He paused. His hands on the table did not move.

"On the fifteenth day I reached a formation I could not account for. The maps listed it as a ridge — an exposed sandstone ridge running roughly northwest to southeast, four hundred meters lateral extent, partially buried. The previous survey, which was sixty years old and done by a man who apparently did not walk the eastern face, had marked it as geology and moved on."

"It was not geology," Aldric said.

"No." Reth looked at him. "I'm a surveyor. I know what rock does. I know what sediment does. I know how weather shapes a formation over time, what the grain looks like, what the fracture patterns tell you. This formation had no grain. It had no fracture pattern. The surface, where the vegetation had been pulled away by wind or animal activity, was smooth. Not worn smooth. Manufactured smooth. A continuous surface without joint or seam for fifty meters before the sediment covered it again."

He stopped. The lamp on the table flickered — a draft from the window, the flame leaning and recovering. Aldric watched it and did not reach for it.

"I went back at dusk," Reth said. "Because I wanted to see how the light caught it. Surveyors do this — you check a formation at different times of day to understand the surface. At dusk the low light hit the exposed section at a raking angle and the surface —"

He stopped again. This time the stop was different. Not the pause of a man organizing his account but the arrest of someone running against the edge of what language could do with what he'd seen.

"It caught the light," he said. "Not the way rock catches light. The way metal catches it. A dull reflectance, uniform across the entire exposed section, the color of storm-cloud with something underneath it. And at certain angles — I moved along the formation, watching — at certain angles the light seemed to go into the surface rather than bouncing off it. As if the material were partially translucent. As if there were depth to it."

He looked at Aldric.

"Four hundred meters. Partially buried. Metallic. No geological explanation. No seam, no joint, no grain. Continuous manufactured surface running under centuries of sediment and root systems and soil. I stood there at dusk and I looked at it and I understood that I was looking at something that was not from the landscape. Something that had arrived in the landscape and the landscape had grown over it and nobody had noticed, or nobody had said."

The room was very quiet.

"I went back to my camp," Reth said. "I wrote my report. I used the language I used. I filed the report with the commission and I waited, and nobody said anything, and nobody came to ask, and I completed the rest of the survey and retired the following year and I have not gone back to the eastern highlands since."

"Why not?" Aldric asked.

Reth looked at him with an expression Aldric recognized because he had been wearing a version of it for two months — the expression of a man who had found something too large for the container he had available and had chosen to seal the container rather than find a larger one.

"Because I was a surveyor," Reth said. "I measured land. I filed reports. That was the scope of what I was. And what I found —" He spread his hands slightly, the first gesture he'd made. "What I found was not land. It was not in the scope. I did my duty. I filed my report. If someone with the authority to act on it had come, I would have told them what I'm telling you. Nobody came."

"I'm here now," Aldric said.

Reth studied him for a long moment. Then he nodded, once, with the finality of a man setting something down that he had been carrying for a very long time.

"Yes," he said. "You are."

---

He gave Aldric directions. Not a map — he would not draw one, would not create a document. He gave them verbally, from memory, with the precision of a man who had walked the terrain and retained every station marker and bearing in his head for four decades. East from New Plymouth along the highland road. The turn at the third river crossing. The climb through the tree line. The bearing from the lightning-split cairn that marked the old commission station. Eleven miles northeast of that, across two gully systems and through the heavy scrub, to the exposed section of the formation.

"The vegetation will be heavier now," Reth said. "Forty years of growth. But the surface is there. Whatever it's made of, it doesn't corrode and it doesn't break down. The plants grow over it but they don't grow into it. You'll see the edge where the scrub stops — a line, too clean, where nothing has taken root. That's where the surface starts."

Aldric committed the directions to memory. He did not write them down. Reth watched him not writing them down and appeared to approve.

"One more thing," Reth said. He had risen from his chair, the interview concluded, his body already orienting toward the door and the end of his obligation. But he stopped.

"At the southeastern extent of the formation, where the surface curves downward into the hillside — the thing goes into the ground, Keeper, it doesn't just sit on it — there is a section where the sediment has been cleared. Not by weather. By hands. Someone went there, not recently, but within the last generation. They cleared the sediment from a section of the curved surface and they left it cleared, and when I found it there were marks in the exposed surface. Not scratches. Depressions. Recesses, shaped with a precision that suggested they were part of the original construction. Small. About the size of a —"

He held up his hand. Thumb and forefinger, a circle. The size of a coin. The size of a disc.

Thirty years of pastoral training held his breath in and held his face still. Beneath that training: the word *wreckage* arriving in a place where he had always used *providence*. A lintel of doctrine cracking. He did not let it show. He did not move his hands from where they rested on the table.

"— and in one of those recesses," Reth said, "there was a mark. A symbol. Pressed into the surface or part of the surface, I couldn't tell which."

He didn't describe the symbol. He didn't need to. The shape of the silence between them was description enough, and Aldric knew — with the certainty of a man who had been counting to four for months and had just heard the count confirmed from a direction he hadn't anticipated — exactly what Reth had seen in the curved metal surface of something four hundred meters long that had come down from the sky ten thousand years ago and been waiting, under sediment and silence, for someone to come back.

"Thank you," Aldric said.

Reth walked him to the door. At the threshold he stopped and looked at Aldric with an expression that was neither warm nor cold — the expression of a man handing over a weight he had measured exactly, knowing what it would cost the person who took it.

"I did my part," Reth said. "Forty years ago. This is yours now."

He closed the door.

---

Brennan was waiting at the corner. He read Aldric's face the way he read everything — thoroughly, quickly, with the controlled urgency of a man who had been asking the question long enough to know when an answer had arrived.

"Well?" he said.

"It's real," Aldric said. "It's there."

They walked back to Brennan's house in silence. The morning had turned bright, the kind of autumn clarity that sharpened every edge and made distances look closer than they were. Students moved through the university district in loose groups, carrying books, talking about things that mattered to them with the pleasant urgency of the young.

At the house, Brennan closed the door and went to the worktable and stood there with his hands on the maps.

"What are you going to do?" he said.

"Go to New Plymouth. Then east."

"Alone?"

Aldric considered this. He considered the face in the undercroft at the Confluence, showing him four shapes around a blue-green world. Twelve maps counting down from four to three. A surveyor who had carried his report for forty years because no one had come to receive it. A recess in a metal surface, shaped like a coin, bearing a symbol he had been tracing in his mind for months.

And the phrase from Brennan's letter, carried west now without yet being fully legible: *for unchanged hands.*

"I don't know yet," he said. It was the most honest thing he'd said in weeks, and it arrived with the relief of honesty after a sustained period without it.

Brennan nodded. He did not push. He was a man who understood the value of letting an answer develop in its own time, and Aldric was grateful for this — for the space, for the patience, for the twenty years of correspondence that had produced, at the end of it, the simple ability to stand in a room together and not require more of each other than what was offered.

But before the subject closed for the night, he gave Aldric one thing.

"You'll be looking for a way in," he said. "A city that size, you won't find the others by knocking on doors. But a question like this collects people — quietly, in the trades, in the back rooms the institution stopped watching centuries ago. Start with the mapmakers. There's a chartmaker in New Plymouth — old, reclusive, a master of the work. I've never corresponded with him; I only know his hand. It surfaces in the same private collections mine does, in the margins of the same impossible old maps, cataloguing the same count I've spent twelve years on." He turned one of the weighted stones on the worktable, a small restless motion. "I can't give you a name you could knock with. I can give you a craft and a quarter. The question has a gravity, Aldric. It collects. You'll find them, or they'll find you."

"Stay tonight," Brennan said. "Leave tomorrow. You'll want the rest."

"I'll want the rest," Aldric agreed.

---

He left Greenford the next morning, west toward New Plymouth, with the surveyor's directions held in his memory like a set of coordinates for something he had not yet decided to find. The road followed the river valley, wider here than the Confluence road, the landscape opening toward the lowlands and the distant suggestion of the coast. His horse moved at an easy pace and he let it, not hurrying. The autumn air was cold in his collar; the road dust rose under the hooves and settled; the saddle shifted with the horse's long strides beneath him, and he let his body take the rhythm.

He thought about Reth's hands on the table, palms down, delivering his report to the institution he'd been waiting for. The specific patience of a man who had filed his account and then waited forty years in the silence that followed, and what that patience had cost, and what it meant that the silence had not been an oversight but a system functioning as designed — the report filed, the report archived, the report never followed up, the question never asked, the answer never required.

He thought about his own institution and its own silences. The sealed room. The Restricted Collection. The careful management of what was known and what was permitted to be known, practiced for so long it had become invisible, the way a wall becomes invisible when you've walked past it every day for thirty years. He was riding west toward a city where, Brennan was sure, others had been doing what he had been doing alone. *Providence*, the word his order used for what the faithful could not see into. He had used it a thousand times in sermons. It meant, in practice, *do not ask.*

The lamp tic found him twice on the road — once at a waypoint where he pinched a candle stub beside the register, once reaching for a lantern at the stable where he changed horses. Both times he caught himself, his thumb and forefinger closing on nothing or on flames that were not his, and both times he let go with the deliberate effort of a man unlearning a habit that had outlived its purpose. The habit was not about lamps. It had never been about lamps. It was about control — the small reliable control of a manageable thing, practiced in place of the larger, less manageable things that were beyond his reach. He understood this now in a way he had not understood it at the Confluence, and the understanding made the habit harder to perform rather than easier to break.

He was the next piece. The thought rode with him as the road straightened and the afternoon light turned long and golden. He had been counting to four for months and a stranger had just counted to four with him from the other side of the country, and the count was no longer his alone to keep.

The moons were visible in the late afternoon sky, pale against the blue, three of them at different heights in their slow orbits. He looked at them the way Reth must have looked at the formation in the highlands — with the knowledge that what he was seeing was not what everyone else saw, that the familiar shapes carried an unfamiliar weight.


## Chapter Eighteen — *[Vane]*
### The Bloom-Giver

---

The tin was light. She knew without opening it. Had known for three days, the way you know a room's dimensions before the lamp catches up — by the quality of the echo when nothing comes back.

Vane sat on the edge of the narrow bed and held the tin in her lap. Maren was out. Market run, she'd said, which meant walking a pattern through the stalls near the fishmonger's quarter where foot traffic was thick enough to dissolve a silhouette. Good fieldcraft for someone who'd learned it from watching rather than training. Maren picked things up. That was the problem with her, or one of them.

The room was small and smelled of tallow and the particular warmth Vane had stopped pretending she didn't track. Maren's warmth. It threaded through everything now — the blanket on the second bed, the chair back, the doorframe where she leaned when she was thinking. Vane used it the way she used any reliable signal. Orientation. Proximity. Threat absence.

She turned the tin over once and set it on the mattress.

The bark-skin on her right arm itched. Above the elbow now, upper arm nearly to the shoulder, the grain creeping toward the joint where the change would stop feeling like a sleeve and start feeling like a shirt. She rolled back her sleeve and looked at it in the grey light from the window — dark grain, tight whorls, the edges angrier than last week. Redder. The border where skin became something else was precise as a tide line and about as negotiable. Under the grain she could feel the arm, still: the muscle, the tendon, the pulse. Not gone. Just wearing something she had not chosen. A tightness when she flexed, as if a thin glove of bark had been drawn up and stitched to her from the inside.

She rolled the sleeve down.

The contact's name was Deren. Older than Cade. Deeper current. A name she'd picked up in Port Aelith from a woman who sold information about information, which was the only kind of broker Vane respected. He operated out of the docks district, southside, where the warehouses backed up against the canal retaining walls and the air tasted of brine and rotting hemp.

She'd sent word yesterday. The reply came back on a folded slip tucked into the frame of the rooming house door: an address, a time, and the figure. The figure was high. She could meet it.

Vane checked the tin one more time with a brief precise motion, thumb across the clasp. Confirmed what she already knew. One wafer left. Maybe none.

She pocketed it, pulled on her coat, and went out.

---

The docks district in late afternoon was a study in practiced disinterest. Longshoremen finishing second shift. Cart traffic thinning as the chandlers closed up. Women with baskets moving toward home with the focused stride of people who'd stopped finding the city interesting years ago. Vane matched their pace. Coat collar up, hands in pockets, right hand curled around the tin because the tremor was worse in open air and she didn't need to advertise it.

The address was on Lintel Street, which ran parallel to the canal before ducking under a stone footbridge and becoming something narrower and less named. The buildings here were old — pre-colonial stonework, the kind the city had paved over and built on top of and then grudgingly preserved when the foundations turned out to be the only thing keeping the canal bank from sliding. Vane counted doorways. Third from the bridge. Iron-banded. No marking.

She knocked the pattern from the slip.

The door opened inward. The man behind it was short, heavy through the shoulders, with the flat affect of someone whose job was to be a surface between the street and whatever lay behind him. He looked at her hands. She showed them, palms up, and he stepped aside.

Stone stairs going down. Of course. The canal district was built on top of the old quarter, and the old quarter was built on top of what came before that, and at some point you stopped calling it a basement and started calling it what it was. Vane went down.

The smell hit her on the seventh step.

Copper-flower. Bloom. But not the refined note she knew from the wafers — not the compressed, preserved version that sat on the tongue like a petal pressed in a book. This was the smell at volume. At source. The way a tannery smells nothing like a leather glove. It filled the stairwell like a temperature, warm and thick and unmistakably biological.

Her hands went flat against the walls. Both of them. The tremor had stopped, which it did sometimes when her body decided to be afraid before she gave it permission.

She kept going down.

The corridor at the bottom was stone, low-ceilinged, lit by two oil lamps mounted in iron brackets. She knew this corridor. She'd been seeing it for months — in the visions that came with the Bloom episodes, the involuntary ones, the flashes she'd catalogued and cross-referenced and filed without ever asking the obvious question about where the images were coming from. This was the corridor. The lamps were in the right places. The ceiling was exactly as low as she'd seen it, close enough to brush her hair if she stood straight.

A door at the end, ajar. Behind it, a room.

Vane stopped walking.

She could hear someone breathing. Not Deren — Deren would be upstairs or elsewhere, the way suppliers were always elsewhere when the product was being shown. The breathing was slow and thick and had a quality to it she'd heard once before. In Cade's back room in Harrowgate. Through a wall. *If she survives the first month.*

She pushed the door open.

---

The room was eight paces by six. She measured it because measuring was what she did when she couldn't do anything else. Stone walls, stone floor, stone ceiling at the height she already knew. A pallet against the far wall. A drain in the floor. A table with collection apparatus — glass, copper tubing, clay vessels sealed with wax. The lamps in here were turned low.

The person on the pallet was hollow.

Vane knew this the way she'd learned to know it over the past months — by the quality of the skin, by the particular translucency at the temples and inner wrists, by the faint luminescence that hollows carried like a watermark. This person's luminescence was dim. Banked. There were puncture marks at the inside of each elbow and at the hollow of the throat, the skin around each one angry and faintly raised, the way skin went when the body was fighting something that had been put into it and would not leave. Sweat beaded at the hairline despite the stone cold. A strand of dark hair had come loose from where someone had tied it back and lay across her cheek. The features under it were ordinary: young, with a narrow nose and a small white scar at the corner of the mouth, the kind you got as a child from falling wrong onto a step. Vane looked. She made herself look.

*Junie,* the thought arrived, unbidden — not a name she knew this woman by, not a name at all, just the kind of name a farmhand girl might have had before someone took her somewhere.

Female. Young, or had been. Breathing the slow thick breath of induced sleep. Restraints at the wrists, padded with cloth that had been changed recently — Deren or whoever ran this kept things clean, which was worse, somehow, than filth would have been. The copper-flower smell was overwhelming here. It came from the skin. From the pores. The secretions that hollows produced during sleep, the ones Vane had spent years consuming in pressed wafer form with a cup of tea in the morning like it was a habit and not a transaction.

She sorted. The operational part of her brain did what it always did — inputs, outputs, cost structure, product architecture — because that was the only way to stand in this room and keep standing. The collection apparatus was designed for continuous harvest. Condensation trays positioned along the body. Copper tubing running to a settling vessel. The wax-sealed clay pots on the table were the intermediate product — raw secretion before pressing and refinement. The wafers in her tin had started in a room like this. On a body like this.

She had known this.

Not the address. Not the face. But the fact. She'd known since Harrowgate, since Cade's back room, since the phrase that had lodged in her like a splinter — *if she survives the first month* — and she'd kept buying. She'd known since the Bloom episodes had started showing her this exact ceiling, this exact quality of lamplight, this exact breathing, and she'd catalogued the visions and never once followed the thread to its source because following the thread would have required standing where she was standing now.

The woman's chest rose and fell. The condensation trays caught the light. Her fingers twitched once in sleep and were still.

Vane turned around and walked back up the stairs.

---

The man at the door looked at her as she came up. His face did the thing faces do when a transaction hasn't been completed — the slight recalibration, the question that isn't asked because asking would be unprofessional.

"Changed my mind," Vane said.

He opened the door. She walked through it.

Lintel Street was exactly as she'd left it. The canal smelled like a canal. Cart wheels on cobblestone. A woman with a basket of dried cod walked past without looking at her. The footbridge threw a shadow across the road that moved with the late sun.

Vane crossed the street and stood against the wall of the opposite building. The stonework was cold through her coat. She put her hands in her pockets and found the tin and held it and stayed.

The tremor came back. Fine, at the fingertips, the way it always started. The bark-skin on her right arm pulsed once, a slow deep itch that she'd stopped scratching weeks ago because scratching didn't help and because the texture under her nails made her think of things she didn't need to think about.

The street changed around her the way streets do. Afternoon foot traffic thinned. A lamplighter came through, then doubled back, then came through again on the other side. The shadow of the footbridge migrated across the cobblestones and climbed the wall to her left. Vane tracked all of it — exits, sightlines, density patterns — because the operational part of her brain didn't stop working just because the rest of her had. The rest of her was in the room. The rest of her was standing in the doorway looking at the condensation trays.

A cost-benefit analysis. She was good at those.

Cost of continued use: the body on the pallet. Not abstractly — she'd had the abstraction for months and it hadn't stopped her. Concretely. That specific ceiling. That specific breathing. The cloth on the restraints changed by someone who kept things clean. The small white scar at the corner of the mouth.

Benefit of continued use: the tracking sense. The ability to find Maren, to read hollow proximity, to do the job she'd assigned herself.

Cost of stopping: the episodes. The shaking. The loss of the tracking sense, probably, though she didn't know the timeline. Operational degradation at the worst possible moment, with Horst in the city and Maren depending on her ability to stay functional. The bark-skin she didn't know about. Nobody knew what long-term Bloom use did, because long-term Bloom users either stopped or stopped being something you could ask.

She held the tin in her pocket and felt its weight, which was almost nothing.

*If she survives the first month.*

Vane pushed off the wall and walked.

---

She took the long way back. Not for countersurveillance, though she ran the pattern anyway — three turns, a doubled block, a pause at a vendor's stall she didn't see. The long way back was for the same reason she'd stood against the wall: she needed to finish the calculation before she arrived, because she wouldn't do it in front of Maren, and she wouldn't do it after, and if she didn't do it now it wouldn't get done.

The calculation was simple. She'd made it complicated for months with operational justifications and tactical necessity and the professional categories she used to keep things sorted, but it was simple. She was consuming a substance made from a captive person's body to track another person she was supposed to be protecting. The tracking target and the production source were the same kind. Hollow. Maren's kind. And Vane had been using one to find the other and calling it fieldwork.

She crossed through the market square. The stalls were closing. A fishmonger was packing ice around the unsold catch and the smell of it cut through everything for a moment — salt and cold and clean — and then the copper-flower was back, ghost of it, memory of it, she couldn't tell. It didn't matter. She could still taste the last wafer she'd taken, three days ago, on the back of her tongue. She would probably taste it for a while.

The rooming house was on a quiet street near the old seminary. Vane let herself in, climbed the stairs, and stood outside the door for a count of ten. Not composing herself. There was nothing to compose. She was standing in the place where a decision had been made and checking whether it was still there.

It was.

She went in.

---

Maren was at the small table by the window, mending a tear in her sleeve with a needle and thread she'd bought at the market. She looked up when Vane came in. Read something. Went back to the needle for a moment, then set it down.

"You went to the docks."

Maren had a way of stating things that made them available for correction without requiring it. Vane had noted this too, the way she noted other reliable signals.

"I went to a Bloom contact." Vane closed the door. Hung her coat. Sat on the edge of her bed, which put her across from Maren with the width of the room between them. "Different from Cade's network. Older supplier. The address was a harvest site."

She said it flat because flat was how it came out. Maren's hands went still on the table.

"The substance is made from hollows." Vane's voice was level, the way it got when she was delivering a field report that contained information no one was going to enjoy. "Captive hollows. The secretions produced during induced sleep. Collected, refined, pressed into wafers. I've known the general mechanism for months. Today I saw the specific production."

Maren didn't speak. Her eyes were steady on Vane's face with that quality of attention that was itself a kind of silence — active, receiving, not filling the space.

"One person," Vane said. "In a stone room under the canal district. Female. Young. Restrained. Sedated. Needle marks at both arms and the throat. They put something into her and collect what her body makes fighting it." She paused. Breathed once. "The room matched the visions I've been having during episodes. I've been seeing the source for months and cataloguing it as sensory noise."

Maren's expression didn't change, which meant it had changed and she was keeping it still. Vane could read that now. She could read a lot of things about Maren now, and some of that was the Bloom, and she didn't want to think about what it meant that the tool she'd used to read Maren was made from someone like Maren.

"I didn't buy any," Vane said. "I didn't free the person. I stood in the street for —" She stopped. Checked. "A while."

"What did you decide?"

Maren's voice was even. Not gentle — Maren had the sense not to be gentle right now, which was one of the things about her that Vane had stopped trying to file under a professional heading.

"I'm stopping."

Two words. They sat in the room like stones dropped on a table.

"The tracking sense may degrade. I don't know the timeline. The tremor will get worse before it gets better. The episodes will intensify. I'll be less operational for a period I can't estimate. This is a poor time for it, with Horst in the Merchant Quarter, and I'm doing it anyway."

She laid it out the way she would have laid out a logistics problem — sequenced, with known variables and acknowledged unknowns. Maren watched her lay it out and didn't interrupt.

"What's in your pack," Maren said. Still not a question.

Vane looked at her. "The tin. One wafer left. Maybe none."

"And you're keeping it."

"For now."

This was the part that could have become something else. Maren could have reached for her hand, said something about courage or the right choice. She didn't. She picked up the needle, looked at it, set it down again.

"All right," Maren said.

"There's a gap," Vane said, and then stopped because she didn't usually explain herself and didn't know why she was starting now. But Maren was watching with that quality of attention, and the words came out anyway. "Between deciding and executing. I've decided. The tin is still in my pack. I haven't destroyed it. I'm going to sit with that for —" She turned her right hand over on her knee and looked at the bark-skin where it vanished under her sleeve. "Until I don't need to anymore."

Maren nodded once.

The room was quiet. Tallow candle, thread on the table, the two narrow beds, the window with its view of the seminary wall. Vane's pack sat against the wall by the door with the tin inside it, weighing almost nothing, containing almost nothing, representing a supply chain that ended in a stone room with a low ceiling and a woman who breathed the slow thick breath of someone kept asleep so that what her body produced could be scraped off and sold to people like Vane.

The tremor was back in her fingertips. She pressed her hands flat on her knees and held them there.

Maren went back to the mending. The needle moved through the cloth in small precise stitches, and the sound it made was the sound of thread pulled through fabric, which was a quiet sound, and it was enough.

---


## Chapter Nineteen — *[Davan]*
### What Edvar Keeps

---

The shop smelled of linseed and old paper — not the sharp chemical bite of fresh oil but the settled, sweet undertone of decades of it soaked into wood and plaster. The walls themselves had absorbed the work done here. Davan thought of tree rings, how a trunk held its years in layers you could only see once it was cut.

Edvar let him in without speaking. That was new. The first three visits had each begun with some small exchange at the threshold — a comment about the weather, or the state of the harbor road, or once a dry observation about the quality of Davan's boots. Tonight the mapmaker simply stepped aside and gestured him through.

The front room was as Davan remembered: the broad drafting table under the north-facing window, the racks of pigment jars along the far wall sorted by hue, the rolled charts in their vertical cubbies like a library built sideways. Tools hung from pegs in an order that was not alphabetical or chronological but made sense to the hand that reached for them — compass, ruling pen, divider, stylus, each within the arc of a seated man's arm. A workspace shaped by forty years of the same motions.

But Edvar walked past the drafting table. Past the pigment racks. He went to the back wall, where a curtain separated the shop from his living quarters, and held it aside.

Davan had not been past the curtain before.

"Boots are fine," Edvar said, reading the hesitation. "Floor's just stone."

The back room was smaller than Davan expected. A cot against one wall, made up with the tight corners of a man who lived alone and saw no reason to be loose about it. A washstand. A shelf of books — not many, but handled often, their spines softened. A single chair near a cold hearth. The room of someone who spent most of his life in the front half of the building and came here only to sleep.

There was a trunk at the foot of the cot. Iron-banded, the wood dark with age, a lock that Edvar opened with a key he wore on a cord inside his shirt. Davan watched him kneel — slowly, one knee at a time, the way a man in his sixties negotiated with a floor — and lift the lid.

Inside, nested in a wrapping of oiled canvas, was something flat and dark.

Edvar lifted it out with both hands. He set it on the cot and folded the canvas back, and the object was roughly the size of a serving tray — rectangular, perhaps two handspans by three, and thin. The surface was dark. Not black, exactly. Not the black of ink or iron or char. A dark that seemed to go down into the material rather than sit on top of it. Light from the single lamp touched it and did not come back.

Davan's skin pulsed once, low amber, at the wrists. He was aware of it and did not try to suppress it. He had learned weeks ago that suppressing made it worse.

"Twenty years," Edvar said. He was still kneeling beside the cot, looking at the face with an expression Davan could not immediately read. Then he could: it was the look of a man opening a door he had kept locked, unsure whether the air on the other side would still be breathable. "I found it in a building collapse on the eastern ridge. The old quarter — before the fire took most of it. There was a cellar that had been sealed. Stones had come down and broken the floor open, and it was in the rubble."

He did not say *I knew what it was.* He did not say *I didn't know what it was.* The omission sat between those two statements like a third option: *I knew it was something.*

"You can touch it," Edvar said.

Davan knelt beside him. The stone floor was cold through his trousers. Beneath it, something else — not cold, not warm, but present. The same quality as Corvel's container at the cooperage meeting, the proximity-sense that was not temperature but registered in the same place temperature did. Something in the face was aware of the space around it, or held a residue of awareness, the way Edvar's front room held the smell of linseed.

He reached out and put his fingertips on the surface.

---

The dark lit.

Not all at once. It began at the point of contact — a pale light spreading from where his fingers pressed, cold and precise, nothing like lamplight. Lamplight was warm and uneven and alive. This was something else. This was light that had been made, designed, given exact properties and held to them. It spread across the surface in a wash that took perhaps three seconds, and then the entire face of the object was illuminated from within.

Davan did not pull his hand away. His skin-light had gone to blue at his throat and was climbing toward something else — building at his collarbones, not yet arrived.

The image assembled itself.

First: a sphere. Blue-green, not large — the size of his closed fist, rendered in the cold light at the center of the dark surface. It turned slowly, and he could see the suggestion of landmasses, oceans, the marbled texture of atmosphere. A world. He knew it the way he knew his own palm lines — not because he had studied it, but because he had always been standing on it.

Then the shapes appeared.

They came in from the edges, or rather the image widened to include them — the perspective pulling back as if the viewer were rising, lifting away from the surface of the blue-green sphere to reveal what moved around it. Orbiting. Shapes in fixed paths, each tracing a line that the cold light rendered as a faint curve.

One. Large, steady, its path a clean circle.

Two. Smaller, closer to the world, moving faster.

Three. Between the other two, its orbit slightly tilted.

Four.

The fourth shape. Smaller, as if further away. As if it had always been the most distant of them. Its orbit was wider than the others, a long slow track around the blue-green world, and its shape — where the first three were rendered as distinct forms, each with enough detail to suggest a body with surface and mass — the fourth was reduced. A point that was almost a shape. Something that had been there when this record was made, caught at the limits of whatever instrument or eye had captured the arrangement.

Davan's skin went green-gold.

Not a pulse or the quick flash that came with a sudden feeling and faded. This was sustained — a glow that started at his wrists and moved up his forearms and across his collarbones and settled. Recognition: the slow certainty of a thing he had already known making itself visible. The same thing the carvings had been — the symbol he'd been cutting into pine and cedar for months without knowing what it was, his hands working out a shape his mind had not yet assembled.

Four.

He counted them again. One, two, three, four. The three he had always known — the three that anyone who looked up at night could count, the moons that moved through the sky in their ancient arrangement. And the fourth. Smaller, further, captured in a record that was older than anything he had ever touched.

"Four," he said. His voice was steady. The green-gold held.

Edvar was watching him, not the face. Davan could feel the quality of that attention — the mapmaker studying him the way he studied coastlines, looking for the features that confirmed or denied what his measurements had predicted.

"The margin notations," Davan said. "The old maps. The ones that show four."

"Yes."

"They weren't wrong."

"No."

Davan looked at the face again. The four shapes continued their orbits, the cold light tracing paths that had been fixed into whatever this material was — fixed and held and waiting for a touch to release them. He thought about how long they had been waiting. Twenty years in Edvar's trunk. Before that, sealed in a cellar beneath a building that had collapsed. Before that —

The fourth shape. Smaller, as if further away. But not further away, not really. The other three were still in the sky. He saw them every night. Whatever this record held was the arrangement as it had been, and the arrangement now held only three.

Something that had been in orbit was no longer in orbit.

"It came down," Davan said.

---

Edvar lowered himself from kneeling to sitting. His back found the side of the cot. He looked at the face — still lit, still turning, the four shapes in their patient loops — and something crossed the mapmaker's face: the expression of a man whose half-thought had just been finished by someone else's voice. Davan knew that look. He'd worn it.

"Twenty years," Edvar said again. But this time the words carried a different weight. Not *I have kept this for twenty years.* Rather: *For twenty years I have known what this meant and have not let myself say it.*

"You saw the four," Davan said. "Every time you opened the trunk. You counted them."

"Yes."

"And you looked up at the sky and counted three."

Edvar's hands were resting on his knees. They were the hands of a man who worked with fine instruments — steady, deliberate, the fingers long and spotted with age. They did not tremble. But there was something coming off the mapmaker that was not visible in his body — a vibration in the emotional register, the frequency of something long held finally being set down.

"I told myself it could be an error in the recording," Edvar said. "A flaw in the material. An artifact of age. I had seventeen plausible explanations for why the record showed four when the sky showed three, and I tested each of them against every old chart and margin notation I could find, and none of them held." He paused. "The fourth shape is consistent across every source I've found that predates the Quiet Century. After that period, every source shows three. The transition is clean. Whatever happened, happened within a single generation."

"Within living memory," Davan said. "Someone saw it happen."

"Someone saw it happen. And then, apparently, everyone agreed not to record it."

They sat with that. The cold light of the face painted them both in blue-white, and Davan's green-gold glow mixed with it, casting the small room in colors that did not belong to any lamp or hearth. He thought about the shape he had been carving. The orbital symbol. His hands had known, working it out of wood the way water worked its way through stone — not by force but by patience, by returning and returning to the same surface until the shape emerged.

The fourth shape on the face was not damaged. Not from this angle, this recording. It was simply distant and small, a body on a wide orbit, far enough out that the instrument or eye that had made this record could barely resolve it. But it was there. It was whole. It was in the sky.

And then it was not.

"Something that size," Davan said slowly, "coming down."

"Yes."

"The impact."

"I've spent years on the calculations." Edvar's voice had shifted — not louder or faster, but denser, the way a man spoke when he was finally using words he had rehearsed in solitude for decades. "The mass required to sustain an orbit of that period. The energy of a descent. Even a partial descent — even a fragment. The numbers are beyond what I have language for. Beyond what anyone in the cooperage group has language for, and several of them are better mathematicians than I am."

"But you've never told them."

"No."

"Why?"

Edvar looked at him. The expression was complex — layered, like the linseed smell, built up over years. Davan read it as best he could. There was protectiveness in it, and something like shame, and beneath both of those a loneliness so deep and so long-held that it had become structural. Part of the foundation. The man had built his life around a room that held a secret he could not share because sharing it meant completing a thought he could not face alone.

"Because I am a mapmaker," Edvar said. "I draw what is there. I record coastlines and elevations and the positions of landmarks relative to fixed points. I do not speculate. I do not theorize. I observe and I render and I verify." He looked down at his hands. "And for twenty years I have been in possession of an observation I cannot render on any map. A thing that was in the sky and is no longer in the sky. And the only conclusion that the evidence supports is that it came down, and that when it came down, it —"

He stopped.

Davan waited. The green-gold at his wrists pulsed once and held.

"— it changed everything," Edvar finished. "The Quiet Century. The gaps in the records. The things we've lost and can't account for losing. If something of that mass struck the surface or broke apart in descent, the consequences would have been —" He shook his head. Not a negation. A man reaching the limit of what solitary thought could carry. "I couldn't complete the sentence. I could never complete the sentence. I would get to that point and stop and wrap it up and put it back in the trunk."

"For twenty years."

"For twenty years."

Davan did not say *I understand.* He did not say *that must have been difficult.* He sat beside the mapmaker on the cold stone floor, and the silence held. The face still turned between them, the four shapes in their ancient, patient orbits, and outside the shop the real sky held three.

---

The lamp had burned down by half before either of them spoke again. Davan had spent the time in the way he spent most of his thinking time — letting his attention move across the details of the room, the textures and proportions, the way the cold light from the face interacted with the warm light from the wick. His thoughts circled. The orbital symbol he'd been carving. The margin notations — *four* written in faded ink on charts so old the paper crumbled at the edges. The cooperage group's arguments about bloodline capacity and unchanged hands. Maren's disc, warm with something that was not temperature. The gray container Corvel had shown them, the one nobody could open, made of a material nobody could identify.

The container.

The thought arrived before he had words for it. A connection forming the way a shape formed under his knife — not all at once, but by the slow removal of everything that was not the shape, until what remained was clear.

"The container," he said.

Edvar turned his head. He had been looking at the face, or through it — the look of a man whose eyes are fixed on something that is not in the room.

"Corvel's container," Davan said. "The gray material. The way it absorbs light instead of reflecting it."

He put his hand on the surface of the face again. Not to activate it — it was still lit — but to feel the material itself. The substance of it. The way it took the light from the lamp and did not give it back. The way it sat in his hand with a density that was not stone, not metal, not wood, not anything he had worked with.

"It's the same," he said.

He looked at Edvar.

The mapmaker's expression was one Davan would remember for a long time afterward. This was the face of a man who had been alone with a question for two decades and was, in the space of an evening, no longer alone with it.

Not surprise. Not relief, exactly. Something older than either. The recognition of company after a very long time alone.

"Yes," Edvar said.

"The same material."

"I believe so. I have believed so since Corvel first brought it to the group, eight years ago. I held it in my hands and I felt —" He gestured at the face. "This. The same quality. The same way it sits against the skin. He found it in the marshland south of the ridge, half-buried in clay that the geological surveys suggest was laid down during the Quiet Century."

"During the impact window."

The words landed in the room like stones dropped into still water — another of Edvar's sentences finished, at last, by someone else's mouth.

"During the impact window," Edvar repeated. Quietly. Testing the words in his own mouth. "Yes."

"So the container isn't from here."

"No. I don't think it is."

"And this —" Davan's fingers rested on the face, the cold light washing over his knuckles, his green-gold glow mixing with the blue-white. "This isn't from here either."

"I found it in a sealed cellar. Old construction — older than the colony records for that district. Whatever was down there had been down there a very long time."

They looked at each other across the lit surface of the face, and the full architecture of what they were sitting with settled into place. Not a revelation. Not the dramatic unveiling of a hidden truth. Something quieter and heavier: the slow, thorough understanding that the world they stood on had been struck by a piece of its own sky, and that the strike had left debris, and that the debris was not rock.

The face was a made thing. The container was a made thing. Whatever had occupied that fourth orbital position had not been a natural body — not a moon of stone and ice, tumbling through space on a path set by gravity alone. It had been something else. Something that could produce a flat dark surface that responded to touch and rendered light with a precision that no lampmaker or glassblower on this world could match.

The green-gold at Davan's collarbones flared and held. He did not fight it. He let it burn.

"The cooperage group," he said after a while. "The arguments about capacity. Unchanged hands. All of it."

"All of it," Edvar said. "Every question they've been circling for years." He folded his hands together in his lap. "If something fell — something made, something that was not a moon — then the question isn't just what it did to the surface when it landed. The question is what it was doing in orbit before it fell. And what it left behind."

"What it left in us," Davan said.

Edvar closed his eyes. Just for a moment. When he opened them, the tiredness in his face was the tiredness of twenty years of solitude laid down all at once, and beneath it was something that might have been gratitude, if gratitude could be that heavy.

"I have never said any of this to another person," he said. "I need you to understand that."

"I understand."

"I have maps and margin notations and geological surveys and this —" he gestured at the face, still patiently displaying its ancient record, "— and I have spent two decades building a case that I could never present. Because presenting it would mean saying what you just said. That something came down. That it was made. That it changed everything. And I am a mapmaker, Davan. I draw coastlines. I am not the person who says these things."

"No," Davan agreed. "But you're the person who found the evidence. And you kept it. And you waited."

"I waited."

Edvar looked at him with that expression again — the one Davan would carry with him when he left tonight, out into the dark streets where three moons hung in a sky that had once held four. The expression of a man whose long patience had been, finally, answered.

"Yes," the mapmaker said. "For someone to ask the right question."


## Chapter Twenty — *[Maren]*
### The Restricted Collection

---

The hoop-set cost four marks, which was robbery for bent iron and a handful of rivets. Maren paid without comment. The apprentice at the counter — sixteen, maybe seventeen, with the permanently reddened hands of someone who spent his days around steam — took the coins and nodded her toward the back without looking up from his ledger.

She'd been told the nod was the signal. Davan had explained it twice, the second time with the patient repetition of someone who understood that details kept people alive. Buy the hoop-set. Wait for the nod. Go through the door behind the stave rack. Down.

Davan went first. The stairs were narrow, stone-edged, built for moving barrels not people. The air changed three steps down — oak shavings and vinegar replaced by mineral cold, the particular dampness of stone that never saw sun. She catalogued it automatically: one exit behind her, none visible ahead, walls close enough to touch both sides with her elbows bent.

The cooperage sat two streets from the river, on a lane where the smell of the workshop covered everything else. She'd noted the approaches on the walk over. Three ways out of the lane. Vane was outside, near the gate, doing whatever Vane did when she waited — which was stand very still in a place where she could see a long way and think about angles. Maren had not asked her to come. Vane had simply been there when she left, and had walked beside her without explaining, and had stopped at the gate with a nod that meant *I'll be here*.

The basement opened up at the bottom of the stairs. Low ceiling, stone floor dark at the edges where the damp crept in. Two lamps on a table in the center, their light reaching about as far as the first ring of chairs and failing after that. The corners were suggestions. The room smelled like tallow and cold earth and, faintly, the chemical bite of tanning solution — someone's clothes, not the room itself.

Six people. She counted them before she counted anything else.

---

A woman at the head of the table, seated, both hands flat on the wood. Fifty or near it. Gray threaded through dark hair pulled back with no concession to style. She had the posture of someone who had spent years bending over texts and had eventually decided to stop bending — spine straight, chin level, the overcorrection become habit. That would be Renner. Davan had described her as *the one who runs it*, which Maren had understood to mean *the one nobody has successfully argued down*.

To Renner's left, a woman with the forearms of someone who worked with heat. Glassblower's arms — ropey, scarred at the wrists where the radiant burn caught you if you leaned wrong. Thirty, maybe younger. She sat with her hands in her lap, which was a choice. People who worked with their hands and then sat on them were making a point about patience. Asha.

Across the table, a square-built man with chemical stains worked into the creases of his knuckles that no amount of washing would remove. Tanner. Corvel. In front of him on the table sat an object Maren's gaze caught on and held: a smooth gray container, forearm-length, the surface so uniformly dark it looked less like a thing and more like the absence of a thing. The lamplight reached it and stopped. Did not reflect. Did not slide off. Simply ended.

She made herself look past it.

An older man in the far chair — sixties, weathered in the way of someone who spent time outdoors but thought indoors. Ink on his fingers, but old ink, in the cuticles. Mapmaker's hands. Edvar. He watched her come down the stairs with an expression that gave nothing and took everything in, and she recognized it because it was the expression she wore in rooms full of strangers. Professional assessment.

A young woman beside Edvar, also with ink stains — fresher ones, on the side of her writing hand where the heel dragged. Cartographer's apprentice, or assistant, or whatever the relationship was.

And Pip. She knew Pip already, or knew of him — Davan's friend from the docks district, the one who found things. Twenty-three and built like a question mark, all forward lean and nervous energy. He gave her a nod when she reached the bottom of the stairs. It was the most welcoming gesture she'd received so far.

Davan stepped to the side, giving the room a clear view of her. "This is Maren," he said. "She's the one I told you about."

The silence that followed was not hostile. It was evaluative. Maren stood in it the way she stood in all silences — with her weight even, her hands at her sides, her face offering exactly as much as she chose to offer. Three years of performing a version of herself that wasn't quite true had given her a very precise understanding of what her face did when she let it do nothing. Nothing was usually the right choice.

"Sit," Renner said. Not an invitation. An allocation of resources — there was a chair, Maren was standing, the inefficiency offended her.

Maren sat.

---

The questioning began without preamble.

"Davan says you have an object," Renner said. "Davan says a number of things. I'd like to hear what you say."

"I have an object," Maren said.

Renner's mouth pressed into something almost approving and almost impatient — the mouth of someone who had been used primarily for precision and had lost the knack of signaling anything softer.

"He also says you're hollow."

The word landed on the table between them. Not thrown — set down, and left there. Maren noted the reactions without turning her head: Asha's hands shifted in her lap. Corvel looked at the object in front of him, then back at Maren. The young cartographer's pen stopped moving. Edvar did nothing, which told her Edvar already knew.

"Yes," Maren said.

"How long?"

"Always. I'm unchanged."

"And you've been concealing it for —"

"Three years."

Renner processed this — the slight narrowing of focus as she fitted the new piece into an existing framework and discovered the framework needed to flex. "Three years in a city that tests for the Chrysalis at employment, at housing registration, at —"

"A hamlet. The testing was at the Rite, once a year, by a deacon from the city."

"How?"

"I'm very good at not being tested."

Another silence — not evaluative now but recalibrating. Pip looked impressed. Asha looked thoughtful. Renner looked like she was revising an estimate upward.

"Show me the object," Renner said.

Maren didn't reach for it yet. "I want to understand what I'm showing it to."

"Meaning?"

"Meaning Davan told me what this group studies. Founding-era material. The gap between what the bloodline claims and what the evidence supports. I want to know what question you're trying to answer before I show you what I might be the answer to."

The room shifted. Not physically — no one moved — but the quality of attention changed. Renner leaned back in her chair. The wood creaked. "All right," she said, and for the first time her voice carried something other than interrogation. Something that sounded, distantly, like the beginning of respect.

---

Renner talked. She talked the way she apparently always talked — in complete sentences, sourced, with the faint impatience of someone who'd had this argument before and won it every time and was tired of winning because winning hadn't moved anything forward.

The bloodline held that Founding-era objects responded to Chrysalis-touched physiology. That the change written into every person by the moons' influence was also a key, a compatibility layer — a *worthiness*, in the word the temple used. The objects left behind were meant for the changed, and only the changed.

"And the evidence for this," Renner said, "is that every person who has ever successfully operated a Founding-era mechanism has been Chrysalis-touched."

"Which is everyone," Asha said, from her chair. Quiet, but with the timing of someone who'd made this exact point before. "Everyone is Chrysalis-touched. So that evidence is —"

"Circular," Renner said. "Yes. Thank you, Asha, I was getting there."

"You were getting there slowly."

"I was getting there *thoroughly*." Renner turned back to Maren. "No unchanged person has ever been in a position to try. Not part of civic life. Not given access to archives. Not brought into contact with recovered objects. The test case doesn't exist."

"Hasn't existed," Asha corrected. Her eyes were on Maren.

The correction sat there. Renner did not look at Asha. She looked, for a fraction of a second longer than the argument required, at the dark surface of Corvel's container. Maren caught it. Renner was not just arguing the bloodline's epistemology; she was arguing herself. Thirty years of archival work inside the temple. Four years of nights and cooperage meetings after. If the test worked, Renner would not only be vindicated. She would be one of the people who had helped the story hold. The lamplight caught a muscle in her jaw and let it go.

"Four years ago," Renner continued, and her voice was the same voice, but something else moved underneath it now, "I found a margin notation in a pre-crash navigational text. The standard astronomical tables list four major orbital bodies. The notation — in what appears to be a contemporary hand, not a later addition — marks the number as wrong. Changes four to three."

"Which is heresy," Corvel said. It was the first time he'd spoken. His voice was lower than Maren expected, and more measured. A man who weighed words before spending them.

"Which is heresy in a text that predates the concept of heresy by approximately two centuries," Renner said. "The notation also includes a symbol. Small, easily missed." She looked at Maren with the directness of someone who had stopped apologizing for knowing more than everyone else in any given room. "Davan tells me you've seen the same symbol."

"On the disc," Maren said.

"On the disc."

The room held its breath. The collective suspension, the moment where a group that had been arguing in theory encountered the possibility of arguing in practice.

"Describe the symbol," Renner said.

"Four arcs, nested. The outermost one runs off the line the others share — drifts away at the end. It's on the edge of the disc. It matches the inner orbital path on pre-crash charts."

Renner looked at Edvar. Something passed between them — a glance with years of shorthand in it. Edvar nodded, a small motion, almost invisible. Whatever the symbol was, Maren had described it correctly.

Somewhere in the cold floor beneath her boots the stone had started to pull the warmth out of her feet. The tallow smell thickened as the lamps burned down; one wick had begun to gutter.

---

"Can I ask you something directly?" Asha said.

"You've been asking indirectly for the last five minutes," Maren said. "Go ahead."

Craftsperson's directness met something it recognized. "Has the disc ever responded to you? Warmth, vibration, light, anything that suggests active function rather than inert material?"

The room stopped.

Maren considered the question. She considered what answering it honestly would mean — not for her, because she'd already walked down the stairs and sat in the chair and answered *yes* to *are you hollow*, which meant she'd already placed her bet. But for the argument. For the balance of power between Renner's insistence on evidence-before-testing and Asha's insistence that the absence of evidence was itself the evidence.

"It's warm," she said. "It pulses. The frequency has increased since I arrived in New Plymouth."

Nobody spoke.

"It started as once every few days. Now it's several times a day. The symbol on the edge — when it pulses, the symbol is warmer than the rest of the surface."

Corvel looked at his container. The gray surface sat there absorbing light, inert, silent, untouched by every Chrysalis-touched hand that had tried it over the eight years he'd had it. He looked at Maren.

Renner's expression had gone very flat. The flatness of someone whose framework was flexing further than she'd anticipated and who was deciding in real time whether to resist or accommodate. "That's —" she started.

"Reported experience," Asha said. "Not evidence. I know. But it's reported experience from an unchanged person handling Founding-era material, which is exactly the test case you said didn't exist."

"I said *hasn't* existed."

"Past tense. Yes. That's rather the point."

---

Outside, somewhere on the workshop floor above, a stave rack was being loaded; the distant knock of wood on wood came down through the floorboards. The lamps guttered again. Pip got up to trim the wicks, and for a moment the light contracted, pulling the room smaller, pushing the corners further into dark. Maren watched him work. His hands were steady. Dock worker's hands, broad across the palm, but he handled the wick-trimmer with the delicacy of someone who found things through sensitivity rather than force. The light steadied, expanded, reached the table again.

The disc was in her coat, in the inner pocket, where it had been since she'd fled north. She could feel it against her ribs — the warmth, the faint rhythm, the pulse that matched nothing she could identify. Not her heartbeat. Not her breathing. Something else, on a schedule of its own, and quickening.

Her father: he had carried this disc. He had gone east. He had not come back.

Vane at the gate, leaning against it in the dark with her arms crossed and her voice stripped to its operational register. *They need more information before heading east. We need to know what this group knows. But we're running out of room, Maren. Horst is in the city.*

Ulla's letter, her father's words filtered through Ulla's handwriting: the hill in the eastern highlands. Something there. Something the disc was for.
