Edvar got down stiffly from the bench and rolled his shoulders. Aldric held the horses' heads while they drank. Davan sat on a stone at the edge of the road and took a piece of bread out of his coat pocket and broke it in half and held out a half.

Maren sat beside him and took the bread.

She bit into it. It tasted of the oven and of an hour that had passed without her in it.

Her father had carried bread east five years ago.

She caught the thought and set it aside. Not away. Aside. She had been doing this with the thought of her father all morning — let the thought come, set it aside, come back to what was in front of her: the bread, the shape of Vane ten paces up the road keeping a sightline east, Aldric murmuring to a horse, Davan eating beside her with his skin-collar at the steady low blue he had been at since the boatyard.

Davan did not ask how she was.

She appreciated that.

She ate her bread. When she had swallowed the last bite she wiped her fingers on the edge of her coat and stood up, because the cart had finished at the trough and the afternoon's walk had started.

---

The disc pulsed.

An hour into the afternoon, on a stretch of road where pasture had given over to upland heath and the wind had come up steady out of the north. She was walking with her hand inside her coat, a habit she had started in the city and had not unmade, her knuckle against the inside pocket where the disc sat.

The pulse came.

Not a jump in temperature. A kind of *arriving* of the warmth — the disc remembering, briefly, that it was a warm object, then forgetting again. Through her knuckle, up her wrist, gone before she would have been able to say aloud that it had happened.

She did not take her hand out. She looked at the road.

*Two pulses today,* she thought. *First was in the dark before the bell. This is the second.*

It was more than yesterday, and yesterday more than the day before. The first had been at the cooperage. The line climbed.

She catalogued the frequency and did not allow herself to draw a conclusion from it. Drawing a conclusion from it was work for a night with a fire in front of her and nothing to decide in the next hour. Right now she was walking east. The disc was walking with her. Both of them were warmer than they had been yesterday. That was the fact.

She walked.

---

Vane came up beside her twenty minutes later.

Not on the cart side. On the road side, at her right elbow, the position Vane took when she wanted to say a thing without the cart hearing. The two of them walked in step for a moment.

"How often," Vane said.

Maren did not pretend.

"Twice today. Both times it's been warmer than before."

"Inside."

"Under my knuckle."

Vane nodded once. "Through the coat?"

"Through the coat. Not through the coat to anyone else. Me only. Knuckle only."

"Good."

Vane did not ask anything else. She walked beside Maren for a few more paces and then let her pace drop and let the cart catch her and passed around the back of it and came up on the other side by Davan. Davan, for his part, had gone still on the road side — which was, Maren saw after the fact, a stillness in response to something his body had read and hers had not. The disc had pulsed, and Davan had felt the pulse as a weather he did not have a name for.

He did not look at her. He did not speak.

His skin at the collar had gone from the low steady blue to a low steady green.

She was not meant to see it. She had seen it anyway. The seeing went aside, with the thought of her father, for later.

---

Aldric fell in beside her in the last hour before dusk.

He had been walking ahead, beside his horse, not mounted. He had not mounted the bay all day. Deference, she had assumed — a Keeper of thirty years did not enter a group of four people who were walking by mounting his horse to the head of the line. He had been walking his horse the way Edvar was driving his cart: at the pace the group set.

Now he slowed until the cart caught him, and he let the cart pass, and he matched her stride.

For the space of two hundred yards he said nothing.

Maren did not look at him. She had learned in the cooperage basement that Aldric's silences were working silences, and the working was not hers to interrupt. She walked.

At the two-hundredth yard he spoke.

"I have been counting," he said.

The sentence arrived the way his sentences did — formally, with the first word doing more work than most people asked of a first word. She had heard him speak two nights ago at the cooperage for the first time. She had catalogued his cadence then. It was with her now. She recognized the opening move.

"Counting what," she said.

"Moons."

"Three."

"Yes."

He walked. He was not looking at the sky. The sky above the heath had begun to do the thing it did at this hour at this season — a high clear winter-blue flattening toward the horizon, and in the east the clear pale edge of a moon not yet risen but gathering.

"My counting has been a professional occupation of mine for two months," Aldric said. "I have been counting what my office taught me was not countable. I have not found the counting useful. I mention this only because I am not going to count aloud while we walk, and I wanted you to know it is not because I am not counting."

"All right."

The two words. She said them the way she said them in rooms where something had settled at depth. Whether something had settled at depth in this exchange she would find out later.

Aldric walked with her for another hundred yards, not counting aloud. Then he fell back to the cart and took a word with Edvar about the bay's shoe, and the cart's pace adjusted a degree, and Maren understood without being told that he had come up to do a piece of work and had done it and had left. The work was arrival. He had needed one sentence with her on a road in daylight that did not involve an object on a table.

She filed it. She walked.

---

They camped at dusk in a sheep-fold Edvar had known about — a dry-stone enclosure in the lee of a rise, three walls standing and one fallen, a dry trough at the open end. A stand of stunted hawthorns inside the ring, branches bare and north-bent. No sheep in it for a season.

Edvar drew the cart up to the fallen wall. Aldric tethered the horses at the windward side of the hawthorns. Davan came back from the stream with two water-skins. Vane walked the perimeter once, stood a long moment on a rise a hundred paces beyond the fold, and came back in without reporting what she had seen.

Maren laid the fire.

She had done this often enough on the road north that her hands did it without her supervision. Tinder. Sticks. A lean of larger ones. Steel against flint. She built it small. The fold walls would hold the light in, but the light of a fire carried at night in country like this, and Vane had not spoken but Maren had understood.

The five of them gathered around it.

Edvar on the stone trough with his knees up like a man half his age. Aldric on a folded blanket with his back against the dry-stone wall. Davan cross-legged on the ground, his back to the open side of the fold, keeping the lookout position without being asked. Maren opposite him with the fire between them, her shoulders against the stand of hawthorns. Vane at the end of the fire on the windward side, closer to the flame than the others, her coat wrapped around her shoulders and her hands inside the cuffs.

Vane kept moving closer to the fire. Maren did not comment.

---

They did not talk much.

Edvar sighed once, the sigh of a man at the end of a day he had agreed to take on and had not regretted yet but might. Aldric took out a small book from his coat — not a journal; an old traveler's almanac — and opened it on his knee and did not read it. The book was a prop that allowed him to keep his hands occupied while his thoughts did the work his hands were not required for.

Davan carved.

He had a piece of pale wood between his hands that he had picked up at the trough stop and had not shown to her at the time. It was hawthorn, cut clean. His knife was small and steady. He carved without watching what he carved. He had done this on the road north, she knew — had carved something of her before he had ever seen her, a pine figure with one arm held close to the body. The carving she had held in the cooperage yard and had said nothing about for a long time.

She did not know what he was carving now. She did not ask. The steady small sound of the blade on wood was, in the fold, a warmer sound than the fire.

Vane was quiet.

She was closer to the fire than was useful, and her hands inside her cuffs were shaking finely at the fingertips — Maren could see it in the motion of the cuff-fabric. Vane's eyes were open, on the fire. Her jaw was set the way a jaw set when a body was running a small internal current and the jaw was one of the places the current surfaced.

Maren did not go over. Going over would have been a gesture, and Vane's body was not asking for a gesture. Maren had watched her mother work through things like this — not withdrawal, her mother had had other demons, but the same shape. A body working through something alone in company. The company mattered. To her mother, the alone had mattered more.

Maren laid another stick on the fire. She set it carefully on the leeward side so the fire wouldn't flare. The flame took it.

---

After a while Aldric closed the almanac.

"I will take the first watch," he said. "If the group will have it."

He did not look at Vane when he said it. He did not look at Maren. He had pitched the sentence to the fire and let the sentence do its own choosing of whom it belonged to. A Keeper's move — and also, the shape of it arriving a beat late, the move of a man in a group in which nobody would delegate to him, who was finding his footing by the oldest move he had: volunteer for a duty before anyone asked him to take it.

He was not trying to lead. He was trying not to be waited on. The effort of it was audible in the sentence.

Vane did not look up from the fire.

"Wake me at second watch," she said. Her voice came out even. The thing in her hands did not show in the voice. "I'll take the dead hour before dawn."

"The dead hour is hard," Aldric said.

"I know."

"I will wake you."

Davan did not volunteer. Maren did not volunteer. Edvar, already, through the silence that followed, had begun to breathe the slow evening breath of a man asleep against a wall.

That was the first watch sorted.

The second watch, Maren understood, was Vane's claim on doing work she could still do. Maren had been making claims like that on the road north, in the days after the ditch, when her ankle had been bad and she had wanted to walk the second shift to prove to herself she could. The claim was not about the watch. It was about not becoming, yet, the person who was being worked around.

Maren filed the parallel. She did not extend it aloud.

---

Before she slept she looked at the sky once.

The three moons were up.

The Steady was low over the northern ridge, pale as milk-water. The rust-tinted one was higher, east and smaller. The fastest had come over the shoulder of the rise in the last hour and stood near the zenith, small and hard and bright. Three. At their stations. Doing whatever it was moons did, above a country that had stopped asking.

She looked at them without counting.

She had been avoiding the moons for three years, since the first Rite she'd faked her way through — eyes low, hood up, attention on the ground. A habit. Avoidance was always a habit eventually. Whether tonight she was breaking it or only putting it down for a minute to rest her neck she did not know.

The disc, under her hand through the coat, pulsed once.

Her father had walked under these moons five years ago and had not come back. He had carried this disc and left it behind for her to carry after him, and she had carried it three years thinking she was carrying grief, and then she had sat in a basement and understood she was carrying a test, and now she was on a road east, first day of five, with a cart and a trunk and a Keeper and a hunter in withdrawal and a woodcarver who had read her before she knew she had been read, and the disc under her hand was warmer than yesterday, and the warmer-than-yesterday was not a sentence she had finished yet.

She was not walking toward a grave. She had stopped walking toward a grave at the cooperage. What she was walking toward she did not yet know. She had thought in the basement that she would arrive at a shape for it before she left the city. She had not. It was still arriving. She did not get to hurry it.

*All right,* she thought. Not said. The two words to herself, the private register.

Not all right. Arranged. Put down.

She turned her face from the moons and put her head against the pack she had rolled up for a pillow. The fire was low. Aldric was at the wall on first watch, almanac closed on his knee, eyes on the open side of the fold. Vane was on the far side of the fire with her hands in her cuffs and her jaw set. Davan was already asleep, the hawthorn piece finished in his palm.

Edvar snored once and settled.

Four pulses.

She had counted the fourth without meaning to. It had come in the last breath before sleep, under her hand, against her ribs. Not loud. Not urgent. Only, every few hours, the disc remembering itself a degree further than it had remembered itself yesterday, and she the one carrying the rememberer, and the road five days long, and she had come one day.

She slept.

---


## Chapter Twenty-Four — *[Davan]*
### The Country Changes

---

The fold was colder at first light than it had been in the night — the way stone cold went, getting further into itself in the small hours and taking until the sun cleared the rise to admit it was morning. Davan woke before anyone but Vane. Vane was up already, a shape against the dry-stone wall where she had been on second watch, her hands inside her cuffs the way she had held them at the fire.

She did not look at him when his eyes opened. She was making a short efficient motion at her wrist with her other hand — tucking a sleeve up over a bit of something that was nobody's business but hers — and she finished the motion before she registered him. The sleeve came down. Her hands went back inside the cuffs.

He rolled his blanket. He stood up. His shoulders knew the shape of sleep on cold ground and complained of it without making much of the complaint.

Across the fold, Aldric was crouched by the ash of the fire, testing with a fingertip whether the coals would come back under a handful of dry grass. They would. He had put grass ready the night before. Davan watched him work and thought, not for the first time since the basement, that there was a man who knew how to be useful without being in the way.

---

They broke camp in the old-motion manner of a party that had not been one long enough to have bad habits yet.

Edvar harnessed the horses, one shoulder into the off-side leather because his left was stiff from the bench the day before. Aldric rolled Maren's tarp with her while she stowed the fire stones. Vane walked the perimeter of the fold once, then once again on a line a hundred paces further out. Davan stowed the water-skins. He put his hand against the wheel-hub as he passed the cart, because the touch told him whether the iron was warm or cold, and cold was the right answer at this hour.

He caught himself at it. He did not name what the catching felt like. It was too early in the day to be naming anything.

The country east of the fold began as upland pasture and did not stay upland pasture for long. By the time the cart was moving it was already a different country.

---

He walked at Edvar's off-wheel for the first hour.

They had fallen into the same loose line as the day before — Vane ahead on foot, Aldric and the bay just behind her, the cart in the middle, Maren and Davan at its wheels.

The road climbed gently. Stone broke through the grass in bigger patches now. The walls along the field margins went from field-clearing walls, wide and rubble-filled, to older walls — narrower, tighter-fitted, built by hands that had cared about the fit and had not been in a hurry.

He ran a finger along one of them at a gate as they passed. The stones were cold. The cold went into his fingertip and did not travel. Old stone did not give warmth back. It took warmth away slowly and politely and handed it down into the earth.

He walked on.

Maybe it was —

He did not finish the thought. It was too early. The shape had been under his fingertip for the length of a touch, no longer.

---

By mid-morning the pasture was behind them.

The country opened out into the beginning of the eastern highlands. Not yet heath. An upland of coarse tussock and bracken gone copper with the season, grass combed in long pale sheets by the wind, rock breaking the surface in slow irregular swells — bedrock, not scattered boulders, the land's own bones coming up through the thin soil that covered them.

And the contour lines thickening.

Not in any map-sense. He had not looked at a map this morning. He meant it in his body. The rises were closer together, and the ground under his boots was not field-ground any more — it was the long muscled shoulder of a country shaped by something larger than weather.

The wind came off the ridge ahead and brought no city in it. No fish. No coal-smoke. Only dry grass, cold rock, and far underneath all of it a note he did not know how to place — not a smell, exactly. A register.

His skin at the wrists did the slow thing it did when he had not named what his skin was doing. The collar of his shirt had opened with the stride, and the skin between the collar and his jaw had a greenish cast. Not the green-gold of the night in Edvar's back room. A quieter shade. Close to the skin. Not reaching for anything.

He did not cover it. Covering made it worse. He walked on at the pace of the cart.

---

At the first short stop — the road bending around the foot of a tor and opening a long sightline east for Vane to stand in — Davan went to Maren.

The cart had been between them most of the morning. He had not minded. There was a working rhythm to their not being in each other's pockets that had been established in New Plymouth, and he had no interest in breaking it. He came over now because she was holding her hand inside her coat again, and he had been watching, without meaning to, the way the hand went in and out of the coat across the morning.

She turned her face toward him as he stopped at her elbow. Her eyes went to his collar, then back to the road.

"Warmer?" he said.

"A bit. More than yesterday."

He nodded. They stood a moment.

"There's something —" Davan said.

He let the word sit. Maren did not ask him to finish it. She was reading his collar for him, because he was evidently not able to read it for himself, and she nodded once, acknowledging what she saw, and did not comment.

"Later," he said.

"All right."

He went back to the off-wheel. Her hand went back into the coat. The cart began to roll. Vane came down off the tor and took her place at the head of the line, and the country opened east, and he walked into it with his skin doing its slow held green and the note underneath.

---

It was not a human note.

He had, for nine months now, been reading emotional residue in rooms — the leavings of bodies that had felt something, the way a room could smell of anger after the angry person had gone, the way the back bench of a chapel held grief soaked into the wood. This was not that. This was older. This was not human at all.

The road was old. The wall-stones along the road were older. The bedrock under the wall-stones was older than either. What he was reading was older than the bedrock — not in a geological sense, because geological sense was not something his body had any instrument for, but in a register sense. The way his proximity-sense had worked in the cooperage with Corvel's container, the way it had worked in Edvar's back room with the face — only bigger now, diffused across the ground, and *under his feet.*

Near noon, he stopped at the verge and put his palm flat on an exposed slab of rock. The rock was cold. The cold went into his palm the way the stone at the wall had. But beneath the cold of the surface, not quite reaching him and not quite failing to, was — the faintest — something the stone did not have on its own account. A carrying. The rock was carrying a thing that was not the rock.

He took his hand away. He walked on.

He did not tell Maren. He would tell her. He was circling. There was a kind of saying that came from the circling, when the circling had gone around enough times that the sentence came out clean, and he was not there yet.

The skin at his wrists stayed green. Low. Steady.

---

They stopped at midday at a place Edvar had picked — a stone outcrop by the verge that served as a table for travelers, a worn groove in its top from generations of packs set down on it. A stream went past below. Vane walked down, drank from her cupped hand, and walked back up without turning her back to the road.

Edvar got down. Aldric unbuckled the bay's brow-band and let her ease her head. Maren set her pack at the foot of the stone. She looked at Davan across it, and she looked at Aldric, and something in her decision-making went quiet in the way he had learned to recognize from the cooperage basement.

She reached into the inside pocket of her coat. She took out the disc. She set it on the stone.

The stone was light gray, pocked from weather, and the disc against it was the dark it was — the dark that had taken the lamplight in Edvar's back room and kept it, the dark that had stopped the lamplight at the cooperage table. In daylight the effect was different. Under lamps the disc had looked like an absence inside a ring of light. Out here it looked like a window cut into the stone into a place the daylight did not reach.

Aldric went still.

Davan had seen him go still in the basement — the hand that had not closed the short distance, the hand that had stopped itself a handspan away. This was the same stillness. It was the stillness of a man whose body had just been shown the thing his body had travelled a thousand miles to come near, and whose body was choosing not to move because moving was not the answer yet.

Maren did not ask. She had asked once, two nights ago. She did not ask twice. She went down to the stream to fill a skin.

Vane, at the upper edge of the outcrop, saw this arrangement and did not comment. She moved thirty paces further up the road, took her stand at a boulder, and watched east. Edvar took a piece of bread out of the cart and broke it, and handed half to Davan without looking at him, and sat on the cart step with his own half and ate slowly.

Aldric crossed to the stone.

He did not kneel. He leaned. His hands went flat against the top of the stone on either side of the disc, not touching it, the way a man's hands went flat against a lectern, and he looked at the disc at the exact distance a man looked at the page on a lectern, and he looked at it for a long time.

Davan had finished his bread before Aldric spoke.

---

"The inner orbit symbol," Aldric said.

His voice was level. It had the precise level of a voice that had held a sentence for a very long time and had decided, now, to let it out in the shape it had been kept in. Davan heard the shape of it — long careful clauses, institutional vocabulary — and underneath the shape he heard the other thing. A second voice under the first. A stumbling in the places where the institutional words wanted to give way.

"I have been counting," Aldric said, "what it describes. For months."

He did not take his eyes off the disc. Edvar stopped chewing. Maren was just coming back up the path from the stream, the skin heavy in her hand, and she saw the shape of the moment and stopped at the edge of it and did not step in.

"The moons," Aldric said. "Three in the sky. The fourth on the disc. Four arcs. Three stations and a — " the word caught. He let the catching stand. " — and a descent." He said the word like a man who had known for weeks it was the right word and had only just now said it aloud. "The fourth arc drifts off. I had taken it for damage in the engraving. I do not take it for damage now."

"It was meant to drift," Davan said quietly.

"Yes."

"Drawn that way on purpose. The symbol is *before.* Before the —"

"Before the descent."

"Yes."

Aldric closed his eyes briefly.

When he opened them he was looking at Maren, not at the disc, and his hands had lifted from the stone and folded themselves at his waist in the small involuntary gesture of a man whose hands had remembered, for a second, that they had been trained to fold. The folding looked awkward on him in plain clothes. He saw that it looked awkward, and he did not unfold, because unfolding would have been another thing.

"I have a thing to tell you," he said. "All of you. That I have been holding." He glanced at Edvar. "Some of it Marrick knows. Some I have not said."

Maren set the waterskin on the stone and sat on her pack. Edvar leaned against the cart wheel with his bread in his hand. Vane did not come in from her boulder. She did not need to. A voice pitched at the stone would reach her, and her eyes would stay east.

Aldric spoke.

---

"There is a chamber," he said, "in the undercroft of the Temple of Confluence. I found it eight weeks before I rode for Greenford."

He said *eight weeks* like a man giving testimony. A scholar gave a duration that way; a priest gave a confession that way; and Aldric was giving it both ways at once.

"Sealed for — I do not know how long. Long. The stone around the door was cut before the temple above it. The door opened on a small room. And in the room were five objects."

He paused.

"Four containers. Smooth. Dark. The same dark as the disc. The same dark as —" he inclined his head minimally toward Edvar, and Edvar nodded minimally back, and the acknowledgment moved between them in the quiet shorthand of men who had spoken some of this already. "— as other objects the group has seen. They stood against the wall in a row like — " he paused again. "Like patient urns."

The phrase had lived in his head for months. He had never said it aloud. Saying it now was itself a small crossing he did not take back.

"And the fifth object was a face."

Maren's hand, at her knee, did the small involuntary reaching he had seen her hand do in the cooperage — a half-inch of motion and then stillness. She brought the hand back onto her thigh.

"Flat," Aldric said. "Dark. The size of a large serving tray. A surface that answered to a hand laid upon it. I laid my hand upon it eight weeks ago and it — " the liturgical word came up into his mouth before he could catch it, and he let it come because it was the word he had, and because they would hear the other word under it " — it *showed me.*"

He was speaking to all of them now.

"An arrangement of bodies. Four of them. Around a world — blue, green, with cloud. A star at the center burning. The three near bodies each with their mass. And the fourth —"

He stopped. He was looking at Davan now. He had heard something, or seen something, that had turned him toward Davan at the word *fourth,* and his pause was the pause of a man who had not expected another voice to be ready.

Davan's skin at the throat was green-gold.

Not pulsing. Sustained. The color it had gone to in Edvar's back room over the face with the lamp burning down by half. He had not felt it arrive. It had arrived while Aldric spoke, at the count of the three, as if his body had been running the count with Aldric and had reached the fourth at the same beat.

He did not cover it.

"Edvar has one too," he heard himself say.

It came out flat. It came out plain. He had been circling it all morning under his ribs, and the circling had come around.

Aldric's expression did not change very much. It adjusted slowly — the look of a man receiving information he had half-expected without letting himself know he expected it.

"A face."

"A face."

"The same kind."

"The same kind."

Aldric breathed out, once, very carefully.

"Show me what yours showed," he said to Davan, and then with a small correcting turn of the head to Edvar. "If you have a way. I know the faces do not travel easily."

"Mine is in the trunk," Edvar said. "The trunk is in the cart. I brought the trunk."

---

They did not open the trunk at the outcrop.

Vane came in from her boulder and said, quietly, without heat, "We are on a road. It is midday. People use this road."

Aldric nodded. Edvar nodded. Maren nodded. The trunk would be opened at camp. The business of the road came back. The horses were watered. The cart moved off. Aldric mounted the bay for the first time all morning — not as a claim on the head of the line, but because Edvar had said, over the cart wheel, that the afternoon was long, and Aldric's knees were older than his pride. Aldric had laughed once, a thin surprised laugh, and had mounted.

They rode and walked east into the afternoon.

---

The trunk was opened at camp.

Camp was a cart-stand, a place used by carters through the highlands for centuries, set into the lee of a stand of ash. The ash trees were bare. Their branches were north-bent, the way the hawthorns at the fold had been. The country had a way of telling you which way the wind lived.

Vane walked the perimeter. Twice. Wider each time. She came in from the second circuit and did not say anything, and her not saying anything meant the same thing as the morning: no one on the back, long sightline, clear.

Edvar brought the trunk down from the cart bed with Davan's help. Aldric held a lamp. Maren sat cross-legged on a blanket against the cart wheel, her coat over her knees, her hand inside her coat where the disc had gone back at noon and had stayed.

Edvar turned the key on the cord inside his shirt.

The face came out of the oiled canvas the way it had come out in the back room — flat, dark, light going in and not coming out. Aldric made a small sound, the sound a man made when his breath caught on a word and let it go without the word coming.

The green-gold Davan had carried through the afternoon had faded into low blue by the time they reached camp. Now, with the face on the canvas in the lamp-light, it came back. Throat. Collarbones. Wrists.

Edvar set the face on the flat of the blanket and looked at Aldric.

"You want to," Edvar said.

"No," Aldric said. "I want Davan to. This is his."

Davan knelt and put his fingers on the surface. The surface lit, cold and pale and precise, spreading from his fingertips, and the image assembled itself in the small circle of lamplight and cold pre-crash glow — the blue-green world, the three near shapes with their masses, and the fourth.

Smaller. Further. Distant.

The distance of the fourth was the thing his body had gone to both times he had seen it — the far-away quality of a point that was almost a shape, something barely resolved, whole at the limit of what the face could render.

Aldric looked at it for a long time.

"Mine showed four," Aldric said at last. "And a burning point. And the world."

"Yes."

"The near three — these three."

"These three."

"And the fourth." He did not finish the sentence. He looked up at Davan. "Yours shows it —"

"— smaller. Further. Distant." Davan said the words as he had said them to Edvar. "That is what I saw."

Aldric nodded.

He did not say what his face had shown him of the fourth. Davan did not ask. The asking was not for tonight. The asking would come — tomorrow, the day after, at some hour the group could do the asking — but tonight the two accounts were on the blanket together and the accounts were enough. Two faces. Two renderings. The fourth shape was present in both, and the how of it could keep until there was light to see it by.

What landed in him, instead, was the other thing.

---

"Brennan's letter," Aldric said. "There was a phrase. *For unchanged hands.*"

Maren did not move. She was listening.

"The surveyor," Aldric said. "Reth. Who walked a formation in the eastern highlands forty years ago — a metallic formation, four hundred meters across, no grain or seam, manufactured. With a recess at its southeastern end. A recess shaped like a coin. With the inner orbit symbol inside it."

He had delivered that sentence in the cooperage. Davan had heard it then. It did different work now. On a road east, under a lamp, over two faces laid on a blanket, the sentence became what it had not been in the basement: *architecture.*

"And Brennan's survey," Aldric said, "of the ground immediately east of the Temple of Confluence. There is a quality to the bedrock there that he — " Aldric reached for a scholar's word and visibly chose another one. "That he has been afraid of. For years. Metallic signatures at depth where metallic signatures should not be. Not the formation Reth walked. A smaller — a scatter. Under the temple. Under the lands adjacent. As though something —"

He stopped. He looked at Davan. He had heard himself approaching a sentence a cartographer had been unable to finish for twenty years, and the hesitation — Davan read it — was not theological. Courtesy, rather.

Davan finished it quietly.

"As though something came apart in the coming down," he said. "And a piece of it went where it went. And another piece of it — pieces of it — went elsewhere."

"Yes."

"The temple is built on a piece of it."

"Above a piece of it. But yes."

The silence after that was the silence at the cooperage table after the disc pulsed, and also not. At the cooperage the silence had been six faces catching one new thing. Here there were four. At the edge of the lamp-circle, where the ring of light failed and the ash-bark began, something moved in the dark — Vane, circling — and the silence extended to the dark and the dark extended to the silence and the country underneath it all carried its carried thing in its cold slow register and said nothing because it had not been asked a question.

Davan's skin at the collarbones stayed green-gold. He did not try to make it do anything else. The country carried what it carried, and his body carried what his body carried, and for a long moment the two of them were simply the same body.

---

Edvar shut the trunk eventually. Slowly, one knee at a time, the old negotiation. The face had been out for perhaps twenty minutes. Enough. The pale glow bled from the canvas seam for a while after, which was why Edvar wrapped the canvas before the wood — the way a man wrapped a candle when he carried it through a draft.

Aldric sat back on his heels. Then he stood and pinched something at his coat that was not a lamp wick and let the fingers fall. He caught the fingers at the fall, this time. He set them flat against his thigh. His face did the small work of a man being honest with his hand about what it had been about to do.

Maren did not comment.

Vane came into the light and sat at the edge of the blanket. She did not look at the trunk. She looked at her hands inside her cuffs.

"You'll want watches," she said. "I'll take first. Aldric second. Davan third."

"The dead hour," Aldric said.

"I'll take the dead hour. I am awake in the dead hour."

Nobody argued with her. Maren looked at Davan. Davan looked back. The look was not an exchange. It was an acknowledgment. There was no sentence any of them had yet that would do the day's work in summary, and they did not reach for the sentence. For now the watch was set, and the face was in the trunk, and the trunk was in the cart, and the cart was in the lee of the ash.

---

He did not sleep for an hour.

He lay on his back with his coat over him and the hawthorn piece from the night before in his hand, and he took the knife out of his belt and carved.

He did not try to see what he was carving. He let his hands work. He had been doing this on the road north; he had been doing it in the docks at lunch; he had been doing it in Edvar's front room in quarter-hours between conversations. His hands would find the shape they found, and then he would look down.

Tonight the shape the hands found was a piece of ground.

Not a shape he had carved before. Not the symbol — the inner orbit symbol had come out of his hands so many times in the last year he could have carved it in the dark and had. This was a different thing. A low slow rise. A shoulder of earth. The top of a thing you would not know was a thing unless you had walked on it. A hill that the woodgrain said was hollow under it — he had gone against the grain with his knife-cut at the top, so the fibre ran straight down inside the shape, and the straight-down of the grain made the top of the rise look like it had something beneath it that was not the rise.

He looked at it in his palm.

He had carved a buried thing.

He had carved it without deciding to, and he had carved it with the note under the country working in his wrists all day. The piece of hawthorn was not the hill east of New Plymouth — the hill was five days and he was on day two — but the shape of what his body had been reading along the road was in his hand now, small, four-fingers long, the wood a pale hawthorn-yellow in the lamp-edge light.

He set it on the blanket beside him.

The green-gold at his collarbones dimmed, at last, to the low steady blue of a body at rest. Across the camp Vane was upright at first watch, her shape against the ash. Maren was already asleep, one hand inside her coat. Edvar had his mouth open in the way of men his age asleep and did not care who saw. Aldric was in his blanket with his eyes closed and was not yet asleep, his hand palm-down on his thigh, the way he had laid it after the disc on the stone.

Davan closed his hand around the carved piece. The hawthorn was cold on one side where the air had reached it and warm on the other side where his palm had been. He kept his palm against the cold side until the cold side was not cold any more, and he held it there past the warming — past the point where holding did anything — because the country under the camp was cold in a way that a palm could not warm, and the hawthorn in his hand was a small thing, and the smallness of it was what he had to give.

He did not finish the thought. He held the hawthorn piece against his ribs under the coat, and the wood went on taking the warmth out of him, slowly, and after a while his hand was on the carved thing and the carved thing was against his heart and the country was under all of it and did not ask anything of him at all.

---


## Chapter Twenty-Five — *[Aldric]*
### The Shape of Old Things

---

The second watch was not the cruelty people said it was.

At the Confluence Aldric had stood the watch before Lauds for thirty years — the hour when the chapel was coldest and the sanctuary lamp needed the longest sight to see to — and he had come to a private understanding with the small hours that they were simply awake. Other hours were fuller. Other hours were more pleasant. But the watch did not pretend to be anything, and a priest could not, after a certain point, tell the difference between the pretence of other hours and their substance, and so the watch had become, in its way, honest.

He sat against the north-bent ash with Edvar's lamp hooded at his feet and watched the open side of the cart-stand through the dark. Vane had taken the first watch and gone to her blanket without speaking; her breathing, after half an hour, had quieted into something that might have been sleep and might only have been the body finishing a day. Maren slept the watchful sleep of a person who had done it in barns. Davan, a man whose body had worked all day at carrying what his wrists had carried, slept without motion. Edvar slept loudly and without apology.

Aldric's hand had reached once, in the first quarter hour, for a wick that was not there. The ember at the lamp had wanted tending in the old way — pinched between thumb and forefinger, the small pastoral trim — and his fingers had come up off his knee and had gone the short distance and had closed on nothing.

He had not caught himself.

His fingers had opened before they closed. Not because he had decided. Because there had been nothing there to close on, and the hand, finding nothing, had come back to his knee without being told.

He had not marked the motion. He had looked at his hand after, on his knee, and the hand had done a thing without his supervision and the thing had been a thing not done.

---

The eastern sky began to think about its morning a long time before it committed to it. Grey at the lip first, then a paler grey, then the thinnest suggestion of colour that was not yet colour. A rook called from some field a mile off and another answered.

The thing the hand had done, which was the not-doing, was a small thing. He noted it.

---

They broke camp in the pale.

Edvar rose stiffly and poked at the coals and found nothing but ash under the stick. "Clean," he said, and the word meant the camp would leave no trace. Vane was out on her perimeter circuit before Aldric had found his second boot. On her return she gave Maren half a shake of the head, no sentence under it — nothing on the back — and went on with her pack.

Aldric put a hand against the bay's warm neck as he cinched. She leaned her head into his shoulder briefly and he let her. He tightened the girth a hole. The hole was new. Two days on the road, two camps, and the weight that had always gone first on an old horse in autumn was going first on her now.

Davan came past him with the water-skins. The young man's skin at the open collar was pale, the ghost of last night's green-gold still in it the way a stain stayed in a cloth after washing. He nodded to Aldric. Aldric nodded back. Neither of them had spoken since the lamp had gone down over the blanket.

The thing on the blanket was in the trunk in the cart. The thing in Aldric's chest was not yet in any container he had words for, and the morning was not going to put it in one. The cart was harnessed. The fold was clean. He mounted the bay.

---

The road took them out of the ash-lee in a long climb.

They were at the foot of Irongate country. The name had always sounded larger than the place. *Irongate* suggested an outpost, a wall, a border-fort holding a line. The reality, as Brennan had told him on the ride east to Greenford, was that Irongate referred not to a construction but to a shape of country — a long narrow gap between two shoulders of high bedrock, running east-northeast, a gate carved by glaciers before there were men to call any of it by any name. The iron in the name was the iron in the rock, a rust-red seam along the north shoulder that bled down into the scree. No fort. No wall. Only the country, narrowed by its own geology, insisting on a line of passage.

They climbed into it through the morning.

The ground changed as they climbed. The coarse tussock of yesterday's upland gave way to shorter grass with more stone in it — heather-ends brown as the year turned, bedrock breaking the turf in wide bare patches, the horizon coming closer on either side and further away in front. Sheep thinned. A single hawk rode the updraft at the edge of the north shoulder and did not cry.

He had been trained to note the change in country the way a geographer noted it, because a geographer was one of the things he had been trained to be among the others, and he found, after a while, that he was also noting it in another register — the way stone *carried,* the way Davan had named it last night at the outcrop without naming it. He did not have Davan's instrument. His instrument was older and clumsier and had been trained on a different sort of ground. But it was not, he thought, a different question.

---

Midmorning they stopped at a bend where a small spring came up through mossed stone and ran across the verge. Vane drank first, cupped-hand, back to the road. The bay drank like a horse that had been thinking about it for an hour. Edvar loosed the cart-harness and rolled his shoulder.

Aldric dismounted. His knees were stiff. He had spent too many hours at a lectern and not enough on a horse, and a man found out what his body had been doing for thirty years when he asked it to do something else for days running.

Maren came up to the spring with her water-skin. She filled it carefully, hand inside the mouth to keep the canvas from taking in moss. When she stood, the disc under her coat pulsed — he could not see the pulse, of course, but he saw its consequence: her free hand went briefly inside the coat at the inner pocket, and her breath went out in the short practical breath of a person registering a weather and moving past it. She saw him watching. She did not pretend she had not.

"More than yesterday," she said. Quietly. Not to him, exactly — pitched at the spring, for anyone at the spring who needed it. "And the warmth keeps up."

She capped the skin.

He nodded. He had no answer to give her — he did not yet know the question well enough to answer it — but the exchange was its own kind of work, and he did not want her to think her report had reached a room without a receiver. He was the receiver the institution had trained up. He would receive.

Vane went further up the road and stood at a knuckle of rock thirty paces on and looked east under her hand. Her back was to them. Her shoulders were set low, the way shoulders set when a body was carrying a thing and deciding to carry it quietly. He did not look at her longer than a stranger at a spring would.

Davan, at the cart-step, offered a piece of dried apple. Aldric took it.

"Thank you."

"The bay work out the stiffness?"

"She does. I don't, at quite the same rate."

Davan's response was a small motion of the head that did not commit to being anything. The apple was sharp and thin and very good.

The disc, under Maren's coat across the cart, did whatever it did. Aldric was not close enough for anything to reach him. Davan, beside him, was close enough; and Davan's skin at the wrist took on a shade of the green and let it go again, like a word said once under the breath and not repeated. Aldric did not ask. He had learned to accept the half-answers of a man working a long question. He gave the apple-stem to the bay, who took it with the dignity of an old horse accepting a sweet.

They moved on.

---

By early afternoon the shoulders of rock had closed in.

The road went along the north side of the gap, along a ledge cut by carters over generations and deepened by rain until it was less a road than a long trough of stone. The south shoulder stood across the gap at half a mile's distance, and the distance did not vary for an hour — the country had found its shape and committed to it. A stream ran at the bottom of the gap, small and loud, and the sound kept up under the cart-wheels the way a low note kept up under a hymn.

Aldric rode at Edvar's off-wheel. He had spoken perhaps five sentences since the spring. He had been thinking instead — the way he had been trained to think at a lectern, long architected clauses, the scholarly way — and he had been thinking it on top of another thing the scholarly way did not quite have a handle on.

The second thing was this:

The temple at the Confluence had been built over a piece of the lander.

He had been carrying this since the lamp-circle last night, since Davan had finished his sentence for him with the proper courtesy of a man who had been finishing his own unfinished sentences for a long time. *The temple is built on a piece of it.* *Above a piece of it. But yes.* He had said *but yes* because the preposition mattered. The temple was not *on* it the way a building was *on* a foundation. The temple was *above* it. Placed to be over.

The distinction had been scholarly when he made it.

Riding east in the iron gap, under a sky that had got larger, it was not scholarly any more.

He had spent thirty years of careful honesty inside an institution that had been built, stone on stone, above a fragment of the thing he was now riding east to find. Every morning of those thirty years he had gone down to the chapel at Lauds and walked an aisle of floor-stones laid above undercroft-stones laid above whatever else lay below the undercroft — and the undercroft, he now knew, was not the bottom. The chamber he had opened two months ago was not the *foundation* of the temple. It was a place cut into the side of a buried thing, and the temple had been put over it. His predecessors, in some century he had not located yet, had understood well enough what they were doing to do it deliberately.

They had not hidden the face in the temple.

They had built the temple to hide the face.

He let the sentence arrive. It arrived slowly, with a settling, the way a vault-stone settled into a vault. He held the reins and let the bay take her pace and let the sentence stand.

His hands on the reins did not fold.

---

They stopped where the north shoulder came down in a tumble and gave the road a kind of shelter. A dry-stone fold older than yesterday's, three walls and most of a fourth. Vane had walked the shelter before they committed to it. She came back with a stone in her hand and turned it over once before setting it down on another stone, and the turning was all the approval anyone was going to get.

Edvar unhitched. The horses cropped the short turf. Maren laid a fire small enough to hide in the lee of the wall. Davan filled the kettle. Aldric unsaddled the bay and tethered her at the leeward wall and rubbed her down with a folded sack.

They were an hour past the eastern edge of Irongate country now, camped on heath that ran russet and wide around them, broken bones of other folds scattered at intervals across it. The road behind climbed back toward the gap they had come through. Ahead it went on east toward a darker line in the far distance — a further ridge, or the beginning of the coast weather a day ahead.

Vane stood at the mouth of the fold for a long time looking back. Not forward. Back. West, the way they had come. He knew what she was looking at. He did not ask.

---

Davan opened the trunk at dusk.

Edvar produced the key from the cord and passed it to him, and Davan unlatched the trunk and lifted out the oiled canvas and laid it on the blanket in the lee of the cart-wheel. Aldric had set a lamp on the stone of the fold wall at the height that would let the light come straight down across the canvas without glare.

The face came out of its wrap.

The undercroft had been the first seeing — his own chamber, his own hand, alone, the candle going down. Last night at the ash-lee had been the second, Davan's hand, Davan's light. This was the third: a fold in Irongate country, a lamp in a wall-niche, four people around a blanket and no stranger at the gate.

Maren sat cross-legged at the head of the blanket. Edvar took the cart-step. Vane was at the mouth of the fold with her back to them and her eyes east. Davan knelt at the blanket's edge and did not put his hand on the face yet.

He looked at Aldric.

"You," Davan said.

Aldric did not answer. He looked at his hand on his knee. The hand had not folded at the waist when he sat down; he had caught the training in time; he had put his hands flat on his thighs as he had learned to put them flat. The hand that Davan was offering the first touch to — his right, the one that had reached a thousand times for a wick that was not there — was not trembling. It did not reach. It sat where he had put it.

"Together," Aldric said, after a moment. "If you will."

Davan nodded.

They laid their palms against the surface at the same time.

The face lit.

Pale. Cold. Familiar to Aldric in a way he had been trying not to feel, because the familiarity was not nostalgia — it was *return,* and he had not wanted to begin this work from a register that could be made sentimental. He breathed through the familiarity. The image assembled.

The blue-green world, first. Then the burning point at centre — not the sun, he corrected himself even as the old word arrived, *the star of this system,* because the old word meant a different set of things than the accurate word and he was past a point in his life where the old set could be allowed to do the work of the new — the star. Then the arrangement.

He counted.

One. Two. Three.

Four.

The same count. The same arrangement in the near three. The same drift in the outer one. It had been the inner three of his undercroft count and it was the inner three of this face's count, and the precision of the matching was one of the small cold mercies Aldric had been granted in the past two months — that he was not mistaken, that the data were the data, that somewhere above or between them there was a system that had been keeping a faithful record and was, through these small dark surfaces, still keeping it.

The fourth shape rendered in the lamp-light.

It was smaller than his had been.

Not by much. A hand-breadth's worth of smaller, if one were looking at the two faces side by side. The shape was roughly the same — a rounded irregular body, not circular, with an extension on one limb that resolved when Aldric leaned closer as a — a *jag*; an angle of broken profile; the asymmetry of a thing whose outline was not the outline a maker would have chosen for it.

"Smaller," Davan said, quietly, his voice very level. "Further. Distant. As I saw it last night."

"Yes."

Aldric did not lift his hand from the surface. He looked at Davan. Davan was looking at the display.

"Mine," Aldric said, "showed it larger. Closer. Or — " he stopped, because the word *closer* had arrived before the accurate word, and he wanted the accurate word. "Not closer. The same. As if from a nearer position. The same object. Nearer. And — " the liturgical word was trying to come up to meet the scientific one, and he let them come together this time; he was tired of keeping them in different rooms " — and broken. Damaged. The jag, larger. The asymmetry legible. From my face it looked as if something had happened to it recently. From yours it looks like a point at the limit of what the instrument can still see."

Davan looked up.

"Same object," Davan said.

"Same object."

"Different where the face is."

"Yes."

---

They did not speak for a long moment.

The lamp did its small work in the wall-niche. The kettle, at the other fire in the lee of the wall, had gone off the boil and was ticking down. Edvar had not moved on the cart-step. Maren had not moved on the blanket. Vane, at the fold's mouth, had turned her head a fraction — not to look but to hear better — and had turned it back.

Aldric understood the shape of the silence.

It was, he thought, the silence of two scholars who had found the same volume in two different libraries and had laid the copies side by side for the first time and had discovered that the annotations differed in the way annotations differed when the marginal hand had been writing from different rooms at different times. Not a contradiction. Not an error. A *position.* The two faces had been in two positions when they had recorded, and the positions had produced two accounts, and the two accounts together — this was the piece landing in him now — the two accounts together were more information than either alone, because the difference between them was itself data.

The faces had not been the same face.

They had been two instruments recording the same event from two vantages. The vantages had been different because the *instruments* had been in different places when they recorded, and the instruments had been in different places because the debris field was wider than either face could, on its own, see.

"The debris field," he said, aloud, because the word had to come out for the sentence to become the sentence. "The debris field is not the thing we've been looking at. The debris field is wider than either of these surfaces alone could tell us."

Davan said: "Yes."

"A face at the Confluence. A face in a collapsed building in your mapmaker's part of the city. Corvel's container in the marshland south of the ridge. Reth's formation in the highlands ahead of us. Brennan's metallic signatures under the Confluence itself. None of these is the same piece. They are all — "

"Pieces," Davan said.

"Pieces."

Aldric lifted his hand from the face. The image held a beat after his hand lifted — these surfaces did that, he had learned, they held an activation briefly after contact ended — and then it began to fade, slowly, the blue-green world going first, the star going last, the four shapes all dimming together. Davan did not lift his hand. He watched the fade. When the face had gone dark he lifted his hand too and set it, palm down, on his thigh.

Maren, at the head of the blanket, spoke for the first time since they had laid the face down.

"How wide," she said.

Aldric looked at her. She was not asking him specifically. She was asking the air above the blanket, and the air was, at this moment, a room the four of them were in together. But the question was his to answer more than the others', and he answered it.

"I don't know."

"Guess."

He considered. He considered Brennan's scatter under the temple — small signatures, a scatter, the sort of thing that resolved under a surveyor's instruments as *debris*, not as *formation.* He considered Reth's formation in the highlands — four hundred meters, a continuous manufactured surface. He considered the marsh. He considered Edvar's face in a collapsed building that had once been a house in the old quarter of a port city that was, on the oldest maps, not there at all.

"At least four separate sites. That is not a guess. That is four confirmed. The field is the distance between the furthest two sites we know about. Confluence to the highlands. That is — sixty miles. A little more."

"Sixty miles," Maren said.

"The places I can confirm. There will be others."

"How many others."

"I don't know."

She nodded, once, and did not push. She was a person, he had come to recognize, who understood when a question had reached the edge of its answer and did not pretend by asking again that the edge could be moved.

The lamp did its work. The canvas, dark, lay on the blanket. The face, under the canvas, was dark under it, the way a hearth-stone was dark under a wrapped cloth.

Edvar shifted on the cart-step. Vane, at the mouth of the fold, took a breath in and let it out and did not turn.

Davan wrapped the face in the canvas, slowly, with the care Aldric had seen Edvar use last night with a candle in a draught. Edvar lowered it into the trunk. The trunk closed. The key went back on the cord into Edvar's shirt.

---

Aldric sat a long time at the fire after the trunk was in the cart.

Maren had gone to the mouth of the fold to speak quietly with Vane, the wind and the stream for privacy. Edvar was asleep under the cart-step. Davan was at his blanket with a piece of wood and a knife and had not begun to carve yet. The lamp in the wall-niche was out. A smaller lamp sat on a stone at the fire's edge, the size of a man's fist.

Aldric looked at it.

He had tended lamps for thirty years. Chapel lamps, sanctuary lamps, tallow dips at wayside-chapels on visitation years. His thumb and forefinger carried a dozen small scars where a wick had done what wicks sometimes did. He looked at his hand. It was on his knee. It was not, at this moment, anywhere near the lamp.

He did not reach.

He sat with the not-reaching. The hand did not do its thing. The lamp did its small yellow work and burned its small length of wick.

He had spent thirty years as a man who managed other people's fires. The instinct that had kept his hand at the wick of every lamp in every chapel of a minor provincial order was the instinct of a man who had loved small tended things and had thought that the tending of small things was what his hand had been given him for.

He had been wrong about what his hand had been given him for.

His hand had been given him for the work that was in front of it now, which was not the tending of small lamps. It was the *opening* of what the institution he had served for thirty years had been built expressly to keep closed. The temple had been put over the face to keep the face under a floor. The next Keeper, if he was going to be honest, was going to be the one who took the floor up.

He did not yet know how a man took up a floor he had sworn to walk on. The *how* was tomorrow's, or the week's, or the year's. The *that* was this hour's, and this hour had given it to him, and he accepted it.

He looked at the small lamp a last time. The wick did not need trimming. The wick was doing its work well enough. The wick was not his wick.

He leaned down and blew.

The flame went. The cold came in at the space where the flame had been. The hand he had not used to put it out stayed on his knee, open, palm up, the pads of the thumb and forefinger resting on air.

---


## Chapter Twenty-Six — *[Vane]*
### Withdrawal

---

The dead hour did not begin at an hour. It began at a change in the cold.

She had come onto second watch at the ash-lee with her hands already numb past useful, and she had sat against the north-bent ash and taken her post because taking her post was what she had said she would do. Aldric had gone to his blanket without pressing. She appreciated the not-pressing. She did not want the Keeper's face looking at her face at the moment her face was doing what her face had started to do in the evenings.

*Tremor both hands. Gut off. Hearing misallocated — low frequencies lifted, high ones thin. Temperature off-register, the kind of wrong-warmth that fools you into taking the coat off. Do not take the coat off.*

She kept the coat on.

The cold at the ash-lee was the cold of an upland camp at the third hour — dry cold, a cold whose signature she knew. She had sat out a hundred of these. Her body knew how to read what was the weather and what was the body.

Tonight the ratio was not clean.

---

At some point in the dark she checked the arm.

Left hand under right sleeve, thumb to the back of the right wrist where the grain had been drying since the decision in the city. The grain was drier than it had been at dusk. She ran her thumb along it once, slowly. The flake lifted at the edge. It did not come off under her thumb. She took her thumb off.

*Not looking at it in lamplight. Not tonight.*

She put the sleeve back.

A rook moved in some field a mile off. Nothing nearer. Her nose told her nothing nearer, which was the information.

Her nose. Still hers.

She let the small fact sit. She did not decorate it.

---

First light came without her deciding to let it.

Aldric rose. She registered him by the long motion of a man his age sitting up, two pauses in it. He took the watch without asking and she yielded it without speaking, and she went to her blanket with her coat still on, and her hands inside the cuffs kept up their tremor. She had stopped rating it by severity. Rating by severity required a baseline and the baseline was now yesterday.

She closed her eyes for the length of two breaths. The body took what it could and gave the rest back.

---

Maren was at the cart when Vane stood up.

She was repacking the water-skins. She looked up once, briefly, and her face did a small thing that registered and did not pretend. The face said: *I have already decided not to ask.*

Vane crossed to her pack and drew out the chapped leather cuffs she used in cold weather and pulled them over the backs of her hands. They took the tremor out of the cuff-motion. They did not take the tremor out of anything else. They were cover.

Maren watched her do this without looking at her.

"There's tea," Maren said. "Short kettle. Davan set it."

"All right."

The cup clinked once against her teeth. She set it down before it clinked a second time.

Maren's free hand went inside her coat as she reached for the kettle-handle. Came out. Went back to the kettle. She did not comment and neither did Vane. Vane catalogued it. *Second of the day. First was before the bell.*

---

They broke camp in the pale.

She walked her circuit of the cart-stand. She had walked a hundred of these. The circuit was thirty-four paces and had five breaks in the ground to be registered and two stumps that resolved on the return pass differently than on the out pass, because early light and late light were different sentences.

On the fifth pace she saw it second.

A dew-track across a shelf of stone. Four hours old, probably. A small animal. Not a person. She should have seen it on the out pass. The missing was the information.

*Re-reading the same stretch. Day three symptom per Cade's old chart. Attention fragments. Compensate: extend the circuit, slow the pace.*

She extended the circuit. She slowed the pace.

She came back in with nothing new to report except the wider circuit, and she set that against the report she had not made and went to the cart.

---

The road out of the ash-lee climbed.

She was ahead by ten paces — the position she had been taking since they left the city. It let her set the pace and put her face into the wind, which this morning was cold out of the north-northeast and kept the wrong-sweat from turning on her under the coat.

Aldric rode behind her with the bay, the cart behind him. She knew their positions without turning her head.

She would have known their positions yesterday by smell — bay sweat, Edvar's old oil, Aldric's travel coat, Maren's coat-collar with the rooming-house soap, Davan's warmth. This morning she knew them by pace and by the direction of the wind. Her nose gave her the bay and gave her Davan at distance and the cart-wood faintly and did not give her Maren and did not give her Aldric.

The baseline had contracted.

*Three feet. Two feet on a wind. Down by an order from what it used to be. Not gone. Contracted.*

She walked.

---

They were into the beginning of Irongate country by mid-morning.

The shoulders of rock began closing in. In the last country they had crossed, a follower at the edge of a sightline could be lost in the weave of heath and rock without having to be skilled. In this country, with the sightlines lengthened by the gap, he had to be actively managing distance.

She knew the tactics. She had used them for ten years.

She was working the problem backward before she knew she was working anything.

*If I were following four people and a cart through this country, I would drop behind by three quarters of a mile at the last rise, wait thirty minutes, then close to half a mile and hold. The gap shape forces me closer than I'd prefer. The rust scree on the north shoulder gives me cover at eye level. I'd take the lower line at the stream if I had to.*

She walked another twelve paces before she registered that she had been, for the length of the thought, inside a man's head that was not hers, at the distance that thought described.

Information.

She did not act on it. One inferential step was not confirmation. She needed the wind.

---

The wind shifted at the mid-morning bend.

She had been waiting for it. The gap turned a quarter-compass off north, and the wind that had been in her face came across at her right shoulder, and the wind that had been going nowhere instructive began to take the back of the line and carry it forward to her.

She turned so her left nostril had the wind's work. The bark grain at the right shoulder carried scent poorly — the wrong instrument.

She breathed in and did the old reading.

Dry grass. Sheep-country mud days old. The rust in the north scree. The horses. Edvar. Maren at the off-wheel, Aldric further back, Davan at the near. Nothing new in the party.

Back of the party — the long back, the ground they had crossed this morning.

Nothing.

She waited.

The wind did not hand you a signature on the first breath. It handed you what was there densest. What was thin and professional and managed arrived, if it arrived, on the second or third.

Second breath.

Cold-metal.

Not a flood of it. A thread. The thin carried note of a person who was not panicking — a person holding himself at the flat register of a body doing its work. Cold-metal without the sour edge of fear. The cleaner cold. The cold of a contract man reading his mark.

She had smelled it before, in her own mouth, when she had been the one doing the reading.

*Professional. Male. Singular. Behind us by a distance the wind is carrying him at this shift.*

She did not turn her head. She did not change pace.

*How long.*

She ran the wind backward in her memory. The chart she had been keeping had holes in it now where the entries for the previous nights should have been. But the ford. At the ford the wind had been up from the harbor mouth across her right shoulder the way it was now, and she had stopped on the plank walkway and put her hand in her coat pocket and taken her hand out with nothing in it, and she had registered at the time that she had been about to smell something and had not.

Now the smell was back. The wind had handed her what it had tried to hand her at the ford, when she had been less broken for the handing.

He had been with them since the ford.

She knew this with the precision you knew a thing when the wind delivered it the second time.

---

She did not tell them at the bend.

She walked to the next reasonable stop — a spring coming up through moss where the road took a half-circle around a knuckle of rock. When Maren came to the spring with her skin, Vane stood at her shoulder and took a cup-full and drank first, back to the road, and said at a pitch meant for the spring and no further:

"One behind us."

Maren's skin-filling hand did not pause.

"How far," Maren said.

"Three-quarter mile. Professional. Singular."

"How long."

"Since the ford."

Maren nodded once.

"Aldric," Maren said, not looking up. "At the cart."

Aldric had come down from the bay. He did not come to the spring; he understood by the angle of Maren's head that the conference was not for him to step into. He went to the cart instead and stood at the off-wheel and said something to Edvar, and Edvar went quiet.

Davan, on the near wheel, stopped walking entirely. His eyes came up to Vane's at the spring. His skin at the open collar took on a thin cool amber for the length of a breath and dropped. He had read the spring from ten paces by reading her face.

Her face had done a thing without her. She was not entirely in the driver's seat of her own face today.

---

They regrouped at the cart a minute later.

Vane pitched her voice low. Not private; just short of carrying.

"A man behind us. Professional. One. Cold. Not in a hurry. The wind just gave him to me. He's been with us since the ford."

Aldric absorbed this without his face doing anything. His hand at his coat did not reach for any wick or any fold.

"Not Horst," Aldric said. Not a question.

"Not Horst. Horst is in New Plymouth. This is one of his. Horst stays with a city contract."

"How certain."

"I trained with him three seasons. He works a city."

"Tactics," Maren said.

Vane took a breath. It caught. She let it catch. She took another.

"Three moves. One: we slow. Quarter-pace. A man dragging a tail wants to find out whether we've noticed him, and a quarter-pace is inside the normal error a cart makes over a day. If he holds his distance at quarter-pace, he's following a contract. If he closes, he's looking."

"Two," Davan said.

"We change camp. We were going to camp at the east edge of Irongate. Edvar had a fold picked. We don't camp there. Edvar?"

"There's an older one on the eastern heath. Used to be a shepherd's. Three walls. I've camped it twice."

"We camp there. If he's working from a route, the empty fold costs him an hour. If he's working from sightline, he's on us already and the extra mile is our profit, not his."

"Three," Maren said.

The tremor in Vane's left hand did its small visible work at the fingertip. She did not hide it.

"I do not walk point today. Davan walks point — not to track; just to hold the pace. I walk back of the cart. My nose is worse than yesterday and worse than it will be in a week, but it's better than any of yours by a margin still worth something. The back of the cart is where the wind brings what the wind brings."

"Can you walk back of the cart," Maren said. Flat. Not the softened voice.

"Yes."

"If you can't."

"You'll see it. You'll put me in the cart."

Maren nodded once.

"All right," she said.

---

They moved on.

Vane took her new position at the back of the cart. She walked with one hand on the tailboard and her weight rolling foot to foot the way she had rolled it on night watches where the body's protest had to be managed without reaching the face. The wind kept up across her right shoulder. The cold-metal thread came and went at the wind's intervals, not at rhythm but at wind. Once every fifteen minutes was enough.

He was closing by a few paces an hour. He was doing the sum she had done an hour ago from inside his head, and he was doing it correctly, and the fact of his doing it correctly was what she had needed to know about him.

*Not green. Contract man. Horst's. Not the raw lads. Works highland routes. Reads rock cover. Knows how carters break the day.*

The analysis began to stretch and snag. *Male. Right-handed, probably. Not young.* The next sentence was going to be a category that did not fit the data. She did not reach for it.

He was a man behind her at three-quarters of a mile. She could smell him. That was enough.

Twice in the hour after, at the off-wheel side, Maren's hand went inside her coat and came out again without comment. Vane did not ask. Davan on the bench had stopped speaking to Edvar at the second of them, and his collar had stayed the low held green Vane had seen yesterday at the outcrop. The two of them had their instrument for this and Vane was not it. That was also enough.

---

Around the middle of the afternoon Davan dropped back from point.

He did not come all the way to her. He came to the near wheel for a few minutes, and then drifted toward her without angling his body — a motion she recognized as one of her own, the kind of approach that did not ask a room for permission.

He stopped at the tailboard two paces from her hand.

His skin at the wrist had a pale amber wash to it that was not fear. It was the pale amber of a body carrying a weight whose name it had not decided on.

She pushed the right cuff back a half-inch — habit, she had been doing it all morning without deciding to — and his eye went to the wrist the way an eye went to a thing that had changed since the last time it had been seen. The dark grain there was a degree narrower than it had been at the city. She had not named this to anyone. His eye noted it and came back to her face without his face doing anything about what his eye had seen.

"Water?" he said.

"No."

"Shoulder?"

"No. The shoulder's all right."

He let this pass. He was not going to ask the question he had come to ask. He was going to stand at the tailboard for the time it took him to stand at the tailboard.

He was reading her.

Not the way Maren was reading her — Maren watched the surface shapes. Davan was reading the thing under the chart. The register he worked in was her body doing something without her permission, and his body took the thing on the far end of the register and had to manage what it did on the way in.

"Don't," she said. Quietly. Not a command.

"Don't what."

"Don't keep your skin where other people can see what it's doing on my account. Turn your back to the wind. Turn your wrist in."

A beat. His collar-color dropped a shade. The amber at the wrist stayed.

"I don't know how to turn it in, exactly," he said. "Yet."

"Then stop standing here."

"All right."

He waited two breaths. He took a step back. He turned, slowly, so the wrist was on the lee side of his body, and he walked back up the near wheel to Edvar's bench, and his skin at the wrist — she watched it for a count of ten — dropped the amber back into blue over thirty paces.

It had cost him to stand at the tailboard and have his skin do that at her. It had cost him more than he had been going to say. He had stood there anyway.

She filed it. Not the way she had filed his warmth a week ago. Differently. The entry sat in its slot crooked.

She did not try to re-cut the stone.

---

By late afternoon the gap had opened.

The shoulders of Irongate fell away and the country ran out east in a long heath whose russet the wind had been combing all day. The fold Edvar had picked two nights ago was at the road-verge with three walls and most of a fourth. Vane walked off the cart, stood at the wall's corner for a count of forty, and came back.

"Pass it," she said.

Edvar clucked to the horses. The cart went on.

"He'll find it empty," Aldric said from the saddle.

"He'll find it empty."

They rode on for the better part of another hour. The wind had dropped. The cold-metal thread had dropped with it. Absence of a reading from the wind was not a reading; it was the wind not working. The contract man was still back there. He would not have turned off a cart he had been following since the ford on the afternoon of day three because a fold came up empty. He would take the empty fold as intelligence and press his pace up a quarter until he had relocated them on sightline.

*Which means he closes by more than a few paces an hour after dusk. Unless he has a lamp and is willing to risk it.*

He would not have a lamp. Lamps in open country at night were amateur work. Horst did not hire amateurs.

She kept walking.

---

They camped at the shepherd's fold an hour past dusk.

Edvar put the cart into the lee of the longest wall. Aldric tethered at the windward. Davan took the water-skins. Maren was building the fire — small, in a pocket of wall that held the light in — before Vane had decided whether to sit down.

She sat down eventually, at the leeward wall, with her back against the stone. Her legs went loose in a way that had not been a decision. Her hands shook badly now without the cuff cover.

Maren came across the fold with a tin cup.

She did not offer it. She set it on the flat stone beside Vane's knee.

"Not tea," Maren said. Quietly. "My mother's mother used to boil this down for women who shook in the winter — sweet-flag root, and a piece of ginger, and a pinch of the dry mint I packed. It's not a cure. It's a thing that slows the hands for a half-hour. Drink it or don't."

Vane looked at the cup.

"Hot?"

"Hot enough. Not scalding. You won't chip the cup."

Vane reached for the cup. She needed two hands. She did not hide the needing. She drank it in three small swallows. The sweet-flag hit the back of the throat first, bitter and resinous the way sweet-flag was, and the ginger came after, and the mint was the thing that made it drinkable. The heat went down and sat where the heat sat when it was doing a job.

Her hands stopped shaking about a minute later. Not all at once. They went from the fine fast tremor to a slower coarser one to a series of long pauses between the tremors to nothing. She breathed out a breath she had not been planning to breathe out.

Maren was already on the other side of the fire turning Davan's knife in the coals to heat a rasher for Edvar.

She had made no production of any of it. She had brought the cup, put it on the stone, said what was in it, and gone back to the fire.
