Vane sat with the empty cup in her lap and the half-hour of quiet hands ahead of her and the old professional ledger in her head trying to book the transaction — *gift, received, cost — * and the ledger refused the entry. The ledger had not been built for this kind of transaction. This was not a gift. Maren had named it. *A thing that slows the hands for a half-hour.* The body was a body; the body did what bodies did; you made it a little more bearable and you went on.

Not care. Craft.

She had been braced for care. Care would have been harder to receive. The craft was easier because the craft did not require a gratitude she did not have the coin for tonight.

---

She slept at first watch against her will.

Aldric took the fire. Davan took the wind side. Maren took the space between her and the open side of the fold, which was, she registered as she was going under, a position Maren had taken without its being discussed. She had gone to sleep on the inside of Maren's shoulder-line by the fold wall, and Maren had placed herself between the open and the inside, and Maren's position was the position a professional would have taken.

Maren was not a professional. Maren was a seventeen-year-old from a hamlet who had learned to read rooms because reading rooms had been the only way to survive her own village. She had brought the same instrument to the fold.

*Not a professional,* Vane corrected. *Somebody who has been forced into the adjacent country without the forced part showing.*

She slept.

---

She dreamed of nothing. The nothing was a mercy.

When she came up out of it the fire had gone down and the wind had dropped and the stars were very cold overhead. Aldric was at the wall on watch. Davan was asleep, the hawthorn piece from the night before out of his coat pocket into his hand. Edvar snored. Maren, three paces to her left, slept with one hand inside her coat and her face turned toward Vane a degree more than the fold would have required.

Vane lay on her back for a while.

Her right arm was out from under the blanket. She had not meant to let it be out. The bark grain across her shoulder met the cold air, and the cold went into the grain and did not travel, the way old stone took cold and kept it. The patch at the back of her wrist — unexamined — flaked a little more against the cuff of her coat. She would know it in the morning. She did not look at it.

The edges were receding.

She had not said this to herself in those words. She thought the words now. The grain at the shoulder-edge was a degree narrower than it had been at the city. The grain at the inside of the elbow was starting to give up the inside of the elbow back to skin. The body was not healing. The body was un-feeding a thing it had been feeding. There was a difference — the difference between a tree that stopped being watered and a tree that was being tended back. The tree that was not being watered did not get its old shape back. It got a smaller shape. It lived at the smaller shape if the other conditions held.

She was going to get a smaller shape.

The shape would have her nose in it. Her nose would be the nose she had been born with, sharpened by ten years of use, un-sharpened by four years of drug. Less than what she had been last week. More than what she would have been without the ten years. A working instrument. Not the overclocked one.

She had smelled one professional man at three-quarters of a mile today on a wind that had taken two breaths to give him up.

The instrument worked.

She lay in the cold with her arm out of the blanket and counted the working instruments she had, the way she had used to count kit at the start of a contract.

*Left hand, shaking, usable. Right hand, shaking, usable. Legs, tired, usable. Eyes, tired, good at distance. Ears, off, coming back in a week. Nose, working. Arm, losing bark at the edges. Knife, in pack. Rope, in pack. Lock-tools, in pack. The man at three-quarters of a mile, catalogued. The party, here, sleeping. The disc in the dark-haired woman's coat, across the fold.*

She got to the end of the list.

She started again.

She stopped at *nose, working* and did not go further.

*Nose, working,* she thought. And then, because the ledger was broken in places tonight but was not broken here: *mine.*

She pulled the blanket up over the arm. She closed her eyes. Across the fold, Aldric made the small motion a man made at a wall on watch when the hour changed — a shift of his weight from one hip to the other — and the fold took the sound and kept it and did not give it to the country outside.

Somewhere out in the heath, at a distance she could no longer smell past the fold wall, a contract man was going to bed down cold and wake at first light and press his quarter-pace.

She would be awake before he was.

The tremor in her left hand started up again under the blanket. She did not catalogue it. The cataloguing, tonight, had done its work.

She slept.

---


## Chapter Twenty-Seven — *[Maren]*
### The Hill

---

Day five opened in a light that did not match the day.

The heath behind them had kept the grey overcast of the coast for two days, a weather that did not break and did not deepen. That morning broke. Maren came out from under the hedgerow where she had slept and stood at the cart-pole and looked east and the east had a pale blue edge above the ridge-line, thin and unconvincing, and the sky behind her was still the sky of the coast. Two weathers. A seam between them running north-to-south along a line Maren could not quite place until the wind gave it to her — the wind had changed at some hour while she slept, and was coming now off the east country, dry, with no salt in it.

Dry air off dry land. That was the eastern weather line and they had crossed it overnight.

She catalogued the camp. Cart in the lee of a long stone wall, Edvar still asleep under the bench, the horses cropping at a forage she did not know the name of. Aldric had the last watch and was sitting with his back to an outcrop at the wall's end, the bay tethered at his knee. Davan was at the water-skins. Vane was not visible.

Vane was not visible because Vane had been up and moving since the watch changed, and Vane worked the perimeter by a pattern Maren had stopped trying to track. Vane would be back when Vane was done.

Her hand was inside her coat at the pocket without her asking it to be there.

The disc was warm against the pad of her thumb. Not the faint warmth of the early morning. A steady warmth that had been there when she woke — that had *woken her*, before the light had. She understood this on the second breath. It pulsed once under her thumb and she registered the pulse and took her hand out.

She did not look at the east.

Not yet. The east would be there when she was ready to look at it. She went to the water-skins first, because the water-skins were what you did before the day, and today was a day on which she wanted to have done the things you did before the day.

---

Davan was filling the second skin at the stream.

He looked up when she came. His skin at the open collar carried a sustained green — not the green-gold of the face, but a held lower register, the green he had walked in for two days and had not mentioned. The color at the wrist was deeper this morning. Not fluttering. Set. As if something he had been holding at bay had given up being held and had simply begun to be.

"Morning," she said.

"Morning."

He passed her the full skin without her asking. She took it and shouldered it and stood beside him at the stream. The stream ran clear over a bed of flat pale stones. Small.

"You slept," she said.

"Some."

"You're lit."

"I know."

He capped the second skin. His hands were steady. She watched them because watching his hands was a way of watching him that did not require him to say anything, and this morning he did not look like a person she wanted to ask questions of. He looked like a person carrying a weight with both hands.

"Since when," she said.

"Since the wind changed."

"The wind changed before dawn."

"Since then."

She nodded.

He did not tell her the rest. The rest would be what Davan brought to the cart when Davan had finished circling it, and she had learned to let him circle. She capped her skin. The water was very cold in the bend of her arm.

"Maren."

She looked at him.

His eyes were not on her. They were on the east, above the ridge-line where the pale blue had opened. He did not lift his hand to point. Pointing was not the register. He said her name and he looked east and his collar-color deepened by a shade she had not seen before — not green-gold, not yet; a green with a warmer edge coming into it at the base, the color of a thing beginning to be ready.

"Yes," she said. "I'll look."

She turned.

---

The ridge was ten miles off. Maybe less. The country east of the heath rolled away in a long slow fall to a plain she could not see the bottom of, and beyond the plain the ridge-line went up in the pale unstained blue of the new weather. Forested at the bottom. Bare rock toward the top. A long crest with three or four bumps along it, the kind of ridge-line that resolved at closer distance into separate hills.

Behind the ridge, further in, higher up, there was a hill that was not any of the bumps.

She did not see it immediately. The light came first. The sun was not up yet — the light was the pre-light, the grey-bluing that filled the east before a dry clear morning — and along the ridge the pre-light was picking out the usual edges: rock-face, tree-line, snow in the higher gullies. Beyond the ridge, in what should have been shadow, something caught a light that wasn't there yet.

A pale dull gleam. Not reflection. Not brightness. A not-quite-shadow in a place that ought to have been shadow. The eye moved past it because the eye was looking for something else, and then the eye came back.

She looked.

The hill was long. She could see the length of it from the heath because the ridge in front of it was lower than it was. Long and low-backed and running roughly east-southeast, rising not in a peak but in a long swell that came up from the land and settled back into it without a summit. Her first thought was *esker* — the word Edvar had taught her for the long glacial ridges south of Stillwater — and her second thought was that eskers did not do that with light. An esker took a light the way grass took it. Softly. This took it the way a wet slate did. Dully. Held for a moment. Released.

It was the wrong shape for a hill.

That was the sentence she arrived at on the second look. Not too large. Not too small. *Wrong.* A hill was a thing that had been shaped by water and weather and the slow compression of the weight of the country above it. This thing had been shaped by something else. The proportions were too long for the height. The back was too even. The flanks curved at a rate a hillside did not curve — curved at an even rate, the rate of a thing that had been made at a scale and then buried.

Centuries of growth. Vines and roots and sediment in every hollow. The long overgrowth had given it the texture of a hill and almost the silhouette of one, and from the valley road — Maren understood this without having been on the valley road — from the valley road at any hour but this one, at any angle but this one, at any light but the sideways pre-light of the wrong weather, the thing would read as a hill.

It was not a hill.

She had known this from Reth's report, carried to her through Aldric on the third evening east. Four hundred meters. No seam. No grain. Manufactured surface. She had heard the words and held them in a room in her head where she held the words for things that were real. Seeing the thing was not the same as having the words for it. Seeing the thing was a door opening in the room, and the thing walking through.

Her hand was inside her coat. The disc was humming.

Not pulsing. *Humming.* A low continuous warmth against her palm that had not been there ten minutes ago at the stream. She closed her fingers around it and opened them and closed them again and the hum did not change, did not fall away at the breath between pulses as it had always done. The breath between pulses had closed up. The disc was doing the thing it had been doing more and more often all month, now without stopping.

"All right," she said. Aloud. To no one.

It meant something had settled.

---

Aldric had come down off the outcrop.

He was on the heath ten paces from her with the bay at his shoulder and he was looking east with both hands on the reins in front of him, palms up, the reins laid across the palms the way a man laid a sentence on a table for reading. She had not heard him come. She had not needed to. She stepped across the heath to him and stood at the bay's off-shoulder and did not speak.

He spoke first.

"Four hundred meters lateral extent."

His voice was measured. The voice she had learned from him was the voice he used to count. A Keeper's voice, he had called it once, without making it a joke — the voice of a man trained to receive an account. He was receiving an account now. The account was the shape on the ridge.

"Running east-southeast," he said. "Low at the southwestern end. Rising to a crest at about the midpoint. The crest is not a summit. It holds level for — I can't estimate from here. Then the curve comes down at the southeastern end. Curves *down*. That is the piece Reth marked. The surface goes into the ground there."

"Goes in," she said.

"He said it. *The thing goes into the ground, Keeper. It doesn't just sit on it.* I did not understand at the time what he was describing. I understand now."

She understood too. The long swell on the ridge was not a ridge-top feature. It was a piece of a thing that had come to rest at an angle in the country, the larger part of it buried under the hillside, the smaller part of it showing. A thing lying down in the earth. A thing that had *come down* and the earth had taken most of it, and the part that still showed had gone under grass and vine and tree and had become, for every eye that had looked at it without the words, a hill.

"Reth's recess," she said.

"Southeastern end. Where the curve goes down."

"How far in."

"From the angle and the light — I can't be precise. An hour's walk from the base. Perhaps less if the way is clear."

"It won't be clear."

"No."

He did not lift his hand to the disc in her coat. He did not ask. She offered it anyway, without taking it out. "It's been humming since dawn."

"Humming."

"Not pulses. Continuous."

He took this in. He had put his hand in his pocket for nothing twice in the last hour — the shape of the lamp-tic without the tic, she thought, not a reach but a ghost of one, fading. His hand on the reins did not move.

"We should go," he said. "Before the rest of the day happens to us."

---

They broke camp without speaking.

Edvar was awake by the time she got back to the cart and did not ask why they were packing early; Edvar had learned a great deal in his sixty years about not asking. Davan folded the canvas. Maren rolled the blankets and packed the kettle wet because there was no time for the kettle to dry. Aldric tightened the bay's girth a hole tighter than yesterday. The bay was losing weight.

Vane came back from her circuit as the last strap went down.

She looked at the east and did not stop the way Maren had stopped at first sight of the hill. Maren understood this later; Vane had seen the hill already, had seen it on her perimeter circuit in the pre-light and had walked the rest of the circuit with what she had seen cataloged and put in its proper slot, which was the slot for the thing they had come here for. Vane's eyes came back from the east and went to Maren and did not stay.

"He's closer," Vane said.

"How close."

"Half a mile. Maybe a little more. He pushed pace last night. He knows we've seen the ground ahead."

"He can see it too."

"He's not looking at it the way you're looking at it."

Maren nodded.

"He won't close before the base of the ridge," Vane said. "Professional. He'll stay at half a mile until he has cover. I'll take the back line. Davan point. Aldric the cart. You with the disc."

"Me with the disc."

"That was the job from the ford. It's still the job."

A beat. Vane's left hand at her side was shaking at a rate Maren had not seen it shake before. She did not hide it. She had stopped hiding it three days ago.

"Can you work," Maren said.

"Yes."

"If you can't."

"You'll know. You'll put me in the cart and go on. The way we agreed."

"All right."

Vane turned. She went to the back of the line and took her place without further sentence. Davan took point. The cart rolled.

Edvar on the bench, the reins in his hands. Maren walked at the off-wheel with her hand inside her coat. She did not take the disc out. Taking the disc out was for the place. The place was ahead of them across five miles of heath and two gullies and a rising tree line and the curve of an approach she had seen all her life in dreams her mother had not lived to interpret for her.

The wind was at her back, pushing her into the east.

---

They dropped off the heath an hour before noon.

The country folded. A dry creek bed in a trough of sedge gave the cart half an hour of difficult going; Edvar worked the reins and Davan walked at the near wheel to steady the trunk; the trunk, Maren knew without being told, was the face in its oiled canvas, and the face in its oiled canvas was part of this going-east in a way the earlier going-east had not been. The face at the fold had been evidence. The face in the trunk today was a piece of the ship riding in a cart toward the ship.

She did not think this for long. She did not want to think it for long. There was a register at which the trip became too clean — *the piece returning to the whole* — and the register was not hers. She was a girl walking east with a thing in her coat. The thing was warm. The east was an hour away.

The ridge came up.

They took it where Edvar had picked the line off an older map — Reth's directions through Aldric, layered with Edvar's road-sense, laid onto a track that hunters had used and farmers before them. The track climbed through the tree-line and out the other side onto a shoulder of bare stone. They left the cart at the shoulder. Edvar stayed with it. He had said, half a mile back, *I'll stand the cart*, and nobody had said otherwise, and Maren understood why he had said it — Edvar was not going the rest of the way; Edvar had gone far enough.

She hugged him, briefly, before she went on.

He put his hand on her hair and took it away.

"Go," he said.

"I'll come back."

"You'll come back when you come back."

---

The approach was half an hour.

Davan walked point at a pace that was not his usual pace. He was moving the way he moved when his skin had work to do and his legs were trying to give his skin room for the work. His collar was green now with the warm edge coming up from underneath — not green-gold. Close to it. A green with a live under-register, as if the color itself had a pulse, the pulse running at a rate that did not match his heart.

Aldric came behind her. The bay had been left with the cart. Aldric walked with a scholar's rolling stride, measured, the rhythm of a man who had walked aisles.

Vane was behind Aldric. She had not changed position. The back line.

Maren walked the middle. She walked with one hand at her coat pocket and the disc humming against her palm through wool and the other hand at the knife at her belt, which she did not need and did not take out, because the hand knew how to be there and she let the hand do what the hand did.

The tree-line thinned.

The slope under her boots was the slope of a hillside but it was not the slope of a hillside. It was too steady. Too even. The angle varied within a range a natural slope did not vary within. The ground was softer than it should have been where the growth had pillowed over it, and harder than it should have been where the growth had given way — and where it had given way, under a split of moss knocked loose by some animal, something pale and flat showed through, for the first time, that was not stone.

She did not stop. Davan, ahead, did not stop. He had seen it too. There was seeing and there was stopping, and today they were going to save their stopping for the thing Reth had described.

They climbed.

---

Davan's skin was green-gold by the time they came out onto the shoulder of the hill.

Not sustained the way it had been at Edvar's. Deeper. A green-gold that had a second color under it, the warmer color she had not seen rise to the surface yet, the color she understood without language was not going to surface until the disc was in the recess. His hand at his side was trembling in the way Vane's had trembled at the cart — not from cost, in his case. From the work of holding.

"Slow," he said. Not to her. To himself.

"All right," she said, because she had it to give him.

Aldric came up beside her. His breathing was the breathing of a fifty-three-year-old man who had walked a slope. His hand at his coat was closed on nothing. No tic. The hand was flat against the wool.

He looked along the hillside.

"There," he said.

She looked where he was looking.

At first only the green. The slope of the hill rolled away from them under the dense overgrowth, vines ropey at the ankle, deeper-rooted plants higher up, a thorn-cover in the middle-ground that she would have taken for a briar-patch in any other country. Under the green was the shape of the hill — curved, long, going down and away to the southeast, the land folding to meet it in a way Maren understood now was the land having folded *around* it, the way water folded around a stone set in a stream.

She followed the curve with her eye down to the southeastern end.

Where the slope went down into the country, the overgrowth changed. The thorn was sparser there. The grass ran in lines. Where the grass ran in lines, there were longer lines underneath — longer than any root, more even than any grass, more deliberate than the lines erosion made in the sides of hills. The lines curved.

Three of them first. Three nested arcs, shallow, faint under the grass, following the curve of the hillside's surface as it went down into the ground.

She counted.

Not three. Four. The fourth arc was further out, drifting from the three, the drift she had looked at on her own disc's edge for three years and had thought was a flaw in the casting and had learned, at the cooperage, was the shape the book of the world made when a cartographer had stood far enough back from his own map to see it.

The inner orbit symbol. On the hill.

"Carved," she said, and as she said it she was already correcting herself because it was the wrong word — her mouth had reached for it before her eye had finished looking, and her eye was telling her a thing her mouth had not caught up to.

"No," Aldric said. Quietly.

She looked longer.

The arcs were not cut into the surface. The grass had grown over them. The roots of the larger plants had followed them — ran along the lines, not across them, the way tree roots in an old orchard followed the rows a plowman had cut two centuries ago. The plants were growing along the lines because the lines had been there first. The lines had been there before there was soil on the hill. The soil had settled into the lines because the lines had been an *unevenness* in the surface below the soil, a depression in whatever the hill was made of, a shape pressed into the hull of the thing before there had been any grass on Gaius at all.

The symbol was not on the hill.

The symbol *was* the hill.

Not the whole hill. The piece of the hill that showed. The material of the hull had been marked at its making with the diagram of the mission it had been part of, and the mark had been there before any hand from this country had ever thought of making a mark, and ten thousand years of roots and vines and sediment had grown across the mark and into it and had become, to the eye looking from a valley road in any ordinary light, *a hill.*

Her disc.

The inner orbit symbol on its edge. Three years of tracing it with her thumb without knowing what she was tracing. A shape made at manufacture — one thing to the maker, something else to all the people who had come after. She thought of her father, who had never seen the disc and the hill in the same room, and who had walked east to try to put them in the same room, and who had not.

Her eye went from the symbol on the hillside to the southeastern end and down.

Down to where the curve of the hill went into the ground.

Down to where, in the quieter place below the drifting fourth arc, the cleared ground Reth had described lay — a patch of earth not overgrown, not by accident, where a hand had cleared sediment from the surface a generation ago and left it cleared, and where the surface below it showed.

A disc-shaped recess.

Dark. Small. Coin-sized at this distance, disc-sized when she got to it.

Her breath went out.

She walked toward it.

---

She found him halfway there.

She was not looking for him. She was looking at the recess, and her body was walking under the part of her that was looking, and her foot at the fifth pace came down on something that was not the springy compressed growth of the hillside. It was harder than that. She looked down.

A leather strap. Flat under her boot. Cracked from years of weather, the oil long gone out of it. She lifted her foot. The strap lay against the ground curving slightly — the curve of a thing that had once gone around another thing. A pack-strap. The kind a man took east on a long journey.

She did not call out.

She went slowly down the slope from the strap, three paces, five, her eye tracking what had been the line of the strap before it went under the growth, and at seven paces the growth opened around a scatter of dark objects arranged at distances no object placed itself at.

A coat. Wool. Once grey, now the color weather made. The shoulders of the coat were not in the shape of a body any more; the shoulders of the coat were in the shape of the ground. Everything that had filled the coat five years ago had gone back to the hill at the rate the hill took things back. Inside the coat — she did not look closely into the coat — were the long pale shapes a body reduced to when the country had worked on it for five years. Bones. Not many, where there should have been many. Animals had come. The animals had taken what animals took. The rest had stayed because the coat had kept the rest together while everything else went.

She knew the coat.

She had not let herself remember it on him — she had been twelve when he went east, old enough to have the memory, and had spent the years since not reaching for it. What she had instead was Ulla's description, given the day Ulla gave her the letter — *your father's coat was a soft grey, with dark facings at the cuffs, and a seam along the shoulder where your grandmother mended it after the winter of the deep snow.* The facings at the cuffs were black-weathered almost beyond reading. The seam at the shoulder was a seam.

She went down to her knees beside the coat. She did not touch it.

The satchel was at what had been his hand.

A leather satchel, also gone dark with weather, the strap broken where the hand had held it. She touched the strap. The leather came apart in her fingers, not at the fracture but two inches above it, like old bread. She drew her hand back.

Inside the satchel — she had to lean to see — were three things she could make out without disturbing it. A small bound book wrapped in what had been oiled cloth; the oil was gone and the cloth was not protecting the book any more, but something in the shape of the binding said the book had been kept dry for a long time. A fire-tin with a flint. A stone.

The stone was the size of an egg. Round. River-smoothed. The kind of stone a man picked up because the shape of the stone felt correct under the thumb and the thumb wanted to keep it.

She looked at the stone and did not take it.

She looked at the coat and did not touch it.

She looked along the hand-line — the line of where his hands had lain when he had settled, in whatever last minute of whatever last hour, into the ground here — and she followed the hand-line down the slope with her eyes.

The hand-line pointed at the recess.

He had been facing it. He had gone as far as he had gone, and he had stopped where he had stopped, and he had stopped four paces short of the recess, and he had lain down with his hands out in front of him and his face toward the thing he had come across the country to put the key into. The key had not been with him. The key had been west in a coal-miner's cottage in a hamlet called Ashwick, in a great-aunt's keeping, waiting for a girl of twelve who did not yet know she would be handed it the year her mother died — or what it was, or that her father had walked east to find the lock it fit. The key had been there, and the lock had been here, and the man who had known about both had lain down four paces from the lock with his hands out.

She did not say anything.

She did not weep. The weeping was a country she could not afford today. She took a breath and did not take another, and then she took another anyway because the body did what the body did, and she sat back on her heels in the grass beside what was left of her father and she put her hand inside her coat and she held the disc against her palm and let it hum.

She had been carrying grief. The cooperage had taught her she was carrying a test. The hill taught her what she had not had the words for. She had been carrying both, and both had been true. Her father had died here. Her father had also left her the thing that opened the thing he had died beside.

*All right,* she thought. Not aloud.

She put her hand on the ground beside the coat — not on the coat — and she held it there for the length of one breath. The ground was warmer than the air. She did not know if that was the hillside or the morning sun or the thing she was kneeling beside. She did not look for an explanation.

She took her hand up.

She stood.

---

Aldric had come within a pace and had stopped.

His eyes were on the coat and the hand-line and the recess in one long look that took them all in as a shape. He did not go closer. He had the sense to know that this was not a room he entered.

"Five years," he said. Quietly.

"Five years."

"He knew where it was."

"Yes."

"He had nothing to put in it."

"He had me. He had me in Ashwick and the disc was with me. He came east anyway."

Aldric closed his eyes for a breath. When he opened them he did not look at her, which was a kindness.

"The recess," he said.

"The recess."

She stepped past the coat.

Davan was at the downslope, ten paces on. He had not come closer to the remains. He had not had to — his skin had told him at the distance his skin told him. The green-gold at his throat was no longer green-gold. It was a color Maren could not name. It had the green in it and the gold in it and something else under both, a warm register coming up from the chest and the wrist both, held, not pulsing, the way his skin had held the recognition at the face but deeper and wider and at a register she had not known a body could hold at. He looked like a lamp with a color inside it the lamp-maker had never tried before.

"Davan."

He looked at her. His pupils were wide.

"Can you stand here," she said.

"Yes."

"All right."

She turned to the recess.

---

It was six feet below her. A short clear distance down the slope.

The cleared section was perhaps three paces across. The surface of the hill in that cleared section was not green. It was not grass and not soil. It was the dark material she had spent three months learning the look of — the material of the face, the material of the container, the material of her own disc — but at a scale that was not possible for a thing to be. Not a coin. A continent of the material, extending off under the soil in every direction, visible only here where someone had scraped a window clear. The surface was smooth. Not polished. Smooth the way the disc was smooth, at the scale of a room.

The recess was in the middle of the cleared section.

Circular. Sunk perhaps a quarter-inch into the surface. The size of the disc exactly. On the inner face of the recess — visible as she stepped down into the cleared place and bent, because the angle from above had not been the angle for reading it — was the same symbol. The inner orbit. Four arcs. The fourth drifting off-true.

Not carved into the inner face. Part of it.

She knelt.

The ground at the edge of the cleared section was firm under her knee. The surface of the hill at the edge of the cleared section was cool under her free hand — she put her hand flat against it, not pressing, only resting, and the cool went into her palm and did not travel. It was not stone. It felt like stone. It felt like nothing else she had touched before.

She took her hand off.

She took the disc out of her coat.

It was warm. Almost hot. The hum in her palm had gone past the point where she could call it a hum; it was a fine continuous pressure, a thing-wanting-to-go-somewhere, and she had understood at the cooperage that this was what the disc did when it was near the thing it had been made to meet, but she had not understood *this*. The disc in her palm was a thing asking to leave her palm. She had been a holder for it for three years and she was about to stop being the holder, and the disc knew.

She looked at the symbol on the edge of the disc.

She looked at the symbol on the inner face of the recess.

The same symbol.

"Papa," she said. Very quietly. The old word. She had not used the word since she was twelve. She used it as a small acknowledgement, the way you tapped a door-frame as you went past a room somebody you had loved had slept in. A courtesy between her and the country.

She did not weep.

She held the disc over the recess.

---

The disc fit.

Her hand was steady. She had expected her hand to shake and her hand did not shake, which was information she tucked in a pocket for later. The disc went into the recess the way a key went into a lock that had been made for the key. Not loose. Not tight. Exactly fitted.

The contact came before the seating. A moment where the material of the disc met the material of the hill — not a click, not a sound at all, but a joining, a thing-become-one-thing under her thumb — and then the seating, a quarter-inch of settle, the disc sinking the rest of the way into the recess and stopping at a depth that was the correct depth.

Her thumb was on the symbol on the back of the disc. She pressed.

She had not been told to press. The pressing was what the hand knew. The thumb went down on the inner orbit and the disc sat the last fraction of its depth into the hillside and then the hum in her palm that had been continuous all morning — *stopped.*

Not faded. *Stopped.*

The disc under her thumb went still. Completely. For the first time since she had taken it out of her coat three years ago and held it for the first time with a hand that knew what it was, the disc was *quiet* in her hand.

A beat.

Her breath was out. She had not inhaled. She inhaled.

The quiet held for the space of that breath.

Then.

---

Under her knee, the hill.

Not a shake. Not a tremor. A *shift* at a register her body caught before her mind caught it — a change in the way the ground was under her, the way a room changed when a furnace came on in another part of a house, before any sound from the furnace had reached you. The change went through the hillside and through her kneecap and up her thigh and stopped somewhere in her ribs.

The air changed.

Not the wind. The *air*. The air between her body and the hillside went from morning-cool to a different cool — a dry held cool that had no weather in it. The air an interior had. The air of a room that had been closed for a very long time, and was no longer closed, and was meeting the morning for the first time across a threshold that had just gone un-sealed.

Somewhere below her — below the hillside, below the disc, below depths she had no measure for — a sound started.

Not a sound at the ear. A sound at the bone. A low continuous register at the edge of hearing, deeper than the disc's hum had been, wider, the sound a thing the size of the hill made when the thing the size of the hill began, after ten thousand years, to do the work it had been built for.

Davan made a sound above her.

She did not look up. She could not look up yet. Her eye was on the disc in the recess, and the disc in the recess was no longer dark. Along the symbol on its face — along the four nested arcs, along the drifting fourth — a faint light had come up. Not blue. Not the blue the disc had given her in the woodcutter's shelter and the Stillwater kitchen and the cooperage basement. A color she had not seen the disc make before. Warmer. The color of morning sun through a leaf, held at its brightest for a breath and then not fading but *settling* — the color finding its working register and staying there.

The symbol lit under her thumb.

The hillside lit under her knee.

Not the whole hillside. The four arcs of the symbol she had seen under the grass, the arcs that had been part of the surface before any of this country's grass had ever grown — the arcs began, faintly, to be visible through the growth. Not bright. Just a suggestion that the material was no longer inert. That something under the hill was waking to the signal at the recess and beginning, with the slow patience of something that had been waiting, to answer.

Aldric, behind her, said one word.

She did not hear it. Her ear was full of the bone-sound. She registered his voice at the level of vibration in the air and did not turn.

Her hand was on the disc. The disc was seated. The seating was done.

The hillside had woken under her knee. The air had changed. The bone-sound was sending. The symbol under her thumb was lit with a color the disc had never produced in her hand before.

She knelt.

She kept her thumb on the symbol.

She did not take it off.

Behind her, down the hand-line that had pointed at this recess for five years, the coat lay in the grass with nothing in it, and a father who had not reached this seating had reached, through her, the thing he had walked east to reach. She did not look back at the coat. She looked at the recess. She looked at the light coming up under her thumb. She kept her thumb where her thumb was.

The hill answered under her.

She held on.

---


## Chapter Twenty-Eight — *[Davan]*
### What Was Inside

---

The hillside was doing something.

He stood three paces up-slope of Maren's knee and did not move. His hand had gone to the side of his coat without his hand asking him, and his hand was flat against his ribs the way a hand went flat against something inside a coat that wanted to get out. He did not name the thing inside his ribs. He kept his hand where his hand was.

Under her thumb the small symbol on the back of the disc was lit in the warmer color. The hillside was lit where the four arcs lay, in the same warmer color but very faint, like coals under ash from a night fire that had been banked for a long time and was, now, being asked a question about whether it was still a fire.

The coals were saying yes.

Not in a rush. A slow spread along the arcs — not brighter exactly, but *more present*, the arcs becoming something the hill was instead of something in the hill — and where the arcs ran under the overgrowth the overgrowth was not burning, was not dying, was only *moving aside*. Vines the thickness of a wrist went slack at their anchor-points, slid a hand's-breadth, re-seated. A clump of bracken that had been wedged between two lines in the surface for who knew how long sagged sideways and lay flat. A low thorn branch that had grown in the crook of the easternmost arc for long enough to have the arc in its grain came away at the root, not torn, not pushed — *released*, the way a thing was released when the surface under it decided it did not need to hold that thing there any longer.

He watched it and his mouth was a little open.

Across a pace of hillside the growth was quietly unbuilding itself.

He thought about the way a tide went out. He thought about the way a shore appeared along the length of a beach, not by the water leaving but by the sand admitting that the sand had always been under the water. The hillside was doing that. What was under the growth had always been under the growth, and the growth was, with a courtesy he did not know how to place, stepping aside.

Aldric was behind him. He had not heard Aldric come up. The older man's breathing was small and even and did not draw attention. Davan kept his eye on the hill.

---

The breach opened.

Not a door. There was no door. There was an unmaking of what had, for a long time, been hillside, along a seam that had always been the seam of the breach — the original wound the crash had cut into the hull, over-grown and sealed by ten thousand seasons of the country working on it. The growth retreated the length of a stride on either side of the seam. A darkness showed in the slope between two curving edges of the smooth material, narrow at the bottom and widening at about chest-height and narrowing again at the top — a passage shaped — the shape arrived to him without his naming it — like a place where a thing had once been torn and where the growth had, in the ten thousand seasons of its patience, filled the tear. What had been a sealed wound was being asked to remember that it was a wound, and was remembering.

The tear stood open.

Cool air came out of it.

Not cold. He had known cold on this hillside all morning — wind off the dew-wet slope, the cold of a stone you put your palm on in autumn. This was not that. This was the air of a closed room — no weather in it, no wind in it, the kind of cool that sat in a vault or a cellar or the back of a long unvisited barn. A held air. The air of an indoors that had been indoors since before anyone in this country had a word for indoors.

The cool air met his face and did not travel past his collar. The way the stone at the gate-wall had handed warmth down into the earth — that was old cold. This was inside cold. It was not trying to do anything to him. It was only being what it had been being, for a very long time, behind the hill.

He inhaled.

The air smelled of nothing. Not dust. Not stone. Not the wet green of the outside hillside. A clean absence of smell that was, he found, its own smell — the way a room smelled when no one had cooked or coughed or wept in it for longer than any person remembered rooms.

Aldric, at his elbow, said a word. He did not catch the word. He thought it might have been a name.

---

Maren was still on her knee.

She did not lift her thumb from the symbol. Her head had not turned. Her hair had come loose at the left temple in the breeze she had climbed through earlier and the loose hair was against her cheek and she did not tuck it back. Her eyes were on the disc.

The disc was lit warmer-leaf-through-sunlight under her thumb, holding steady.

He understood — and understood was the wrong word; his body knew — that the disc's stillness and the hillside's opening were the same motion. The disc had stopped humming because the hillside had taken up the hum. The signal was going somewhere past the hill. His body read it as an outgoing direction, the way you could tell from the set of a person's shoulders whether they were speaking to someone in front of them or over their shoulder. The hill was speaking over its shoulder, upward, at something far enough away that it took the full register of the hill to say anything at all.

A resonance came up through his boot-soles.

Slow. Not the bone-sound Maren was carrying under her knee. A thing above that, a long low note that was not a note, a carrying. The rock underfoot was carrying a sending, the way the exposed slab by the verge yesterday had carried the older register under his palm — only that had been a whisper and this was a word.

The word was being said very quietly.

The word was being said very far.

He did not try to hear it. He was not the one it was being said to. He was only in the room where the saying was being done.

---

"Davan."

Maren's voice. Low. She had not turned.

"Here."

"Go in."

He looked at her. Her eyes had stayed on the disc. Her thumb had not moved. The set of her shoulder told him she had decided something before she had spoken, and the decision was that his being here at her knee was not what this moment was for. His being inside was what this moment was for.

"Maren —"

"I have this," she said. "You have that."

His body said yes before his mouth did. He nodded. She did not see the nod. She was busy holding a hillside open for him.

"Aldric comes," he said.

"Yes."

"Vane outside."

"Yes."

"Edvar at the cart."

"Yes."

The exchange was the exchange they had built on the road — small, functional, each word a placed stone. He did not cross to her. She did not need crossing-to. He turned and looked at Aldric, who was already looking at the breach, and the two of them stepped forward together.

His hand had come off his ribs. The coming-off had done itself without him.

---

He went in first.

Aldric was half a pace behind him and did not argue. The passage was narrow enough that a man had to turn his shoulder slightly to enter and broad enough that he did not have to duck. The curving edges where growth had been were smooth in the way the outer hillside was smooth — the disc-smooth at the scale of a room, cool-that-did-not-travel, not stone, felt like stone but not. He did not touch them with his hands. His body did not want to touch them yet. His body wanted to learn what the air was, first.

The air inside was the air that had come out, only more of it.

Three steps in, the outside sound stopped. Not faded. Stopped. Wind had gone. The small creak of Edvar's cart-harness down-slope was gone. The small river-talk far at the valley floor was gone. The world had closed behind him, not as a trap but as a completion — a space that was its own space now, whole inside its own boundary.

His breath arrived at the front of his ears.

Aldric's breath, a pace behind, came quicker than yesterday — Aldric was not in his body at this moment the way the Aldric at the outcrop yesterday had been in his body, steady; this Aldric was in the body of a man approaching the thing he had travelled thirty years to approach.

He did not turn to look at Aldric. He kept walking.

The floor had a give that was not the give of earth. A floor. It had not been earth under his boot since two steps in. The change had passed under his boot-heel and he had not marked it and now, attending, he could feel the material through his sole — the same cool density he had felt in Corvel's container at the cooperage table, in Edvar's face the night of the green-gold, in the hillside under Maren's knee. Floor of it. Walls of it. He was inside a room made of the material the debris field had been scattering pieces of across sixty miles of country for long enough that no song still knew what the pieces were.

A piece of his mind, far back, said: *all of one thing*.

He did not chase the thought. He circled it and let it settle and walked on.

---

The passage opened.

Not into a large room. Into a space the size of a winter byre — low-ceilinged, roughly oblong, the air of it cool and held. The walls were the dark material. The floor was the dark material. There was light, and the light was coming from nowhere he could name — not lamps, not windows. The light was in the walls in a way he could not parse and did not try to. It was the cold-precise light of the face, scaled up — light that had been *made* to the specification of seeing-by, not the specification of warming.

The banks came to him first.

Along the two long walls, at about hip-height, ran low shelves. Not shelves like the ones in Edvar's shop, made of wood and set with pegs. Shelves as *part* of the wall — the wall deepened into shelf at that register, the way stone in a country chapel sometimes had a recess carved into it for a lamp. The shelves ran the length of the room on both sides. On the shelves, row upon row, sat objects.

He did not know the word for what they were.

Small. A man could have held one in two hands without strain. Flat on one side and curved on the other, like a river-stone that had been shaped by a hand that cared about its shape. Dark, the same dark as the walls. A dozen and a dozen and a dozen of them, going down the length of both walls in a pattern so regular that the pattern was the first thing he read — not a row, a *system*. Each object at the same distance from the next. Each at the same height. Each oriented the same way.

He stopped at the first shelf and did not touch.

Aldric, a pace behind him, made a sound. It was not a word. It was the sound a man made when a sentence he had been holding all his life had just been shown the object it was about.

"Davan."

"Yes."

"Do you see."

"Yes."

They stood at the end of the first shelf and looked at the hundred quiet objects on it, and the hundred quiet objects on the wall opposite. Davan did not count. Counting was Aldric's work. His own work was something else and he did not know it yet.

His shoulders opened.

He had not known his shoulders had been closed, on the road and at the recess and in the passage. They had been. They opened now, by a small margin, the way shoulders opened when a body discovered that the room it had walked into wanted nothing from it. The room did not want him. It had been doing the work it had been set to do, and the work had never required anyone.

This was, he understood slowly and without saying it, not a weapon room. It was not a treasure room. It was not the room any song about this hill had taught anyone to imagine.

It was a room full of kept things.

---

He walked to the nearest shelf.

He went slowly. Not out of fear. Out of a courtesy — the courtesy a body found when it came into a room where a great deal of attending had been done and the attending had been done by no one for a very long time. He walked as he might have walked through a chapel in a village where the chapel was not his, where the people who had built it were under the earth, where the only thing left of their work was the work itself and the work deserved not to be walked on carelessly.

He stopped a hand's-breadth from the first object on the shelf.

He did not pick it up. He had no right to pick it up yet. Maybe he never would. The picking up was Aldric's hand's work when Aldric's hand arrived at it, and the hand had not.

He put his palm at the level of the object, close but not touching, and he let his proximity-sense do its work.

The sense came up from his palm and from the underside of his forearm and met the object. Not a heat. Not a cold. A *presence* — the same presence he had read off the face in Edvar's back room, off Corvel's container at the cooperage table, off the slab at the verge yesterday, off the hillside under Maren's thumb. The same quality. Only *different in kind*. The face had been an instrument. The container had been sealed. The hill was an opening-outward. This —

This was holding.

The object was holding something the way a barn held grain, or a jar held seed, or a cellar held a winter's worth of roots. Holding was what it was for. Whatever was in it had been put in it to be there, and the putting had been done with the whole intent of the being-there — the object was not a box that had happened to be used for storage, it was a *storage*, made for storage, the shape of its being was to hold.

His palm came lower. Not touching. The courtesy held.

Behind the holding was the quality he had been reading all morning and did not have a name for. It came up at him from the whole shelf — every object on the shelf at once, braided together so that no single object was the source — a register his body read and could not place.

It was not loneliness.

He tried the word and the word did not fit. The objects were not lonely. Loneliness was a thing bodies felt when they were built for company and were not in company. These objects had never been built for company. They had been built for *being there*. The difference was a whole language apart.

*Attending without attender*, a piece of his mind said.

He did not push the thought. He set it down at the back of the shelf of his mind where the unfinished sentences lived.

His hands had known her shape before he'd met her. The room had taught him why: hands kept things ahead of the rest of you, and asked nothing.

The register pressed gently up at him and he let it press. He stood in the full weight of it. His breath went out.

His skin began to light.

---

He did not see it. He had never seen his own skin-light the way other people saw it. He read it by second-hand — the way a room's walls took his color when he was lit, the way Maren sometimes nodded at his collar, the way Lera on the barge had named her colors for him. He knew it by how the shelf-material in front of him changed tone under his palm.

The shelf-material changed tone.

The dark, at his palm-range, took on a warmth. Not the green-gold of Edvar's back room. Not the held green of yesterday's road. Not the blue of the shelter at Stillwater the disc had given Maren. Something he did not have a word for.

It was, if he had to reach for the word — the color of honey held up to a lamp, only cooler. The color of the edge of dawn a morning nobody had seen, a morning the country was having for the first time.

It was not green. It was not amber. It was not gold.

It was a color his skin had not produced before.

It was in him, at his wrists and the hollow of his throat and the underside of his jaw, and he knew it was there because the walls of the storage room knew it was there — the dark material held the color for him, gave it back at his palm-range, carried it a hand's-breadth into the material and then held it, *keeping* it, because *keeping* was what the material did.

He did not pull his hand back. He did not step away.

He held where he was, and the storage room held where it was, and for a beat the two of them were at the same register, lit in a color that was neither fully his nor fully the material's but something the two of them were making by standing this close to one another.

Behind him, Aldric made a small sound.

---

"Davan."

Aldric's voice. Careful. Low.

"Here."

"Your skin."

"I know."

"I have not seen that color."

"No."

A pause. The pause was not awkward. The pause was the pause of a man reading a new word on a page in a language he knew, taking the moment the new word required, putting the word into the place in his understanding that had been made for it.

"All right," Aldric said. The word was not Maren's word. In Aldric's mouth it was a different instrument — an acknowledgment of the page-edge, not a settling.

Davan lowered his palm to his thigh without turning. He took a step back from the shelf. The color at his wrist did not fade. It held, steady, at a register lower than the peak it had reached while his palm was at the material, and the register it held at was a register he had not held at before. The new register lived in the tendons of his forearm. The skin there was, in the best-set way a body could be, *working*.

Aldric crossed behind him to the long wall.

Davan did not watch. He listened. Aldric's steps were slow and even and stopped at a point on the opposite shelf. A breath went out of the older man that was the breath of a man lowering himself to a bench. He was crouching. Davan heard the small sound of knees, then the slower sound of hands — placed on thighs, palms down, the gesture of a scholar sitting before a page he had not yet earned the right to turn.

Aldric said one word.

He said it in the liturgical register.

Davan did not catch it. It was, he thought, the word for a place in a liturgy — a fixed point, the point at which a reader laid hands on the page before beginning — and Aldric had said it because Aldric's body had reached the part of the room where that word was the word his body had. He had not chosen the word. The word had arrived because the room had asked for it.

Davan turned, slowly, at the shoulder.

Aldric was on both knees. His hands were flat on the tops of his thighs. His back was straight. He was looking at the objects on the shelf in front of him with the attention of a man whose whole life had been instrument-building for this exact length of looking. His lips moved once. Nothing came out. He closed his mouth.

He was not reading. He was *seeing what was to be read*.

Davan recognized the quality in him. He had seen it in Edvar, in the back room, across the lit face. He had seen it in himself. It was the quality of a man at a threshold who knew that crossing would require a different instrument than the one he had used to get to the threshold, and who was, for a last held moment, being the version of himself that had walked to the threshold, before becoming the version of himself that would cross.

Aldric did not cross. Not yet.

Davan did not ask him to.

---

Behind them, the passage was open. The cool air was still moving from the room outward and downward along the hillside. Davan could feel the direction of it at his back — a breath from indoors going out to meet the country — and the direction told him that the storage was *venting*, that its air, held for as long as its air had been held, was being given back to the hillside and the hillside was giving the hillside's air in.

He walked a half-turn to the center of the room.

He stood there and he let his proximity-sense widen.

It did not feel like widening. It felt like *admitting*. The sense had already been full — it had been reading the room since the passage — and what widened was his willingness to let it read. He stood at the center of the room and stopped fending.

The room came in.

Not at force. The room did not press. The room was *available*, and his body took availability for what it was, and the register that had been braided along the shelves became the register of the space. He stood inside a held thing. He had been inside rooms that had held feeling — a back bench, a barge cabin, the cooperage basement — and every one of those rooms had held human warmth or human cold, the leavings of bodies. This room held neither. This room held the *shape* of waiting without any person in the shape. It was a waiting that had gone on for so long that the waiting had taken the shape of the material.

*What was inside*, a piece of his mind said.

He let the piece say it. He did not finish the sentence. The sentence did not need finishing. What was inside was what was inside. The body that was reading it was the body that was reading it. The two facts sat in the middle of the room and did not ask anything of him.

His skin was at the new color.

He knew it by the walls. The walls around him had taken a low warmth from him. He was at the center of a room that was, in a register small enough to miss and wide enough to fill the room, the color he was making.

Somewhere above the hill — very far up, past the ceiling of the chamber, past the crest of the hill, past the blue of the morning the day was having outside — a resonance was happening that he could not hear. The direction of it arrived in his back the way the venting in his back had arrived. There was an outward. There was a receiving. Somewhere far, very far, something was receiving what the hill was sending, and was sending back. The sending-back came in under the room as a pressure his inner ear caught and dismissed before his inner ear would name it.

He did not name it.

He did not have the geography of it. He had only the register: a saying and an answer, at a distance the word *distance* was not built for.

He drew breath.

He stood.

---

Maren's voice arrived behind him, at the passage.

"Davan."

Small. Not stressed. Just placing him.

"Here," he said, over his shoulder.

"Aldric."

"Here," Aldric said, from the far shelf.

A pause. The pause was Maren at the outside of the passage, reading the inside of the passage the way she read a room by ear-and-light before stepping into it.

"Vane is on the slope," she said. "She has scent. West. One man. Far yet."

"How far," Aldric said. Not a question he expected to get a number on. A way of setting the fact in the room.

"Far enough to be a later thing. She wanted you to know."

"Yes," Aldric said.

"I am here," Maren said. "The disc is seated. I am not moving."

"Good," Davan said.

Another pause. Then:

"Is it a place."

"It is a place," Davan said.

"I thought it would be."

"It has —" he said, and let the word not finish because the word that wanted to come was *holdings* and *holdings* was a word that wanted to be Aldric's for a minute before it was his.

Maren did not push.

"All right," she said. Small. From the passage.

---

Aldric rose.

He rose slowly, as a man in his fifties rose from both knees when the knees had been on a cool hard floor for longer than the knees would have chosen. His hands pushed on his thighs and he came up to a stand with the small involuntary sound a body made when it straightened. He turned. He looked at Davan.

His face was the face he had worn at the camp at the edge of Irongate country, on the other side of the small lamp. Only more of it.

"They're records," he said.

"Yes," Davan said.

"All of them."

"Yes."

"Nothing else. No instruments. No —" his hand moved and stopped and came back to his thigh. "No *control* of anything. No lever. No dial. No — weapon, though I do not know why I expected a weapon. No tool at all except these." He gestured at the shelves. "The whole room is one thing."

"Yes."

"They are records."

He said it the third time the way a man said a thing when he was making the saying itself a vessel, carrying the saying from the place the saying had landed in his body to the place the saying needed to go in order to become a thing he had said to another person. Davan held the vessel with him. He did not interrupt it. Aldric's hand had gone inside his robe, had found the cord of the lamp he was not carrying, had not been there, had come back out without the tic finishing. Davan registered this and did not say it.

"It is," Aldric said, softer, "all of what was kept. Not what was held to be used. What was held to be *kept.*"

"Yes."

"Oh," Aldric said.

It was, Davan thought, the smallest and most accurate sound a priest had made in a long while.

---

Davan crossed back to the shelf where his palm had been.

He did not put his palm back against the material. He put it near, again — the same hand's-breadth. The material did not give his color back this time. It had given it back and the giving was done and the color was in him, not in the wall. His skin held steady at the register the wall had taught him, and the wall held at its own register — quieter now, a little — and the two of them were at separate registers and the separation was correct.

He chose one of the objects on the shelf.

Not to pick up. To put a finger on. He chose the one nearest the center of the row, because the center of a row was where a row's attention was most evenly distributed and because he did not want to be the person who chose an end. He extended one finger of his right hand — his carving finger, the one that had known the inner-orbit shape before his mind had — and he set the pad of it against the flat side of the object. Very lightly.

The object was neither warm nor cold. It was at the same register as the walls. It took his fingerprint's oil the way the wall would have taken it, and gave nothing back, and was what it was.

His pad was on the object. He kept it there.

He did not lift it. He did not press harder. He kept the contact at exactly the register at which two things could be, at once, two things — the object still the object, his finger still his finger, the material the material, the body the body.

A held point.

His skin at the wrist was at the new color and the new color was not fading.

Aldric stood at the far shelf and did not cross to him and did not speak. The room was the room.

Outside, through the length of the passage, through the breach, through the morning where Maren's thumb was on the disc and Vane was on the slope with a scent and Edvar was at the cart below with the horses — the country was doing whatever the country did. Davan did not go to find out.

The storage held.

He held with it.

And he held his pad on the object, and did not take it off, and did not turn it over, and did not look to see if there was a marking on the underside, and did not carry it back to the passage for Aldric, and did not claim it, and did not pick it up.

He stayed at the shelf, with his finger on the one kept thing his finger had chosen, and waited for Aldric to be ready to begin.

---


## Chapter Twenty-Nine — *[Aldric]*
### The Record

---

Davan's finger-pad was on the one kept thing his finger had chosen.

Aldric read the younger man's back the way he had read the backs of novices at prayer — not the head, not the shoulder, but the set of things below the shoulder blade where a body admitted the weight it was carrying. Davan had gone past admitting. He was *in* the weight, steady, his pad against the object and the object against the material of the shelf, and the tension of the contact was a courtesy.

The courtesy had been offered.

He rose from the far shelf. The knees did what they did. He placed his palms flat on the tops of his thighs, and the gesture arrived in two languages at once — the hands-flat of a man approaching a book whose cover had not been opened in his lifetime, and the hands-flat of a priest lowering himself before the blessed sacrament. He let them both be. He was tired of keeping them in different rooms.

He crossed the floor of the storage.

The dark material did not ring under his boot. His step made the dense small sound of a floor made, at its making, to answer back the way the floor of a library answered back — not more, not less. He knew the sound of a floor that had been made for reading. This was a reading floor.

He stopped a pace short of Davan and looked at the object under the finger-pad.

It sat at the same height as its neighbors: flat on the upper side, curved beneath. Small notation along one edge in a script Aldric did not know. *Did not know* was the honest phrase — the letters, if they were letters, were at the boundary where his eye reported *written* without reporting *in what*. Not wholly foreign: he had seen that quality once before, on the lintel-stone above the undercroft doorway, in light he had been holding with the wrong hand.

"Davan."

"Here."

"May I."

"Yes."

The pad did not lift. The invitation had been there since before Aldric had asked for it, and the asking had been a thing he needed to do for himself — the last gesture of a man trained to ask before he opened. The training was not wrong. It was, this once, not the instrument the room required. But the gesture gave him the edge of the page to put his hand on, and the edge was, for a man of his vocation, the place reading began.

He put his palms at the sides of the object.

He lifted it.

The weight was the weight a maker chose when the maker had wanted a thing carried without noting the carrying. Hand-weight. He brought it a pace back so the wall-light fell across the upper face without shadow of his own body, and he turned it so the edge-notation faced him, and he waited for his eye to do what his eye did.

It did not come all at once. Of the seven languages he had at different depths, none of them was this. But the shapes along the edge were doing something familiar in an unfamiliar alphabet. They were doing *numbering*.

A catalog number.

The word settled. Not a prayer. A catalog number — the mark a body of kept material put on each of its items so the order could be known without reading. He had spent thirty years in rooms where the first act of respect for a shelf was to acknowledge its catalog before picking up a single book.

He had begun to read.

---

He sat down on the floor.

Not with the dignity of a Keeper — with the practicality of a man whose knees had been on the cold material of the far shelf longer than the knees had asked to be. He sat cross-legged so that his eye and the upper face of the object were at the same height. He put his hands flat on his thighs.

The upper face was not blank. As his eye settled it resolved into what he understood as *written* — dense, the way a page of close-set type was dense, the letters in a careful hand running in lines. He was not yet trying to read the lines. He was looking at their arrangement. Paragraphs. A marker every fifteenth line that stood out as a different sort of symbol, the way a scribe marked, in the margin of a chronicle, the year.

This was a log. A record-in-sequence. The shape a document took when its purpose was *to note, with date, what was true*, and not to persuade any reader of any thing at all.

At the top of the upper face, set off from the body by the white space a careful hand gave a heading, were two shapes. The inner-orbit figure. And a mark he did not know.

He looked at the second mark for a long moment. The scholar did not place it. But the body gave him a register the scholar had not thought of: the mark was a *seal*. The shape above the opening of a document when the document wished to announce the authority under which it had been written.

The seal was not a church's seal. The Keepers' order had a seal; he knew the weight of it. This was not that weight. This was the stamp of an *office* — an enterprise. It had left this record under the stamp of its enterprise, ten thousand seasons ago, and the stamp had waited here to be seen.

He lowered his eye to the body of the text. He did not try to read it. He noted that a four-letter unit appeared repeatedly, the first letter of which was the inner-orbit figure itself, doing the work of a letter the way a figure did the work of a letter when a language had begun in a pictogram and moved on from the pictogram without forgetting that the pictogram had once been the word.

*The word had once been the thing.*

He set the object back in its catalog position. He stood. The knee protested; he let it.

He walked the shelf, establishing order. This long wall, written registers. The opposite wall, at the same catalog distance — the same register at different delivery. Voiced. Small indentations on the upper faces, the sort of surface that lit when a hand settled on it. The written on the left, the voiced on the right, at the same dates, in the same hand.

An archive under two registers.

He did not cross to the voiced wall yet. He was not ready. He did not want to hear a voice before he had read a document. The order mattered to him — the written before the voiced — because the written was the form his thirty years had prepared him for.

He chose a second object further along the first shelf.

---

He sat again.

The upper face was divided by ruling into a grid he recognized across languages. A manifest grid: items left, quantities middle, disposition right. His eye had looked at a thousand such grids in estate inventories, temple-stock ledgers, pilgrim-supply accounts. He read the columns by what the column did, not what it said.

First column: *items*. Second column: *number*. Third column: *where assigned*.

The *where assigned* column had four distinct symbols, repeated down the column in different combinations. Three were variants of each other — small changes on a common shape. The fourth was a shape apart.

He looked at the fourth symbol for a long time.

An oblong body, with a small notation at one extremity that resolved, when his eye adjusted, into the figure of the descent line — the drift of the fourth arc from the carving on the hillside outside, rendered here at the scale of a column entry, doing the work of a location-marker. The fourth symbol was a place.

The place was where he was sitting.

He counted the column. Most entries carried one of the three variants. The remainder — a small percentage — had the fourth. *Three of four, assigned to the three.* *One of four, assigned to the lander.* Including, in the rows whose first-column entries he could partly parse, rows that appeared to list people.

Crew.

The manifest was a crew manifest. It allocated crew to four destinations. Three were orbital stations. One was the surface. The count on the stations was greater than the count on the surface.

*Three for station, one for descent.*

He said the phrase in his head in English-of-the-country. The phrase was what the manifest said, and the manifest was the source, and the source was in his hand, and his hand was steady.

He had come to the mission profile.

He held it for a long breath. He had inferred this shape from the diagrams, from the faces, from Brennan's *for unchanged hands*, from Reth's *four hundred meters, no grain*, from the four arcs on the hillside and what the light had done at the crest of the ridge three mornings running. Inference had been the instrument his life had built. But inference was always subject to the possibility that the data were partial and the pattern, though coherent, was not the pattern. This was not inference. The office had filed its own manifest. The manifest said what the pattern had said.

A quieter sentence arrived under the first.

*The three that remained were not survivors. They were on post.*

He let it stand. He had been carrying, without exactly carrying, a framing of the three moons as *the ones that made it* — the shape the country had given itself over ten thousand years, because the shape had the consolations a story made for the living wanted. *Four fires in the sky. One came down to die.*

It was not the shape of the document.

The document did not say *the three survived*. The document said *three for station*. The three had been tasked. They had gone to their assigned places and had stayed there because staying was their work. The work had not ended because the surface contingent had not arrived to mark it ended. They were *performing protocol*. They had been performing protocol for ten thousand seasons. The protocol had held.

He closed his eyes a moment. Not prayer. The small motion of a man accommodating a scale he had not built a room in his head for yet. When he opened them, the manifest was still the manifest, and the column was still the column.

His hand went inside his robe for the lamp-cord. It did not. The hand started and stopped at the robe's shoulder and came back down to his thigh, and he did not see the hand. His eye had gone back to the manifest.

---

The third object was on the second shelf.

He crossed on his knees — the body had admitted its preference, and the Keeper accepted — and chose the object at the second shelf's start. A different format: not log, not manifest. Panels on the upper face, each holding a diagram. Cross-sectional. The interior of a vessel, seen from the side, compartments indicated.

He knew what he was looking at before the scholar named it. A ship. The same shape the face in the undercroft had rendered as the fourth of the four — the lander — but here intact, at original design, before the damage. The compartments carried labels he could not read. But some carried the stamp of the seal. Some carried the four-arc figure. And at the ship's stern —

His breath caught.

A compartment label there carried a figure he had seen in his own sealed chamber, on the inward-facing curve of the four smooth containers. He had recorded it in his journal two months ago. He had thought it a house-mark, a maker's sigil, a decoration.

Now it was a compartment label.

The containers in his sealed chamber had come from *that compartment*. The mark on the containers was the mark of their origin hold. Four storage holds at the ship's stern, each labeled with its sub-figure — three of a family, one apart, the same grid of differentiation as the manifest's destinations — and the fourth hold had held the containers the Confluence temple had been built over.

He sat with this. He had not assembled the last step before. He had approached it — the temple-built-atop, the debris-field-under-the-floor — in his own chamber, in a dry-stone fold, riding east. But he had not put the containers on the manifest. He had not understood that the objects he had knelt before for two months were *inventoried*. That an office had filed them. That his life had been spent tending a building that was standing on top of an inventoried cargo hold.

He was not angry. He tested the anger; it was not there. Anger required that someone had done something to him, and the office whose stamp sat at the top of the first object had not done anything to him specifically. The office had filed its documents. The office had assumed its colony would arrive. The colony had not arrived. The documents had stayed where they were filed. Two centuries of Keepers at a time had done what their hands had been given them for, without knowing that the hands were tending a floor built above an archive.

His life had been what his life had been.

The register was — he tried the word and the word fit — *quiet*. He was very quiet. He sat in the quiet, and the quiet was not peace; it was the quiet of a long thing landing.

---

He stood.

He crossed to the voiced wall.

The wall was the same material as the passage, as the storage floor, as the hillside above them. He had understood this abstractly in the passage coming in; standing at the wall he understood it otherwise. The shelf and the hill were one piece.

He did not yet lift one. He looked at the spacing of them. The archive kept its forms in the forms that befit them. The written were the written and the voiced were the voiced, at the same dates, in the same hand. The voiced were what he had been afraid of. He was still the Keeper whose life had been mostly text. A voice from the office would make the office a *people*, and people, even dead for ten thousand years, entered a room as people entered a room.

He took one register from the shelf.

Carefully. Hand-weight. The upper face carried a single panel of indentation, and his thumb, held clear of it, felt the cool-precise difference between panel and surrounding surface — the area that invited contact. He was carrying the object to the floor where he could set it down and begin, and the carrying itself was the thinking he was doing about whether to make the contact.

He sat in the middle of the floor.

He set it down. He thought of Davan, still at the far wall, still with his pad at the one object he had chosen. He thought of Maren at the recess outside — the hum through the material of the hill, the whole hillside held open because of the pressure of one finger against a small mark. He thought of Brennan in Greenford, whose eight-page letter had said *for unchanged hands* and had not, in the letter, explained the phrase because the phrase had not needed explaining between them. Brennan would have to be told. Aldric did not know yet by what channel. The channels he had been trained to use would suppress what he sent through them. The channel would have to be elsewhere.
