Cael would have read the letter by now, and Cael would do whatever Cael decided to do, and that decision would arrive when it arrived.

He set his palm on the panel.

---

A voice began.

He did not understand the words.

The voice was a man's. Middle-register. Tired. Not performing. Not preaching. Not reporting to a superior who expected brevity. The voice spoke at the cadence of a man who had sat down at the end of a day he had not expected to have, saying what had to be said because no one else would say it. The vowels were long. Some of the consonants were sounds Aldric's language did not use. But the tone was a tone Aldric had heard thirty years' worth of — in the cells of dying pilgrims, at the bedsides of men setting down their work. It was the tone of ending.

He closed his eyes.

He let the voice finish.

He understood nothing of the words. But in the middle, the same four-letter unit he had read on the first object — the figure-word, the inner-orbit figure doing the work of a letter — came up in the man's speech as a name. The man said it with the care a man used when saying a name. It was the name of the mission, or the ship, or the office; he could not tell which. Saying it was something the man had decided he was going to do before the ending.

After the name, the voice became quieter. A pause. Then a single word with the shape of a sign-off.

The voice was finished.

Aldric lifted his palm.

He had never believed, even in his worst scholarly years, that the people who had made the figure on the door of the Confluence had been anything other than people. But he had held them as figures from an *office* — a faceless enterprise, the sort of thing a theology used to put scale under its story. The voice corrected the scale. The office had been staffed by a man, among other men. The man had been tired at a certain hour. The man had said what he had said. The man had probably not known what would follow the ending he was at. The man had done the work of speaking the name.

He put the voiced register back.

---

He crossed again to the written shelf and picked up the ship diagram and carried it back to the manifest, and he reached for a third written object further down the shelf — its upper face glimpsed from the catalog, the format of a *study*, a report on a subject. He carried that one back too and laid it beside the other two. He was triangulating. He was doing what a Keeper did when the documents had begun to talk to each other.

The third object.

The subject-heading used a word he had learned by its shape in the first log's body: *native*. The body was denser here — paragraphs, tables, diagrams at the page-foot. He took a long moment to understand the diagrams as cellular, because he had never actually seen a diagram of a cell, only descriptions of them in the natural-philosophical treatises of the Greenford library. But these were cells. The cells held the same kind of structure in both versions of the side-by-side panel, and the difference between the two was the difference he had been trying not to see.

The left version had a small figure inside the cell that the right version did not.

The figure was the inner-orbit figure, rendered in the small.

He looked at it for a long time.

The office had used the same figure for many things. It had been, for ten thousand years on Gaius, the emblem of the mission. It had become, by a distance, the emblem of theology. It had become, by a further distance, a carving on the doors of a temple, a mark on the edge of a disc, the shape of an obsessive young man's knife in pine. Here, at its earliest, it was the office's stamp for *mission-related* — the notation an office put in a corner of a diagram to say *this is ours*. The left version of the cell had been marked *ours*. The right had not.

The left was the version the Chrysalis had made.

The right was the version before.

The cells were not different species. They were the same species at two states, and the states were before-and-after contact with a subject, and the subject was named in the heading by the word *native*, and the native was the organism the report was on.

He laid the report down.

He did not move for a breath. The floor of the storage held him the way a floor held a man, and he let it.

The Chrysalis was not a gift.

The Chrysalis was not a sacrament.

The Chrysalis had been waiting on Gaius when the colony ship came down through the sky it had no business being in, and the Chrysalis had entered the surviving colonists through soil and air and water. The Chrysalis had not *chosen* them. The Chrysalis had done what the Chrysalis did. Those who had not died of it had changed. The ones who had not changed had been the *unchanged*. The office had a word for them. The word was on the right version of the cell: *native-resistant baseline*. He did not know the alphabet but he knew the grammar and he knew the shape of a technical phrase.

*Native-resistant baseline.*

He said the phrase in English.

He said it in the Keepers' Latin.

He said it in the old ritual tongue of the Confluence, in which the Chrysalis Rite's oldest prayers were chanted — and the phrase in that tongue did not fit. The ritual tongue did not have a word for *baseline*. The ritual tongue had a word for *chosen*.

*Chosen* had always been how the bloodline described itself. He had never trusted the word; the word had always rung to him like a word put into a theology by its beneficiaries. But he had trusted the structure the word stood in — that the bloodline had a *capacity* the other lines did not have, and the capacity was the foundation of ten thousand years of doctrine about the administration of Founding-era objects.

The capacity was in the right-hand cell.

The capacity was: the cell did not admit the organism. The cell was native-baseline. A native-baseline body was the body the ship's systems had been designed to recognize, because the ship's systems had been designed before the colony had encountered the organism. A native-baseline body was the baseline the office had assumed. A native-baseline body was the body for whom the disc functioned as a crew ID, because the disc had been made for the body that remained in the condition the office had cataloged it in.

The bloodline were native-baseline — which was not the same as unchanged in the sense the bloodline used the word. They had not been left untouched. They had been immune. Immune was the mission-office word. *Unchanged* was the word the bloodline had built its theology around, and the two words described the same cell and meant a different thing.

They had not been chosen. They had been immune.

Immune was a smaller word than chosen. Immune was a smaller word than *gifted*. Immune described a property. The property was a genetic trait the descendants had carried forward, and the trait had been the key to the pre-crash tools, and the access to the tools had built the whole theology their descendants' descendants believed.

He thought about the Autumnal Rite. He had sat at the Confluence's high altar for thirty Autumnal Rites and watched the participants approach for the mark. He had named each mark by its coming. He had read the readings and sung the canticles and invoked the gift-givers. The gift-givers had never given anything. The gift-givers had been a category a minority had built to describe their own immunity as a blessing, so that they could live with having access to the tools of the ship and with the rest of the population not having that access.

The temple had been built over a debris-field. The altar had been laid over the bloodline's immunity. The Rite he had administered for thirty years had celebrated, as a gift, a biological event that had killed most of the colonists and had changed the rest.

He did not feel the anger now either.

What arrived in its place he could only name as tenderness — not pity, not charity in the doctrine's sense, not the word he'd spent thirty years lifting with both hands in sermons. Something smaller and more accurate than any of those. Not for the office. Not for the bloodline. For the long slow shape of a story a people had made out of their own biology because making the story had been how those people had lived with their biology. The Chrysalis had come. The Chrysalis had done what it did. The unchanged had held the key and the key had worked, and the tools had worked for them and not for their neighbors, and the unchanged had done what human beings had always done when they had access and other people did not — they had built a story in which their access was legitimate, and the story had got more beautiful over time, and eventually the liturgy had been what the country understood about itself. The liturgy had been beautiful. He had loved it. He had *loved* it. He did not now un-love it. He looked at it across the distance the right-hand cell had put him on the other side of, and he saw it the way he saw, at a remove, a thing he had been given as a child and had carried a long way and was now setting down because the object under his hand required both hands.

He set the study back.

---

Maren spoke from the passage.

"Aldric. Davan."

Low. Placing them.

"Here," Aldric said.

"Here," Davan said.

"A voice," she said. The word was careful. She had heard something, or she had felt it through the material. "I felt it under my hand. The pulse went shorter for a count."

"A voice," Aldric said. "The register carried. I did not think the carrying would reach you."

"It did not reach. The hill answered something. I read the answer."

"Yes."

A pause. Then: "Is it bad."

He considered.

"No," he said. "But —"

"All right," she said. The word arrived in her two-word register.

"I will be longer," he said.

"Yes."

---

He reached for a fourth object.

The next in the row. Its catalog figure next in sequence. He suspected a study — the format, from a distance, was the study-format. He brought it to his lap and set it with the other three he had laid out on the floor. Four objects in a line now, from four shelf-positions. He had assembled in an hour the shape he had not been able to see from the outside of the hull.

Its heading used two words he had learned.

*For the colony.*

And a third word he had not yet seen. A heading-word: centered, larger, its letters spaced the way a title was spaced. The heading said, if he had read it right — and he thought he had read it enough right — *archive, colonial-genetic, Mission Office, Hold-IV*.

He had stopped breathing for a moment. The body caught itself and began again, because the body did what the body did, and he let it.

The object he held was the study-document whose subject was the contents of the four smooth containers in his undercroft.

Hold IV. The hold at the stern of the lander whose four containers had been found intact, at the Confluence, in the secondary debris scatter he had not known until this month was a debris scatter. The hold had carried an *archive*.

He read what he could read of the body. A description — short, because a document describing a genetic archive did not need to be long; the archive was the archive. He could not read most of the caption-words, but one recurring figure was the figure for *preserved*. Another was *viable*. A third was *human*. A fourth he guessed from position as a unit-word for the kind of thing a biology archive was made of — *sample*, or *material*.

Preserved. Viable. Human.

The four containers held preserved, viable, human material.

Material from the native-baseline population.

Material the mission had brought with it to seed the colony's long-term genetic health from, before the mission had encountered the Chrysalis, before the encounter had made baseline-cells into something else across most of the colonists. The colony had carried with it the old version of itself, cryopreserved in holds, bound for a thousand-year health plan whose plan-makers had not anticipated a native organism that would make the plan a different kind of plan by breakfast.

The plan had survived. In the holds. In the containers. Under the floor of his temple.

A further entry near the bottom caught his eye. Smaller print. A footnote — his eye knew the shape of a footnote across languages — bracketed, formatted as a contingency clause. He did not try to read the text word-by-word. He saw the figure-words he had been collecting: *native*, *resistance*, *baseline*. And a further figure whose position in the clause was the position of a *requires*.

He read the footnote as best he could.

The footnote said — in the shape he was certain of, even if the grammar was not — that opening the archive containers required a condition. The condition was the absence of the native organism from the opener's biology. The requirement was absolute. The archive had been sealed with a biological lock that recognized baseline. A body carrying the organism could not open the hold. The lock had been designed, in office-of-the-mission before-the-crash, to guard the preserved material against contamination by foreign biology — and it did not weigh intent, or direction, or need. It read one thing: whether the body at the seal carried the organism. A body that carried it was refused.

He made himself put it in the plainest words he had, because the plain words were the ones that would matter later. *Immune was not the same as untouched.* The lock did not ask whether the organism was *active* in a body. It asked whether the organism was *present* — and on this planet, after ten thousand years, the organism was present in everyone.

So the holds could not be opened by any living person on Gaius. Even the bloodline, whose immune systems had only ever held the active organism off, carried it worked into the cell at the root of the line. Even Maren — whose body had never hosted it — had spent seventeen years on a world saturated with it. The lock was set to the pre-organism baseline, and that baseline was a population that no longer walked anywhere on Gaius. The only pre-organism humans left were the ones inside the holds.

Opening the holds required making a person who was the pre-organism baseline.

Undoing the Chrysalis in them first.

He said the word in his mind. He said *undoing*. He said it in three languages. It sat in each of them like a stone — a stone a man had lifted out of a stream-bed and had set on the bank. The stream moved on. The stone was on the bank. The stone was now a choice.

He did not yet have the choice in his hand.

He was at the threshold of the choice. He was not yet across it.

Across it was the next Keeper's ground. The next Keeper was going to be the Keeper who took the floor up, which he had said to himself three nights ago and which was, now, not a sentence he had said but a description of the work in front of him. The floor was already partially up. He was sitting on the floor of the storage that was under the floor of the chapel. He had not *taken it up* in the sense of a decision-taken; he had *walked through* it in the sense of a route-walked. The taking-up would be the coming-back-out, and the going-back-to-the-Confluence, and the saying-of-what-had-been-found, and the channel-built-by-hand for saying it in.

That work was tomorrow's work. This hour's work was to receive what was in front of him.

He received.

He set the archive-document back at its catalog position — the others too, in order, respecting the row — though he did not yet stand. He remained seated. He let his breath even.

He thought about the next object. He thought he might cross to the voiced-register wall and let a second voice into the room. He was composing his next motion in his head the way he composed the order of a vigil — what came first, what came second.

---

Davan spoke.

"Aldric."

Low. The carrying register Davan used when he had something he did not want to carry across the floor at volume. Aldric turned his head. Davan was still at his shelf. He had not moved his pad. But his face was at the passage.

"Vane."

Aldric had heard nothing at the passage. He listened and heard nothing still, and then the scrape of a boot on the material at the threshold that was not the scrape of a boot he knew yet, and then Vane's voice, dry, at the mouth of the passage.

"Aldric. Davan. Come to the angle."

The voice a hunter used when a hunter was not yet telling you to move fast. It was telling him to stop what he had been doing.

He stood. The knee protested; he let it protest.

"How near," he said.

"Near enough. His boot is on the slope, above the cart. He is going quiet. He has not seen Edvar yet — Edvar is flat in the southern lee and knows. One man. The only scent on the slope. The other four are not here today."

"All right."

He heard his own voice in the storage's register and heard it carry the way the material carried — close, warm, the voice held a hand's-breadth by the wall before the wall gave it back. His voice sounded older to him than he had heard it sound in some while. It was the voice of a man who had just read what he had read and had his hands flat on his thighs a minute ago and was going to walk out because a follower of Horst's was at the breach.

He looked once at the shelves. He had put the four objects back in their catalog places. The voiced register was back on its opposite shelf. The row he had made on the floor was gone. A library paid the courtesy of leaving a shelf as it found it. The courtesy he owed was that courtesy, because he did not yet know whether he would return, and if he did not return, the next reader should find the catalog intact.

He turned toward the passage.

Davan had come off the shelf. His pad had lifted at a rate slower than a lift — he had asked his pad to leave the surface and the pad had done it, and the departure had been a courtesy. Davan's color was low. His wrists lit faintly at the new register; the peak was past.

"Ready," Davan said.

"Yes."

They crossed to the mouth of the passage together.

Aldric set his hand on the wall of the passage as he went out. He had not touched it on the way in; he had not had permission yet. On the way out, the wall gave a cool to his palm without his asking — a cool that did not travel. The palm carried it the length of two steps and then the palm was his palm again. The giving had been the final beat of the reading. The archive, in the register it had, had acknowledged that he had read.

The passage was narrow at top, widening at chest-height, narrow again at the bottom, as it had been coming in, and the air moved past him toward the outside. Ahead, a pale square of morning at the end of the passage. Maren's silhouette at the recess, her thumb on the disc, her back not turned, her hand not moving. She had not moved in the time he had been inside. The hill was being held open under her pad. The held-open-ness had been the condition of his reading. It was still the condition.

Beyond Maren's knee, on the slope above the cart, a second silhouette moved along the north shoulder — small at that distance, the shape of a man who did not know he was visible yet, coming down through the low scrub with the quiet of a professional. Edvar, beyond Maren, was not visible from the passage angle; but a thin line of horse-breath steamed up from the lee of the stone where the cart was, and the breath was even, the breath of a horse being held at its halter by a hand that knew the horse.

Aldric stopped at the angle Vane had named.

He did not step further. He did not step back.

Behind him the storage held. Ahead of him was the hillside with the disc in its recess and Maren's thumb on the disc, and the follower on the slope, and the morning, and the work of the day which had not yet been done.

A boot scraped on scree above the cart. Quietly, not quietly enough. He heard it at the angle the way he would have heard a footfall in a chapel aisle during a vigil — the small sound that said the vigil was no longer alone. Vane, invisible at the shoulder below, made no sound at all. Davan, at his shoulder inside the passage, was holding the new color low at his wrists.

The boot came down on the scree again. One pace nearer.

He breathed in. He breathed out.

The reading had not finished. The world had come in anyway.

---


## Chapter Thirty — *What the Moons Keep*

---

### *Vane*

The boot came down on scree a second time and she had his weight.

Not his face. His pace. Pace gave her a body's long habit the way a voice gave her its owner's country. Toe placed before heel, knee absorbing what the scree wanted to do to him. Horst's instructor had been a woman named Jax in the Threshing Yard. Vane had taken the same course in her third year. The carriage on the slope was Jax's carriage.

*One of my cohort's, or one of the next.*

She catalogued without moving. Flat in the north-shoulder lee, a knuckle of stone giving her a shadow the man would not break against until he was three paces past. Wind off her right shoulder. Breathing through her mouth — cold tracking, the nose reserved for the man and nothing incidental.

Seventy paces to Edvar and the cart. Edvar flat in the southern lee, the off-horse at the cheek-strap. The horse knew him and had not blown.

*Sixty.*

Waxed grey coat short-cut at the hip. A sword — Horst's men always wore a sword, and a sword set a civilian off by a hundred yards, which was half its point. No bow. No Wound-maker.

*Fifty.*

Right hand on the knife, hilt turned so the blade came edge-up if the drawing was what this became. Left hand with a loop of fine cord bunched in the palm. She had decided on the cord in the five minutes before he made the crest. She did not want blood on the hill. The hill had been bled on enough.

Her left hand was steady. Withdrawal had redistributed the tremor overnight — hands to shoulders, which the coat hid. The shoulders were vibrating at the clavicle. The hands would do cord-work when cord-work came.

*Forty.*

She heard Aldric step at the passage mouth. The knee that did not come up clean made a small asymmetric sound a trained ear would catch at ten paces. The contract man was at forty, carriage at eighty percent of attention; Jax's course did not teach a man to listen for a knee in a passage he did not know existed. The sound did not reach him.

Maren, up at the recess, had not moved. Vane had checked her three times from the lee angle — thumb on the disc, back to the slope, the hillside under her knee in the low warmth Davan had said was *waking up*. Vane did not know what was waking. Her job was a man on a slope; other people had other jobs today, and the disciplines held when the disciplines held.

The man paused.

Not at a wind-sign. At something he had heard through the surface. The bone-sound. It had registered at the level his training told him was not weather and not heart. He put his hand inside his coat.

He drew — not the sword. A small pale thing, palm-sized. Not the inner orbit symbol; a different glyph, the one the bloodline's domestic office used for field-report. He held it flat. He was going to report.

*Before he engages, he confirms.* The right move for a contract man who had walked three days and found a hillside humming under its own grass. The thing he had found was outside his brief.

She came up off her knee.

Not fast. Fast came at the end. Up at the rate grass came up in wind, foot placed at the marked toe-spot, three paces in the slide-step she had been taught the year of her scarring, in at his back at an angle past the carry of his sword-side, cord over his wrist and twisted before he could take the hand-disc up to his mouth to speak.

He was good. He turned in the cord and did not lose the disc, and his left was coming for the sword and had it half drawn when her knee went into the back of his right knee and her free hand came up under his jaw and her knife was at the soft hollow under his ear before his weight transferred.

"Drop it," she said. Threshing Yard register. "Sword. Hand-disc. Keep the voice out of it."

He let the sword re-sheathe. The move of a man taught that a sheathed sword was still a sword, and a lost sword in contract accounting was a cost. She approved. She did not remove the knife.

"Hand-disc."

He lowered it slowly between his boots and set it on the grass with his fingertips.

"Name."

"Mara."

Contract name. Fine.

"Horst."

"Yes."

"Brief."

"Authenticate signature. Secure the disc. Prefer the holder alive. Report before close."

"You were reporting."

"I was."

"Outcome."

"Signature confirmed. Holder at the recess. Company four. Request instruction."

"Request instruction. Smart. What were you going to do if the instruction was *close*."

"What I was trained to do."

"Alive."

"Preferred."

"If preferred failed."

He did not answer. The silence answered.

She had known this was the answer. She had known it since the ford. The professional part of her had filed it. The other part had filed it somewhere heavier.

"Not today," she said.

His jaw was flat against the knife.

"You are going to kneel. You are going to tell me where the other four of Horst's team are. Then you are going to walk back to New Plymouth and tell Horst *signature did not register*. You can say you lost contact. You can say the disc was a decoy. Tell him anything except the truth."

"The truth."

"What is on the other side of this hill. Horst's office does not have a brief for it. If you tell him the truth, the domestic office comes with five teams, and the five teams make the hill a place where the question of what to do with what's inside it is decided by people whose job is to decide questions that way. You are one man. I am one woman. We decide this like one and one."

Her shoulders vibrated at the clavicle. She kept the knife where it was.

"If that sounds like mercy, it is not. It is a contract. If Horst's men come over this ridge in the next month, I find you first. Alone. In a house at an hour of my choosing. You know I can."

"I know who you were."

"Then kneel."

He knelt.

---

She bound him at the wrist and ankle and pushed him gently onto his side. She did not gag him — a gag on an unwilling man in open country was a thing that killed men who had not been meant to be killed.

"Where are the other four."

"One on the valley road with the horses. Two in New Plymouth with Horst. One in Stillwater on a different matter."

"The one with the horses holds station until sundown. You will return by sundown without a signal. You will ride for New Plymouth with him. You will report *signature did not register* and take your fee and not come east again. The one in Stillwater: whatever his matter is, it is not this hill. He gets the same report when he surfaces."

"I will not come east again."

"Good."

She stood. The vibration had moved to a band across her ribs and the band was the price. She walked up-slope keeping to the lee, touched Edvar's shoulder in passing — a palm on the coat, the way you touched an old dog that had held its stand. Edvar did not turn. The horse did not blow.

"Clear," she said. For his ear.

"I'm here."

"The cart stays. I'll come for you when."

"I'll be here when."

She climbed the last twenty paces to the passage mouth. Maren still at the recess, hair at her cheek, thumb on the disc. Had heard none of it, or had heard all of it and done the work of not turning.

Vane put her palm on the wall at the angle — the cool held at the surface, as Aldric had said it would — and she called in, low.

"Aldric. Davan. The slope is held. Mine. The one who was coming is off his feet."

A pause inside. Then Aldric, low: "Thank you."

She did not answer. *Thank you* was not a thing she took yet. She stood at the angle with her hand on the wall and the vibration in her ribs and the sword and the hand-disc at her hip, and the day's work — in the sense it was ever done — was done.

Under her boots, through the hill, something was being said. Something was being answered. Not her language. Not anyone's language she had been hired to read.

She stood the angle anyway. Which was, she thought, the new thing. You stood angles for languages that were not yours.

---

### *Davan*

He had not left the shelf.

His pad was still against the object. He had said *ready* at the passage mouth and his body had turned with Aldric's toward the passage, and then his body had turned back.

The one kept thing his finger had chosen had not asked him to stay. The room had not asked him. The staying had been his.

Outside, on the slope, a man was being spoken to by Vane. He could not hear the words. The passage at that angle did not carry voice. But he had — in the underside of his forearms where the new color lived — the quality of it. A man's fear, reined in. A professional discipline holding the fear flat. The cost of the holding. Vane's own pressure on the slope, which he had come to know at the cart in the last three days — not anger. *Economy*. A woman doing the least motion that produced the correct result, because the economy was what had been left her that was still hers.

His wrists were lit low. He did not look at them. He had stopped looking at them a long way back. He had come, by the slow route a body took to its own instruments, to trust what his wrists said the way an older carpenter trusted what his plane said.

He did not want to pick the object up.

He wanted — and the wanting was a new register — to ask the object a question. Not in words. In the register his skin had made with the wall's register, when the new color had settled in him and the wall had held the color and the holding had been the thing the material did because *keeping* was what the material did.

He put his palm against the flat side of the object. Not the pad of a finger this time. The palm.

The object was at the same register as the walls. He had expected this. He had not expected the difference — the object was *denser*. The wall held what came near it; the object kept what was already inside it. The object was a wall in miniature, closed on a thing that had been put in it to be there, and what had been put in it was pressing gently outward against the inside of its own holding the way a seed pressed gently outward against the inside of a seed.

He read the seed.

He did not try to read *what* was in the object. He had not earned the what. The what was a different work — an opening work, a beginning-of-a-line-of-people work, a work none of the four of them was the instrument for. He read, instead, the quality of the holding. The long patience of it. The absence of any hurry.

What was being held in this room, and in the four containers under Aldric's temple, was — Aldric had said it last night through the small lamp at camp — *preserved, viable, human*. The thing under his palm was kin to those four, and not the same: the part the hull had kept when the rest was carried west, generations before any temple stood over the cache that took it.

*A whole line of people, kept.*

*Not kept like a coat kept. Not kept like a memory kept. Kept the way a seed is kept — alive at a register where the life is paused, and the life is still the life, and the life is waiting without knowing it is waiting.*

*And if we were to wake them, we would have to become a thing we are not, first.*

He took his palm off.

His wrist-light did not fade. It lived in the tendon. It had been living there since yesterday morning, and it would live there for the rest of his life. The color was not his mutation any more. The color was what his mutation had been reaching for all twenty years, and had reached — the color of the edge of dawn a morning nobody had seen, a morning the country was having for the first time.

He turned from the shelf.

Aldric was at the passage-mouth and past it, at the angle where Vane had called him. Davan did not go to the angle. He had not been called. He crossed to the center of the floor and stood there with his hands at his sides and let his wrists hold their light, and he said — not aloud; to the room — the thing he needed to say before he left.

*I am not going to ask you to be opened.*

He stood a moment with the sentence in his chest.

*I am not going to ask anyone to be opened.*

The walls took his color and held it a hand's breadth and gave it back. The color between his body and the material was a color neither his nor the material's, and he had come, in the last half-hour, to think of that color as the color *deciding* made when a body decided against the grain of the thing it might, under other pressure, have said yes to.

He looked down at his hands. The light in his wrists was the color it had been — the color he had not named yet, the color that was not amber and not gold and not the blue of calm but was something his body had arrived at on its own without asking him. He turned his hands over. The color moved with the turning. Decided.

The originals he was kin to — the right-hand cell in Aldric's study, the holds at this ship's stern, the four containers under the Confluence — were his ancestors. Kin across a distance his body did not quite have the words for.

He did not know what he would have come to think in a year, or ten. Today, this hour, the answer his body gave was: *let them rest. Let the Chrysalis be what the Chrysalis is. I am the Chrysalis now. What I owe the originals is the attention, not the un-making.*

He was not going to say this to Aldric yet. Aldric had another instrument. Maren had another. Vane had another. He said it to the room.

The room held it without answering, the way the room held everything it was asked to hold. The lack of answer was not silence. The lack of answer was what answering meant when you were a room whose work was holding. The room's yes and no were the same thing: *held.*

He walked, slowly, toward the passage.

---

### *Aldric*

The passage carried his step and handed it back.

He stopped at the angle Vane had named.

Beyond the recess, beyond Maren's shoulder, down-slope — a flat register of grass and rust and a man's boot in it at the rest-angle a boot took when the boot was not planning to move again for a while. Vane was coming up from the lee with the carriage of a worker whose work had landed. The vibration in the set of her ribs he caught at the second look. Not there at the cart in the grey morning. There now. She had spent something to do what she had done.

He had asked her to spend it, without asking.

He would keep the account.

The *mine* in her call went down like a stone in still water. He did not step past it. The slope was hers. The storage behind him was — he did not yet have a verb for it — *attended*. The hill was Maren's knee. The cart was Edvar's hand. The breach was the morning's. He was the one at the angle between the passage and the slope, weight on the balls of his feet the way a man stood when his body did not yet know whether the next move was forward or back.

His body did not have to know.

"Thank you," he said. Down the lee. She did not answer, which was the correct not-answering.

He put his palm against the wall of the passage and let the cool be, and he thought, for the second time in an hour, the sentence he had been composing for three months.

*The next Keeper is going to be the one who took the floor up.*

He had been carrying it as something about a future Aldric — three months older, five years older — who had gone back to the Confluence and had, with the slow patience of institutional change, persuaded the Temple to admit what the chamber under the altar held. The composed thing was the wrong composition.

*The next Keeper is not a future me. The next Keeper is me, now. The floor has been taken up. I am standing on the taken-up floor.*

It did not land as fear. He tried the word the way a man tried a garment he had not worn for a season; the garment did not fit. What he carried was closer to *consequence*. Thirty years of weight at a certain balance, now being re-balanced, and the re-balancing was the body learning the new load-point. The hand no longer went to the lamp-cord because the lamp-cord was not the instrument.

Three things, then, in the register a man had three things when he had stopped composing and had started moving:

*The records are not a find. The records are a floor. The floor is under my life's work. The life's work does not continue the way it was going.*

*The floor does not belong to me. It belongs to anyone who will have to live with what the floor is — which is every living person on Gaius.* He stopped here. *Belong* was a property word; this was not property. What it was — owed, maybe; *owed* to anyone who had to live with it. *I am not the one who tells them. I am the one who does not stop them from being told.*

He thought of Crane — the danger of an officer of a company whose shareholder was a theology, and whose shareholder had just taken a loss of ten thousand years of dividend. He thought of the long letter he was going to have to write. It would not be to Brennan. Would not be to Cael. *The letter is to the next reader,* he thought. *The next reader is not a Keeper. The next reader is the country.*

He walked out of the passage into the morning.

Pale blue sky, wind-thinned, the overnight weather cleared by the dry wind off the country. He did not look up. He was not ready. He looked across the slope to Maren, stopped one pace short, put his hands flat on the tops of his thighs the way a man put his hands when his hands were honoring a work that was not his.

"The storage held," he said. Low. "Davan is coming out."

"Yes."

"The man."

"Vane."

"Yes."

A pause.

"The records say. I have read what I could read. I will read again. It was as we suspected and more. There is something we will need to decide about, and it is not a decision I am going to decide for the four of us."

"All right."

Her thumb had not moved. Her shoulder was the shoulder of a girl who had knelt at a recess for two hours and would kneel at it for another two if the hill asked her. He could not tell, from where he stood, whether she was breathing. Then he could. She was.

"When you are ready," he said.

"When I am ready."

His hand at his side did not move toward any cord. The hand was at his thigh. The thigh was his thigh. The hand was his hand.

He stepped back two paces and turned toward where the passage met the morning, and he waited for Davan to come out.

---

### *Maren*

Something was beginning under her thumb.

Not new. Continuing. The hum had stopped and the bone-sound had begun and the saying-and-answer — Davan's phrase, from the outcrop at dusk — had been running under the hill since the seating. Her knee had gone to the kind of numbness where the numbness was its own sensation. Her thumb was on the symbol.

The disc was warm and lit and not hers any more and still hers. *Hers* was not a category of having. *Hers* was a category of carrying. She had carried the disc for three years and the disc had carried her for three years, and now it was carrying her and the hill both, and the carrying was the relation, and the relation did not belong to anyone.

She had not wept at her father's coat. She was not going to weep at the recess either. The weeping country was a country for later. Her body was doing a different work today.

Behind her, on the slope, she had heard Vane. Not the words. The pace. The slide-step Vane had practiced on the rooming-house floor in New Plymouth. The pitched-low professional register Vane had used once on the road from the woodcutter's shelter when she had been talking a bolted trail-mare down. Maren had not turned. Turning would have been a question, and she had not had a question to ask.

Aldric had come out and stood beside her and not touched her, and had said *when you are ready*, and had stepped back two paces to give her the recess. The giving of the recess was the same thing Edvar had done at the shoulder of bare stone when he had put his hand on her hair and taken it away. Two older men had said *go* this morning in the only word each had for *go*, and both had let her go.

She was still at the recess.

Under her thumb a second resonance had arrived.

The bone-sound was the continuous condition, the tide's pull of being at the recess. The second came up through the first at about the height of her hip — Davan's *far*, the saying-and-answer at a distance the word *distance* was not built for. It was in the underside of her jaw where jaw met neck. A place her body had used, when she had been a child, to tell her when a frog had moved in the rushes on the other side of the pond at Ashwick before her eye had caught it.

Something far was answering.

The answer had been coming for two hours. It was close now, to whatever *close* meant at this distance.

She kept her thumb on the symbol. She waited.

---

The answer arrived as voice.

Not at her ear. At the hill. The storage behind her took a breath the way rooms took breaths, and into the breath the voice came — not a new voice; the one Aldric had heard at the voiced wall and had told them about by the camp lamp last night. A middle-register man's voice, tired, at the cadence of a man who had sat down at the end of a day he had not expected to have. Aldric had said: *the words did not resolve*.

The words resolved. Not because any of them had learned the language. Because the saying-and-answer — the *far* Davan had been reading — had arrived at *close*, and in the arriving it had brought with it the matching half of the record that had been locked against the voice in the wall for ten thousand seasons. The answer laid itself over the saying the way a transparent page laid over a page it had been drawn to fit.

Not translated. *Paired.* Maren heard, with one ear, the long vowels and the consonants her country's tongue did not use; with another ear — if a body had an ear for this — she heard the sense.

The man was saying: *This is the captain of the fourth ship.*

A pause. A breath. The breath of a man who had just set something down on a desk he was not going to pick up again.

*We failed our descent. The lander is down in the eastern foothills. Most of us did not reach the ground. Those of us who did are not, I think, going to remain who we are for very long. The world is in us. There is no way out of it.*

Another pause.

*We have moved what could be moved from the holds to the secondary cache at the river-meeting site. The archive goes with it. The archive — it does not open. Not with us. Not any more. It is meant for whoever comes after, if there is a whoever, if the after is kind.*

*To the three still on station: we did what we could. You are not coming for us. I am sorry. Hold your post as long as the post will hold you. When it does not hold you any longer, go home. We are home already, in a way. The children will be a new people. I think they will be a people worth the coming.*

A longer pause.

*This is the last of the log I am going to record. There is a morning outside and I am going to go sit in it. I am told there is a hillside to the east that moves in the light. It is our own hull. Imagine. I am going to go look at it.*

The man said the mission name — the four-letter unit, the inner orbit figure doing the work of a letter. Aldric had told them about this. The man said it the way a man said a name. Then a single word with the shape of a sign-off. A man getting up from a desk.

The voice was finished.

Maren did not move.

She had heard the whole of it on her knee at the recess, and the hearing had gone through her like a bell rung at a distance of ten thousand seasons, and the bell had rung as a bell rings when the hand that strikes it is, just before striking, alive.

Aldric, behind her, had made no sound.

Davan, at the passage mouth, had made no sound.

Vane, at the angle, had made no sound.

She heard them, through the saying-and-answer, being four people who had heard the same voice at the same moment and had been, each of them, the only person in the world who had heard it, because each of them had heard it in the register their own body had made to catch it.

The captain had looked at a hillside in the morning and had gone out to sit in the light. Ten thousand years ago. Before the word *Ashwick*. Before the word *Keeper*. Before the word *bloodline*. The captain had gone out to sit.

Under her thumb the disc was warm. Warmer than her hand, warmer than the morning air, warmer than anything she had carried for three years without knowing what she was carrying. The warmth moved up through her thumb and into the base of her wrist. She stayed where she was, the knee long past sensation, and let the warmth come.

---

The answer arrived as light.

Above her. A thinning of the morning across the whole east sky, the way a cloud thinned when a cloud was about to give up. There was no cloud. The morning had been clear for an hour. For three seconds the color of daylight acquired an under-register — a second color under the pale blue — that did not belong to morning.

She lifted her eye from the disc.

Her knee was past caring about knees. She only lifted her eye — from the recess to the slope-line, from the slope-line to the crest of the eastern ridge, from the crest of the ridge to the sky above the ridge.

The sky above the ridge held three moons.

---

She had been looking away from the moons for three years.

She had known they were in the sky. She had looked at her boots. She had looked at the fire. She had looked at Davan's face or Vane's back or Aldric's hands. She had not looked up. Looking up had been a thing some version of herself had done when she had been a child and had not yet known the moons were a country she had not been invited into, and that version had been, for three years, one she had kept at a distance while the version with the disc in her coat kept her eyes on the ground.

Now she looked up.

Three moons, full-bright together, over the eastern hills. The ridge of the country ran under them in a long dark line. The sky between them was the dry pale blue of the eastern weather, and the moons were each what they were and were also, as she looked, three of a thing.

Her eye gave her what her eye had not given her in three years. Not that they were ships. Her eye did not know ships. Her eye gave her that they were a set. That the sky was holding them at the positions a sky held things at when the things had been placed at the positions and had stayed. They were not drifting. They were *at*. At the places they were at. At the work they were at. Doing what they had been doing, which was — because her body had learned the word in the passage, and at the recess, and in the captain's quiet voice a minute ago — *holding*.

They were holding their stations.

They had been holding them the whole time she had been walking. They had been holding them when her father had walked east and had lain down four paces short of the recess. They had been holding them when her mother had died in the winter and she had held her mother's comb the first time with the full understanding it was her mother's.

A memory she had not carried came up under the moons-look.

She had been five. Her father in the courtyard at dusk. He had lifted her onto his shoulder and pointed at the sky and said *look*, and she had looked, and the moons had been three of a set even then, and he had not said what they were, but he had made her look.

He had been making her look.

She had forgotten.

She looked in the way he had taught her before she had had the words to know what looking was, and she let what was in the sky be in the sky, and what was in her be in her, and for a moment, the two of them were at a register that did not have a name.

The register was the register of the Tidemark harbor-master, whose words she had read at eleven without remembering afterward that she had read them. The words had sat in her. She knew them as the condition at her sternum when she looked up at three full-bright moons over an eastern ridge with her thumb on a disc that had been her father's, knowing herself to be carrying a thing worn into the shape of a thing you were born knowing.

Grief that did not belong to her personally.

Grief that was hers anyway.

She let it be hers.

She did not cry.

---

The four of them were going to disagree.

She knew this with the part of her the captain's voice had opened.

She could hear the shape of it already. Aldric would want the country told — the whole long lie of the Rite laid open, because a man who had given thirty years to it could not leave it standing for the next child to kneel under. Davan would want the holds left to rest: attention, he would say, not opening; you did not un-make a world to wake the one it had buried. Vane would want no one told at all. She was at the angle below with her back to the slope, already at the next motion the way she was always already at the next motion — and she had sent a man down the hill just now with a lie in his mouth precisely so that no one with five teams and a brief would come to decide this the way such people decided things.

And Maren did not yet know what she wanted. The disc had been her father's; the hill had taken him four paces short of the recess. She had a stake the other three did not, and no position yet to set beside theirs. She would have to find it at the cart, in the grass, with the three of them watching her find it.

The captain had said *the children will be a new people*. He had said *I think they will be a people worth the coming*. He had said it ten thousand years ago and had gone out to sit in the morning and had not come back.

The disagreement was going to be the thing they made, together, instead of the decision.

She breathed in the eastern air. The air was dry and held and smelled of the grass and the rust and the faint indoors that still came down the passage from the storage. She lifted her thumb.

The disc stayed seated. The symbol stayed lit. The bone-sound did not stop. The saying-and-answer held. Whatever had been begun did not need her thumb to continue. Her thumb had been the beginning. The continuing was the hill's work.

She put her hand on her thigh. She did not stand yet.

She looked once more at the three moons over the eastern hills. The moons did not look back, because the moons were not in the business of looking. They were at their stations. They were keeping.

She let them keep.

She stood.

She turned from the recess and walked, with the stiff knee, up the slope toward the passage where Davan and Aldric were waiting and where Vane was coming up from the lee.

The records were inside the hill, and the four containers were under the floor of a temple six days' ride to the west, and none of what was in either place was going to be resolved today. What would be resolved today was that they were going to walk down to Edvar at the cart, and eat something, and sit in the grass, and begin to disagree. The disagreement was going to be the beginning.

She looked up one more time.

She had not looked in three years. She had looked twice in three minutes. She was going to look tomorrow.

She walked up the slope.

Behind her the hill kept keeping. Above her the moons kept their stations. Ahead were three people who did not agree, and one old man at a cart, and a contract man tied in the grass, and a long day of which most had not yet happened.

She kept walking.

---
